“By God,” he said, “Church! State! Army! H.M. Ministry: H.M. Opposition: H.M. City Man.﻿ ﻿… All the governing class! All rotten! Thank God we’ve got a navy!﻿ ﻿… But perhaps that’s rotten too! Who knows! Britannia needs no bulwarks﻿ ﻿… Then thank God for the upright young man and the virtuous maiden in the summer fields: he Tory of the Tories as he should be: she suffragette of the militants: militant here on earth﻿ ﻿… as she should be! As she should be! In the early decades of the twentieth century however else can a woman keep clean and wholesome! Ranting from platforms, splendid for the lungs: bashing in policemen’s helmets.﻿ ﻿… No! It’s I do that: my part, I think, miss!﻿ ﻿… Carrying heavy banners in twenty mile processions through streets of Sodom. All splendid! I bet she’s virtuous. But you don’t have to bet. It isn’t done on certainties. You can tell it in the eye. Nice eyes! Attractive back. Virginal cockiness.﻿ ﻿… Yes, better occupation for mothers of empire than attending on lewd husbands year in year out till you’re as hysterical as a female cat on heat.﻿ ﻿… You could see it in her: that woman: you can see it in most of ’em! Thank God then for the Tory, upright young married man and the suffragette kid﻿ ﻿… Backbone of England!﻿ ﻿…”
He killed another flower.
“But by God! we’re both under a cloud! Both!﻿ ﻿… That kid and I! And General Lord Edward Campion, Lady Claudine Sandbach, and the Hon. Paul, M.P. (suspended), to spread the tale.﻿ ﻿… And forty toothless fogies in the club to spread it: and no end visiting books yawning to have your names cut out of them, my boy!﻿ ﻿… My dear boy: I so regret: your father’s oldest friend.﻿ ﻿… By Jove, the pistachio nut of that galantine! Repeating! Breakfast gone wrong: gloomy reflections! Thought I could stand anything: digestion of an ostrich.﻿ ﻿… But no! Gloomy reflections: I’m hysterical: like that large-eyed whore! For same reason! Wrong diet and wrong life: diet meant for partridge shooters over the turnips consumed by the sedentary. England the land of pills﻿ ﻿… *Das Pillen-Land*, the Germans call us. Very properly﻿ ﻿… And, damn it: outdoor diet: boiled mutton, turnips: sedentary life﻿ ﻿… and forced up against the filthiness of the world: your nose in it all day long!﻿ ﻿… Why, hang it, I’m as badly off as she. Sylvia’s as bad as Duchemin!﻿ ﻿… I’d never have thought that﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“天哪，”他说，“教会！国家！陆军！陛下的大臣：陛下的反对党：陛下的城里人。……整个统治阶级！全烂了！谢天谢地咱们还有海军！……可兴许海军也烂了！谁知道呢！不列颠无需堡垒……那就谢天谢地，谢夏天田野里正直的青年和贞静的少女：他是托利党人中的托利，理当如此；她是激进派里的女权斗士：在地上战斗……也理当如此！理当如此！二十世纪早期的年月里，一个女人不这样，还能怎样保持干净康健！在讲坛上慷慨陈词，最养肺；敲碎警察的头盔。……不！敲头盔的事是我干的：那是我的一份，我想，小姐！……打着沉重的旗帜，在所多玛的街市上走二十英里的游行队列。样样精彩！我打赌她是贞洁的。其实不用赌。赌注不押在定局上。看眼睛就知道。眼睛好看！背影诱人。处女的傲态。……是的，比起年年月月伺候淫荡的丈夫、直到自己发情母猫似的歇斯底里，这可是帝国母亲们更好的营生。……你从她身上看得出来：那种女人：从她们大多数人身上都看得出来！那么感谢上帝，为了正直的托利党已婚青年和女权小斗士……英格兰的脊梁！……”
他又杀了一朵花。
“可是天哪！咱们俩都罩在阴云底下！俩人！……那孩子和我！还有爱德华·坎皮恩勋爵将军、克劳迪娜·桑德巴赫夫人、还有那位尊敬的保罗议员（停职中），负责散布故事。……还有俱乐部里四十个没牙的老朽负责散布：数不清的留言簿张着大口，等着把你的名字划掉，我的孩子！……我亲爱的孩子：我非常遗憾：令尊最老的朋友。……哎呀，那肉冻里的开心果！返上来了！早餐吃坏了：阴郁的杂念！原以为自己什么都扛得住：鸵鸟一样的消化力。……不顶事！阴郁的杂念：我歇斯底里了：像那个大眼睛的荡妇！同一个缘故！错的食谱和错的生活：给芜菁地里打松鸡的人备下的饭食，喂给了坐办公室的。英格兰，药丸之国……德国人管咱们叫 Das Pillen-Land（药丸国）。名副其实。……而且，见鬼：户外的食谱：煮羊肉、芜菁：案牍的生活……又被硬按到全世界的污秽跟前：一整天鼻子都埋在里头！……咳，我的境况竟同她一样糟。西尔维娅同杜什明一样糟！……这我可从没想到过……

===Original===

No wonder meat’s turned to uric acid﻿ ﻿… prime cause of neurasthenia.﻿ ﻿… What a beastly muddle! Poor Macmaster! He’s finished. Poor devil: he’d better have ogled this kid. He could have sung: ‘Highland Mary’ a better tune than ‘This is the end of every man’s desire’﻿ ﻿… You can cut it on his tombstone, you can write it on his card that a young man tacked on to a paulo-post pre-Raphaelite prostitute.﻿ ﻿…”
He stopped suddenly in his walk. It had occurred to him that he ought not to be walking with this girl!
“But damn it all,” he said to himself, “she makes a good screen for Sylvia﻿ ﻿… who cares! She must chance it. She’s probably struck off all their beastly visiting lists already﻿ ﻿… as a suffragette!”
Miss Wannop, a cricket pitch or so ahead of him, hopped over a stile: left foot on the step, right on the top bar, a touch of the left on the other steps, and down on the white, drifted dust of a road they no doubt had to cross. She stood waiting, her back still to him.﻿ ﻿… Her nimble footwork, her attractive back, seemed to him, now, infinitely pathetic. To let scandal attach to her was like cutting the wings of a goldfinch: the bright creature, yellow, white, golden and delicate that in the sunlight makes a haze with its wings beside thistle-tops. No; damn it! it was worse; it was worse than putting out, as the bird-fancier does, the eyes of a chaffinch.﻿ ﻿… Infinitely pathetic!
Above the stile, in an elm, a chaffinch said: “Pink! pink!”
The imbecile sound filled him with rage; he said to the bird:
“Damn your eyes! *Have* them put out, then!” The beastly bird that made the odious noise, when it had its eyes put out, at least squealed like any other skylark or tomtit. Damn all birds, field naturalists, botanists! In the same way he addressed the back of Miss Wannop: “Damn your eyes! *Have* your chastity impugned then? What do you speak to strange men in public for! You know you can’t do it in this country. If it were a decent, straight land like Ireland where people cut each other’s throats for clean issues: Papist versus Prot﻿ ﻿… well, you could! You could walk through Ireland from east to west and speak to every man you met.﻿ ﻿… ‘Rich and rare were the gems she wore﻿ ﻿…’ To every man you met as long as he wasn’t an Englishman of good birth: *that* would deflower you!” He was scrambling clumsily over the stile. “Well!

===Chinese===

怪不得肉都变成尿酸了……神经衰弱的头号根由。……一塌糊涂！可怜的麦克马斯特！他完了。可怜的家伙：他还不如当初就对这个孩子眉来眼去。他本可以唱《高原的玛丽》，总比《这是每个男人欲望的尽头》调子好……可以把这句刻上他的墓碑，也可以写在他钉在一张前拉斐尔派半吊子妓女名片的卡片上。…”
他遽然收住脚。他忽然想到，自己不该同这姑娘并肩走路！
“可是见鬼，”他自语，“她给西尔维娅当好挡箭牌……管他呢！她只好碰运气。他们那些臭留言簿八成早把她划掉了……谁叫她是女权分子！”
沃诺普小姐在他前头约一个板球场远的地方，单脚跳过栅栏梯：左脚踏上横档，右脚踩上梯顶，左脚再在另一侧横档上一点，落在大路那边白茫茫的浮尘里——这条路他们无疑得穿过去。她站着等，背仍朝着他。……她那轻快的脚下功夫、她那诱人的背影，此刻在他看来无限凄楚。让丑闻沾上她，就像铰掉红雀的翅膀：那鲜亮的生灵，黄的、白的、金的、纤巧的，在阳光下于蓟顶旁边用翅膀撒出一团光雾。不；见鬼！比这更坏；比养鸟人剜掉苍头燕雀的眼睛还坏。……无限凄楚！
栅栏梯上头一棵榆树里，一只苍头燕雀叫：“Pink! pink!”
这白痴的叫声叫他心头火起；他对那鸟说：
“该死你的眼睛！那就叫人剜了去吧！”这制造可憎噪音的混账鸟，眼睛一旦被剜，至少会像别的云雀山雀一样尖叫。该死的一切鸟、一切田野博物学家、一切植物学家！他又照样对沃诺普小姐的背影说：“该死你的眼睛！那就让人糟践你的贞洁吧！谁叫你跟陌生男人搭话！你知道在这个国家不行。要是这是个正派的、直来直去的地方，比如爱尔兰，人们为干净的议题互相割喉：天主教对基督新教……那你就行！你可以从东到西横穿爱尔兰，同你遇见的每个男人说话。……‘她佩的珠宝贵又奇……’同你遇见的每个男人，只要他不是个出身高贵的英格兰人：那才会玷污你！”他手脚笨拙地翻着栅栏梯。“好！

===Original===

*be* deflowered then: *lose* your infantile reputation. You’ve spoken to strange pitch: you’re defiled﻿ ﻿… with the benefit of Clergy, Army, Cabinet, Administration, Opposition, mothers and old maids of England.﻿ ﻿… They’d all tell you you can’t talk to a strange man, in the sunlight, on the links without becoming a screen for some Sylvia or other.﻿ ﻿… Then *be* a screen for Sylvia: *get* struck off the visiting books! The deeper you’re implicated, the more bloody villain I am! I’d like the whole lot to see us here: that would settle it.﻿ ﻿…”
Nevertheless, when at the roadside he stood level with Miss Wannop who did not look at him, and saw the white road running to right and left with no stile opposite, he said gruffly to her:
“Where’s the next stile? I hate walking on roads!” She pointed with her chin along the opposite hedgerow. “Fifty yards!” she said.
“Come along!” he exclaimed, and set off at a trot almost. It had come into his head that it would be just the beastly sort of thing that would happen if a car with General Campion and Lady Claudine and Paul Sandbach all aboard should come along that blinding stretch of road: or one alone: perhaps the General driving the dogcart he affected. He said to himself:
“By God! If they cut this girl I’d break their backs over my knee!” and he hastened. “Just the beastly thing that *would* happen.” The road probably led straight in at the front door of Mountby!
Miss Wannop trotted along a little in his rear. She thought him the most extraordinary man: as mad as he was odious. Sane people, if they’re going to hurry﻿—but *why* hurry!﻿—do it in the shade of field hedgerows, not in the white blaze of county council roads. Well, he could go ahead. In the next field she was going to have it out with him: she didn’t intend to be hot with running: let him be, his hateful, but certainly noticeable eyes, protruding at her like a lobster’s; but she cool and denunciatory in her pretty blouse.
There was a dogcart coming behind them!
Suddenly it came into her head: that fool had been lying when he had said that the police meant to let them alone: lying over the breakfast-table.﻿ ﻿… The dogcart contained the police: after them! She didn’t waste time looking round: she wasn’t a fool like Atalanta in the egg race. She picked up her heels and sprinted.

===Chinese===

那就被玷污吧：丢掉你那幼稚的名声。你同陌生的球场搭了话：你被玷污了……带着教士、军队、内阁、行政、反对党、英格兰的母亲们和老姑娘们的祝福。……他们全会告诉你：在太阳底下、在球场上，你不同某个西尔维娅之类当挡箭牌，就不能同陌生男人说话。……那就给西尔维娅当挡箭牌吧：让人从留言簿上划掉吧！你被牵得越深，我越是血腥的恶棍！我倒巴不得他们全体瞧见我们在这儿：那就一了百了了。…”
话虽如此，等他到了路边、同看也不看他的沃诺普小姐站平了，望着那条白路朝左右伸展开去、对面并没有栅栏梯，他还是粗声粗气地对她说：
“下一个梯子在哪儿？我讨厌在路上走！”她用下巴朝对面绿篱一指。“五十码！”她说。
“快走！”他喊道，几乎小跑起来。他脑子里冒出一个念头：倘若坎皮恩将军和克劳迪娜夫人还有保罗·桑德巴赫全坐在一辆汽车里，偏偏驶过那段晃眼的大路，那正是会发生的那种混账事：或者单独一个：也许将军驾着他喜欢的那辆双轮马车。他自语：
“天哪！他们要是敢小看这姑娘，我把他们摁在膝盖上折断他们的脊梁！”他脚下加紧。“偏偏就是会发生的混账事。”这条路八成正对着芒特比的前门！
沃诺普小姐在他身后不远处一路小跑。她认为他是天下顶顶古怪的男人：可恶得同疯癫一样彻底。神志正常的人要是想赶路——可赶什么路呢！——也会在田边绿篱的荫凉里赶，不会在郡议会修的大白道上晒着赶。好吧，让他前头走。到了下一块田里，她要同他把话说清楚：她不打算跑得一身热汗：由他去，他那双可恨却确实显眼的眼睛，龙虾似的朝她凸着；而她自己凉凉静静、义正词严，穿着她漂亮的上衣。
身后来了一辆双轮马车！
她脑子里蕦地闪过：那家伙在早餐桌上说警察不会来找她们，是撒谎。……马车里坐着警察：追她们来了！她不浪费工夫回头；她可不像蛋赛里的阿塔兰忒那样傻。她提起脚跟就冲刺。

===Original===

She beat him by a yard and half to the kissing-gate, white in the hedge: panicked; breathing hard. He panted into it, after her: the fool hadn’t the sense to let her through first. They were jammed in together: face to face, panting! An occasion on which sweethearts kiss in Kent: the gate being made in three, the inner flange of the V moving on hinges. It stops cattle getting through: but this great lout of a Yorkshireman didn’t know: trying to push through like a mad bullock! Now they were caught. Three weeks in Wandsworth gaol.﻿ ﻿… Oh hang.﻿ ﻿…
The voice of Mrs. Wannop﻿—of course it was only mother! Twenty feet on high or so behind the kicking mare, with a good, round face like a peony﻿—said:
“Ah, you can jam my Val in a gate and hold her﻿ ﻿… but she gave you seven yards in twenty and beat you to the gate. That was her father’s ambition!” She thought of them as children running races. She beamed down, round-faced and simple, on Tietjens from beside the driver, who had a black, slouch hat and the grey beard of St. Peter.
“My dear boy!” she said, “my dear boy; it’s such a satisfaction to have you under my roof!”
The black horse reared on end, the patriarch sawing at its mouth. Mrs. Wannop said unconcernedly: “Stephen Joel! I haven’t done talking.”
Tietjens was gazing enragedly at the lower part of the horse’s sweat-smeared stomach.
“You soon will have,” he said, “with the girth in that state. Your neck will be broken.”
“Oh, I don’t think so,” Mrs. Wannop said. “Joel only bought the turnout yesterday.”
Tietjens addressed the driver with some ferocity:
“Here; get down, you,” he said. He held, himself, the head of the horse whose nostrils were wide with emotion: it rubbed its forehead almost immediately against his chest. He said: “Yes! yes! There! there!” Its limbs lost their tautness. The aged driver scrambled down from the high seat, trying to come down at first forward and then backwards. Tietjens fired indignant orders at him:
“Lead the horse into the shade of that tree. Don’t touch his bit: his mouth’s sore. Where did you get this job lot? Ashford market: thirty pounds: it’s worth more.﻿ ﻿… But, blast you, don’t you see you’ve got a thirteen hands pony’s harness for a sixteen and a half hands horse. Let the bit out: three holes: it’s cutting the animal’s tongue in half.﻿ ﻿… This animal’s a rig. Do you know what a rig is?

===Chinese===

她抢先一码半到了绿篱间那扇白色的亲吻门前：慌了神，喘着粗气。他喘着气跟在她身后挤进去：这傻瓜没有先让她过去的脑子。两人卡在了门里：脸对脸，喘成一团！在肯特，这本是情人们接吻的场合：这种门造作三片，V 字形的内活页装在铰链上。它能拦住牛：可这个约克郡大笨汉不知道：像头发疯的小公牛一样硬往里拱！这下被逮住了。温德斯沃思监狱蹲三个礼拜。……唉，罢了。…
沃诺普太太的声音——当然只是母亲！——在尥蹶子的母马后头二十来英尺高处，一张红扑扑的圆脸像朵牡丹——说：
“哈，你能把我的瓦尔卡在门里、抱住她……可她让你七码、二十里还是抢在你前头到了门边。那是她父亲的夙愿！”她把两人当作赛跑的孩子。她从车夫旁边笑吟吟地俯视着蒂金斯，圆脸、纯朴；那车夫戴黑毡帽，蓄着圣彼得式的灰胡子。
“亲爱的孩子！”她说，“亲爱的孩子；你能住到我家檐下，真是叫人高兴！”
黑马前蹄腾空立起，老族长锯着它的嘴。沃诺普太太若无其事地说：“斯蒂芬·乔尔！我话还没说完。”
蒂金斯怒冲冲瞪着马汗污的肚子下缘。
“快完了，”他说，“肚带都那样了。会摔断脖子的。”
“哦，我不这么想，”沃诺普太太说。“乔尔昨天才置办的这一套。”
蒂金斯相当凶地冲车夫发话：
“喂，你，下来。”他说。他自己擎住马头；马鼻孔激动得张得老大：几乎立刻就用额头蹭他的胸口。他说：“对！对！好！好！”马腿上的僵劲松了。年迈的车夫从高座上手脚并用地往下爬，先想朝前下，又想朝后下。蒂金斯朝他连珠炮似的下命令：
“把马牵到那棵树荫里去。别碰它的嚼口：嘴破了。你这套家伙哪儿淘来的？阿什福德集市：三十镑：值更多。……可你瞎了吗，这是给十三手高的矮马备的挽具，套在十六手半高的马上。把衔环放出来：放三格：它把牲口的舌头都快勒成两半了。……这牲口是匹半骟马。你知道什么是半骟马？

===Original===

If you give it corn for a fortnight it will kick you and the cart and the stable to pieces in five minutes one day.” He led the conveyance, Mrs. Wannop triumphantly complacent and all, into a patch of shade beneath elms.
“Loosen that bit, confound you,” he said to the driver. “Ah! you’re afraid.”
He loosened the bit himself, covering his fingers with greasy harness polish which he hated. Then he said:
“Can you hold his head or are you afraid of that too? You *deserve* to have him bite your hands off.” He addressed Miss Wannop: “Can *you*?”
She said:
“No! I’m afraid of horses. I can drive any sort of car; but I’m afraid of horses.”
He said:
“Very proper!” He stood back and looked at the horse: it had dropped its head and lifted its near hind foot, resting the toe on the ground: an attitude of relaxation.
“He’ll stand now!” he said. He undid the girth, bending down uncomfortably, perspiring and greasy: the girth-strap parted in his hand.
“It’s true,” Mrs. Wannop said. “I’d have been dead in three minutes if you hadn’t seen that. The cart would have gone over backwards﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens took out a large, complicated, horn-handled knife like a schoolboy’s. He selected a punch and pulled it open. He said to the driver:
“Have you got any cobbler’s thread? Any string? Any copper wire? A rabbit wire, now? Come, you’ve got a rabbit wire or you’re not a handy man.”
The driver moved his slouch hat circularly in negation. This seemed to be Quality who summons you for poaching if you own to possessing rabbit wires. Tietjens laid the girth along the shaft and punched into it with his punch.
“Woman’s work!” he said to Mrs. Wannop, “but it’ll take you home and last you six months as well﻿ ﻿… But I’ll sell this whole lot for you tomorrow.”
Mrs. Wannop sighed:
“I suppose it’ll fetch a ten pound note﻿ ﻿…” She said: “I ought to have gone to market myself.”
“No!” Tietjens answered: “I’ll get you fifty for it or I’m no Yorkshireman. This fellow hasn’t been swindling you. He’s got you deuced good value for money, but he doesn’t know what’s suited for ladies; a white pony and a basket-work chaise is what you want.”
“Oh, I like a bit of spirit,” Mrs. Wannop said.
“Of course you do,” Tietjens answered: “but this turnout’s too much.”
He sighed a little and took out his surgical needle.
“I’m going to hold this band together with this,” he said.

你要是喂它两个礼拜燕麦，不定哪天它五分钟里就把你、把车、把马房全踢散架。”他把这辆车马连同得意洋洋、心满意足的沃诺普太太一并牵进榆树荫下。
“把嚼口松开，混账，”他对车夫说。“啊！你怕。”
他自己动手松了嚼口，手指糊满了油乎乎的挽具油——他顶讨厌这个。然后他说：
“你牵得住它的头吗，还是这个你也怕？你就该叫它把你的手咬掉。”他转向沃诺普小姐：“你呢？”
她说：
“不！我怕马。什么样的汽车我都能开；可我怕马。”
他说：
“怕得对！”他退后一步看看马：马垂下了头，提起靠里侧的后腿，蹄尖点地：一副松弛的架势。
“它现在站得住了！”他说。他解开肚带，别扭地弯着腰，汗涔涔、油腻腻：肚带带子在他手里断成两截。
“是真的，”沃诺普太太说。“不是你看见，我三分钟就没命了。车都得向后翻…”
蒂金斯掏出一把大而复杂、角柄的刀，像学童用的。他挑出打孔锥，拔开。对车夫说：
“你有补鞋线吗？绳子？铜丝？兔丝呢？来，你有兔丝，不然就算不得个巧人。”
车夫把毡帽转了个圈表示没有。这看来是一位你若认领了兔丝就传你去问偷猎的“老爷”。蒂金斯把肚带顺车辕放平，拿锥子扎眼。
“女人的活计！”他对沃诺普太太说，“可它能把您拉回家，还能再顶半年……不过明天我替您把这整套卖了。”
沃诺普太太叹口气：
“我猜能卖十镑钞吧……”她说：“我本该自己上集市的。”
“不！”蒂金斯答，“我给您卖五十，不然我不算约克郡人。这家伙没坑您。他给您置办得顶划算，只是他不懂什么配太太们；您要的是一匹白矮马、一辆藤编敞车。”
“哦，我倒喜欢有点烈性的，”沃诺普太太说。
“您当然喜欢，”蒂金斯答：“可这一套火气太过了。”
他微微叹口气，掏出那根手术针。
“我打算拿这个把带子攢住，”他说。

“It’s so pliant it will make two stitches and hold forever.﻿ ﻿…”
But the handy man was beside him, holding out the contents of his pockets: a greasy leather pouch, a ball of beeswax, a knife, a pipe, a bit of cheese and a pale rabbit wire. He had made up his mind that *this* Quality was benevolent and he made offering of all his possessions.
Tietjens said: “Ah,” and then, while he unknotted the wire:
“Well! Listen﻿ ﻿… you bought this turnout of a higgler at the back door of the Leg of Mutton Inn.”
“Saracen’s ’Ed!” the driver muttered.
“You got it for thirty pounds because the higgler wanted money bad. *I* know. And dirt cheap.﻿ ﻿… But a rig isn’t everybody’s driving. All right for a vet or a horse-coper. Like the cart that’s too tall!﻿ ﻿… But you did damn well. Only you’re not what you were, are you, at thirty? And the horse looked to be a devil and the cart so high you couldn’t get out once you were in. And you kept it in the sun for two hours waiting for your mistress.”
“There wer’ a bit o’ lewth ’longside stable wall,” the driver muttered.
“Well! he didn’t like waiting,” Tietjens said placably. “You can be thankful your old neck’s not broken. Do this band up, one hole less for the bit I’ve taken in.”
He prepared to climb into the driver’s seat, but Mrs. Wannop was there before him, at an improbable altitude on the sloping watch-box with strapped cushions.
“Oh, no, you don’t,” she said, “no one drives me and my horse but me or my coachman when I’m about. Not even you, dear boy.”
“I’ll come with you then,” Tietjens said.
“Oh, no, you don’t,” she answered. “No one’s neck’s to be broken in this conveyance but mine and Joel’s,” she added: “perhaps tonight if I’m satisfied the horse is fit to drive.”
Miss Wannop suddenly exclaimed:
“Oh, *no*, mother.” But the handy man having climbed in, Mrs. Wannop flirted her whip and started the horse. She pulled up at once and leaned over to Tietjens:
“*What* a life for that poor woman,” she said. “We must *all* do all we can for her. She could have her husband put in a lunatic asylum tomorrow. It’s sheer self-sacrifice that she doesn’t.”
The horse went off at a gentle, regular trot.
Tietjens addressed Miss Wannop:
“What hands your mother’s got,” he said, “it isn’t often one sees a woman with hands like that on a horse’s mouth.﻿ ﻿… Did you see how she pulled up?﻿ ﻿…”

它那么柔韧，缝两针，管保一劳永逸。…”
可那巧人已凑到他身边，捧出他口袋里的全部家当：一只油腻的皮囊、一球蜂蜡、一把刀、一只烟斗、一小块干酪、一根发白的兔丝。他拿定主意，这位“老爷”是善人，便把全部家产奉献出来。
蒂金斯说：“啊。”随后，一边解着兔丝一边说：
“好！听着……你这套家伙，是从‘羊腿酒馆’后门外一个贩子手里买的。”
“撒拉森人头！”车夫咕哝。
“三十镑到手，因为那贩子急着等钱用。我知道。便宜透了。……可半骟马不是谁都驾驭得了的。兽医、马贩子凑合。……可你干得不赖。只是你到三十岁就不是当年的你了吧？那马瞧着像恶魔，车又高，一进去就出不来。你还让它在太阳底下晒了两个钟头等你们太太。”
“马房墙根有一搭背阴儿，”车夫咕哝。
“是啊！它不爱等，”蒂金斯心平气和地说。“你那条老脖子没断，就谢天谢地吧。把带子装上，衔环收一格，带子我收短了。”
他准备爬上车夫的座位，可沃诺普太太抢先上去了，高得离谱地坐在斜装着绑带坐垫的瞭望座上。
“哦，那可不行，”她说，“赶我和我的马的，只能是我，或者我在场时的我的车夫。连你也不行，亲爱的孩子。”
“那我送您一段。”蒂金斯说。
“哦，那也不行，”她答。“这条车上只断得了我跟乔尔两个人的脖子，”她添一句：“兴许今晚就断，要是我相看下来这马还赶得。”
沃诺普小姐突然喊：
“哦，别，妈。”可巧人已经爬上车，沃诺普太太一甩鞭，把马惊动起来。她立刻勒住，朝蒂金斯俯下身：
“那可怜的女人过的是什么日子啊，”她说。“咱们全都得尽力帮她。她明天就能把丈夫送进疯人院。她不肯，纯粹是自我牺牲。”

蒂金斯对沃诺普小姐说：

He was aware that, all this while, from the roadside, the girl had been watching him with shining eyes: intently even: with fascination.
“I suppose you think that a mighty fine performance,” she said.
“I didn’t make a very good job of the girth,” he said. “Let’s get off this road.”
“Setting poor, weak women in their places,” Miss Wannop continued. “Soothing the horse like a man with a charm. I suppose you soothe women like that too. I pity your wife.﻿ ﻿… The English country male! And making a devoted vassal at sight of the handy man. The feudal system all complete.﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“Well, you know, it’ll make him all the better servant to you if he thinks you’ve friends in the know. The lower classes are like that. Let’s get off this road.”
She said:
“You’re in a mighty hurry to get behind the hedge. Are the police after us or aren’t they? Perhaps you were lying at breakfast: to calm the hysterial nerves of a weak woman.”
“I wasn’t lying,” he said, “but I hate roads when there are field-paths﻿ ﻿…”
“That’s a phobia, like any woman’s,” she exclaimed.
She almost ran through the kissing-gate and stood awaiting him:
“I suppose,” she said, “if you’ve stopped off the police with your high and mighty male ways you think you’ve destroyed my romantic young dream. You haven’t. I don’t want the police after me. I believe I’d *die* if they put me in Wandsworth﻿ ﻿… I’m a coward.”
“Oh, no, you aren’t,” he said, but he was following his own train of thought, just as she wasn’t in the least listening to him. “I daresay you’re a heroine all right. *Not* because you persevere in actions the consequences of which you fear. But I daresay you can touch pitch and not be defiled.”
Being too well brought up to interrupt she waited till he had said all he wanted to say, then she exclaimed:
“Let’s settle the preliminaries. It’s obvious mother means us to see a great deal of you. *You’re* going to be a mascot too, like your father. I suppose you think you are: you saved me from the police yesterday, you appear to have saved mother’s neck today. You appear, too, to be going to make twenty pounds profit on a horse deal. You say you will and you seem to be that sort of a person﻿ ﻿… Twenty pounds is no end in a family like ours﻿ ﻿… Well, then, you appear to be going to be the regular *bel ami* of the Wannop family﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“I hope not.”

===Chinese===

他察觉，这一路，姑娘在道旁用发亮的眼睛望着他：简直目不转睛：着了迷。
“我猜你觉得这是好一场表演。”她说。
“肚带我收拾得不算漂亮，”他说。“咱们离开这条路吧。”
“把可怜的、软弱的女人们各安其位，”沃诺普小姐接着说。“像使符咒的男人一样安抚马。我猜你安抚女人也是这一套。我可怜你太太。……英格兰乡村男性！又一眼收服了忠心耿耿的仆从。封建制度全套齐活。…”
蒂金斯说：
“这个嘛，你要知道，他若知道你在行的人里有朋友，往后当你的仆役当得更好。下等人就是这样。咱们离开这条路吧。”
她说：
“你这么急不可耐地要躲到绿篱后头。警察到底追不追我们？兴许你早餐桌上撒了谎：好安抚一个弱女子的歇斯底里神经。”
“我没撒谎，”他说，“只是有田间小路的时候，我讨厌大路…”
“那是病，跟女人的一样，”她喊道。
她几乎是跑着穿过亲吻门，站住等他：
“我猜，”她说，“你用你那套高高在上的男人做派把警察打发掉之后，就以为摧毁了我的浪漫青春美梦。才没有呢。我不想警察追我。他们要是把我关进温德斯沃思，我相信我会死……我是个胆小鬼。”
“哦，你不算，”他说，可他正顺着自己的思路走，正如她压根没在听他。“我敢说你够格当女英雄。倒不是因为你明知后果可怕还坚持行动。而是我敢说你摸得着沥青而不沾黑。”
她教养太好，不肯打断，等他把想说的话全说完，才喊道：
“咱们把预备事项谈定吧。妈妈显然打算叫我们同你多多来往。你也要当个吉祥物了，跟你父亲一样。我猜你自己也这么想：昨天你救了我免遭警察，今天你像是救了妈妈的脖子。你还像是要在一场马生意上赚二十镑。你说到做到，你像是那种人……二十镑在我们这样的家里可不得了……那么好吧，你像是铁定要当沃诺普家正式的“美朋友”了…”
蒂金斯说：
“但愿不是。”

===Original===

“Oh, I don’t mean,” she said, “that you’re going to rise to fame by making love to all the women of the Wannop family. Besides, there’s only me. But mother will press you into all sorts of odd jobs: and there will always be a plate for you at the table. Don’t shudder! I’m a regular good cook﻿—*cuisine bourgeoise* of course. I learned under a real professed cook, though a drunkard. That meant I used to do half the cooking and the family was particular. Eating people are: county councillors, half of them, and the like. So I know what men are﻿ ﻿…” She stopped and said good-naturedly: “But do, for goodness’ sake, get it over. I’m sorry I was rude to you. But it *is* irritating to have to stand like a stuffed rabbit while a man is acting like a regular Admirable Crichton, and cool and collected, with the English country gentleman air and all.”
Tietjens winced. The young woman had come a little too near the knuckle of his wife’s frequent denunciations of himself. And she exclaimed:
“No! That’s not fair! I’m an ungrateful pig! You didn’t show a bit more side really than a capable workman must who’s doing his job in the midst of a crowd of incapable duffers. But just get it out, will you? Say once and for all that﻿—you know the proper, pompous manner: you are not without sympathy with our aims: but you disapprove﻿—oh, immensely, strongly﻿—of our methods.”
It struck Tietjens that the young woman was a good deal more interested in the cause﻿—of votes for women﻿—than he had given her credit for. He wasn’t much in the mood for talking to young women, but it was with considerably more than the surface of his mind that he answered:
“I don’t. I approve entirely of your methods: but your aims are idiotic.”
She said:
“You don’t know, I suppose, that Gertie Wilson, who’s in bed at our house, is wanted by the police: not only for yesterday, but for putting explosives in a whole series of letter-boxes?”
He said:
“I didn’t﻿ ﻿… but it was a perfectly proper thing to do. She hasn’t burned any of my letters or I might be annoyed; but it wouldn’t interfere with my approval.”
“You don’t think,” she asked earnestly, “that we﻿ ﻿… mother and I﻿ ﻿… are likely to get heavy sentences for shielding her. It would be beastly bad luck on mother. Because she’s an anti﻿ ﻿…”
“I don’t know about the sentence,” Tietjens said, “but we’d better get her off your premises as soon as we can.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“哦，我不是说，”她说，“你要靠向沃诺普家全体女人示爱而名利双收。再说，也只有我一个。可妈妈会把各种零活都派给你：饭桌上永远给你留一个位子。别哆嗦！我是把好厨子——当然是家常菜。我跟一位真正科班的厨娘学的，虽然是个酒鬼。那就是说，一半的饭常是我做的，家里人嘴又刁。吃饭的人都是：郡议员占一半，诸如此类。所以我懂男人是什么…”她顿住，好脾气地说：“不过看在老天的份上，快说完了它吧。抱歉我方才无礼。可是，一个男人当众表演全能克莱顿，冷静自若，一副英格兰乡绅气派全套，而你却得像只填絮的兔子站着——确实叫人光火。”
蒂金斯瑟缩了一下。年轻姑娘这话，太接近他妻子历数他罪状时的那根骨头了。她喊道：
“不！这不公道！我是头没良心的猪！你其实一点没摆架子，不过是个能干的匠人在一群无能的废物中间干自己的活，架子总是要一点的。可你就说出来吧，好不好？干脆一句话——你知道那套堂皇的做派：你不无同情于我们的目标：但你反对——哦，极其、强烈地反对——我们的手段。”
蒂金斯忽然觉得，这姑娘对妇女选举权这项事业的热心，远超出他的估计。他不大有同年轻姑娘谈话的心境，但他答话用的心思，远不止一层皮：
“我不反对。我完全赞成你们的手段；你们的目标才是蠢的。”
她说：
“我猜你不知道，格蒂·威尔逊——正躺在我们家床上——是被警察通缉的：不光为昨天，还为往整整一排信箱里放炸药。”
他说：
“我不知道……不过那件事办得完全正当。她没烧掉我的信，不然我也许会恼；可那也不妨碍我赞成。”
“你不觉得，”她恳切地问，“我们……妈和我……窝藏她会判重刑吧。那对妈妈就太倒霉了。因为她是反女权派…”
“判不判刑我不知道，”蒂金斯说，“可咱们最好尽快把她从你们家弄走。…”

===Original===

She said:
“Oh, you’ll *help*?”
He answered:
“Of course, your mother can’t be incommoded. She’s written the only novel that’s been fit to read since the eighteenth century.”
She stopped and said earnestly:
“Look here. *Don’t* be one of those ignoble triflers who say the vote won’t do women any good. Women have a rotten time. They do, really. If you’d seen what I’ve seen, I’m not talking through my hat.” Her voice became quite deep: she had tears in her eyes: “*Poor* women *do*!” she said, “little insignificant creatures. We’ve *got* to change the divorce laws. We’ve *got* to get better conditions. *You* couldn’t stand it if you knew what I know.”
Her emotion vexed him, for it seemed to establish a sort of fraternal intimacy that he didn’t at the moment want. Women do not show emotion except before their familiars. He said drily:
“I daresay I shouldn’t. But I don’t know, so I can!”
She said with deep disappointment:
“Oh, you *are* a beast! And I shall never beg your pardon for saying that. I don’t believe you mean what you say, but merely to say it is heartless.”
This was another of the counts of Sylvia’s indictment and Tietjens winced again. She explained:
“You don’t know the case of the Pimlico army clothing factory workers or you wouldn’t say the vote would be no use to women.”
“I know the case perfectly well,” Tietjens said: “It came under my official notice, and I remember thinking that there never was a more signal instance of the uselessness of the vote to anyone.”
“We can’t be thinking of the same case,” she said.
“We are,” he answered. “The Pimlico army clothing factory is in the constituency of Westminster; the Undersecretary for War is member for Westminster; his majority at the last election was six hundred. The clothing factory employed seven hundred men at 1s. 6d. an hour, all these men having votes in Westminster. The seven hundred men wrote to the Undersecretary to say that if their screw wasn’t raised to two bob they’d vote solid against him at the next election.﻿ ﻿…”
Miss Wannop said: “Well then!”
“So,” Tietjens said: “The Undersecretary had the seven hundred men at eighteenpence fired and took on seven hundred women at tenpence. What good did the vote do the seven hundred men? What good did a vote ever do anyone?”

===Chinese===

她说：
“哦，你要帮忙？”
他答：
“当然，你母亲不能受连累。她写出了十八世纪以来唯一值得一读的小说。”
她站住，恳切地说：
“听着。千万别做那种说选举权对女人没好处的卑劣轻浮鬼。女人的日子烂透了。真的。你要是见过我见过的，就不是瞎说。”她的嗓音变得很低沉：眼睛里有了泪：“可怜的女人们真苦！”她说，“小小的、无足轻重的生灵。我们非改离婚法不可。我们非争到好些的境遇不可。要是你知道我知道的事，你就受不了。”
她的激动叫他恼，因为这似乎建立起一种弟兄姊妹般的亲近，而他此刻并不想要。女人只当着自家人流露感情。他干巴巴地说：
“我多半受不了。可我不知道，所以受得了！”
她深深失望地说：
“哦，你可真是个畜生！这句我说了，永远不求你原谅。我不信你说的是真心，可光这么说，就没心肝。”
这又是西尔维娅控诉状里的一条，蒂金斯又瑟缩了一下。她解释道：
“你不知道平利科军服厂的案子，不然你就不会说选举权对女人没用了。”
“那案子我一清二楚，”蒂金斯说：“它过过我的公事，我记得我当时就想，再没有比它更显著的例子，说明选举权对谁都无用。”
“咱们想的不会是同一个案子。”她说。
“是同一个，”他答。“平利科军服厂在威斯敏斯特选区；陆军次官是威斯敏斯特的议员；他上次当选的多数是六百票。军服厂雇着七百名男工，时薪一先令六便士，全都在威斯敏斯特有投票权。七百人联名写信给次官：工钱不涨到两先令，下回选举他们就抱团投票反对他。…”
沃诺普小姐说：“那不就结了！”
“于是，”蒂金斯说，“次官把一先令六的七百男工全炒了，另雇七百女工，十便士一个钟头。选举权给那七百男工带来什么好处？选举权几时给谁带来过好处？”

===Original===

Miss Wannop checked at that and Tietjens prevented her exposure of his fallacy by saying quickly:
“Now, if the seven hundred women, backed by all the other ill-used, sweated women of the country, had threatened the Undersecretary, burned the pillar-boxes, and cut up all the golf greens round his country-house, they’d have had their wages raised to half-a-crown next week. That’s the only straight method. It’s the feudal system at work.”
“Oh, but we couldn’t cut up *golf* greens,” Miss Wannop said. “At least the W.S.P.U. debated it the other day, and decided that anything so unsporting would make us *too* unpopular. I was for it personally.”
Tietjens groaned:
“It’s maddening,” he said, “to find women, as soon as they get in Council, as muddleheaded and as afraid to face straight issues as men!﻿ ﻿…”
“You won’t, by the by,” the girl interrupted, “be able to sell our horse tomorrow. You’ve forgotten that it will be Sunday.”
“I shall have to on Monday, then,” Tietjens said. “The point about the feudal system﻿ ﻿…”
Just after lunch﻿—and it was an admirable lunch of the cold lamb, new potatoes and mint-sauce variety, the mint-sauce made with white wine vinegar and as soft as kisses, the claret perfectly drinkable and the port much more than that, Mrs. Wannop having gone back to the late professor’s wine merchants﻿—Miss Wannop herself went to answer the telephone.﻿ ﻿…
The cottage had no doubt been a cheap one, for it was old, roomy and comfortable; but effort had no doubt, too, been lavished on its low rooms. The dining-room had windows on each side and a beam across; the dining silver had been picked up at sales, the tumblers were old cut glass; on each side of the ingle was a grandfather’s chair. The garden had red brick paths, sunflowers, hollyhocks and scarlet gladioli. There was nothing to it all, but the garden-gate was well hung.
To Tietjens all this meant effort. Here was a woman who, a few years ago, was penniless, in the most miserable of circumstances, supporting life with the most exiguous of all implements. What effort hadn’t it meant! and what effort didn’t it mean? There was a boy at Eton﻿ ﻿… a senseless, but a gallant effort.
Mrs. Wannop sat opposite him in the other grandfather’s chair; an admirable hostess, an admirable lady. Full of spirit in dashes; but tired.

===Chinese===

沃诺普小姐被这话噎住；蒂金斯不给她拆穿自己诡辩的机会，赶紧说：
“可要是那七百女工，背靠着全国所有受虐待、被血汗的女工们，去吓唬那次官，烧邮筒，把他乡间宅子四周的高尔夫果岭全刓了，不出一个礼拜工钱就涨到两先令六。这才是唯一直截了当的法子。这是封建制度在运转。”
“哦，可我们刓不得高尔夫果岭，”沃诺普小姐说。“反正妇女社会政治同盟前两天辩论过，认定这么不光彩的事会叫我们太不得人心。我个人是赞成的。”
蒂金斯呻吟一声：
“真叫人抓狂，”他说，“女人一进议事会，就跟男人一样浆糊脑袋、一样不敢直面正题！…”
“顺便说一句，”姑娘插嘴，“明天你可卖不了我们的马。你忘了明天是礼拜天。”
“那我只好礼拜一卖，”蒂金斯说。“封建制度这一条…”
午饭过后——那是一顿极好的午饭，冷羊肉、新土豆、薄荷酱那一类：薄荷酱兑的白葡萄酒醋，软得像吻；红酒完全喝得，波特更远不止喝得，沃诺普太太回头找上了亡教授当年的酒商——沃诺普小姐亲自去接了电话。…
这小屋当初想必便宜：因为它老、宽敞、舒适；可那些低矮的房间里无疑也倾注过心血。饭厅两面开窗，横着一根梁；餐具银器是从拍卖会上淘的，酒杯是老雕花玻璃；炉边每侧一把高背扶手椅。园子里是红砖小径、向日葵、蜀葵、红剑兰。这一切本没什么，可园门挂得端正。
在蒂金斯眼里，这一切都是心血。这是一位几年前还不名一文、境况凄惨到极点、靠天下最简陋的工具维持生计的女人。那意味着多少心血！如今又意味着多少心血！有个儿子在伊顿……一场没头脑、却英勇的努力。
沃诺普太太坐在他对面另一把高背椅里；一位极好的女主人，一位极好的夫人。一阵一阵的精神；可累了。

===Original===

As an old horse is tired that, taking three men to harness it in the stable yard, starts out like a stallion, but soon drops to a jog-trot. The face tired, really; scarlet-cheeked with the good air, but seamed downward. She could sit there at ease, the plump hands covered with a black lace shawl, and descending on each side of her lap, as much at ease as any other Victorian great lady. But at lunch she had let drop that she had written for eight hours every day for the last four years﻿—till that day﻿—without missing a day. Today being Saturday, she had no leader to write:
“And, my darling boy,” she had said to him. “I’m giving it to you. I’d give it to no other soul but your father’s son. Not even to﻿ ﻿…” And she had named the name that she most respected. “And that’s the truth,” she had added. Nevertheless, even over lunch, she had fallen into abstractions, heavily and deeply, and made fantastic misstatements, mostly about public affairs.﻿ ﻿… It all meant a tremendous record.﻿ ﻿…
And there he sat, his coffee and port on a little table beside him; the house belonging to him.﻿ ﻿…
She said:
“My dearest boy﻿ ﻿… you’ve so much to do. Do you think you ought really to drive the girls to Plimsoll tonight? They’re young and inconsiderate; work comes first.”
Tietjens said:
“It isn’t the distance﻿ ﻿…”
“You’ll find that it is,” she answered humorously. “It’s twenty miles beyond Tenterden. If you don’t start till ten when the moon sets, you won’t be back till five, even if you’ve no accidents.﻿ ﻿… The horse is all right, though﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“Mrs. Wannop, I ought to tell you that your daughter and I are being talked about. Uglily!”
She turned her head to him; rather stiffly. But she was only coming out of an abstraction.
“Eh?” she said, and then: “Oh! About the golf-links episode.﻿ ﻿… It must have looked suspicious. I daresay you made a fuss, too, with the police, to head them off her.” She remained pondering for a moment, heavily, like an old pope:
“Oh, you’ll live it down,” she said.
“I ought to tell you,” he persisted, “that it’s more serious than you think. I fancy I ought not to be here.”
“Not here!” she exclaimed. “Why, where else in the world should you be? You don’t get on with your wife; I know. She’s a regular wrong ’un. Who else could look after you as well as Valentine and I?”

===Chinese===

像一匹老马的累：进马院上挽具要三个人侍候，出厩时还像匹种马，不久便塌成一路碎步。脸上才是真的累；好空气吹得两颊通红，可皱纹道道向下。她能这样安然坐着，丰满的双手罩在黑蕾丝披巾里，垂在膝两侧，同任何维多利亚时代的贵妇人一样安闲。可午饭时她说漏了：这四年她每天写八个钟头——直到今天——一天没落下。今天是礼拜六，没有社论要写：
“而且，我最亲爱的孩子，”她当时对他说，“我把它交给您。除了您，我只肯给令尊的儿子一个人。连……都不给。”她说了她最敬重的那位的名字。“这是真话。”她又补一句。可就是吃着饭，她也时时坠入沉思，沉沉地、深深地，说出些荒唐的错话，多半是关于时政的。……这一切意味着何等惊人的纪录。…
而他就坐在那儿，咖啡和波特酒攽在手边小桌上；这宅子像是归了他所有。…
她说：
“我最亲爱的孩子……您有那么多事要做。您真觉得今夜该驾车送姑娘们去普利姆索尔？她们年轻，不知轻重；功课要紧。”
蒂金斯说：
“倒不是路远…”
“您会发现就是路远，”她幽默地答。“过了滕特登还有二十英里。您要等十点月亮落了再动身，不出岔子也得五点才回得来。……马倒是没问题…”
蒂金斯说：
“沃诺普太太，我该告诉您，人家在议论您女儿和我。难听得很！”
她把头转向他；相当僵硬。可她不过正从沉思里浮出来。
“嗯？”她说，随后：“哦！高尔夫球场那一出。……看着想必可疑。我猜您为了替她甩开警察，也闹了一场。”她像位年迈的教皇，沉沉地又想了一刻：
“哦，会熬过去的。”她说。
“我该告诉您，”他坚持道，“事情比您想的严重。我想我不该在这儿。”
“不该在这儿！”她喊。“哎呀，天底下您还该在哪儿？您同您太太处不来；我知道。她是个不折不扣的坏家伙。还有谁能像瓦伦丁和我这样照看您？”

===Original===

In the acuteness of that pang, for, after all, Tietjens cared more for his wife’s reputation than for any other factor in a complicated world, Tietjens asked rather sharply why Mrs. Wannop had called Sylvia a wrong ’un. She said in rather a protesting, sleepy way:
“My dear boy, nothing! I’ve guessed that there are differences between you; give me credit for some perception. Then, as you’re perfectly obviously a right ’un, she must be a wrong ’un. That’s all, I assure you.”
In his relief Tietjens’ obstinacy revived. He liked this house; he liked this atmosphere; he liked the frugality, the choice of furniture, the way the light fell from window to window; the weariness after hard work; the affection of mother and daughter; the affection, indeed, that they both had for himself, and he was determined, if he could help it, not to damage the reputation of the daughter of the house.
Decent men, he held, don’t do such things, and he recounted with some care the heads of the conversation he had had with General Campion in the dressing-room. He seemed to see the cracked washbowls in their scrubbed oak settings. Mrs. Wannop’s face seemed to grow greyer, more aquiline; a little resentful! She nodded from time to time; either to denote attention or else in sheer drowsiness:
“My dear boy,” she said at last, “it’s pretty damnable to have such things said about you. I can see that. But I seem to have lived in a bath of scandal all my life. Every woman who has reached my age has that feeling.﻿ ﻿… Now it doesn’t seem to matter﻿ ﻿…” She really nodded nearly off: then she started. “I don’t see﻿ ﻿… I really don’t see how I can help you as to your reputation. I’d do it if I could: believe me.﻿ ﻿… But I’ve other things to think of.﻿ ﻿… I’ve this house to keep going and the children to keep fed and at school. I can’t give all the thought I ought to to other people’s troubles.﻿ ﻿…”
She started into wakefulness and right out of her chair.
“But what a beast I am!” she said, with a sudden intonation that was exactly that of her daughter; and, drifting with a Victorian majesty of shawl and long skirt behind Tietjens’ high-backed chair, she leaned over it and stroked the hair on his right temple:
“My dear boy,” she said. “Life’s a bitter thing. I’m an old novelist and know it.

===Chinese===

这一下刺得真切——毕竟，在这纷乱的人世上，蒂金斯看重的莫过于妻子的名誉了——他相当尖利地问沃诺普太太凭什么说西尔维娅是坏家伙。她带着几分抗议的、瞌睡的口气说：
“亲爱的孩子，没什么！我猜你们之间有些不睦；总得承认我有点眼力。再说，您分明是个好家伙，那她自然是个坏家伙。就这么回事，我向您担保。”
蒂金斯一宽心，犟劲又上来了。他喜欢这宅子；喜欢这里的气氛；喜欢这份节俭、这些家具的取舍、光从一扇窗落到另一扇窗的方式；劳作之后的倦怠；母女之间的恩爱；还有她们俩对他本人的恩爱；他打定主意，只要做得到，就不叫这家女儿的名声受损。
正派的男人，他持这个主张，不做这种事；于是他把更衣室里同坎皮恩将军那番谈话的要点，仔仔细细复述了一遍。他仿佛又看见那些嵌在擦白的橡木里的、开着裂的洗脸盆。沃诺普太太的脸似乎更灰了些、鼻梁更钩了些；带一点愠意！她不时点头；或表示在听，或干脆因为瞌睡：
“亲爱的孩子，”她终于说，“这种话落到自己头上，是相当难堪。我看得出来。可我这一辈子，像是就泡在丑闻的澡盆里。凡是活到我这年纪的女人，都有这个感受。……如今倒无所谓了……”她真的几乎点头睡着：随即一惊。“我看不出……我真看不出，在您名声这件事上我怎么帮得上您。能帮我一定帮：相信我。……可我要操心的事太多。……我要撑起这个家，要孩子们吃饱、上学。别人家的烦恼，我匀不出该匀的心思。…”
她猛地全醒过来，整个人出了椅子。
“可我真是个畜生！”她说，那突来的腔调同她女儿一模一样；她带着维多利亚式的披巾长裙的威仪，飘到蒂金斯高背椅背后，俯身抚着他右边太阳穴上的头发：
“亲爱的孩子，”她说。“人生是苦的。我是个老小说家，我知道。

===Original===

There you are working yourself to death to save the nation with a wilderness of cats and monkeys howling and squalling your personal reputation away.﻿ ﻿… It was Dizzy himself said these words to me at one of our receptions. ‘Here I am, Mrs. Wannop,’ he said.﻿ ﻿… And﻿ ﻿…” She drifted for a moment. But she made another effort: “My dear boy,” she whispered, bending down her head to get it near his ear: “My dear boy; it doesn’t matter; it doesn’t really matter. You’ll live it down. The only thing that matters is to do good work. Believe an old woman that has lived very hard; ‘Hard lying money’ as they call it in the navy. It sounds like cant, but it’s the only real truth.﻿ ﻿… You’ll find consolation in that. And you’ll live it all down. Or perhaps you won’t; that’s for God in His mercy to settle. But it won’t matter; believe me, as thy day so shall thy strength be.” She drifted into other thoughts; she was much perturbed over the plot of a new novel and much wanted to get back to the consideration of it. She stood gazing at the photograph, very faded, of her husband in side-whiskers and an immense shirtfront, but she continued to stroke Tietjens’ temple with a subliminal tenderness.
This kept Tietjens sitting there. He was quite aware that he had tears in his eyes; this was almost too much tenderness to bear, and, at bottom his was a perfectly direct, simple and sentimental soul. He always had bedewed eyes at the theatre, after tender love scenes and so avoided the theatre. He asked himself twice whether he should or shouldn’t make another effort, though it was almost beyond him. He wanted to sit still.
The stroking stopped; he scrambled on to his feet:
“Mrs. Wannop,” he said, facing her, “it’s perfectly true. I oughtn’t to care what these swine say about me, but I do. I’ll reflect about what you say till I get it into my system﻿ ﻿…”
She said:
“Yes, yes! My dear,” and continued to gaze at the photograph:
“But,” Tietjens said; he took her mittened hand and led her back to her chair: “What I’m concerned for at the moment is not my reputation, but your daughter Valentine’s.”
She sank down into the high chair, balloon-like and came to rest.
“Val’s reputation!” she said, “Oh! you mean they’ll be striking *her* off their visiting lists. It hadn’t struck me. So they will!” She remained lost in reflection for a long time.
Valentine was in the room, laughing a little.

===Chinese===

你在这儿拼命，想救这个国家，身边一荒原的猫和猴嚎着、闹着把你的私名撕了。……这话是迪兹本人，在我们家一次招待会上对我说的。‘我在这儿呢，沃诺普太太，’他说。……还有……”她飘了一忽儿。可她又提起精神：“亲爱的孩子，”她低声说，低下头凑近他耳朵：“亲爱的孩子；不要紧；真不要紧。你会熬过去的。顶要紧的是做好工作。相信一个活得很苦的老太婆；用海军的话说，那是‘苦熬出来的真章’。听着像唱高调，可这是唯一真的道理。……你会在里面找到安慰的。你会把这一切熬过去的。兴许熬不过去；那就要凭上帝的慈悲来定夺了。可那也不要紧；相信我，你的日子如何，你的力量也必如何。”她飘进别的念头里去了；她正为一部新小说的情节大伤脑筋，巴不得回去琢磨。她站着凝望她丈夫那张褪了色的相片——络腮胡、偌大的硬胸衬——可手仍旧下意识地抚着蒂金斯的太阳穴。
这使蒂金斯一直坐着没动。他清清楚楚知道自己眼里有泪；这样的温柔几乎叫人承不住，何况他骨子里本是个直统统、简单而多愁的魂灵。他看戏每逢柔情场面必定眼眶带潮，所以避着戏院。他两次问自己要不要再争一争，虽然几乎力不能胜。他想坐着不动。
抚弄停了；他慌忙爬起来：
“沃诺普太太，”他说，面对着她，“是真的。这些畜生怎么说我，我本不该在乎，可我在乎。您的话我会反复思量，直到化进心里……”
她说：
“是，是！亲爱的。”仍望着相片：
“可是，”蒂金斯说；他拉起她戴露指手套的手，引她回椅子上：“我眼下挂心的不是我的名声，是您女儿瓦伦丁的。”
她气球似的落进高背椅，稳稳停住。
“瓦尔的名声！”她说，“哦！你是说他们要把她也从留言簿上划掉。我倒没想到。可不是嘛！”她又久久地出了神。
瓦伦丁在房间里，浅浅地笑。

===Original===

She had been giving the handy man his dinner, and was still amused at his commendations of Tietjens.
“You’ve got one admirer,” she said to Tietjens. “ ‘Punched that rotten strap,’ he goes on saying, ‘like a gret ol’ yaffle punchin’ a ’ollow log!’ He’s had a pint of beer and said it between each gasp.” She continued to narrate the quaintnesses of Joel which appealed to her; informed Tietjens that “yaffle” was Kentish for great green woodpecker; and then said:
“You haven’t got any friends in Germany, have you?” She was beginning to clear the table.
Tietjens said:
“Yes; my wife’s in Germany; at a place called Lobscheid.”
She placed a pile of plates on a black japanned tray.
“I’m so sorry,” she said, without an expression of any deep regret. “It’s the ingenious clever stupidities of the telephone. I’ve got a telegraph message for you, then. I thought it was the subject for mother’s leader. It always comes through with the initials of the paper which are not unlike Tietjens, and the girl who always sends it is called Hopside. It seemed rather inscrutable, but I took it to have to do with German politics and I thought mother would understand it.﻿ ﻿… You’re not both asleep, are you?”
Tietjens opened his eyes; the girl was standing over him, having approached from the table. She was holding out a slip of paper on which she had transcribed the message. She appeared all out of drawing and the letters of the message ran together. The message was:
“Righto. But arrange for certain Hullo Central travels with you. Sylvia Hopside Germany.”
Tietjens leaned back for a long time looking at the words; they seemed meaningless. The girl placed the paper on his knee, and went back to the table. He imagined the girl wrestling with these incomprehensibilities on the telephone.
“Of course if I’d had any sense,” the girl said, “I should have known it couldn’t have been mother’s leader note; she never gets one on a Saturday.”
Tietjens heard himself announce clearly, loudly and with between each word a pause:
“It means I go to my wife on Tuesday and take her maid with me.”
“Lucky you!” the girl said, “I wish I was you. I’ve never been in the Fatherland of Goethe and Rosa Luxembourg.” She went off with her great tray load, the table cloth over her forearm. He was dimly aware that she had before then removed the crumbs with a crumb-brush.

===Chinese===

她方才给那巧人开饭，还在为他对蒂金斯的赞不绝口感到好笑。
“你收了个崇拜者，”她对蒂金斯说。“‘给那烂带子打眼，’他翻来覆去地说，‘跟只大绿啄木鸟啄空木头似的！’他灌下一品脱啤酒，喘一口说一句。”她接着讲乔尔的种种趣事——那些正合她胃口；告诉蒂金斯“yaffle”是肯特话，指大绿啄木鸟；然后说：
“你在德国没有朋友吧，有吗？”她开始收拾桌子。
蒂金斯说：
“有；我太太在德国；一个叫洛布谢德的地方。”
她把一摞盘子码上黑漆托盘。
“真遗憾，”她说，并不带多少深切的遗憾。“这是电话机那些机灵的聪明的蠢货。我给你留了个电报。我还当是妈妈的社论题目呢。电报老是带着报头的字母缩写打过来，同蒂金斯差不离，管发报的姑娘又总叫霍普赛德。看着相当费解，我当它同德国政治有关，想着妈妈会懂。……你们俩没都睡着吧？”
蒂金斯睁开眼；姑娘已从桌边走到他跟前，递上一张她抄好转交的纸条。她整个人像脱了形，纸条上的字母也串成一片。电文是：
“好。但安排喂总机一定随行。西尔维娅 霍普赛德 德国。”
蒂金斯仰靠回去，久久望着这几个字；看着毫无意义。姑娘把纸条放在他膝上，回到桌边。他想象姑娘在电话里同这些天书搏斗的情景。
“当然，我要是有点脑子，”姑娘说，“早该知道这不可能是妈妈的社论提示；礼拜六她从来收不到。”
蒂金斯听见自己清清楚楚、一字一顿地宣布：
“意思是：我礼拜二去接太太，带上她的女仆。”
“你走运！”姑娘说，“我要是你就好了。我还从来没去过歌德和罗莎·卢森堡的祖国呢。”她端着满满一大托盘走了，桌布搭在小臂上。他恍恍惚惚记得，先前她已用屑刷扫去了面包屑。

===Original===

It was extraordinary with what swiftness she worked, talking all the time. That was what domestic service had done for her; an ordinary young lady would have taken twice the time, and would certainly have dropped half her words if she had tried to talk. Efficiency! He had only just realised that he was going back to Sylvia, and of course to Hell! Certainly it was Hell. If a malignant and skilful devil﻿ ﻿… though the devil of course is stupid and uses toys like fireworks and sulphur; it is probably only God who can, very properly, devise the long ailings of mental oppressions﻿ ﻿… if God then desired (and one couldn’t object but one hoped He would not!) to devise for him, Christopher Tietjens, a cavernous eternity of weary hopelessness.﻿ ﻿… But He had done it; no doubt as retribution. What for? Who knows what sins of his own are heavily punishable in the eyes of God, for God is just?﻿ ﻿… Perhaps God, then, after all, visits thus heavily sexual offences.
There came back into his mind, burnt in, the image of their breakfast-room, with all the brass, electrical fixings, poachers, toasters, grillers, kettle-heaters, that he detested for their imbecile inefficiency; with gross piles of hothouse flowers﻿—that he detested for their exotic waxennesses!﻿—with white enamelled panels that he disliked and framed, weak prints﻿—quite genuine of course, my dear, guaranteed so by Sotheby﻿—pinkish women in sham Gainsborough hats, selling mackerel or brooms. A wedding present that he despised. And Mrs. Satterthwaite, in negligee, but with an immense hat; reading the *Times* with an eternal rustle of leaves because she never could settle down to any one page; and Sylvia walking up and down because she could not sit still, with a piece of toast in her fingers or her hands behind her back. Very tall; fair; as graceful, as full of blood and as cruel as the usual degenerate Derby winner. Inbred for generations for one purpose: to madden men of one type.﻿ ﻿… Pacing backwards and forwards, exclaiming: “I’m bored! Bored!”; sometimes even breaking the breakfast plates﻿ ﻿… And talking! Forever talking; usually, cleverly, with imbecility; with maddening inaccuracy; with wicked penetration, and clamouring to be contradicted; a gentleman has to answer his wife’s questions.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

她一边不停地说话，一边手脚那么利索，真叫称奇。这就是家务操持在她身上干出的事；换成寻常的小姐，工夫要花一倍，想说话，一半的话准得掉在地上。效率！他这才刚刚意识到，自己要回到西尔维娅身边去了，当然，也就是回到地狱！千真万确是地狱。倘若一个恶毒而精明的魔鬼……可魔鬼当然是蠢的，只会玩烟火硫磺那些玩具；能正正经经设计出精神压迫那漫长的煎熬的，多半只有上帝……倘若上帝存心（人无从异议，只盼他不至于！）给克里斯托弗·蒂金斯设计一个洞窟般的、疲惫无望的永恒。……可他已经这么做了；无疑作为报应。报应什么？谁知道人自己的哪些罪在天主眼里罪不可赦，因为天主是公义的？……那么，也许上帝终究就是这样重惩色欲之罪。
回到他脑中的，是烙进去的他们早餐室的景象：满屋黄铜电器——煮蛋器、烤面包架、烤架、烧水炉——那蠢而无用的东西他一概憎恶；大团大团的温室花卉——那异域的蜡质叫他憎恶！——白珐琅的镶板他不喜欢，还有装了框的软塌塌的版画——当然都是真迹，亲爱的，苏富比保真——粉红脸的女人戴着假庚斯博罗帽卖鲭鱼或扫帚。一件他鄙夷的结婚礼物。还有萨特思韦特太太，穿着便服、却戴着偌大的帽子；永远哗啦啦翻着《泰晤士报》，因为她从来不能在某一页上安顿下来；西尔维娅走来走去，因为她坐不住，指间捏一片吐司，或者双手背在身后。非常高挑；金发；优雅，血气充盈，同寻常退化的德比赛马冠军一样残忍。世代近亲交配，只为一个目的：把一种男人逼疯。……踱过来，踱过去，喊：“我闷！闷！”有时干脆摔早餐碟子。……还说！说个不停；通常很聪明，聪明得愚妄；错得叫人发疯；一眼洞穿、居心狠毒，又吵着要人反驳；绅士总得回答妻子的问题。……

===Original===

And in his forehead the continual pressure; the determination to sit put; the décor of the room seeming to burn into his mind. It was there, shadowy before him now. And the pressure upon his forehead.﻿ ﻿…
Mrs. Wannop was talking to him now; he did not know what she said; he never knew afterwards what he had answered.
“God!” he said within himself, “if it’s sexual sins God punishes, He indeed is just and inscrutable!”﻿ ﻿… Because he had had physical contact with this woman before he married her; in a railway carriage; coming down from the Dukeries. An extravagantly beautiful girl!
Where was the physical attraction of her gone to now? Irresistible; reclining back as the shires rushed past.﻿ ﻿… His mind said that she had lured him on. His intellect put the idea from him. No gentleman thinks such things of his wife.
No gentleman thinks.﻿ ﻿… By God; she must have been with child by another man.﻿ ﻿… He had been fighting the conviction down all the last four months.﻿ ﻿… He knew now that he had been fighting the conviction all the last four months whilst, anaesthetised, he had bathed in figures and wave-theories.﻿ ﻿… Her last words had been: her very last words: late: all in white she had gone up to her dressing-room, and he had never seen her again; her last words had been about the child﻿ ﻿… “Supposing,” she had begun﻿ ﻿… He didn’t remember the rest. But he remembered her eyes. And her gesture as she peeled off her long white gloves.﻿ ﻿…
He was looking at Mrs. Wannop’s ingle; he thought it a mistake in taste, really, to leave logs in an ingle during the summer. But then what are you to do with an ingle in summer? In Yorkshire cottages they shut the ingles up with painted doors. But that is stuffy, too!
He said to himself:
“By God! I’ve had a stroke!” and he got out of his chair to test his legs.﻿ ﻿… But he hadn’t had a stroke. It must, then, he thought, be that the pain of his last consideration must be too great for his mind to register, as certain great physical pains go unperceived. Nerves, like weighing machines, can’t register more than a certain amount, then they go out of action. A tramp who had had his leg cut off by a train had told him that he had tried to get up, feeling nothing at all.﻿ ﻿… The pain comes back though﻿ ﻿…
He said to Mrs. Wannop, who was still talking:
“I beg your pardon. I really missed what you said.”
Mrs.

===Chinese===

而他额头上那持续的压迫感；那个坐定了不动摇的决心；房间的陈设像烧进了他的脑子。此刻那一切就幽幽地在他眼前。还有额头上的压迫。……
沃诺普太太此刻在同他说话；他不知道她说的是什么；事后他也始终不知道自己答了什么。
“上帝！”他在心里说，“若说天主惩罚的是色欲之罪，他可真是公义而不可测！……”因为他婚前同这女人有过肉体的接触；在一节火车包厢里；从“公爵林”下来的时候。一个漂亮得没边的姑娘！
她身上的肉感诱惑力如今哪儿去了？无法抗拒；斜倚着，任窗外郡县飞掠而过。……他的心说她曾引诱他。他的理智把这念头挥开。绅士不这样想自己的妻子。
绅士不想。……天哪；她那阵子怀的多半是别的男人的孩子。……这确信他压了整四个月。……他现在才知道，这四个月他一直在同这确信搏斗，一边像打了麻药似的，泡在数字和波浪理论里。……她最后的话是：她真正的临别之言：很晚了：她一身白衣上楼回梳妆室去，从此他再没见过她；她最后的话是关于那孩子的……“假如，”她起了个头……后面的他记不得了。可他记得她的眼睛。记得她剥下长长白手套的手势。……
他正望着沃诺普太太的炉床；他想，夏天里炉膛还留着木柴，实在是趣味上的失着。可夏天拿炉床怎么办呢？约克郡的农舍用漆过的板门把炉床封起来。可那样又闷！
他自语：
“天哪！我中风了！”他起身试腿。……可他没中风。那么，他想，必是最后那个念头的痛太大，大到他的心录不下来，正如某些巨大的肉体之痛反而感觉不到。神经同磅秤一样，超过一定分量就录不进，就此失灵。一个被火车轧断腿的流浪汉告诉过他，他曾想爬起来，一点也不觉得疼。……可疼过后会回来……
他对还在说话的沃诺普太太说：
“恕我失神。您方才的话我真没听进去。”
沃

===Original===

Wannop said:
“I was saying that that’s the best thing I can do for you.”
He said:
“I’m really very sorry: it was that that I missed. I’m a little in trouble, you know.”
She said:
“I know: I know. The mind wanders; but I wish you’d listen. I’ve got to go to work, so have you. I said: after tea you and Valentine will walk into Rye to fetch your luggage.”
Straining his intelligence, for, in his mind, he felt a sudden strong pleasure: sunlight on pyramidal red roof in the distance: themselves descending in a long diagonal, a green hill: God, yes, he wanted open air. Tietjens said:
“I see. You take us both under your protection. You’ll bluff it out.”
Mrs. Wannop said rather coolly:
“I don’t know about you both. It’s you I’m taking under my protection (it’s *your* phrase!) As for Valentine: she’s made her bed; she must lie on it. I’ve told you all that already. I can’t go over it again.”
She paused, then made another effort:
“It’s disagreeable,” she said, “to be cut off the Mountby visiting list. They give amusing parties. But I’m too old to care and they’ll miss my conversation more than I do theirs. Of course, I back my daughter against cats and monkeys. Of course, I back Valentine through thick and thin. I’d back her if she lived with a married man or had illegitimate children. But I don’t approve, I don’t approve of the suffragettes: I despise their aims: I detest their methods. I don’t think young girls ought to talk to strange men. Valentine spoke to you and look at the worry it has caused you. I disapprove. I’m a woman: but I’ve made my own way; other women could do it if they liked or had the energy. I disapprove! But don’t believe that I will ever go back on any suffragette, individual, in gangs; my Valentine or any other. Don’t believe that I will ever say a word against them that’s to be repeated﻿—*you* won’t repeat them. Or that I will ever write a word against them. No, I’m a woman and I stand by my sex!”
She got up energetically:
“I must go and write my novel,” she said. “I’ve Monday’s instalment to send off by train tonight. You’ll go into my study: Valentine will give you paper; ink; twelve different kinds of nibs. You’ll find Professor Wannop’s books all round the room. You’ll have to put up with Valentine typing in the alcove. I’ve got two serials running, one typed, the other in manuscript.”
Tietjens said:
“But *you*!”

===Chinese===

普太太说：
“我是说，这是我能为您做的最好的事。”
他说：
“真对不起：偏偏这句没听见。我有点心事，您知道。”
她说：
“我知道：我知道。人的心思会跑；可你听一听吧。我得干活，你也得。我说：喝过茶，你和瓦伦丁走到拉伊去取你的行李。”
他打起精神听——因为他心里蕦地涌起一阵强烈的欢喜：远处红金字塔的屋顶映着阳光；他们俩沿一道绿坡长长的斜线走下去；上帝，是的，他渴望旷野——蒂金斯说：
“我明白了。您把我们俩都置于您的保护之下。您要把这事硬顶过去。”
沃诺普太太相当冷冷地说：
“你们俩谈不上。我只把你一个人置于保护之下（这是你的说法！）至于瓦伦丁：她自作的床，就得自己去躺。这话我全说过了。不能再说一遍。”
她顿了顿，又打起精神：
“被芒特比的客名单除名，不好受，”她说。“他们家的聚会有趣。可我老得不在乎了，再说他们惦记我的谈锋，胜过我惦记他们的。当然，我护着我的女儿，对着那群猫猴。当然，我死心塌地护着瓦伦丁。她就是同有妇之夫同居、生私生孩子，我也护她。可我不赞成，我不赞成女权分子：我鄙夷她们的目标：我憎恶她们的手段。我认为年轻姑娘不该同陌生男人搭话。瓦伦丁同你搭了话，瞧给你惹来多少麻烦。我不赞成。我是女人：可我是自己闯出来的；别的女人肯干、有力气，也闯得出来。我不赞成！可别以为我会回头咬任何一个女权分子，单个的、成群的；我的瓦伦丁或任何别的。别以为我会说一句将来传得出去的反对她们的话——你不会去传的。或者写一个字反对她们。不，我是女人，我站在我的性别一边！”
她劲头十足地起身：
“我得去写小说了，”她说。“周一那期的稿子今晚要赶火车寄走。你进我书房去：瓦伦丁会给你纸、墨水、十二种笔尖。沃诺普教授的书满屋都是。瓦伦丁要在凹室里打字，你将就些。我两部连载同时写着，一部打字稿，一部手稿。”
蒂金斯说：
“那您呢！”

===Original===

“I,” she exclaimed, “I shall write in my bedroom on my knee. I’m a woman and can. You’re a man and have to have a padded chair and sanctuary.﻿ ﻿… You feel fit to work? Then: you’ve got till five, Valentine will get tea then. At half-past five you’ll set off to Rye. You’ll be back with your luggage and your friend and your friend’s luggage at seven.”
She silenced him imperiously with:
“Don’t be foolish. Your friend will certainly prefer this house and Valentine’s cooking to the pub and the pub’s cooking. And he’ll save on it.﻿ ﻿… It’s *no* extra trouble. I suppose your friend won’t inform against that wretched little suffragette girl upstairs.” She paused and said: “You’re *sure* you can do your work in the time and drive Valentine and her to that place﻿ ﻿… Why it’s necessary is that the girl daren’t travel by train and we’ve relations there who’ve never been connected with the suffragettes. The girl can live hid there for a bit.﻿ ﻿… But sooner than you shouldn’t finish your work I’d drive them myself﻿ ﻿…”
She silenced Tietjens again: this time sharply:
“I tell you it’s *no* extra trouble. Valentine and I *always* make our own beds. We don’t like servants among our intimate things. We can get three times as much help in the neighbourhood as we want. We’re liked here. The extra work you give will be met by extra help. We could have servants if we wanted. But Valentine and I like to be alone in the house together at night. We’re very fond of each other.”
She walked to the door and then drifted back to say:
“You know I can’t get out of my head that unfortunate woman and her husband. We must *all* do what we can for them.” Then she started and exclaimed: “But, good heavens, I’m keeping you from your work﻿ ﻿… The study’s in there, through that door.”
She hurried through the other doorway and no doubt along a passage, calling out:
“Valentine! Valentine! Go to Christopher in the study. At once﻿ ﻿… at﻿ ﻿…” Her voice died away.

===Chinese===

“我！”她喊，“我在卧室里、在膝盖上写。我是女人，做得到。你是男人，得有软垫椅和圣所。……你觉得干得动？那好：你写到五点，瓦伦丁到时候端茶。五点半你们动身去拉伊。七点你带着行李、你朋友和你朋友的行李回来。”
她威严地止住他：
“别犯傻。你朋友肯定更喜欢这宅子和瓦伦丁的饭菜，而不是小客栈和小客栈的饭菜。还能省一笔。……一点不额外添麻烦。我猜你朋友不至于告发楼上那个可怜的小女权姑娘吧。”她顿了顿，说：“你拿得准赶得完工作、还驾得了车送瓦伦丁和她去那个地方……非去不可的缘故是：那姑娘不敢坐火车，我们在那边有亲戚，同女权分子从没沾过边。姑娘可以在那儿躲一阵。……可你要是赶不完工作，我宁可自己去送……”
她又止住蒂金斯，这回很干脆：
“我告诉你，一点不额外添麻烦。瓦伦丁和我向来自己铺床。我们不喜欢外人动我们贴身的东西。这一带我们要多少帮手有多少，多出三倍都请得到。我们在这儿人缘好。你们添的活，添的帮手就抵了。我们请得起仆人。可瓦伦丁和我喜欢夜里家里只有我们俩。我们非常亲。”
她走到门口，又飘回来补一句：
“你不知道，我心里总放不下那个可怜的女人和她的丈夫。咱们全都得尽力帮他们。”随后她一惊，喊道：“哎呀老天，我耽误你干活了……书房在那边，穿过那道门。”
她匆匆穿过另一道门，想必是沿着走廊去了，一路喊：
“瓦伦丁！瓦伦丁！去书房陪克里斯托弗。马上……马……”她的声音远去了。

### VII / 七
===Original===

Jumping down from the high step of the dogcart the girl completely disappeared into the silver: she had on an otter-skin toque, dark, that should have been visible. But she was gone more completely than if she had dropped into deep water, into snow﻿—or through tissue paper. More suddenly, at least! In darkness or in deep water a moving paleness would have been visible for a second: snow or a paper hoop would have left an opening. Here there had been nothing.
The constation interested him. He had been watching her intently and with concern for fear she should miss the hidden lower step, in which case she would certainly bark her shins. But she had jumped clear of the cart: with unreasonable pluckiness, in spite of his: “Look out how you get down.” He wouldn’t have done it himself: he couldn’t have faced jumping down into that white solidity﻿ ﻿…
He would have asked: “Are you all right?” but to express more concern than the “look out,” which he had expended already, would have detracted from his stolidity. He was Yorkshire and stolid: she south country and soft: emotional: given to such ejaculations as “I hope you’re not hurt,” when the Yorkshireman only grunts. But soft because she was south country. She was as good as a man﻿—a south country man. She was ready to acknowledge the superior woodenness of the north.﻿ ﻿… That was their convention: so he did not call down: “I hope you’re all right,” though he had desired to.
Her voice came, muffled, as if from the back of the top of his head: the ventriloquial effect was startling:
“Make a noise from time to time. It’s ghostly down here and the lamp’s no good at all. It’s almost out.”
He returned to his constations of the concealing effect of water vapour. He enjoyed the thought of the grotesque appearance he must present in that imbecile landscape. On his right an immense, improbably brilliant horn of a moon, sending a trail as if down the sea, straight to his neck: beside the moon a grotesquely huge star: in an extravagant position above them the Plough, the only constellation that he knew; for, though a mathematician, he despised astronomy. It was not theoretical enough for the pure mathematician and not sufficiently practical for daily life. He had of course calculated the movements of abstruse heavenly bodies: but only from given figures: he had never looked for the stars of his calculations.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

姑娘从双轮马车的高踏板上一跃而下，整个人没入了那片银色：她戴一顶深色水獅皮小便帽，本该看得见的。可她消失得干干净净，比掉进深水、掉进雪里还彻底——或穿过一层棉纸。至少更突然！黑暗里或深水里，一个移动的白色身影还能看见一秒钟：雪或纸圈总会留下一个洞口。这里却什么也没留下。
这景象叫他入迷。他一直专注而关切地望着她，生怕她踩空那级暗藏的下踏板——真踩空了，小腿骨准得磕破。可她干脆利落地跳下了车：胆子大得没道理，全不顾他那句“下的时候当心”。他自己都不会这么干：他没法面对往那片白色的实体里跳……
他本想问一声“你没事吧”，可在他已经用掉的那句“当心”之外再表关切，就有损他的木讷了。他是约克郡人，木讷；她是南方人，软和：多情：动不动就“希望你没伤着”——约克郡人只啍一声。软和是因为她是南方人。她像男人一样靠得住——南方男人。她随时肯承认北方人木头似的高级。……这是他们之间的默契：所以他没有朝下面喊“希望你没事”，虽然他想喊。
她的声音传上来，闷闷的，像从他后脑勺顶上发出来：那腹语效果吓人一跳：
“时不时弄点声响出来。这底下鬼气森森，灯也一点不顶用。快灭了。”
他回到他对水汽遮蔽效应的观察。他想到自己站在那片白痴风景里的滑稽样子，颇觉有趣。他右手边一弯硕大得不像话的灿烂月牙，撒下一条水道似的光，直通到他脖颈；月亮旁边一颗大得古怪的星；它们上方一个夸张的位置悬着北斗——他唯一认得的星座；因为虽是数学家，他鄙视天文学。对纯数学家它不够理论，对日常生活又不够实用。他当然计算过深奥天体的运行：但只照给定数字算：他从没寻找过他计算中的那些星。……

===Original===

Above his head and all over the sky were other stars: large and weeping with light, or as the dawn increased, so paling that, at times, you saw them; then missed them. Then the eye picked them up again.
Opposite the moon was a smirch or two of cloud; pink below, dark purple above; on the more pallid, lower blue of the limpid sky.
But the absurd thing was this mist!﻿ ﻿… It appeared to spread from his neck, absolutely level, absolutely silver, to infinity on each side of him. At great distances on his right black tree-shapes, in groups﻿—there were four of them﻿—were exactly like coral islands on a silver sea. He couldn’t escape the idiotic comparison: there wasn’t any other.
Yet it didn’t actually spread from his neck: when he now held his hands, nipple-high, like pallid fish they held black reins which ran downwards into nothingness. If he jerked the rein, the horse threw its head up. Two pricked ears were visible in greyness: the horse being sixteen two and a bit over, the mist might be ten foot high. Thereabouts﻿ ﻿… He wished the girl would come back and jump out of the cart again. Being ready for it he would watch her disappearance more scientifically. He couldn’t of course ask her to do it again: that was irritating. The phenomenon would have proved﻿—or it might of course disprove﻿—his idea of smoke screens. The Chinese of the Ming dynasty were said to have approached and overwhelmed their enemies under clouds of﻿—of course, not acrid﻿—vapour. He had read that the Patagonians, hidden by smoke, were accustomed to approach so near to birds or beasts as to be able to take them by hand. The Greeks under Paleologus the﻿ ﻿…
Miss Wannop’s voice said﻿—from beneath the bottom board of the cart:
“I wish you’d make some noise. It’s lonely down here, besides being possibly dangerous. There might be dicks on each side of the road.”
If they were on the marsh there certainly would be dykes﻿—why did they call ditches “dykes,” and why did she pronounce it “dicks”?﻿—on each side of the road. He could think of nothing to say that wouldn’t express concern and he couldn’t do that by the rules of the game. He tried to whistle “John Peel!” But he was no hand at whistling. He sang:
“D’ye ken, John Peel at the break of day﻿ ﻿…” and felt like a fool. But he kept on at it, the only tune that he knew.

===Chinese===

他头顶上、满天上，是别的星：大的，淌着光，或者随着黎明渐深而变淡，淡得时而看见、时而丢失，随后又被眼睛重新拾起。
月亮对面抹着一两痕云；底下粉红，上面深紫；衬在清明天上更苍白的下层蓝上。
可这雾才荒唐！……它仿佛从他脖颈那里铺开去，绝对水平，绝对银白，朝他两侧铺向无穷。极远的右手边，一丛丛黑色的树影——一共四丛——活脱是银海上的一座座珊瑚岛。他摆脱不了这个白痴的比方：也没有别的比方。
其实它并不是从他脖颈铺开的：此刻他双手举到胸口那么高，像两条苍白的鱼，攥着黑缰绳，缰绳向下没入虚无。他一抖缰，马就昂头。灰蒙蒙里看得见两只竖起的耳朵：这马十六手二寸出头，雾想必有十英尺高。差不多吧……他巴不得姑娘回来，再从车上跳出去一回。这回他有了准备，可以更科学地观察她的消失。当然不能请她再跳一回：那叫人冒火。那现象本可以证实——当然也可能证伪——他的烟幕理论。据说中国明朝人就是驾着一片——当然不呛人的——云气接近敌人、将其淹没的。他读到过，巴塔哥尼亚人藏在烟里，能凑到飞禽走兽跟前，伸手就捉。帕莱奥洛古斯麾下的希腊人……
沃诺普小姐的声音说——从车底板底下：
“你倒是弄点声响啊。这底下冷清，说不定义危险。路两边说不定都有沟。”
要是在沼泽上，路两边倒真有沟——他们干吗管沟叫“沟渠”，她干吗又把它念成“迪克斯”？——他想不出一句不表关切的话，而按规矩他不能表关切。他试着吹《约翰·皮尔》，可他不善吹口哨。他唱起来：
“你可认识约翰·皮尔，在天破晓时……”自觉像个傻子。可他唱下去，他唯一会的一支曲子。

===Original===

It was the Yorkshire Light Infantry quickstep: the regiment of his brothers in India. He wished he had been in the army: but his father hadn’t approved of having more than two younger sons in the army. He wondered if he would ever run with John Peel’s hounds again: he had once or twice. Or with any of the trencher-fed foot packs of the Cleveland district, of which there had been still several when he had been a boy. He had been used to think of himself as being like John Peel with his coat so grey﻿ ﻿… Up through the heather, over Wharton’s place; the pack running wild; the heather dripping; the mist rolling up﻿ ﻿… another kind of mist than this south country silver sheet. Silly stuff! Magical! That was the word. A silly word.﻿ ﻿… South country﻿ ﻿… In the north the old grey mists rolled together, revealing black hillsides!
He didn’t suppose he’d have the wind now: this rotten bureaucratic life!﻿ ﻿… If he had been in the army like the two brothers, Ernest and James, next above him﻿ ﻿… But no doubt he would not have liked the army. Discipline!﻿ ﻿… He supposed he would have put up with the discipline: a gentleman had to. Because noblesse oblige: not for fear of consequences﻿ ﻿… But army officers seemed to him pathetic. They spluttered and roared: to make men jump smartly: at the end of apoplectic efforts the men jumped smartly. But there was the end of it.﻿ ﻿…
Actually, this mist was not silver, or was, perhaps, no longer silver: if you looked at it with the eye of the artist﻿ ﻿… With the exact eye! It was smirched with bars of purple; of red; of orange: delicate reflections: dark blue shadows from the upper sky where it formed drifts like snow.﻿ ﻿… The exact eye: exact observation: it was a man’s work. The only work for a man. Why, then, were artists soft: effeminate; not men at all: whilst the army officer, who had the inexact mind of the schoolteacher, was a manly man? Quite a manly man: until he became an old woman!
And the bureaucrat then? Growing fat and soft like himself, or dry and stringy like Macmaster or old Ingleby? They did men’s work: exact observation: return No. 17642 with figures exact. Yet they grew hysterical: they ran about corridors or frantically rang table bells, asking with high voices of querulous eunuchs why form ninety thousand and two wasn’t ready.

===Chinese===

那是约克郡轻步兵的快步进行曲：他驻印度的几个哥哥的团。他但愿自己进过军队：可他父亲不赞成两个以上的小儿子从军。他不知道还会不会跟约翰·皮尔的猎犬再跑一回：他跑过一两次。或者克利夫兰地区那些“食客喂养”的徒步猎犬——他小时候还有好几群呢。他惯常把自己想成约翰·皮尔，穿着那件灰外套……跑上石楠坡，越过沃顿家的地界；猎犬撒开了欢；石楠滴着水；雾往上翻卷……同这南方银被似的雾是两样的雾。傻东西！有魔力！就是这个词。一个傻词。……南方……北方，灰苍苍的老雾涌在一处，露出黑黢黢的山坡！
他估摸自己如今没这脚力了：这该死的官僚生活！……要是他像两个哥哥欧内斯特和詹姆斯那样进了军队——紧挨他上头的两个……不，他多半不会喜欢军队。纪律！……他想他会受得了纪律：绅士只能受着。因为位高则任重：不是怕后果……可军官们在他眼里实在可怜。他们又叫又吼：为叫兵跳得利索：脑血管爆裂般的一番努力到头，兵跳利索了。也就到头了。……
其实这雾并不是银的，或者说，兴许不再是银的了：若用艺术家的眼睛看……用精确的眼睛看！它抹着紫的、红的、橙的条痕：精致的映色：上天的深蓝阴影落在它上面，堆出雪一样的皱襞。……精确的眼睛：精确的观察：那是男人的工作。男人唯一的工作。可为什么艺术家偏软：娘们气；全然不是男人：而满脑子教书匠式模糊思维的军官，倒是男子汉？十足的男子汉：直到变成老娘们儿！
那官僚呢？养得像他自己这样肥软，或者像麦克马斯特、老英格尔比那样干瘪如柴？他们干的是男人的活：精确的观察：第一七六四二号报表，数字精确。可他们渐渐歇斯底里：在走廊里乱跑，发疯似的按铃，用阉人似的尖嗓子质问第九万零二号表格为什么还没好。

===Original===

Nevertheless men like the bureaucratic life: his own brother, Mark, head of the family: heir to Groby.﻿ ﻿… Fifteen years older: a quiet stick: wooden: brown; always in a bowler-hat, as often as not with his racing-glasses hung around him. Attending his first-class office when he liked: too good a man for any administration to lose by putting on the screw.﻿ ﻿… But heir to Groby: what would that stick make of the place?﻿ ﻿… Let it, no doubt, and go on pottering from the Albany to race meetings﻿—where he never betted﻿—to Whitehall, where he was said to be indispensable.﻿ ﻿… Why indispensable? Why in heaven’s name? That stick who had never hunted, never shot: couldn’t tell coulter from plough-handle and lived in his bowler-hat!﻿ ﻿… A “sound” man: the archetype of all sound men. Never in his life had anyone shaken his head at Mark and said:
“You’re *brilliant*!” Brilliant! That stick! No, he was indispensable!
“Upon my soul!” Tietjens said to himself, “that girl down there is the only intelligent living soul I’ve met for years.”﻿ ﻿… A little pronounced in manner sometimes; faulty in reasoning naturally, but quite intelligent, with a touch of wrong accent now and then. But if she was wanted anywhere, there she’d be! Of good stock, of course: on both sides!﻿ ﻿… But, positively, she and Sylvia were the only two human beings he had met for years whom he could respect: the one for sheer efficiency in killing: the other for having the constructive desire and knowing how to set about it. Kill or cure! The two functions of man. If you wanted something killed you’d go to Sylvia Tietjens in the sure faith that she would kill it: emotion: hope: ideal: kill it quick and sure. If you wanted something kept alive you’d go to Valentine: she’d find something to do for it﻿ ﻿… The two types of mind: remorseless enemy: sure screen: dagger﻿ ﻿… sheath!
Perhaps the future of the world, then, was to women? Why not? He hadn’t in years met a man that he hadn’t to talk down to﻿—as you talk down to a child: as he had talked down to General Campion or to Mr. Waterhouse﻿ ﻿… as he always talked down to Macmaster. All good fellows in their way.﻿ ﻿…
But why was he born to be a sort of lonely buffalo: outside the herd? Not artist: not soldier: not bureaucrat: not certainly indispensable anywhere: apparently not even sound in the eyes of these dim-minded specialists﻿ ﻿… An exact observer.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

话虽如此，男人们喜欢官僚生活：他自己的哥哥马克，一家之长：格罗比的继承人。……大他十五岁：一根安静的木头：木头：棕色；永远戴圆顶礼帽，十有八九还挂着赛马望远镜。高兴才去他那个一等衙门点个卯：这样的人才哪个政府也舍不得上螺丝拧走。……可他是格罗比的继承人：那根木头会把那地方弄成什么样？……多半由它去，从奥尔巴尼寓所溜达到赛马会——他从不下注——再溜达到白厅，据说那里少不了他。……为什么少不了？天底下为什么？那根木头从不打猎，从不打枪：分不清犁铧和犁柄，就住在他的圆顶礼帽里！……一个“端正”的人：一切端正人的原型。一辈子没有谁对着马克摇过头说：
“你真聪明！”聪明！那根木头！不，他是少不了的！
“我的天！”蒂金斯自语，“底下那姑娘，是我多少年来遇到的唯一有头脑的活人。”……做派有时略过了点；推理自然有毛病，但相当聪明，偶尔带一点口音小疵。可哪里用得着她，她准在哪里！当然有好血统：两边都是！……可实实在在说，她同西尔维娅，是他多年遇到的仅有的两个他敬得起的人：一个杀人杀得绝对利落；一个有建设的愿望、又知道怎么着手。非生即死！人的两大职能。你想要什么死掉，去找西尔维娅·蒂金斯，稳稳相信她会叫它死：情感：希望：理想：死得又快又准。你想要什么活下去，去找瓦伦丁：她会给它找事情做……两种心智：无情的仇敌：可靠的屏障：匕首……鞘！
那么，世界的未来兴许归女人？为什么不？他多少年没遇到过一个他不必俯就着说话的男人了——像对孩子那样俯就：他俯就着同坎皮恩将军、同沃特豪斯先生说过话……像他永远俯就着同麦克马斯特说话。各有各的好处。……
可为什么他生来就是一头离群的野牛：在牛群外头？不是艺术家：不是军人：不是官僚：显然哪儿也少不了他：在这些心思昏昧的专家眼里，他竟然连“端正”都算不上……一个精确的观察者。……

===Original===

Hardly even that for the last six and a half hours:

> “Die Sommer Nacht hat mirs angethan:

Das war ein schwiegsame Reiten﻿ ﻿…”

he said aloud.
How could you translate that: you couldn’t translate it: no one could translate Heine:

> “It was the summer night came over me:

That was silent riding﻿ ﻿…”

A voice cut into his warm, drowsy thought:
“Oh, you *do* exist. But you’ve spoken too late. I’ve run into the horse.” He must have been speaking aloud. He had felt the horse quivering at the end of the reins. The horse, too, was used to her by now. It had hardly stirred﻿ ﻿… He wondered when he had left off singing “John Peel.”﻿ ﻿… He said:
“Come along, then: have you found anything?”
The answer came:
“Something﻿ ﻿… But you can’t talk in this stuff﻿ ﻿… I’ll just﻿ ﻿…”
The voice died away as if a door had shut. He waited: consciously waiting: as an occupation! Contritely and to make a noise he rattled the whip-stock in its bucket. The horse started and he had to check in quickly: a damn fool he was. Of course a horse would start if you rattled a whip-stock. He called out:
“Are you all right?” The cart might have knocked her down. He had, however, broken the convention. Her voice came from a great distance:
“I’m all right. Trying the other side﻿ ﻿…”
His last thought came back to him. He had broken their convention: he had exhibited concern: like any other man.﻿ ﻿… He said to himself:
“By God! Why not take a holiday: why not break all conventions?”
They erected themselves intangibly and irrefragably. He had not known this young woman twenty-four hours: not to speak to: and already the convention existed between them that he must play stiff and cold, she warm and clinging.﻿ ﻿… Yet she was obviously as cool a hand as himself: cooler, no doubt, for at bottom he was certainly a sentimentalist.
A convention of the most imbecile type.﻿ ﻿… Then break all conventions: with the young woman: with himself above all. For forty-eight hours﻿ ﻿… almost exactly forty-eight hours till he started for Dover.﻿ ﻿…

> “And I must to the greenwood go,

Alone: a banished man!”

===Chinese===

过去六个半钟头，连这个也谈不上：

> “Die Sommer Nacht hat mirs angethan:

Das war ein schwiegsame Reiten…”

他大声念道。
这怎么译：译不了：谁也译不了海涅：

> “是那夏夜对我施了魔法：

那是一场沉默的骑行……”

一个声音切进他温热困倦的思绪：
“哦，你还在啊。可你说得太晚了。我撞上马了。”他想必一直在出声说话。他一直觉着缰绳尽头那马在打颤。马这会儿也惯了她。它几乎没动……他纳闷自己是什么时候停下唱《约翰·皮尔》的。……他说：
“那就上来：发现什么了吗？”
回答传来：
“一点……可这玩意儿里没法说话……我就……”
声音远去，像一扇门关上了。他等着：有意识地等：当作一件事干！为赔罪、也为弄点声响，他把鞭杆在鞭桶里摇得咔咔响。马一惊，他赶忙收缰：自己真是个蠢货。摇鞭杆马当然要惊。他喊：
“你没事吧？”车也许把她撞倒了。可他破了默契。她的声音从老远传来：
“没事。换另一边试试……”
方才的念头又回来了。他破了他们的默契：他表露了关切：同随便哪个男人一样。……他自语：
“天哪！何不放个假：何不把一切规矩都破了？”
那些规矩无形无质、却又不可摇撼地立在那里。他认识这年轻姑娘还不到二十四小时：话都没说过几句：他们之间却已经立起了一条默契：他必须板着冷着，她热着依着。……其实她分明同他自己一样是个冷手：比他更冷也未可知，因为他骨子里实在是个多愁人。
一条白痴透顶的默契。……那就破掉一切规矩：同这年轻姑娘：尤其同他自己。四十八小时……离他动身去多佛，几乎恰好四十八小时。……

> “而我必须去绿林，

独自一人：一个被放逐的人！”

===Original===

Border ballad! Written not seven miles from Groby!
By the descending moon: it being then just after cockcrow of midsummer night﻿—what sentimentality!﻿—it must be half-past four on Sunday. He had worked out that to catch the morning Ostend boat at Dover he must leave the Wannops’ at 5:15 on Tuesday morning, in a motor for the junction.﻿ ﻿… What incredible cross-country train connections! Five hours for not forty miles.
He had then forty-eight and three-quarter hours! Let them be a holiday! A holiday from himself above all: a holiday from his standards: from his convention with himself. From clear observation: from exact thought: from knocking over all the skittles of the exactitudes of others: from the suppression of emotions.﻿ ﻿… From all the weariness that made him intolerable to himself.﻿ ﻿… He felt his limbs lengthen, as if they too had relaxed.
---
Well, already he had had six and a half hours of it. They had started at 10 and, like any other man, he had enjoyed the drive, though it had been difficult to keep the beastly cart balanced, the girl had had to sit behind with her arm round the other girl who screamed at every oak tree.﻿ ﻿…
But he had﻿—if he put himself to the question﻿—mooned along under the absurd moon that had accompanied them down the heaven: to the scent of hay: to the sound of nightingales, hoarse by now, of course﻿—in June he changes his tune; of corncrakes, of bats, of a heron twice, overhead. They had passed the blue-black shadows of corn stacks, of heavy, rounded oaks, of hop oasts that are half church tower, half finger-post. And the road silver grey, and the night warm.﻿ ﻿… It was midsummer night that had done that to him.﻿ ﻿…

> Hat mir’s angethan.

Das war ein schwiegsame Reiten.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

边地谣曲！写的地方离格罗比不到七英里！
凭那西沉的月亮：眼下正是仲夏夜鸡鸣之后——多愁善感！——想必是礼拜天四点半。他算好了，要赶多佛早晨的奥斯坦德船，他必须礼拜二早上五点一刻离开沃诺普家，坐汽车去枢纽站。……这乡下火车接驳真是没法说！不到四十英里花五个钟头。
那么他还有四十八又四分之三小时！就当它是个假！首先是抛开自己的假：抛开他的标准：抛开他同自己的默契。抛开清晰的观察：抛开精确的思考：抛开把别人精确性的木瓶撞个精光：抛开压抑情感。……抛开那一切叫他连自己都受不了的疲惫。……他觉得四肢都舒长了，仿佛它们也松了劲。
---
其实这假他已经放了六个半钟头了。他们十点动身，像随便哪个男人一样，他一路受用着这趟车，虽然那辆破车不好保持平衡，姑娘只好坐在后头，搂着另一个姑娘——那姑娘见了每棵橡树都要尖叫。……
可他——若扪心自问——是在那轮陪着他们西沉的荒唐月亮底下出神地一路晃过去的：闻着干草香：听着夜莺——这会儿嗓子自然已哑——六月里它就换调了——听秧鸡、蝙蝠，还有一只鹭鸶，两次从头顶掠过。他们驶过谷物垛蓝黑的影子、沉重浑圆的橡树的影子、烤酒花炉的影子——半是教堂塔、半是路牌。路是银灰的，夜是暖的。……是仲夏夜对他施了魔法。……

> Hat mir's angethan.

Das war ein schwiegsame Reiten.…

===Original===

Not absolutely silent of course: but silentish! Coming back from the parson’s, where they had dropped the little London sewer rat, they had talked very little.﻿ ﻿… Not unpleasant people the parson’s: an uncle of the girl’s: three girl cousins, not unpleasant, like the girl but without the individuality﻿ ﻿… A remarkably good bite of beef: a truly meritorious Stilton and a drop of whisky that proved the parson to be a man. All in candlelight. A motherly mother of the family to take the rat up some stairs﻿ ﻿… a great deal of laughter of girls﻿ ﻿… then a restart an hour later than had been scheduled.﻿ ﻿… Well, it hadn’t mattered: they had the whole of eternity before them: the good horse﻿—*really* it was a good horse!﻿—putting its shoulders into the work.﻿ ﻿…
They had talked a little at first; about the safeness of the London girl from the police now; about the brickishness of the parson in taking her in. She certainly would never have reached Charing Cross by train.﻿ ﻿…
There had fallen long periods of silences. A bat had whirled very near their off-lamp.
“What a large bat!” she had said. “*Noctilux major.﻿ ﻿…*”
He said:
“Where do you get your absurd Latin nomenclature from? Isn’t it *phalœna*﻿ ﻿…” She had answered:
“From White﻿ ﻿… The *Natural History of Selborne* is the only natural history I ever read.﻿ ﻿…”
“He’s the last English writer that could write,” said Tietjens.
“He calls the downs ‘those majestic and amusing mountains,’ ” she said. “Where do you get your dreadful Latin pronunciation from? Phal﻿ ﻿… i﻿ ﻿… i﻿ ﻿… na! To rhyme with Dinah!”
“It’s ‘*sublime* and amusing mountains,’ not ‘majestic and amusing,’ ” Tietjens said. “I got my Latin pronunciation, like all public schoolboys of today, from the German.”
She answered:
“You would! Father used to say it made him sick.”
“Caesar equals Kaiser,” Tietjens said.﻿ ﻿…
“Bother your Germans,” she said, “they’re no ethnologists; they’re rotten at philology!” She added: “Father used to say so,” to take away from an appearance of pedantry.
A silence then! She had right over her head a rug that her aunt had lent her; a silhouette beside him, with a cocky nose turned up straight out of the descending black mass. But for the square toque she would have had the silhouette of a Manchester cotton-hand: the toque gave it a different line; like the fillet of Diana.

===Chinese===

当然不是绝对沉默：只是近乎沉默！从牧师家回来的一路——他们把那只伦敦小阴沟鼠摆在了那里——他们几乎没说话。……牧师家的人不坏：那姑娘的伯父：三个表妹，不叫人讨厌，像那姑娘，只是没有那份个性……牛肉相当好：斯提尔顿奶酪真的够味，还有一指威士忌，证明牧师是个男人。都在烛光里。一位慈母模样的主妇领着鼠上了楼……姑娘们的笑声闹了好一阵……随后上路，比原定晚了一个钟头。……嗐，不要紧：他们眼前是整个的永恒：那匹好马——真的是匹好马！——肩膀往活里使劲。……
起初他们稍稍说了几句：说伦敦姑娘如今警察再也找不着了；说牧师收留她的直心肠。她坐火车当然永远到不了查令十字。……
随后是长长的静默。一只蝙蝠在他们的近灯前打了好几个旋。
“好大的蝙蝠！”她说过。“Noctilux major…”
他说过：
“你这荒唐的拉丁命名法哪儿学的？那不是 phalœna 吗……”她答过：
“跟怀特学的……《塞尔伯恩博物志》是我读过的唯一一部博物志。…”
“他是最后一个会写的英国作家。”蒂金斯说。
“他把丘陵叫作‘那些庄严而有趣的山’，”她说。“你那可怕的拉丁发音又是哪儿学的？Phal…i…i…na！同‘黛娜’押韵！”
“是‘崇高而有趣的山’，不是‘庄严而有趣’，”蒂金斯说。“我的拉丁发音，同如今所有公学的学生一样，是从德国人那儿学的。”
她答：
“你们当然！父亲常说，那叫他恶心。”
“Caesar 等于 Kaiser。”蒂金斯说。……
“去你的德国人，”她说，“他们算不得民族学家；语言学业一塌糊涂！”她补一句：“父亲常这么说。”好撇清掉掉书袋的嫌疑。
随后是一阵静默！她头顶上罩着伯母借她的一方毯子；在他旁边是一道剪影，一只翘翘的鼻子从那团下坠的黑影里直直戳出来。若不是那顶方顶帽，她就是曼彻斯特棉布工人的剪影；那帽子给它换了条线；像狄安娜的束发带。

===Original===

It was piquant and agreeable to ride beside a quite silent lady in the darkness of the thick Weald that let next to no moonlight through. The horse’s hoofs went clock, clock: a good horse. The near lamp illuminated the russet figure of a man with a sack on his back, pressed into the hedge, a blinking lurcher beside him.
“Keeper between the blankets!” Tietjens said to himself: “All these south country keepers sleep all night.﻿ ﻿… And then you give them a five quid tip for the weekend shoot.﻿ ﻿…” He determined that, as to that too he would put his foot down. No more weekends with Sylvia in the mansions of the Chosen People.﻿ ﻿…
The girl said suddenly; they had run into a clearing of the deep underwoods:
“I’m not stuffy with you over that Latin, though you were unnecessarily rude. And I’m not sleepy. I’m loving it all.”
He hesitated for a minute. It was a silly-girl thing to say. She didn’t usually say silly-girl things. He ought to snub her for her own sake.﻿ ﻿…
He had said:
“I’m rather loving it too!” She was looking at him; her nose had disappeared from the silhouette. He hadn’t been able to help it; the moon had been just above her head; unknown stars all round her; the night was warm. Besides, a really manly man may condescend at times! He rather owes it to himself.﻿ ﻿…
She said:
“That was nice of you! You might have hinted that the rotten drive was taking you away from your so important work.﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh, I can think as I drive,” he said. She said:
“Oh!” and then: “The reason why I’m unconcerned over your rudeness about my Latin is that I know I’m a much better Latinist than you. You can’t quote a few lines of Ovid without sprinkling howlers in.﻿ ﻿… It’s *vastum*, not *longum*﻿ ﻿… ‘*Terra tribus scopulis vastum procurrit*’﻿ ﻿… It’s *alto*, not *coelo*﻿ ﻿… ‘*Uvidus ex alto desilientis.﻿ ﻿…*’ How could Ovid have written *ex coelo*? The *c* after the *x* sets your teeth on edge.”
Tietjens said:
“*Excogitabo!*”
“That’s purely canine!” she said with contempt.
“Besides,” Tietjens said, “*longum* is much better than *vastum*. I hate cant adjectives like ‘vast.’﻿ ﻿…”
“It’s like your modesty to correct Ovid,” she exclaimed. “Yet you say Ovid and Catullus were the only two Roman poets to *be* poets. That’s because they *were* sentimental and used adjectives like *vastum*.﻿ ﻿… What’s ‘Sad tears mixed with kisses’ but the sheerest sentimentality!”

===Chinese===

在韦尔德浓密、几乎不透月色的黑暗里，赶车走在一位全然沉默的女士身旁，别有风味。马蹄嗒、嗒：好马。近灯照亮一个背上扛口袋的赭色人影，紧贴进绿篱，身边一条眨眼的猎狗。
“守林人值班睡大觉！”蒂金斯自语：“南方这些守林人整夜睡。……回头周末打猎，你还得赏他们五镑小费。…”他打定主意，这件事上他也要硬到底。再不同西尔维娅去“上帝选民”们的宅邸度周末了。……
姑娘忽然开口；他们正驶入矮林深处的一片开阔地：
“那拉丁文的事我不跟你计较，虽然你鲁莽得没必要。我也不困。我太喜欢这一路了。”
他踌躇了一分钟。这是句傻姑娘话。她平常不说傻姑娘话。为她好，他本该抢白她。……
他说的是：
“我也相当喜欢！”她正望着他；剪影上那鼻子不见了。他没能忍住；月亮正悬在她头顶；她四周全是无名的星；夜是暖的。再说，一个真正男子气的男人，偶尔屈尊也是应该的！他倒欠着自己这一点。……
她说：
“你真好！你本可以暗示：这趟破车正把你从你那顶要紧的公事里拖开。…”
“哦，我赶着车也能想事。”他说。她说：“哦！”随后：“你那番拉丁文的鲁莽我不放在心上，是因为我知道我的拉丁文比你强得多。你引不了几行奥维德，不撒一把错字。……是 vastum，不是 longum……‘Terra tribus scopulis vastum procurrit’……是 alto，不是 coelo……‘Uvidus ex alto desilientis…’奥维德怎么会写 ex coelo？x 后头跟个 c，叫人牙酸。”
蒂金斯说：
“Excogitabo!”
“纯属狗腔！”她轻蔑地说。
“再说，”蒂金斯说，“longum 比 vastum 好得多。我讨厌 vast 这类陈腐形容词。…”
“亏你说得出纠正奥维德，”她喊道。“可你还说奥维德和卡图卢斯是罗马仅有的两个算得上诗人的诗人。那正因为他们多愁善感、用的就是 vastum 这类形容词。……‘悲伤的泪混着吻’，还不是纯粹的多愁善感！”

===Original===

“It ought, you know,” Tietjens said with soft dangerousness, “to be ‘Kisses mingled with sad tears’﻿ ﻿… ‘*Tristibus et lacrimis oscula mixta dabis*’.﻿ ﻿…”
“I’m hanged if I ever could,” she exclaimed explosively. “A man like you could die in a ditch and I’d never come near. You’re desiccated even for a man who has learned his Latin from the Germans.”
“Oh, well, I’m a mathematician,” Tietjens said. “Classics is not my line!”
“It *isn’t*,” she answered tartly.
A long time afterwards from her black figure came the words:
“You used ‘mingled’ instead of ‘mixed’ to translate *mixta*. I shouldn’t think you took English at Cambridge, either! Though they’re as rotten at that as at everything else, father used to say.”
