而且每隔一阵，她不露痕迹地夹带一点要麦克马斯特得的东西。……比方这枚C.B.……她把‘该给麦克马斯特一枚C.B.’的念头，灌注进才子一、二、三号的脑子。……才子一号同副恩俸秘书吃午饭——那位秘书专管文学荣典，靠同才子们吃午饭收集风声。……”
西尔维娅问：
“你干吗借给麦克马斯特那么多钱？”
“记住，”蒂金斯接着自己的话头，“这完全合乎规矩。这个国家的恩俸就是这样分发的；也理应这样。唯一干净的办法。杜什明太太撑麦克马斯特，因为他干他的行当是头等好手。而她在才子们中间是一股影响，因为她干她那一行是头等人物。……她代表着真正体面的苏格兰人的更高、更雅的道德。用不了多久，皇家学院晚会的请帖，经她一句话发不发，就要停发了。皇家恩俸晚宴她已经这样办了。再往后一点，等麦克马斯特因为猛挫法国人的锐头封了爵士，她在更显赫的集会里也有一小份。……那路人总得找人讨主意呀。好，总有一天你要引一位初入社交的少女。你就领不到请帖。……”
“那我倒庆幸，”西尔维娅喊，“我给布朗尼的叔叔写了信告那女人的状。今天早上我还悔了点儿，因为照格洛维娜的说法，你的窟窿大得见鬼。……”
“布朗尼的叔叔是谁？”蒂金斯问。“什么勋爷……勋爷……那位银行家！我知道布朗尼在他叔叔的银行里。”
“波特斯凯索！”西尔维娅说。“请你别装作忘了人家的名字。你装过头了。”
蒂金斯的脸又白了一层。……
“波特斯凯索，”他说，“是律师学院宿营委员会的主席，当然。你写信给他了？……”
“对不起，”西尔维娅说。“我是说，我说你装忘的那句对不住。……我写信给他说，作为学院住户，我抗议你的情妇——他当然知道你们这层关系！——每礼拜五蒙着厚纱溜进来，每礼拜六凌晨四点溜出去。”
“波特斯凯索勋爷知道我这层关系，”蒂金斯开了腔。
“他在火车上看见她在你怀里，”西尔维娅说。“布朗尼为这事气得不行，提出要冻结你的透支，把你在外面的支票都盖‘存款不足’退回来。”
“为了讨好你？”蒂金斯问。

===Original===

“*Do* bankers do that sort of thing? It’s a new light on British society.﻿ ﻿…”
“I suppose bankers try to please their women friends, like other men,” Sylvia said. “I told him very emphatically it wouldn’t please me﻿ ﻿… But﻿ ﻿…” She hesitated: “I wouldn’t give him a chance to get back on you. I don’t want to interfere in your affairs. But Brownie doesn’t like you.﻿ ﻿…”
“He wants you to divorce me and marry him?” Tietjens asked.
“How did you know?” Sylvia asked indifferently. “I let him give me lunch now and then because it’s convenient to have him manage my affairs, you being away.﻿ ﻿… But of course he hates you for being in the army. All the men who aren’t hate all the men who are. And, of course, when there’s a woman between them the men who aren’t do all they can to do the others in. When they’re bankers they have a pretty good pull.﻿ ﻿…”
“I suppose they have,” Tietjens said, vaguely; “of course they would have.﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia abandoned the blind-cord on which she had been dragging with one hand. In order that light might fall on her face and give more impressiveness to her words, for, in a minute or two, when she felt brave enough, she meant really to let him have her bad news!﻿—she drifted to the fireplace. He followed her round, turning on his chair to give her his face.
She said:
“Look here, it’s all the fault of this beastly war, isn’t it? Can you deny it?﻿ ﻿… I mean that decent, gentlemanly fellows like Brownie have turned into beastly squits!”
“I suppose it is,” Tietjens said dully. “Yes, certainly it is. You’re quite right. It’s the incidental degeneration of the heroic impulse: if the heroic impulse has too even a strain put on it the incidental degeneration gets the upper hand. That accounts for the Brownies﻿ ﻿… all the Brownies﻿ ﻿… turning squits.﻿ ﻿…”
“Then why do you go on with it?” Sylvia said. “God knows I could wangle you out if you’d back me in the least little way.”
Tietjens said:
“Thanks! I prefer to remain in it.﻿ ﻿… How else am I to get a living?﻿ ﻿…”
“You know then,” Sylvia exclaimed almost shrilly. “You know that they won’t have you back in the office if they can find a way of getting you out.﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh, they’ll find that!” Tietjens said.﻿ ﻿… He continued his other speech: “When we go to war with France,” he said dully.﻿ ﻿…

“银行家也干这种事？倒是对英国社会的一个新认识。……”
“我想银行家同别的男人一样，也要讨好他们的女性朋友，”西尔维娅说。“我一字一句告诉他，那讨好不了我……可是……”她迟疑了一下：“我不打算给他一个回头咬你的机会。我不想干涉你的事。可布朗尼不喜欢你。……”
“他要你跟我离婚，好嫁他？”蒂金斯问。
“你怎么知道？”西尔维娅淡淡地问。“我时不时让他请顿午饭，因为你不在，有他替我打点事务方便。……当然他恨你当了兵。没当兵的男人个个恨当兵的，这是常理。而且，两头中间一有个女人，没当兵的就使出全身解数整当兵的。他们又是银行家，手面就宽了。……”
“想必是的，”蒂金斯恍惚地说；“他们自然手面宽。……”
西尔维娅松开了一只手一直拽着的帘绳。为了叫光照在她脸上、给她的话添分量——因为再过一两分钟，等她胆子养足，她可真要把那坏消息给他了！——她朝壁炉飘过去。他跟着她转，坐在椅子上把脸对着她。
她说：
“听我说，这都怪这场混账的战争，是不是？你能否认吗？……我是说，像布朗尼这样正派、绅士气的人，都变成了混账窝囊废！”
“我想是的，”蒂金斯木木地说。“对，肯定是。你说得很对。这是英雄冲动附带的蜕化：英雄冲动绷得太匀太久了，附带的蜕化就占了上风。这解释了布朗尼们……所有的布朗尼们……怎么都成了窝囊废。……”
“那你为什么还干下去？”西尔维娅说。“老天知道，只要你帮我一星半点儿的劲儿，我就能把你弄出来。”
蒂金斯说：
“谢谢！我宁愿留下。……不然我拿什么谋生？……”
“那么你知道了，”西尔维娅几乎尖声喊。“你知道他们只要寻出法子，就不让你回司里了。……”
“哦，法子他们会寻的！”蒂金斯说。……他接上他另一套话头：“等我们同法国开战的时候，”他木木地说。……

And Sylvia knew he was only now formulating his settled opinion so as not to have his active brain to give to the discussion. He must be thinking hard of the Wannop girl! With her littleness: her tweed-skirtishness.﻿ ﻿… A provincial miniature of herself, Sylvia Tietjens.﻿ ﻿… If she, then, had been miniature, provincial.﻿ ﻿… But Tietjens’ words cut her as if she had been lashed with a dog-whip. “We shall behave more creditably,” he had said, “because there will be less heroic impulse about it. We shall﻿ ﻿… half of us﻿ ﻿… be ashamed of ourselves. So there will be much less incidental degeneration.”
Sylvia, who by that was listening to him, abandoned the consideration of Miss Wannop and the pretence that obsessed her of Tietjens saying four words, against a background of books at Macmaster’s party. She exclaimed:
“Good God! What are you talking about?﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens went on:
“About our next war with France.﻿ ﻿… We’re the natural enemies of the French. We have to make our bread either by robbing them or making catspaws of them.﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia said:
“We can’t! We couldn’t﻿ ﻿…”
“We’ve got to!” Tietjens said. “It’s the condition of our existence. We’re a practically bankrupt, overpopulated, northern country: they’re rich southerners, with a falling population. Towards 1930 we shall have to do what Prussia did in 1914. Our conditions will be exactly those of Prussia then. It’s the﻿ ﻿… what is it called?﻿ ﻿…”
“But﻿ ﻿…” Sylvia cried out. “You’re a Franco-maniac.﻿ ﻿… You’re thought to be a French agent.﻿ ﻿… That’s what’s bitching your career!”
“I am?” Tietjens asked uninterestedly. He added: “Yes, that probably *would* bitch my career.﻿ ﻿…” He went on, with a little more animation and a little more of his mind:
“Ah! *that* will be a war worth seeing.﻿ ﻿… None of their drunken rat-fighting for imbecile boodlers﻿ ﻿…”
“It would drive mother mad!” Sylvia said.
“Oh, no it wouldn’t,” Tietjens said. “It will stimulate her if she is still alive.﻿ ﻿… Our heroes won’t be drunk with wine and lechery: our squits won’t stay at home and stab the heroes in the back. Our Minister for Waterclosets won’t keep two and a half million men in any base in order to get the votes of their women at a General Election﻿—that’s been the first evil effects of giving women the vote!

西尔维娅知道，他这不过是把他定型的见解搬出来应付，好省下他那活络的脑筋不去应付这场争论。他心里准在苦苦地想那个万诺普丫头！想她那小个子：她那条花呢裙子。……西尔维娅·蒂金斯的一件外省缩样。……假如她自己当年也曾缩过、外省过。……可蒂金斯的话抽在她身上，像挨了猎犬鞭。“我们会行为得更体面，”他说过，“因为这一回英雄冲动要少些。我们……半数人……会为自己害臊。所以附带的蜕化会少得多。”
西尔维娅本来在听他说了，这时丢开万诺普小姐、也丢开了那个魔住她的幻想——蒂金斯在麦克马斯特家书墙前说四个字。她喊：
“老天！你在说什么呀？……”
蒂金斯接着说：
“说我们同法国的下一次战争。……我们是法国人的天敌。我们要么抢他们、要么拿他们当爪牙，才挣得来面包。……”
西尔维娅说：
“我们不能！我们不能够……”
“必须能！”蒂金斯说。“这是我们生存的条件。我们是个实际破产、人满为患的北方国家；他们是人口日减的南方富人。到1930年前后，我们就得干普鲁士1914年干的事。到那时我们的处境会同当年的普鲁士一模一样。这就是那个……叫什么来着？……”
“可是……”西尔维娅喊。“你是个法兰西迷。……人家都说你是法国奸细。……坏你前程的就是这个！”
“是吗？”蒂金斯不甚在意地问。又道：“对，那多半真会坏我的前程。……”他接着说，多了几分生气，也多用了几分心思：
“啊！那才是一场值得看的战争。……再没有他们那套醉醺醺的鼠斗，替白痴贪官去打……”
“那要把母亲逼疯的！”西尔维娅说。
“哦，才不会，”蒂金斯说。“她若还活着，那只会给她打气。……我们的英雄们不会醉醺醺地泡在酒和欲里：我们的窝囊废不会蹲在家里朝英雄背后捯刀。我们的厕所大臣不会为了大选捞女人选票，把两百五十万男人扣在哪座基地上——那是给女人选权的头一桩恶果！”

With the French holding Ireland and stretching in a solid line from Bristol to Whitehall, we should hang the Minister before he had time to sign the papers. And we should be decently loyal to our Prussian allies and brothers.﻿ ﻿… Our Cabinet won’t hate them as they hate the French for being frugal and strong in logic and well-educated and remorselessly practical. Prussians are the sort of fellows you can be hoggish with when you want to.﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia interjected violently:
“For God’s sake stop it. You almost make me believe what you say is true. I tell you mother would go mad. Her greatest friend is the Duchesse Tonnerre Chateaulherault.﻿ ﻿…”
“Well!” Tietjens said. “Your greatest friends are the Med﻿ ﻿… Med﻿ ﻿… the Austrian officers you take chocolates and flowers to. That there was all the row about﻿ ﻿… we’re at war with *them* and you haven’t gone mad!”
“I don’t know,” Sylvia said. “Sometimes I think I am going mad!” She drooped. Tietjens, his face very strained, was looking at the tablecloth. He muttered: “Med﻿ ﻿… Met﻿ ﻿… Kos﻿ ﻿…” Sylvia said:
“Do you know a poem called ‘Somewhere’? It begins: ‘Somewhere or other there must surely be﻿ ﻿…’ ”
Tietjens said:
“I’m sorry. No! I haven’t been able to get up my poetry again.”
Sylvia said:
“*Don’t!*” She added: “You’ve got to be at the War Office at 4:15, haven’t you? What’s the time now?” She extremely wanted to give him her bad news before he went; she extremely wanted to put off giving it as long as she could. She wanted to reflect on the matter first; she wanted also to keep up a desultory conversation, or he might leave the room. She didn’t want to have to say to him: “Wait a minute, I’ve something to say to you!” for she might not, at that moment, be in the mood. He said it was not yet two. He could give her an hour and a half more.
To keep the conversation going, she said:
“I suppose the Wannop girl is making bandages or being a Waac. Something forceful.”
Tietjens said:
“No; she’s a pacifist. As pacifist as you. Not so impulsive; but, on the other hand, she has more arguments. I should say she’ll be in prison before the war’s over.﻿ ﻿…”
“A nice time you must have between the two of us,” Sylvia said. The memory of her interview with the great lady nicknamed Glorvina﻿—though it was not at all a good nickname﻿—was coming over her forcibly.
She said:
“I suppose you’re always talking it over with her?

===Chinese===

等法国人占了爱尔兰、从布里斯托到白厅摆开一条实心战线，我们就会趁大臣来不及签字先把他吊死。而我们会对我们的普鲁士盟友兄弟保持像样的忠诚。……我们的内阁不会恨他们，像恨法国人那样——恨他们节俭、逻辑过硬、教育良好、实际得不留情面。普鲁士人是那种你想撒野就能跟他们撒撒野的家伙。……”
西尔维娅激烈地插进来：
“看上帝份上别说了。你几乎叫我相信你说的全是真的。我告诉你，母亲会疯的。她最好的朋友就是托内尔·夏托勒罗公爵夫人。……”
“可不是！”蒂金斯说。“你最好的朋友是那些梅德……梅德……你给送巧克力和鲜花的奥地利军官。闹出那场风波的不就是他们……我们正跟他们打仗，你也没疯！”
“说不清，”西尔维娅说。“有时我觉得我快疯了！”她蔫了下去。蒂金斯绷着脸盯着桌布，喃喃：“梅德……梅特……科什……”西尔维娅说：
“你知道一首叫《在某处》的诗吗？开头是：‘在某处或别处，必定……’”
蒂金斯说：
“抱歉。不知道！我的诗再也背不起来了。”
西尔维娅说：
“别介！”又道：“你4点15得到陆军部，是吧？现在几点了？”她极想在他走前把坏消息给他；又极想能拖多久拖多久。她想先把这事想清楚；也想把闲谈续下去，不然他会出屋。她不想非对他说“等一等，我有话跟你说！”——因为那一刻她兴许不在兴头上。他说还不到两点。他还能陪她一个半钟头。
为了把话续下去，她说：
“我猜那位万诺普姑娘在卷绷带，要么当了女兵。反正是个来劲的差事。”
蒂金斯说：
“不；她是个和平主义者。跟你一样和平主义。没你冲动；可另一方面，她论据更多。我看战争结束前她要蹲监狱。……”
“夹在我们两个中间，你这日子想必好过得很，”西尔维娅说。同那位绰号格洛维娜的贵妇的那次会面的记忆——虽说这绰号实在不怎么样——正有力地涌上她心头。
她说：
“我猜你天天同她讨论这些？

===Original===

You see her every day.”
She imagined that that might keep him occupied for a minute or two. He said﻿—she caught the sense of it only﻿—and quite indifferently that he had tea with Mrs. Wannop every day. She had moved to a place called Bedford Park, which was near his office: not three minutes’ walk. The War Office had put up a lot of huts on some public green in that neighbourhood. He only saw the daughter once a week, at most. They never talked about the war; it was too disagreeable a subject for the young woman. Or rather, too painful.﻿ ﻿… His talk gradually drifted into unfinished sentences.﻿ ﻿…
They played that comedy occasionally, for it is impossible for two people to live in the same house and not have some common meeting ground. So they would each talk: sometimes talking at great length and with politeness, each thinking his or her thoughts till they drifted into silence.
And, since she had acquired the habit of going into retreat﻿—with an Anglican sisterhood in order to annoy Tietjens, who hated convents and considered that the communions should not mix﻿—Sylvia had acquired also the habit of losing herself almost completely in reveries. Thus she was now vaguely conscious that a greyish lump, Tietjens, sat at the head of a whitish expanse: the lunch-table. There were also books﻿ ﻿… actually she was seeing a quite different figure and other books﻿—the books of Glorvina’s husband, for the great lady had received Sylvia in that statesman’s library.
Glorvina, who was the mother of two of Sylvia’s absolutely most intimate friends, had sent for Sylvia. She wished, kindly and even wittily, to remonstrate with Sylvia because of her complete abstention from any patriotic activity. She offered Sylvia the address of a place in the city where she could buy wholesale and ready-made diapers for babies which Sylvia could present to some charity or other as being her own work. Sylvia said she would do nothing of the sort, and Glorvina said she would present the idea to poor Mrs. Pilsenhauser. She﻿—Glorvina﻿—said she spent some time every day thinking out acts of patriotism for the distressed rich with foreign names, accents or antecedents.﻿ ﻿…
Glorvina was a fiftyish lady with a pointed, grey face and a hard aspect; but when she was inclined to be witty or to plead earnestly she had a kind manner. The room in which they were was over a Belgravia back garden.

===Chinese===

天天见她。”
她盘算这话够他应付一两分钟。他答了——她只抓了个大意——说得很淡：他每天同万诺普太太吃茶。万诺普太太搬到了一个叫贝德福德帕克的地方，离他官署不远：步行不到三分钟。陆军部在那附近的公共草地上盖了一大片铁皮屋。那女儿他一礼拜至多见一回。他们从不谈战争；对那位年轻女士，那题目太不堪。或者不如说，太痛。……他的话渐渐拖成了没头没尾的句子。……
这出喜剧他们偶尔演一演：两个人住在同一座屋里，总不能没有一块公共落脚地。于是两人各自说话：有时说得又长又客气，各想各的心事，渐渐沉入沉默。
而且，自从她养成进避静的习惯——进一家圣公会女修会，存心气蒂金斯：他恨修院，又认为教派不该掺和——西尔维娅又养成了几乎整个人沉进白日梦的习惯。所以此刻她恍恍惚惚只觉一团灰色的东西、蒂金斯，坐在一片发白的长方形一端：午饭桌。还有书……其实她看见的是另一个身影、另一些书——格洛维娜丈夫的书；因为那位贵妇是在那位政治家的书房里接待西尔维娅的。
格洛维娜，西尔维娅两位顶顶贴心朋友的母亲，派人叫西尔维娅去。她想——好心，甚至不乏风趣地——劝一劝西尔维娅，怪她完全不参加爱国活动。她给了西尔维娅一个城里铺子的地址，那里批发现成的婴儿尿布，西尔维娅买了可以当作亲手做的，献给哪家慈善会都行。西尔维娅说她才不干，格洛维娜便说要把这个主意转给可怜的皮尔森豪泽太太。她——格洛维娜——说她每天都要花些工夫，替那些名字、口音或来历带外国味儿的落难富太太想爱国的事做。……
格洛维娜五十上下，尖尖的灰脸，一副硬相；可她要俏皮、或要恳切求人的时候，倒有几分和气。她们所在的那间屋子在一座贝尔格莱维亚后花园的上头。

===Original===

It was lit by a skylight and the shadows from above deepened the lines of her face, accentuating the rather dusty grey of the hair as well as both the hardness and the kind manner. This very much impressed Sylvia, who was used to seeing the lady by artificial light.﻿ ﻿…
She said, however:
“You don’t suggest, Glorvina, that I’m the distressed rich with a foreign name!”
The great lady had said:
“My dear Sylvia; it isn’t so much you as your husband. Your last exploit with the Esterhazys and Metternichs has pretty well done for *him*. You forget that the present powers that be are not logical﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia remembered that she had sprung up from her leather saddleback chair, exclaiming:
“You mean to say that those unspeakable swine think that *I’m*﻿ ﻿…”
Glorvina said patiently:
“My dear Sylvia, I’ve already said it’s not you. It’s your husband that suffers. He appears to be too good a fellow to suffer. Mr. Waterhouse says so. I don’t know him myself, well.”
Sylvia remembered that she had said:
“And who in the world is Mr. Waterhouse?” and, hearing that Mr. Waterhouse was a late Liberal Minister, had lost interest. She couldn’t, indeed, remember any of the further words of her hostess, as words. The sense of them had too much overwhelmed her.﻿ ﻿…
She stood now, looking at Tietjens and only occasionally seeing him, her mind completely occupied with the effort to recapture Glorvina’s own words in the desire for exactness. Usually she remembered conversations pretty well; but on this occasion her mad fury, her feeling of nausea, the pain of her own nails in her palms, an unrecoverable sequence of emotions had overwhelmed her.
She looked at Tietjens now with a sort of gloating curiosity. How was it possible that the most honourable man she knew should be so overwhelmed by foul and baseless rumours? It made you suspect that honour had, in itself, a quality of the evil eye.﻿ ﻿…
Tietjens, his face pallid, was fingering a piece of toast. He muttered:
“Met﻿ ﻿… Met﻿ ﻿… It’s Met﻿ ﻿…” He wiped his brow with a table-napkin, looked at it with a start, threw it on the floor and pulled out a handkerchief.﻿ ﻿… He muttered: “Mett﻿ ﻿… Metter.﻿ ﻿…” His face illuminated itself like the face of a child listening at a shell.
Sylvia screamed with a passion of hatred:
“For God’s sake say *Metternich*﻿ ﻿… you’re driving me mad!”

===Chinese===

天窗采光，头顶落下的影子加深了她脸上的纹路，把她头发的灰、把她的硬与和气一齐加重了。这给西尔维娅的印象极深，她惯看这位贵妇的是灯下。……
可她说：
“格洛维娜，你该不会是说，我就是那种带外国名字的落难富人吧！”
贵妇说：
“亲爱的西尔维娅；倒不是你，是你丈夫。你同那些埃斯特哈齐们、梅特涅们的最后一桩壮举，把他差不多断送了。你忘了如今的当权派是不讲逻辑的……”
西尔维娅记得自己从皮面高背椅里蹦了起来，喊：
“你是说，那些说不出口的猪认为我……”
格洛维娜耐心地说：
“亲爱的西尔维娅，我说过了不是你。受罪的是你丈夫。可他偏偏是个好得不像会受罪的人。沃特豪斯先生这么说的。他本人我不熟。”
西尔维娅记得自己问过：“沃特豪斯先生又是哪路神仙？”听说是位前自由党内阁大臣，就没了兴趣。往后女主人还说了什么，她竟一个字也记不得了。那些话的分量把她压垮了。……
此刻她站着，望着蒂金斯，只是偶尔看见他；她的心整个忙于一件事：为了求个精确，把格洛维娜的原话追回来。平常她记谈话记得相当好；可那一回，她的疯狂的怒火、她的恶心、指甲掐进掌心的痛、一整串再也捾不回的情绪，把她淹了。
她此刻望着蒂金斯，带着一种幸灾乐祸的好奇。怎么可能呢：她所认识的最体面的人，竟被下流无据的谣言压成这样？这叫人疑心，体面本身自带凶眼的光。……
蒂金斯脸色灰白，指间捻着一片吐司，喃喃：
“梅特……梅特……是梅特……”他用餐巾抹了抹额头，一惊，看看它，撦在地上，掏出手绢。……又喃喃：“梅特……梅特……”他的脸忽然亮了，像孩子贴着贝壳听海。
西尔维娅带着一股憎恨的激情尖叫：
“看上帝份上说梅特涅！……你快把我逼疯了！”

===Original===

When she looked at him again his face had cleared and he was walking quickly to the telephone in the corner of the room. He asked her to excuse him and gave a number at Ealing. He said after a moment:
“Mrs. Wannop? Oh! My wife has just reminded me that Metternich was the evil genius of the Congress of Vienna.﻿ ﻿…” He said: “Yes! Yes!” and listened. After a time he said: “Oh, you could put it stronger than that. You could put it that the Tory determination to ruin Napoleon at all costs was one of those pieces of party imbecility that, etc.﻿ ﻿… Yes; Castlereagh. And of course Wellington.﻿ ﻿… I’m very sorry I must ring off.﻿ ﻿… Yes; tomorrow at 8:30 from Waterloo.﻿ ﻿… No; I *shan’t* be seeing her again.﻿ ﻿… No; she’s made a mistake.﻿ ﻿… Yes; give her my love﻿ ﻿… goodbye.” He was reversing the earpiece to hang it up, but a high-pitched series of yelps from the instrument forced it back to his ear: “Oh! *War babies!*” he exclaimed. “I’ve already sent the statistics off to you! No! there *isn’t* a marked increase of the illegitimacy rate, except in patches. The rate’s appallingly high in the lowlands of Scotland; but it always *is* appallingly high there.﻿ ﻿…” He laughed and said good-naturedly: “Oh, you’re an old journalist: you won’t let fifty quid go for that.﻿ ﻿…” He was breaking off. But: “*Or*,” he suddenly exclaimed, “here’s another idea for you. The rate’s about the same, probably because of this: half the fellows who go out to France are reckless because it’s the last chance, as they see it. But the other half are made twice as conscientious. A decent Tommie thinks twice about leaving his girl in trouble just before he’s killed.﻿ ﻿… The divorce statistics are up, of course, because people will chance making new starts within the law.﻿ ﻿… Thanks﻿ ﻿… thanks﻿ ﻿…” He hung up the earpiece.﻿ ﻿…
Listening to that conversation had extraordinarily cleared Sylvia’s mind. She said, almost sorrowfully:
“I suppose that that’s why you don’t seduce that girl.” And she knew﻿—she had known at once from the suddenly changed inflection of Tietjens’ voice when he had said “a decent Tommie thinks twice before leaving his girl in trouble!”﻿—that Tietjens himself had thought twice.
She looked at him now almost incredulously, but with great coolness. Why *shouldn’t* he, she asked herself, give himself a little pleasure with his girl before going to almost certain, death.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

她再看他时，他的脸已经晴了，正快步走向屋角的电话。他请她原谅，报了个伊灵的号码。过了一刻他说：
“万诺普太太吗？哦！我太太刚提醒我，梅特涅是维也纳会议的凶神。……”他说：“对！对！”听着。过了一阵说：“哦，你还可以说得更重些。你可以说，托利党不惜代价毁掉拿破仑的决断，是那种党的白痴行径之一，等等。……对；卡斯尔雷。当然还有威灵顿。……抱歉，我得挂了。……对；明早8点30，滑铁卢车站。……不；我不会再见她了。……不；她弄错了。……对；替我问她好……再见。”他正要把听筒倒挂回去，话机里一串高音的尖叫又把它逼回耳边：“哦！战争婴儿！”他喊。“统计数字我已经寄给你了！不！私生率并没有显著上升，个别地方除外。苏格兰低地的比率吓死人的高；可那里历来就吓死人的高。……”他笑了，好脾气地说：“哦，你是老报人了：那可不能白扔五十镑。……”他正要挂断，忽又喊：“要不，再给你一个想法。比率大体持平，缘故多半是：去法国的人里，一半想着这是最后一遭，撒开了浪；另一半却加倍地有良心。一个像样的汤姆米，临死前对撾下姑娘惹一身麻烦，是要三思的。……离婚统计当然上去了，因为人们甘愿在法律里头赌一把重新开头。……谢谢……谢谢……”他挂上了听筒。……
听这通电话，把西尔维娅的头脑洗得异乎寻常地清。她近乎哀伤地说：
“我猜，这就是为什么你不诱那姑娘。”而且她知道——蒂金斯说到“一个像样的汤姆米，临撾下姑娘惹一身麻烦之前，是要三思的”时，声调陡然一变，她当下就知道：蒂金斯自己也三思过。
此刻她望着他，几乎不敢信，又极其冷静。他为什么不呢？她自问：明天去几乎必死的路上，何不给自己跟那姑娘讨一点点快乐？

===Original===

She felt a real, sharp pain at her heart.﻿ ﻿… A poor wretch in such a devil of a hole.﻿ ﻿…
She had moved to a chair close beside the fireplace and now sat looking at him, leaning interestedly forward, as if at a garden party she had been finding﻿—*par impossible!*﻿—a pastoral play not so badly produced. Tietjens was a fabulous monster.﻿ ﻿…
He was a fabulous monster not because he was honourable and virtuous. She had known several very honourable and very virtuous men. If she had never known an honourable or virtuous woman except among her French or Austrian friends, that was, no doubt, because virtuous and honourable women did not amuse her or because, except just for the French and Austrians, they were not Roman Catholics.﻿ ﻿… But the honourable and virtuous men she had known had usually prospered and been respected. They weren’t the great fortunes, but they were well-offish: well spoken of: of the country gentleman type﻿ ﻿… Tietjens.﻿ ﻿…
She arranged her thoughts. To get one point settled in her mind, she asked:
“What really happened to you in France? What is really the matter with your memory? Or your brain, is it?”
He said carefully:
“It’s half of it, an irregular piece of it, dead. Or rather pale. Without a proper blood supply.﻿ ﻿… So a great portion of it, in the shape of memory, has gone.”
She said:
“But *you*!﻿ ﻿… without a brain!﻿ ﻿…” As this was not a question he did not answer.
His going at once to the telephone, as soon as he was in the possession of the name “Metternich,” had at last convinced her that he had not been, for the last four months, acting hypochondriacal or merely lying to obtain sympathy or extended sick leave. Amongst Sylvia’s friends a wangle known as shell-shock was cynically laughed at and quite approved of. Quite decent and, as far as she knew, quite brave menfolk of her women would openly boast that, when they had had enough of it over there, they would wangle a little leave or get a little leave extended by simulating this purely nominal disease, and in the general carnival of lying, lechery, drink and howling that this affair was, to pretend to a little shell-shock had seemed to her to be almost virtuous. At any rate if a man passed his time at garden parties﻿—or, as for the last months Tietjens had done, passed his time in a tin hut amongst dust heaps, going to tea every afternoon in order to help Mrs.

===Chinese===

……她心口真真切切地疼了一下。……一个陷在见鬼的窟窿里的可怜虫。……
她挪到壁炉边一把椅子上坐下，感兴趣地前倾着身子望着他，仿佛在游园会上看一出不算太糟的牧歌戏——万万想不到的！蒂金斯是一头神话里的怪物。……
他是神话里的怪物，不因为他体面、有德。体面有德的男人她认识过好几位。她若除了法国、奥地利朋友从没认识过一位有德体面的女人，那多半因为有德体面的女人引不起她的兴味，或者因为——法国人奥地利人除外——她们不是天主教徒。……可她认识的那些体面有德的男人，多半都发达、受人尊敬。成不了巨富，可也宽裕：有口皆碑：乡绅型……蒂金斯。……
她理了理想绪。为把一点钉死在心里，她问：
“你在法国到底出了什么事？你的记忆力到底怎么了？还是脑子？”
他斟酌着说：
“有一半——不规则的一块——死了。或者说白了。供血不行了。……所以那一大块，连同它装的记忆，没了。”
她说：
“可是你！……没了脑子！……”这不算问句，他便不答。
他一拿到“梅特涅”这名字就直奔电话，这一下总算让她信了：这四个月来他并不是疑病发作，也不是为讨人怜、多骗病假在撒谎。西尔维娅的朋友圈里，对一种叫“炮弹休克”的门道人人冷笑着心照不宣，还挺赞成。她那些女朋友的相当体面——就她所知也相当勇敢——的男人，会公开夸口：在那边受够了，就装装这门纯名义上的病，弄几天假、或把假续一续。在眼下这个谎话、淫欲、酒和嚎叫的全民狂欢里，装一点炮弹休克，在她看几乎算得上德行了。至少，一个在游园会里消磨时间的男人——或者，像蒂金斯近几个月这样，蹲在垃圾堆当中的铁皮屋里、每天下午去吃茶、为了帮万

===Original===

Wannop with her newspaper articles﻿—when men were so engaged they were, at least, not trying to kill each other.
She said now:
“Do you mind telling me what actually happened to you?”
He said:
“I don’t know that I can very well.﻿ ﻿… Something burst﻿—or ‘exploded’ is probably the right word﻿—near me, in the dark. I expect you’d rather not hear about it?﻿ ﻿…”
“I want to!” Sylvia said.
He said:
“The point about it is that I *don’t* know what happened and I don’t remember what I did. There are three weeks of my life dead.﻿ ﻿… What I remember is being in a C.C.S. and not being able to remember my own name.”
“You *mean* that?” Sylvia asked. “It’s not just a way of talking?”
“No, it’s not just a way of talking,” Tietjens answered. “I lay in bed in the C.C.S.﻿ ﻿… Your friends were dropping bombs on it.”
“You might not call them my friends,” Sylvia said.
Tietjens said:
“I beg your pardon. One gets into a loose way of speaking. The poor bloody Huns then were dropping bombs from aeroplanes, on the hospital huts.﻿ ﻿… I’m not suggesting they knew it was a C.C.S.; it was, no doubt, just carelessness.﻿ ﻿…”
“You needn’t spare the Germans for me!” Sylvia said. “You needn’t spare any man who has killed another man.”
“I was, then, dreadfully worried,” Tietjens went on. “I was composing a preface for a book on Arminianism﻿ ﻿…”
“You haven’t written a book!” Sylvia exclaimed eagerly, because she thought that if Tietjens took to writing a book there might be a way of his earning a living. Many people had told her that he ought to write a book.
“No, I hadn’t written a book,” Tietjens said, “and I didn’t know what Arminianism was﻿ ﻿…”
“You know perfectly well what the Arminian heresy is,” Sylvia said sharply; “you explained it all to me years ago.”
“Yes,” Tietjens exclaimed. “Years ago I could have, but I couldn’t then. I could now, but I was a little worried about it then. It’s a little awkward to write a preface about a subject of which you know nothing. But it didn’t seem to me to be discreditable in an army sense.﻿ ﻿… Still it worried me dreadfully not to know my own name. I lay and worried and worried and thought how discreditable it would appear if a nurse came along and asked me and I didn’t know. Of course my name was on a luggage label tied to my collar; but I’d forgotten they did that to casualties.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

诺普太太弄报上的文章——男人这样忙着时，至少不至于忙着互相残杀。
她此刻说：
“你介意告诉我，你实际出了什么事吗？”
他说：
“我说不好说得上。……有什么东西在我旁边炸了——‘爆炸’多半是准确的字眼——黑地里。我猜你不爱听这个？……”
“我要听！”西尔维娅说。
他说：
“要害在于：我不知道出了什么事，也不记得我干了什么。我一生里有三个礼拜是死的。……我记得的是躺在一家战地伤员清理站（C.C.S.）里，想不起自己的名字。”
“你是说真的？”西尔维娅问。“不是一种说法？”
“不是说法，”蒂金斯答。“我躺在清理站的床上。……你的朋友们正朝它扔炸弹。”
“你可以不叫他们我的朋友，”西尔维娅说。
蒂金斯说：
“恕我失言。说话一不留神就带出成见。那些可怜的血腥的德国佬当时正从飞机上往医院棚屋扔炸弹。……我不是说他们知道那是清理站；无疑只是马虎。……”
“用不着替德国人替我留情！”西尔维娅说。“凡杀过人的男人，你都不用替他留情。”
“我当时烦恼得要命，”蒂金斯接着说。“我正在给一部论阿民念主义的书写序……”
“你没有写过书！”西尔维娅急切地喊；她想，蒂金斯若写起书来，倒许是条谋生之路。多少人对她说过他该写书。
“没写过，”蒂金斯说，“我也不知道阿民念主义是什么……”
“阿民念异端是什么你清楚得很，”西尔维娅厉声说；“多年前你还整套讲给我听过。”
“是，”蒂金斯喊。“多年前我讲得出，那时讲不出。现在又讲得出了，可当时真为这个犯愁。给自己一无所知的题目写序，多少有点别扭。但在军队的意义上，我看不算丢人。……可我不知道自己的名字，这叫我烦透了。我躺着，愁啊愁啊，想：要是护士过来问，我答不上，多难看。当然，我的名字写在拴在我领子上的行李签上；可我忘了他们对伤员都这么办。”

Then a lot of people carried pieces of a nurse down the hut: the Germans’ bombs had done that of course. They were still dropping about the place.”
“But good heavens,” Sylvia cried out, “do you mean they carried a dead nurse past you?﻿ ﻿…”
“The poor dear wasn’t dead,” Tietjens said. “I wish she had been. Her name was Beatrice Carmichael﻿ ﻿… the first name I learned after my collapse. She’s dead now of course.﻿ ﻿… That seemed to wake up a fellow on the other side of the room with a lot of blood coming through the bandages on his head.﻿ ﻿… He rolled out of his bed and, without a word, walked across the hut and began to strangle me﻿ ﻿…”
“But this isn’t believable,” Sylvia said. “I’m sorry, but I can’t believe it.﻿ ﻿… You were an officer: they *couldn’t* have carried a wounded nurse under your nose. They must have known your sister Caroline was a nurse and was killed﻿ ﻿…”
“Carrie,” Tietjens said, “was drowned on a hospital ship. I thank God I didn’t have to connect the other girl with her.﻿ ﻿… But you don’t suppose that in addition to one’s name, rank, unit, and date of admission they’d put that I’d lost a sister and two brothers in action and a father﻿—of a broken heart, I daresay.﻿ ﻿…”
“But you only lost one brother,” Sylvia said. “I went into mourning for him and your sister.﻿ ﻿…”
“No, two,” Tietjens said; “but the fellow who was strangling me was what I wanted to tell you about. ……随后好些人抬着一位护士的碎块走过棚屋：自然是德国佬的炸弹干的。四下里还在落。”
“可是老天爷，”西尔维娅喊，“你是说，他们抬着一个死护士从你眼前过？……”
“那可怜的人儿没死，”蒂金斯说。“我倒情愿她死了。她叫比阿特丽丝·卡迈克尔……我昏厥之后学会的第一个名字。当然她现在死了。……这好像惊醒了屋子那头一个家伙，他头上绷带里渗出好多血。……他滚下床，一声不吭，穿过棚屋，动手掐我……”
“这叫人没法信，”西尔维娅说。“对不起，我没法信。……你是军官：他们不能把受伤的护士抬到你鼻子底下。他们该知道你妹妹卡罗琳就是护士，就是死在这上头的……”
“卡丽，”蒂金斯说，“是死在医院船上淹死的。谢天谢地，我不必把那位姑娘同她联想到一块儿。……可你总不至于以为：人家在姓名、军衔、部队、入院日期之外，还替我注明‘阵亡一妹两兄、丧父一名’——父亲嘛，多半是心碎死的。……”
“可你只折了一个兄弟，”西尔维娅说。“我还为他和你妹妹戴过孝。……”
“不，两个，”蒂金斯说；“不过我想告诉你的是掐我的那个家伙。他发出一声声刺耳的尖叫，来了许多护理兵，把他从我身上拉下去，满身坐住。随后他开始喊‘费丝！’。他喊：‘费丝！……费丝！……费丝！’……照我心跳算，两秒钟一声，直喊到凌晨四点，他死了。……我说不清那是宗教的呼号还是一个女人的名字；反正我相当讨厌他，因为我的苦刑——如果说得上苦刑——是他开的头。……从前我认得一个叫费丝的姑娘。哦，不是情事：我父亲的总园丁、一个苏格兰人的女儿。要害是：他每喊一声费丝，我就自问：‘费丝……费丝什么来着！’我就是想不起我父亲总园丁的姓。”
西尔维娅正想着别的，问：
“到底姓什么？”
蒂金斯答：
“不知道，我至今不知道。”

===Original===

The point is that when I knew that I didn’t know *that* name, I was as ignorant, as *uninstructed*, as a newborn babe and much more worried about it.﻿ ﻿… The Koran says﻿—I’ve got as far as K in my reading of the Encyclopædia Britannica every afternoon at Mrs. Wannop’s﻿—‘The strong man when smitten is smitten in his pride!’﻿ ﻿… Of course I got King’s Regs, and the M.M.L. and Infantry Field Training and all the A.C.I.s to date by heart very quickly. And that’s all a British officer is really encouraged to know﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh, Christopher!” Sylvia said. “*You* read that Encyclopædia; it’s pitiful. You used to despise it so.”
“That’s what’s meant by ‘smitten in his pride,’ ” Tietjens said. “Of course what I read or hear now I remember.﻿ ﻿… But I haven’t got to M, much less V. That was why I was worried about Metternich and the Congress of Vienna. I *try* to remember things on my own, but I haven’t yet done so. You see it’s as if a certain area of my brain had been wiped white. Occasionally one name suggests another. You noticed, when I got Metternich it suggested Castlereagh and Wellington﻿—and even other names.﻿ ﻿… But that’s what the Department of Statistics will get me on. When they fire me out. The real reason will be that I’ve served. But they’ll pretend it’s because I’ve no more general knowledge than is to be found in the Encyclopædia: or two-thirds or more or less﻿—according to the duration of the war.﻿ ﻿… Or, of course, the real reason will be that I won’t fake statistics to dish the French with. They asked me to, the other day, as a holiday task. And when I refused you should have seen their faces.”
“Have you *really*,” Sylvia asked, “lost two brothers in action?”
“Yes,” Tietjens answered. “Curly and Longshanks. You never saw them because they were always in India. And they weren’t noticeable﻿ ﻿…”
“*Two!*” Sylvia said. “I only wrote to your father about one called Edward. And your sister Caroline. In the same letter.﻿ ﻿…”
“Carrie wasn’t noticeable either,” Tietjens said. “She did Charity Organisation Society work.﻿ ﻿… But I remember: you didn’t like her. She was the born old maid﻿ ﻿…”
“Christopher!” Sylvia asked, “do you still think your mother died of a broken heart because I left you?”
Tietjens said:
“Good God; no. I never thought so and I don’t think so. I *know* she didn’t.”
“*Then!*” Sylvia exclaimed, “she died of a broken heart because I came back.

===Chinese===

……要害是：当我发觉连那个姓都不知道时，我就像新生的婴儿一样无知、一样没受过教育，而且烦恼得多。……《古兰经》说——我每天下午在万诺普太太家按字母读《不列颠百科全书》，已经读到K了——‘强人受击，击中其傲！’……当然，《英王条令》、《军法手册》、《步兵操典》、和截至目前的全部陆军委员会通令，我很快都背回来了。一个英国军官真正被鼓励知道的，也就这些……”
“哦，克里斯托弗！”西尔维娅说。“你居然读那部百科全书；可怜见的。你从前多看不起它。”
“‘击中其傲’就是这个意思，”蒂金斯说。“如今我读到、听到的，自然都记得。……可我还没读到M，更别说V。所以我才为梅特涅和维也纳会议犯愁。我自己试着回忆，可到现在还没成。是这样：仿佛我脑子里的某一整块被擦白了。偶尔一个名字带出另一个。你看见了：我一想起梅特涅，它就带出卡斯尔雷和威灵顿——还带出别的名字。……可统计局将来就要拿这个整我。等他们把我开掉。真理由会是我服过役。可他们会装作是因为我的常识超不出一部《不列颠百科全书》：或者三分之二、或多或少——看战争打多久。……当然啦，真正的理由还会是：我不肯伪造统计数字去坑法国人。前几天他们把这事当假期作业派给我。我回绝时，你真该看看他们的脸。”
“你真的，”西尔维娅问，“阵亡了两个兄弟？”
“是，”蒂金斯答。“卷毛和长腿。你没见过他们，他们一直在印度。而且他们不起眼……”
“两个！”西尔维娅说。“我只给你父亲写过一封信，为一个叫爱德华的。还有你妹妹卡罗琳。同一封信里。……”
“卡丽也不起眼，”蒂金斯说。“她做慈善组织协会的事。……可我记得：你不喜欢她。她是天生的老姑娘……”
“克里斯托弗！”西尔维娅问，“你还认为你母亲是因为我离开你心碎死的吗？”
蒂金斯说：
“老天；不。我从没那么认为，现在也不。我知道她不是。”
“那么——！”

===Original===

﻿ ﻿… It’s no good protesting that you don’t think so. I remember your face when you opened the telegram at Lobscheid. Miss Wannop forwarded it from Rye. I remember the postmark. She was born to do me ill. The moment you got it I could see you thinking that you must conceal from me that you thought it was because of me she died. I could see you wondering if it wouldn’t be practicable to conceal from me that she was dead. You couldn’t, of course, do that because, you remember, we were to have gone to Wiesbaden and show ourselves; and we couldn’t do that because we should have to be in mourning. So you took me to Russia to get out of taking me to the funeral.”
“I took you to Russia,” Tietjens said, “I remember it all now﻿—because I had an order from Sir Robert Ingleby to assist the British Consul-General in preparing a Blue Book statistical table of the Government of Kiev.﻿ ﻿… It appeared to be the most industrially promising region in the world in those days. It isn’t now, naturally. I shall never see back a penny of the money I put into it. I thought I was clever in those days.﻿ ﻿… And of course, yes, the money was my mother’s settlement. It comes back﻿ ﻿… yes, of course﻿ ﻿…”
“Did you,” Sylvia asked, “get out of taking me to your mother’s funeral because you thought I should defile your mother’s corpse by my presence? Or because you were afraid that in the presence of your mother’s body you wouldn’t be able to conceal from me that you thought I killed her?﻿ ﻿… Don’t deny it. And don’t get out of it by saying that you can’t remember those days. You’re remembering now: that I killed your mother: that Miss Wannop sent the telegram﻿—why don’t you score it against her that she sent the news?﻿ ﻿… Or, good God, why don’t you score it against yourself, as the wrath of the Almighty, that your mother was dying while you and that girl were croodling over each other?﻿ ﻿… At Rye! Whilst I was at Lobscheid﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens wiped his brow with his handkerchief.
“Well, let’s drop that,” Sylvia said. “God knows I’ve no right to put a spoke in that girl’s wheel or in yours. If you love each other you’ve a right to happiness and I daresay she’ll make you happy. I can’t divorce you, being a Catholic; but I won’t make it difficult for you other ways, and self-contained people like you and her will manage somehow. You’ll have learned the way from Macmaster and his mistress.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

”西尔维娅喊道，“她是心碎死的，因为我回来了。……你不这么想也没用。你父亲去世的电报在洛布谢德拆开时你的脸，我记得。万诺普小姐从拉伊转来的。邮戳我记得。她生来就是跟我作对的。电报一到手我就看见你在想：得瞒住我，你觉得她的死是因为我。我看见你在盘算，把她死了这件事瞒住我行不行。当然办不到，你记得，我们原定去威斯巴登亮相的；去不成了，因为要戴孝。所以你带我去了俄国，好躲开带我去葬礼。”
“我带你去俄国，”蒂金斯说，“如今我全记得了——因为我奉罗伯特·英格尔比爵士之命，协助英国总领事编一本蓝皮书的统计表，关于基辅政府的。那时候那地方看去是全世界工业上最有指望的区域。如今自然不是了。我投进去的钱一个子儿也回不来了。我那时还自以为精明。……对了，那钱是我母亲名下的产。想起来了……对，当然……”
“你躲开带我参加你母亲的葬礼，”西尔维娅问，“是因为你觉得我一到场就玷污你母亲的遗体？还是因为你怕守着你母亲的遗体，你瞒不住我：你认为是我害死了她？……别不认。也别拿‘那些日子记不得了’搪塞。你现在正记着呢：是我害死了你母亲；电报是万诺普小姐发的——她送来了消息，你怎么不记在她账上？……再不然，老天，你怎么不记在你自己账上，当作全能上帝的震怒：你同那姑娘卿卿我我的时候，你母亲正咽气？……在拉伊！我在洛布谢德的时候……”
蒂金斯用手绢抹了抹额头。
“好了，这个不谈了，”西尔维娅说。“老天知道，我没资格给那姑娘、给你使绊子。你们要是相爱，就有权求幸福；她也多半能让你幸福。我是天主教徒，不能同你离婚；可别的方面我不为难你们；你们同她这样的自了汉，总有办法。”

===Original===

But, oh, Christopher Tietjens, have you ever considered how foully you’ve used *me*!”
Tietjens looked at her attentively, as if with magpie anguish.
“If,” Sylvia went on with her denunciation, “you had once in our lives said to me: ‘You whore! You bitch! You killed my mother. May you rot in hell for it﻿ ﻿…’ If you’d only once said something like it﻿ ﻿… about the child! About Perowne!﻿ ﻿… you might have done something to bring us together﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“That’s, of course, true!”
“I know,” Sylvia said, “you can’t help it.﻿ ﻿… But when, in your famous county family pride﻿—though a youngest son!﻿—you say to yourself: And I daresay if﻿ ﻿… Oh, Christ!﻿ ﻿… you’re shot in the trenches you’ll say it﻿ ﻿… oh, between the saddle and the ground! that you never did a dishonourable action.﻿ ﻿… And, mind you, I believe that no other man save one has ever had more right to say it than you﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“You believe that!”
“As I hope to stand before my Redeemer,” Sylvia said, “I believe it.﻿ ﻿… But, in the name of the Almighty, how could any woman live beside you﻿ ﻿… and be forever forgiven? Or no: not forgiven: ignored!﻿ ﻿… Well, be proud when you die because of your honour. But, God, you be humble about﻿ ﻿… your errors in judgment. *You* know what it is to ride a horse for miles with too tight a curb-chain and its tongue cut almost in half.﻿ ﻿… You remember the groom your father had who had the trick of turning the hunters out like that.﻿ ﻿… And you horsewhipped him, and you’ve told me you’ve almost cried ever so often afterwards for thinking of that mare’s mouth.﻿ ﻿… Well! Think of *this* mare’s mouth sometimes! You’ve ridden me like that for seven years﻿ ﻿…”
She stopped and then went on again:
“Don’t you know, Christopher Tietjens, that there is only one man from whom a woman could take ‘*Neither I condemn thee*’ and not hate him more than she hates the fiend!﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens so looked at her that he contrived to hold her attention.
“I’d like you to let me ask you,” he said, “how I could throw stones at you? I have never disapproved of your actions.”
Her hands dropped dispiritedly to her sides.
“Oh, Christopher,” she said, “don’t carry on that old play acting. I shall never see you again, very likely, to speak to. You’ll sleep with the Wannop girl tonight: you’re going out to be killed tomorrow. *Let’s* be straight for the next ten minutes or so.

===Chinese===

办法嘛，你们可以从麦克马斯特同他的情妇那里学。……可是，哦，克里斯托弗·蒂金斯，你想过没有，你把我用得多脏！”
蒂金斯凝神望着她，仿佛喜鹊般的沉痛。
“要是，”西尔维娅继续她的控诉，“你一生里哪怕对我说过一回：‘你这婊子！你这贱货！你害死了我母亲。愿你为此烂在地狱里……’你哪怕只说过一回这样的话……为了孩子！为了佩罗恩！……你倒许能把我们往一块儿拉一拉……”
蒂金斯说：
“这话，当然对！”
“我知道，”西尔维娅说，“你忍不住。……可你抱着你们家郡望的那份名贵的骄傲——虽说是个小儿子！——自忖的时候：我料想若是……哦，基督！……等你倒在战壕里你会说……哦，在坠马落地的转瞬之间说：自己一生没干过一件不体面的事。……告诉你，我相信除了一个人，没有谁比你更有资格说这话……”
蒂金斯说：
“你相信！”
“我盼着站到救赎主面前说这话，”西尔维娅说，“我就是相信。……可是，凭全能上帝的名义，哪个女人挨着你活得下去……还永远被饶恕？不：连饶恕都不是：是被无视！……好，你死的时候尽管为你的体面骄傲。可是，上帝啊，你对……你在判断上的错处，倒要谦卑些。……你懂那是什么滋味：缰链勒得太紧、马舌几乎割成两半，骑着马跑上多少里。……你记得你父亲从前那个马夫，就有这手绝活，把猎马收拾成那样。……你抽了他一马鞭；你自己说过，往后一想起那匹母马的嘴，你多少回差点哭出来。……好！那也偶尔想想这匹母马的嘴！你就是这样骑了我七年……”
她顿住，又接着说：
“克里斯托弗·蒂金斯，你不知道吗：‘我也不定你的罪’这话，女人只肯从一个男人嘴里受下，还不恨他——恨也恨不过恨魔鬼！……”
蒂金斯那样望着她，把她的目光定住了。
“我想请你允许我问一句，”他说，“我有什么资格扔石头打你？我从不曾不赞成你的任何行为。”
她的双手颓然垂到身侧。
“哦，克里斯托弗，”她说，“别再演那出老戏了。多半再没机会同你说话了。你今晚要同万诺普姑娘睡：明天要去送死。

===Original===

And give me your attention. The Wannop girl can spare that much if she’s to have all the rest﻿ ﻿…”
She could see that he was giving her his whole mind.
“As you said just now,” he exclaimed slowly, “as I hope to meet my Redeemer I believe you to be a good woman. One that never did a dishonourable thing.”
She recoiled a little in her chair.
“Then!” she said, “you’re the wicked man I’ve always made believe to think you, though I didn’t.”
Tietjens said:
“No!﻿ ﻿… Let me try to put it to you as I see it.”
She exclaimed:
“No!﻿ ﻿… I’ve been a wicked woman. I have ruined you. I am not going to listen to you.”
He said:
“I daresay you have ruined me. That’s nothing to me. I am completely indifferent.”
She cried out:
“Oh! Oh!﻿ ﻿… Oh!” on a note of agony.
Tietjens said doggedly:
“I don’t care. I can’t help it. Those are﻿—those *should* be﻿—the conditions of life amongst decent people. When our next war comes I hope it will be fought out under those conditions. Let us, for God’s sake, talk of the gallant enemy. Always. We have *got* to plunder the French or millions of our people must starve: they have *got* to resist us successfully or be wiped out.﻿ ﻿… It’s the same with you and me﻿ ﻿…”
She exclaimed:
“You mean to say that you don’t think I was wicked when I﻿ ﻿… when I ‘trepanned’ is what mother calls it?﻿ ﻿…”
He said loudly:
“*No!*﻿ ﻿… You had been let in for it by some brute. I have always held that a woman who has been let down by one man has the right﻿—has the duty for the sake of her child﻿—to let down a man. It becomes woman against man: against one man. I happened to be that one man: it was the will of God. But you were within your rights. I will never go back on that. Nothing will make me, ever!”
She said:
“And the others! And Perowne.﻿ ﻿… I know you’ll say that anyone is justified in doing anything as long as they are open enough about it.﻿ ﻿… But it killed your mother. Do you disapprove of my having killed your mother? Or you consider that I have corrupted the child﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“I don’t.﻿ ﻿… I want to speak to you about that.”
She exclaimed:
“You *don’t*﻿ ﻿…”
He said calmly:
“You know I don’t﻿ ﻿… while I was certain that I was going to be here to keep him straight and an Anglican I fought your influence over him. I’m obliged to you for having brought up of yourself the considerations that I may be killed and that I am ruined. I am.

===Chinese===

接下来十分钟，咱们说直话吧。你也给我点注意。万诺普姑娘既然要得到其余的一切，这点总匀得出……”
她看得出，他把整个心思给了她。
“方才你说，”他缓缓地说，“盼着站到救赎主面前——我相信你是个好女人。一个从没干过不体面之事的人。”
她在椅子里微微一缩。
“那么！”她说。“你就是我一直在假装认为的那种恶人——其实并没认为。”
蒂金斯说：
“不！……让我试着照我看到的讲给你听。”
她喊：
“不！……我是个坏女人。我毁了你。我不要听你说。”
他说：
“你多半毁了我。那与我无关。我全无所谓。”
她喊出声来：
“哦！哦！……哦！”是痛楚的调子。
蒂金斯执拗地说：
“我不在乎。我没有办法。这些是——本该是——体面人之间生活的条件。等我们打下一场战争，我希望就在这些条件下打。看上帝份上，让我们谈谈勇敢的敌人吧。永远谈。我们必须掠夺法国人，否则我们几百万人要挨饿；他们必须成功地抵抗我们，否则就要被消灭。……你我之间也是一样……”
她喊：
“你是说，你不认为我那时候……用母亲的话说‘诱猎’的时候——是坏的？……”
他朗声说：
“不！……你是让哪个畜生坑了。我一向认为：被一个男人坑了的女人，有权——为了孩子甚至有义务——去坑一个男人。那就成了女人对男人：对某一个男人的战争。我碰巧是那一个男人：这是上帝的旨意。可你在你的权利之内。这一点我永远不改口。什么都改变不了我，永远！”
她说：
“那后来那些呢！还有佩罗恩。……我知道你要说：只要人做得够公开，做什么都情有可原。……可那害死了你母亲。你不赞成我害死你母亲吗？或者你认为我把孩子带坏了……”
蒂金斯说：
“我不。……我正想同你谈这个。”
她喊：
“你不……”
他说：
“你知道我不……当我确信自己还会留在世上、看顾他走正路、做个国教徒时，我同你争夺过对他的影响。”

===Original===

I could not raise a hundred pounds between now and tomorrow. I am, therefore, obviously not the man to have sole charge of the heir of Groby.”
Sylvia was saying:
“Every penny I have is at your disposal﻿ ﻿…” when the maid, Hullo Central, marched up to her master and placed a card in his hand. He said:
“Tell him to wait five minutes in the drawing-room.”
Sylvia said:
“Who is it?”
Tietjens answered:
“A man.﻿ ﻿… Let’s get this settled. I’ve never thought you corrupted the boy. You tried to teach him to tell white lies. On perfectly straight Papist lines. I have no objection to Papists and no objection to white lies for Papists. You told him once to put a frog in Marchant’s bath. I’ve no objection to a boy’s putting a frog in his nurse’s bath, as such. But Marchant is an old woman, and the heir to Groby should respect old women always and old family servants in particular.﻿ ﻿… It hasn’t, perhaps, struck you that the boy is heir to Groby﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia said:
“If﻿ ﻿… if your second brother is killed.﻿ ﻿… But your eldest brother﻿ ﻿…”
“He,” Tietjens said, “has got a French woman near Euston station. He’s lived with her for over fifteen years, of afternoons, when there were no race meetings. She’ll never let him marry and she’s past the childbearing stage. So there’s no one else﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia said:
“You mean that I may bring the child up as a Catholic.”
Tietjens said:
“A *Roman* Catholic.﻿ ﻿… You’ll teach him, please, to use that term before myself if I ever see him again﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia said:
“Oh, I thank God that he has softened your heart. This will take the curse off this house.”
Tietjens shook his head:
“I think not,” he said, “off you, perhaps. Off Groby very likely. It was, perhaps, time that there should be a Papist owner of Groby again. You’ve read Spelden on sacrilege about Groby?﻿ ﻿…”
She said:
“Yes! The first Tietjens who came over with Dutch William, the swine, was pretty bad to the Papist owners﻿ ﻿…”
“He was a tough Dutchman,” Tietjens said, “but let us get on! There’s enough time, but not too much.﻿ ﻿… I’ve got this man to see.”
“Who is he?” Sylvia asked.
Tietjens was collecting his thoughts.
“My dear!” he said. “You’ll permit me to call you ‘my dear’? We’re old enemies enough and we’re talking about the future of our child.”
Sylvia said:
“You said ‘our’ child, not ‘the’ child﻿ ﻿…”

西尔维娅正说：“我每一文钱都听你调遣……”女仆“喂，总机”走到主人跟前，把一张名片放进他手里。他说：

西尔维娅说：

“他，”蒂金斯说，“在尤斯顿车站旁边养了个法国女人。除了赛马日，十五年来天天下午同她过。她绝不会让他结婚，她也过了生育的年纪。所以别无人选……”
西尔维娅说：
“你是说我可以把孩子当天主教徒养。”
蒂金斯说：

西尔维娅说：

蒂金斯正收拢思绪。
“亲爱的！”他说。“请允许我称你‘亲爱的’？咱们够老的对头了，又是在谈咱们孩子的将来。”
西尔维娅说：

===Original===

Tietjens said with a great deal of concern:
“You will forgive me for bringing it up. You might prefer to think he was Drake’s child. He can’t be. It would be outside the course of nature.﻿ ﻿… I’m as poor as I am because﻿ ﻿… forgive me﻿ ﻿… I’ve spent a great deal of money on tracing the movements of you and Drake before our marriage. And if it’s a relief to you to know﻿ ﻿…”
“It *is*,” Sylvia said. “I﻿ ﻿… I’ve always been too beastly shy to put the matter before a specialist, or even before mother.﻿ ﻿… And we women are so ignorant﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“I know﻿ ﻿… I know you were too shy even to think about it yourself, hard.” He went into months and days; then he continued: “But it would have made no difference: a child born in wedlock is by law the father’s, and if a man who’s a gentleman suffers the begetting of his child he must, in decency, take the consequences: the woman and the child must come before the man, be he who he may. And worse-begotten children than ours have inherited statelier names. And I loved the little beggar with all my heart and with all my soul from the first minute I saw him. That may be the secret clue, or it may be sheer sentimentality.﻿ ﻿… So I fought your influence because it was Papist, while I was a whole man. But I’m not a whole man any more, and the evil eye that is on me might transfer itself to him.”
He stopped and said:
“For I must to the greenwood go. Alone a banished man.﻿ ﻿… But have him well protected against the evil eye﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh, Christopher,” she said, “it’s true I’ve not been a bad woman to the child. And I never will be. And I will keep Marchant with him till she dies. You’ll tell her not to interfere with his religious instruction, and she won’t﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said with a friendly weariness:
“That’s right﻿ ﻿… and you’ll have Father﻿ ﻿… Father﻿ ﻿… the priest that was with us for a fortnight before he was born to give him his teachings. He was the best man I ever met and one of the most intelligent. It’s been a great comfort to me to think of the boy as in his hands﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia stood up, her eyes blazing out of a pallid face of stone:
“Father Consett,” she said, “was hung on the day they shot Casement. They dare not put it into the papers because he was a priest and all the witnesses Ulster witnesses.﻿ ﻿… And yet I may not say this is an accursed war.”
Tietjens shook his head with the slow heaviness of an aged man.

“你说了‘咱们的’孩子，不是‘那’孩子……”
蒂金斯带着深切的关切说：
“请原谅我提起这个。你也许宁愿认为他是德雷克的孩子。不可能是。那不合自然之理。……我如今这么穷，是因为……原谅我……我花过一大笔钱，查你同德雷克婚前一段的行踪。如果这对你是个安慰……”
“是安慰，”西尔维娅说。“我……我一直臊得厉害，不敢把这事摆到专家面前，连母亲面前也不敢。……我们女人多无知啊……”
蒂金斯说：
“我知道……我知道你臊得连自己都不敢往深里想。”他把月份日子一一道来；随后接着说：“可那本来也不会有什么两样：婚内生的孩子，法律上就是丈夫的；一个男人既然是绅士，受下了这孩子的成胎，就得像样地担起后果：女人和孩子必须放在男人前头，不管他是谁。比咱们这孩子出身更糟的，继承过的名位多了去了。而我，从见他那第一分钟起，就全心全魂地爱那小家伙。这也许是要害的线索，也许只是滥情。……所以，当我还是个囫囵人时，我同你争夺过对他的影响，因为那是天主教的。可我不再是个囫囵人了，压在我身上的凶眼也许会传到他身上。”
他停下来，说：
“因为我必须去绿林。独一个亡命的人。……但要把他好好护住，别沾凶眼……”
“哦，克里斯托弗，”她说，“真的，我对孩子不算个坏女人。往后也永远不是。玛钱特我会留在他身边到她死。你会嘱咐她别干涉他的宗教教导，她不会的……”
蒂金斯带着友善的倦意说：
“这就对了……那么你要请那位神……神……他出生前同我们在一起住了两礼拜的那位神父，来教导他。他是我平生遇到过的最好的人，也是最聪明的人之一。想到那孩子在他手里，一直是我的大安慰……”
西尔维娅站起来，一双眼睛从石头般苍白的脸上喷出火来：
“康塞特神父，”她说，“在枪毙凯斯门特那天被绞死了。他们不敢登报，因为他是神父，而证人又全是阿尔斯特人。……可我竟连‘这是一场该咒的战争’都不许说。”
