“You may for me﻿ ﻿…” he said. “You might ring the bell, will you? Don’t go away﻿ ﻿…”
He sat with the blue gloom of that enclosed space all over him, lumped heavily in his chair.
“Spelden on sacrilege,” he said, “may be right after all. You’d say so from the Tietjenses. There’s not been a Tietjens since the first Lord Justice cheated the Papist Loundeses out of Roby, but died of a broken neck or of a broken heart: for all the fifteen thousand acres of good farming land and iron land, and for all the heather on the top of it.﻿ ﻿… What’s the quotation: ‘Be ye something as something and something and ye shall not escape.﻿ ﻿…’ What is it?”
“Calumny!” Sylvia said. She spoke with intense bitterness.﻿ ﻿… “Chaste as ice and cold as﻿ ﻿… as you are﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“Yes! Yes!﻿ ﻿… And mind you none of the Tietjens were ever soft. Not one! They had reason for their broken hearts.﻿ ﻿… Take my poor father﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia said:
“*Don’t!*”
“Both my brothers were killed in Indian regiments on the same day and not a mile apart. And my sister in the same week: out at sea, not so far from them.﻿ ﻿… Unnoticeable people. But one can be fond of unnoticeable people﻿ ﻿…”
Hullo Central was at the door. Tietjens told her to ask Lord Port Scatho to step down.﻿ ﻿…
“You must, of course, know these details,” Tietjens said, “as the mother to my father’s heir.﻿ ﻿… My father got the three notifications on the same day. It was enough to break his heart. He only lived a month. I saw him﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia screamed piercingly:
“Stop! stop! stop!” She clutched at the mantelpiece to hold herself up. “Your father died of a broken heart,” she said, “because your brother’s best friend, Ruggles, told him you were a squit who lived on women’s money and had got the daughter of his oldest friend with child﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“Oh! Ah! Yes!﻿ ﻿… I suspected that. I know it, really. I suppose the poor dear knows better now. Or perhaps he doesn’t.﻿ ﻿… It doesn’t matter.”

===Chinese===

蒂金斯摇了摇头，带着老人那种迟缓的沉重。
“在我这儿你可以说……”他说。“你按一下铃，行吗？别走开……”
他坐着，那一方封闭空间里的蓝色幽暗整个罩着他，沉沉地堆在椅子里。
“斯佩尔登论渎神罪，”他说，“说不定到底是对的。从蒂金斯家这几代看，你会这么说。自第一位大法官从天主教徒朗兹家手里把罗比讹诈到手，就没有一个蒂金斯得好死：不是断颈就是心碎：尽管有那一万五千英亩上好的农田铁矿田，和山顶上整个的紫花荒原。……那句引文怎么说来着：‘你们怎样怎样又怎样怎样，就不得逃脱。……’是什么？”
“诽谤！”西尔维娅说。她说得苦到根里。……“贞洁如冰，冷得像……像你一样……”
蒂金斯说：
“是！是！……告诉你，蒂金斯家的人从没有软的。一个也没有！他们心碎，碎得有理。……想想我可怜的父亲……”
西尔维娅说：
“别说了！”
“我两个兄弟同一天死在印度的团队里，相距不出一英里。我妹妹死在同一个礼拜：在海上，离他们不远。……不起眼的人。可不起眼的人也能叫人疼……”
“喂，总机”到了门口。蒂金斯让她请波特斯凯索勋爵下楼。……
“这些细节你当然该知道，”蒂金斯说，“作为我父亲继承人的母亲。……我父亲同一天接到三份阵亡通知。这足以碎掉一颗心。他只活了一个月。我见过他……”
西尔维娅尖声喊：
“住口！住口！住口！”她抓住壁炉台撑住自己。“你父亲是心碎死的，”她说，“因为你兄弟的至交拉格尔斯告诉他：你是个吃女人软饭的窝囊废，还让你最老的朋友的女儿怀了孩子……”
蒂金斯说：
“哦！啊！是！……我疑心过这个。其实我知道。想必那可怜人现在明白过来了。也许还不明白。……不要紧。”

### II / 二
===Original===

It has been remarked that the peculiarly English habit of self-suppression in matters of the emotions puts the Englishman at a great disadvantage in moments of unusual stresses. In the smaller matters of the general run of life he will be impeccable and not to be moved; but in sudden confrontations of anything but physical dangers he is apt﻿—he is, indeed almost certain﻿—to go to pieces very badly. This, at least, was the view of Christopher Tietjens, and he very much dreaded his interview with Lord Port Scatho﻿—because he feared that he must be near breaking point.
In electing to be peculiarly English in habits and in as much of his temperament as he could control﻿—for, though no man can choose the land of his birth or his ancestry, he can, if he have industry and determination, so watch over himself as materially to modify his automatic habits﻿—Tietjens had quite advisedly and of set purpose adopted a habit of behaviour that he considered to be the best in the world for the normal life. If every day and all day long you chatter at high pitch and with the logic and lucidity of the Frenchman; if you shout in self-assertion, with your hat on your stomach, bowing from a stiff spine and by implication threaten all day long to shoot your interlocutor, like the Prussian; if you are as lachrymally emotional as the Italian, or as drily and epigramatically imbecile over inessentials as the American, you will have a noisy, troublesome and thoughtless society without any of the surface calm that should distinguish the atmosphere of men when they are together. You will never have deep armchairs in which to sit for hours in clubs thinking of nothing at all﻿—or of the off-theory in bowling. On the other hand, in the face of death﻿—except at sea, by fire, railway accident or accidental drowning in rivers; in the face of madness, passion, dishonour or﻿—and particularly﻿—prolonged mental strain, you will have all the disadvantage of the beginner at any game and may come off very badly indeed. Fortunately death, love, public dishonour and the like are rare occurrences in the life of the average man, so that the great advantage would seem to have lain with English society; at any rate before the later months of the year 1914.

===Chinese===

有人说过：英国人那种在感情事务上自我克制的特有习惯，使他在非常压力的时刻处于极大的劣势。在日常生活的细务上，他无懈可击、不可动摇；可在突然面对除人身危险之外的任何事情时，他动辄——实在是几乎必然——彻底垮掉。这至少是克里斯托弗·蒂金斯的看法，所以他非常害怕同波特·斯卡索勋爵的会面——因为他怕自己已濱临崩溃点。
蒂金斯既已在习惯上、在他能控制的那部分性情上择定做一个格外地道的英国人——因为人虽无法选择出生的国度和血统，但只要有勤勉和决心，是可以看住自己、切实改变自己那些本能习惯的——他便完全自觉地、蓄意地采用了一套他认为是世上最宜于正常生活的行为习惯。倘若你从早到晚，日复一日，用法国人的逻辑和明晰高声畅谈；倘若你像普鲁士人那样，一手按着肚子上的帽子大声自命不凡，从僵硬的脊梁上鸭躬，整天整天暗示要枪毙了你交谈的对手；倘若你像意大利人那样动辄落泪，或者像美国人那样在无关紧要的事上干巴巴地掉警句蠢话，那么你得到的社会将喧闹、多事而欠思考，全无男人们聚处应有的那种表面宁静。你永远不会在俱乐部深陷的扶手椅里一坐几个钟头，什么也不想——或者只想投球的外旋理论。可另一方面，一旦面对死亡——海上失事、火灾、火车事故或落水除外；面对疯狂、激情、耻辱，或者——尤其是——长期的精神紧张，你就落得个初学者的全部劣势，下场可能极惨。所幸死亡、爱情、公开的耻辱等等，在普通人的生活里是罕有之事；所以大便宜似乎在英国社会一边；至少，在一九一四年最后几个月之前是这样。

===Original===

Death for man came but once: the danger of death so seldom as to be practically negligible: love of a distracting kind was a disease merely of the weak: public dishonour for persons of position, so great was the hushing up power of the ruling class, and the power of absorption of the remoter Colonies, was practically unknown.
Tietjens found himself now faced by all these things, coming upon him cumulatively and rather suddenly, and he had before him an interview that might cover them all and with a man whom he much respected and very much desired not to hurt. He had to face these, moreover, with a brain two-thirds of which felt numb. It was exactly like that.
It was not so much that he couldn’t use what brain he had as trenchantly as ever: it was that there were whole regions of fact upon which he could no longer call in support of his argument. His knowledge of history was still practically negligible: he knew nothing whatever of the humaner letters and, what was far worse, nothing at all of the higher and more sensuous phases of mathematics. And the comings back of these things was much slower than he had confessed to Sylvia. It was with these disadvantages that he had to face Lord Port Scatho.
Lord Port Scatho was the first man of whom Sylvia Tietjens had thought when she had been considering of men who were absolutely honourable, entirely benevolent﻿ ﻿… and rather lacking in constructive intelligence. He had inherited the management of one of the most respected of the great London banks, so that his commercial and social influences were very extended: he was extremely interested in promoting Low Church interests, the reform of the divorce laws and sports for the people, and he had a great affection for Sylvia Tietjens. He was forty-five, beginning to put on weight, but by no means obese; he had a large, quite round head, very high-coloured cheeks that shone as if with frequent ablutions, an uncropped, dark moustache, dark, very cropped, smooth hair, brown eyes, a very new grey tweed suit, a very new grey Trilby hat, a black tie in a gold ring and very new patent leather boots that had white calf tops. He had a wife almost the spit of himself in face, figure, probity, kindliness and interests, except that for his interest in sports for the people she substituted that for maternity hospitals. His heir was his nephew, Mr.

===Chinese===

对人说来死亡只有一回：死亡的危险少得几乎可以不计：教人神魂颠倒的爱情只是弱者的疾病；至于体面人的公开耻辱，既然统治阶级搭盖子的本事大、远方殖民地消化的容量也大，实际上闻所未闻。
蒂金斯此刻发现自己被这一切迎面赶上，而且是累积着、突然地压来；他面前还有一场也许把这些全包括在内的会面，对象又是一位他十分敬重、极不愿伤害的人。何况他还要带着一个三分之二已经麻木的脑子来面对。实际情况就是那样。
倒不是说他不能像从前那样锋利地使用他仅存的脑子；而是有一整片、一整片的事实领域，他再也无法调用来支撑自己的论证了。他的历史知识照旧少得可怜：他对人文学科一无所知，而糟糕得多的是，对数学中更高的、更诉诸感官的境界一无所知。而这些东西回潮的速度，远比他向西尔维娅承认的慢。他就是带着这些劣势去面对波特·斯卡索勋爵的。
波特·斯卡索勋爵，是西尔维娅·蒂金斯当初盘算“绝对正派、宅心仁厚……而又相当缺乏建设性智力”的男人时头一个想到的人。他继承管理着伦敦最受敬重的大银行之一，商界和社会影响都极广：他热衷于促进低教会派的事业、离婚法改革和平民体育，而且他极喜欢西尔维娅·蒂金斯。他四十五岁，开始发福，但绝算不上肥胖；一颗很大、很圆的头，两颊红扑扑的，亮得像刚洗过多少遍；一撇不修剪的深色唇髭，深色、剪得极短、油光的头发，棕色眼睛，一套崭新的灰粗呢西装，一顶崭新的灰特软呢帽，一条金环黑领带，一双崭新的、白犊皮高帮的漆皮靴。他的太太，脸型、身材、操守、仁厚和志趣都同他像一个模子，只是把平民体育换成了产科医院。他的继承人是他的外甥

===Original===

Brownlie, known as Brownie, who would also be physically the exact spit of his uncle, except that, not having put on flesh, he appeared to be taller and that his moustache and hair were both a little longer and more fair. This gentleman entertained for Sylvia Tietjens a gloomy and deep passion that he considered to be perfectly honourable because he desired to marry her after she had divorced her husband. Tietjens he desired to ruin because he wished to marry Mrs. Tietjens and partly because he considered Tietjens to be an undesirable person of no great means. Of this passion Lord Port Scatho was ignorant.
He now came into the Tietjens’ dining-room, behind the servant, holding an open letter: he walked rather stiffly because he was very much worried. He observed that Sylvia had been crying and was still wiping her eyes. He looked round the room to see if he could see in it anything to account for Sylvia’s crying. Tietjens was still sitting at the head of the lunch-table: Sylvia was rising from a chair beside the fireplace.
Lord Port Scatho said:
“I want to see you, Tietjens, for a minute on business.”
Tietjens said:
“I can give you ten minutes﻿ ﻿…”
Lord Port Scatho said:
“Mrs. Tietjens perhaps﻿ ﻿…”
He waved the open letter towards Mrs. Tietjens. Tietjens said:
“No! Mrs. Tietjens will remain.” He desired to say something more friendly. He said: “Sit down.”
Lord Port Scatho said:
“I shan’t be stopping a minute. But really﻿ ﻿…” and he moved the letter, but not with so wide a gesture, towards Sylvia.
“I have no secrets from Mrs. Tietjens,” Tietjens said. “Absolutely none﻿ ﻿…”
Lord Port Scatho said:
“… No, of course not﻿ ﻿… But﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“Similarly, Mrs. Tietjens has no secrets from me. Again absolutely none.”
Sylvia said:
“I don’t, of course, tell Tietjens about my maid’s love affairs or what the fish costs every day.”
Tietjens said:
“You’d better sit down.” He added on an impulse of kindness: “As a matter of fact I was just clearing up things for Sylvia to take over﻿ ﻿… this command.” It was part of the disagreeableness of his mental disadvantages that upon occasion he could not think of other than military phrases. He felt intense annoyance. Lord Port Scatho affected him with some of the slight nausea that in those days you felt at contact with the civilian who knew none of your thoughts, phrases or preoccupations.

===Chinese===

布朗利先生，人称布朗尼，将来的体格也准同舅舅一个模子，只是因为还没发福，显得高些，唇髭和头发都略长略淡。这位先生对西尔维娅·蒂金斯怀着一种阴郁而深沉的激情，他自认这份心完全正派，因为他指望在她同丈夫离婚之后娶她。至于蒂金斯，他要毁掉，一半因为想娶蒂金斯太太，一半因为在他看来蒂金斯是个家底有限、不受欢迎的人。这份激情，波特·斯卡索勋爵一无所知。
此刻他拿着一封拆开的信，跟在仆人身后走进蒂金斯家的饭厅；他走得相当僵硬，因为他心烦得很。他看见西尔维娅哭过，还在拭眼睛。他环视屋子，想找出能解释西尔维娅落泪的东西。蒂金斯还坐在午餐桌主位上：西尔维娅正从壁炉边的椅子上起身。
波特·斯卡索勋爵说：
“我想同你谈一分钟，蒂金斯，谈点正事。”
蒂金斯说：
“我可以给你十分钟……”
波特·斯卡索勋爵说：
“蒂金斯太太是不是……”
他把那封拆开的信朝蒂金斯太太那边摆了摆。蒂金斯说：
“不！蒂金斯太太留下。”他想说句客气些的话，就说：“请坐。”
波特·斯卡索勋爵说：
“我坐不了一分钟。不过说真的……”他又把信朝西尔维娅那边动了动，这回幅度小些。
“我对蒂金斯太太没有秘密，”蒂金斯说。“绝对没有……”
波特·斯卡索勋爵说：
“……当然，没有……可是……”
蒂金斯说：
“同样，蒂金斯太太对我也没有秘密。也是绝对没有。”
西尔维娅说：
“当然，我女仆的情事、每天鱼的价钱，我是不告诉蒂金斯的。”
蒂金斯说：
“你最好坐下。”他出于一时的好意添了一句：“其实我正收拾各种事，好交给西尔维娅接手……这个指挥权。”他的脑力残障有一样讨厌之处：时不时只会想得出军事用语。他恼恨极了。波特·斯卡索叫他微微作呕，那种感觉，同那年头你一接触到一个对你的心事、用语、牵挂一无所知的平民时的恶心一样。

===Original===

He added, nevertheless equably:
“One has to clear up. I’m going out.”
Lord Port Scatho said hastily:
“Yes; yes. I won’t keep you. One has so many engagements in spite of the war﻿ ﻿…” His eyes wandered in bewilderment. Tietjens could see them at last fixing themselves on the oil stains that Sylvia’s salad dressing had left on his collar and green tabs. He said to himself that he must remember to change his tunic before he went to the War Office. He must not forget. Lord Port Scatho’s bewilderment at these oil stains was such that he lost himself in the desire to account for them.﻿ ﻿… You could see the slow thoughts moving inside his square, polished brown forehead. Tietjens wanted very much to help him. He wanted to say: “It’s about Sylvia’s letter that you’ve got in your hand, isn’t it?” But Lord Port Scatho had entered the room with the stiffness, with the odd, high-collared sort of gait that on formal and unpleasant occasions Englishmen use when they approach each other; braced up, a little like strange dogs meeting in the street. In view of that, Tietjens couldn’t say “Sylvia.”﻿ ﻿… But it would add to the formality with unpleasantness if he said again “Mrs. Tietjens!” *That* wouldn’t help Port Scatho.﻿ ﻿…
Sylvia said suddenly:
“You don’t understand, apparently. My husband is going out to the front line. Tomorrow morning. It’s for the second time.”
Lord Port Scatho sat down suddenly on a chair beside the table. With his fresh face and brown eyes suddenly anguished he exclaimed:
“But, my dear fellow! You! Good God!” and then to Sylvia: “I beg your pardon!” To clear his mind he said again to Tietjens: “*You!* Going out tomorrow!” And, when the idea was really there, his face suddenly cleared. He looked with a swift, averted glance at Sylvia’s face and then for a fixed moment at Tietjens’ oil-stained tunic. Tietjens could see him explaining to himself with immense enlightenment that *that* explained both Sylvia’s tears and the oil on the tunic. For Port Scatho might well imagine that officers went to the conflict in their oldest clothes.﻿ ﻿…
But, if his puzzled brain cleared, his distressed mind became suddenly distressed doubly. He had to add to the distress he had felt on entering the room and finding himself in the midst of what he took to be a highly emotional family parting.

===Chinese===

但他还是平心静气地补了一句：
“总得交代清楚。我要出去了。”
波特·斯卡索勋爵连忙说：
“是；是。不多留你。虽说是战时，人的应酬还是这么多……”他的眼睛茫然四顾。蒂金斯看见它们终于落在自己衣领和绿领章上西尔维娅拌沙拉的调料留下的油渍上。他对自己说，去陆军部之前得记得换上衣。千万别忘。波特·斯卡索勋爵对这些油渍的困惑之大，竟叫他一门心思想解释它们。……你看得见那些缓慢的念头在他那方方正正、擦得发亮的棕色额头里移动。蒂金斯很想帮帮他。他想说：“是为你手里那封西尔维娅的信来的吧，是不是？”可波特·斯卡索勋爵进屋时带着一股僵硬、一种领着高领子式的古怪步态——英国人在正式而不快的场合彼此接近时用的那种；全身绷紧，有点像街上相遇的生狗。有这一层，蒂金斯就没法说“西尔维娅”。……可他要是再说一遍“蒂金斯太太！”，只会再添一层叫人不快的形式。那帮不了斯卡索。……
西尔维娅突然说：
“你显然不明白。我丈夫明早要去前线了。这是第二回。”
波特·斯卡索勋爵猛地坐在桌边一张椅子上。他那张新鲜的脸、那双棕眼睛骤然痛苦，喊道：
“可是，老兄！你！我的天！”随后又对西尔维娅：“恕我失态！”为理清脑子，他又对蒂金斯说了一遍：“你！明天就走！”待这念头真正落了地，他的脸豁然开朗。他飞快地、避开正眼地瞟了一眼西尔维娅的脸，又直直地盯了一刻蒂金斯沾油的上衣。蒂金斯看得出，他正带着大彻大悟向自己解释：这一下把西尔维娅的眼泪和上衣的油渍全解释通了。因为波特·斯卡索满可以想象，军官们是穿着最旧的衣服上阵的。……
可是，他困惑的脑子清了，痛苦的心却骤然加倍痛苦。他本就难受——进门就发现自己撞进了一场他认作生离死别的高度感情场面——如今又要添上一层。

===Original===

And Tietjens knew that during the whole war Port Scatho had never witnessed a family parting at all. Those that were not inevitable he would avoid like the plague, and his own nephew and all his wife’s nephews were in the bank. That was quite proper for, if the ennobled family of Brownlie were not of the Ruling Class﻿—who had to go!﻿—they were of the Administrative Class, who were privileged to stay. So he had seen no partings.
Of his embarrassed hatred of them he gave immediate evidence. For he first began several sentences of praise of Tietjens’ heroism which he was unable to finish and then getting quickly out of his chair exclaimed:
“In the circumstances then﻿ ﻿… the little matter I came about﻿ ﻿… I couldn’t of course think﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“No; don’t go. The matter you came about﻿—I know all about it of course﻿—had better be settled.”
Port Scatho sat down again: his jaw fell slowly: under his bronzed complexion his skin became a shade paler. He said at last:
“You know what I came about? But then﻿ ﻿…”
His ingenuous and kindly mind could be seen to be working with reluctance: his athletic figure drooped. He pushed the letter that he still held along the tablecloth towards Tietjens. He said, in the voice of one awaiting a reprieve:
“But you *can’t* be﻿ ﻿… aware﻿ ﻿… Not of this letter﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens left the letter on the cloth; from there he could read the large handwriting on the blue-grey paper:
“Mrs. Christopher Tietjens presents her compliments to Lord Port Scatho and the Honourable Court of Benchers of the Inn﻿ ﻿…” He wondered where Sylvia had got hold of that phraseology: he imagined it to be fantastically wrong. He said:
“I have already told you that I know about this letter, as I have already told you that I know﻿—and I will add that I approve!﻿—of all Mrs. Tietjens’ actions﻿ ﻿…” With his hard blue eyes he looked brow-beatingly into Port Scatho’s soft brown orbs, knowing that he was sending the message: “Think what you please and be damned to you!”
The gentle brown things remained on his face; then they filled with an expression of deep pain. Port Scatho cried:
“But good God! Then﻿ ﻿…”
He looked at Tietjens again. His mind, which took refuge from life in the affairs of the Low Church, of Divorce Law Reform and of Sports for the People, became a sea of pain at the contemplation of strong situations.

===Chinese===

而蒂金斯知道，整个大战期间，波特·斯卡索从没亲眼见过一场家庭送别。能躲的他像躲瘟疫一样躲；他自己的外甥、他太太的全部外甥都在银行里。这也完全正当：布朗利封了爵的这一家若不算统治阶级——那阶级非去不可！——便属行政阶级，有留下的特权。所以他一场送别也没见过。
他对这种场面的又窘又恨，立刻露了形迹。他先起了好几个夸蒂金斯英勇的头，都没说完，随即霍地离椅，喊道：
“那么，在这情形之下……我来的那点小事……我当然想都不能想……”
蒂金斯说：
“不；别走。你来的那件事——我当然全知道——还是了结了好。”
斯卡索又坐下：下巴慢慢垂下来：古铜色脸皮底下泛出一层灰白。他终于说：
“你知道我为什么来？那么……”
他那副赤诚良善的心智看得出在勉力运转：他运动员似的身架塌了下去。他把还捏着的信顺着桌布推向蒂金斯。他用的腔调，像候着缓刑的人：
“可你不至于……知道……总不至于这封信……”
蒂金斯没有把信从桌布上拿起来；从那里他读得出蓝灰纸上那些大字：“克里斯托弗·蒂金斯太太向波特·斯卡索勋爵及本院荣誉律师团敬致问候……”他纳闷西尔维娅从哪儿捾来的这套措辞：他料想错得离谱。他说：
“我已经告诉过你，我知道这封信；正如我已经告诉过你，我知道——并且我要添一句，我赞成！——蒂金斯太太的全部举动……”他用淡而硬的蓝眼睛逼视着斯卡索温软的棕色眼球，心里知道自己发出的话是：“随你怎么想，去你的！”
那对温柔的棕色东西一直望着他的脸；随即盛满了深重的痛苦。斯卡索喊：
“可是老天爷！那么……”
他又看看蒂金斯。他那颗惯于躲进低教会派、离婚法改革和平民体育里避世的头脑，一遇到强刺激的场面，便成了苦海。

===Original===

His eyes said:
“For heaven’s sake do not tell me that Mrs. Duchemin, the mistress of your dearest friend, is the mistress of yourself, and that you take this means of wreaking a vulgar spite on them.”
Tietjens, leaning heavily forward, made his eyes as enigmatic as he could; he said very slowly and very clearly:
“Mrs. Tietjens is, of course, not aware of *all* the circumstances.”
Port Scatho threw himself back in his chair.
“I don’t understand!” he said. “I do not understand. How am I to act? You do not wish me to act on this letter? You can’t!”
Tietjens, who found himself, said:
“You had better talk to Mrs. Tietjens about that. I will say something myself later. In the meantime let me say that Mrs. Tietjens would seem to me to be quite within her rights. A lady, heavily veiled, comes here every Friday and remains until four of the Saturday morning.﻿ ﻿… If you are prepared to palliate the proceeding you had better do so to Mrs. Tietjens﻿ ﻿…”
Port Scatho turned agitatedly on Sylvia.
“I can’t, of course, palliate,” he said. “God forbid.﻿ ﻿… But, my dear Sylvia﻿ ﻿… my dear Mrs. Tietjens.﻿ ﻿… In the case of two people so much esteemed!﻿ ﻿… We have, of course, argued the matter of principle. It is a part of a subject I have very much at heart: the granting of divorce﻿ ﻿… civil divorce, at least﻿ ﻿… in cases in which one of the parties to the marriage is in a lunatic asylum. I have sent you the pamphlets of E. S. P. Haynes that we publish. I know that as a Roman Catholic you hold strong views.﻿ ﻿… I do not, I assure you, stand for latitude﻿ ﻿…” He became then simply eloquent: he really had the matter at heart, one of his sisters having been for many years married to a lunatic. He expatiated on the agonies of the situation all the more eloquently in that it was the only form of human distress which he had personally witnessed.
Sylvia took a long look at Tietjens: he imagined for counsel. He looked at her steadily for a moment, then at Port Scatho, who was earnestly turned to her, then back at her. He was trying to say:
“Listen to Port Scatho for a minute. I need time to think of my course of action!”
He needed, for the first time in his life, time to think of his course of action.

===Chinese===

他的眼睛说：
“看在老天的份上，千万别告诉我：你最挚友的情妇杜什明太太，也就是你自己的情妇，而你是借这个法子对他们施一种庸俗的毒计。”
蒂金斯沉沉前倾，尽量叫自己的眼睛莫测高深；他说得极慢、极清楚：
“蒂金斯太太自然并不了解全部情况。”
斯卡索整个人朝椅背上一仰。
“我不明白！”他说。“我不明白。叫我怎么办？你不愿我照这封信行事？你不能！”
蒂金斯定了定神，说：
“这个你最好同蒂金斯太太谈。我自己过一会儿还要说几句。眼下我只想说：依我看，蒂金斯太太完全在她权利之内。一位戴厚面纱的太太，每星期五来这儿，待到星期六凌晨四点。……你若打算宽谅这一举动，最好也向蒂金斯太太宽谅……”
斯卡索激动地转向西尔维娅。
“我当然不能宽谅，”他说。“老天不容。……可是，亲爱的西尔维娅……亲爱的蒂金斯太太。……这样的两位备受敬重的人！……原则问题嘛，我们当然讨论过。那是我很上心的一件事：批准离婚……至少是民事离婚……在婚姻一方进了疯人院的情形下。海恩斯那些小册子，我们出的，我都寄给你了。我知道，作为罗马天主教徒，你持激烈的见解。……我向你担保，我不主张放宽……”随即他简直口若悬河：这事他真心上心，他有个妹妹多年嫁了个疯子。这又是他唯一亲眼见过的人间苦难，所以他讲起那种境况的煎熬来格外动人。
西尔维娅久久地看了蒂金斯一眼：他猜是求主意。他静静看了她一刻，又看看恳切朝向她的斯卡索，再看回她。他想说的是：
“听斯卡索讲一分钟。我需要时间想想对策！”
他平生头一回，需要时间想对策。

===Original===

He had been thinking with his under mind ever since Sylvia had told him that she had written her letter to the benchers denouncing Macmaster and his woman; ever since Sylvia had reminded him that Mrs. Duchemin in the Edinburgh to London express of the day before the war had been in his arms, he had seen with extraordinary clearness a great many north country scenes though he could not affix names to all the places. The forgetfulness of the names was abnormal: he ought to know the names of places from Berwick down to the vale of York﻿—but that he should have forgotten the incidents was normal enough. They had been of little importance: he preferred not to remember the phases of his friend’s love affair; moreover, the events that happened immediately afterwards had been of a nature to make one forget quite normally what had just preceded them. That Mrs. Duchemin should be sobbing on his shoulder in a locked corridor carriage hadn’t struck him as in the least important: she was the mistress of his dearest friend; she had had a very trying time for a week or so, ending in a violent, nervous quarrel with her agitated lover. She was, of course, crying off the effects of the quarrel which had been all the more shaking in that Mrs. Duchemin, like himself, had always been almost too self-contained. As a matter of fact he did not himself like Mrs. Duchemin, and he was pretty certain that she herself more than a little disliked him; so that nothing but their common feeling for Macmaster had brought them together. General Campion, however, was not to know that.﻿ ﻿… He had looked into the carriage in the way one does in a corridor just after the train had left.﻿ ﻿… He couldn’t remember the name.﻿ ﻿… Doncaster﻿ ﻿… No!﻿ ﻿… Darlington; it wasn’t that. At Darlington there was a model of the Rocket﻿ ﻿… or perhaps it isn’t the Rocket. An immense clumsy leviathan of a locomotive by﻿ ﻿… by﻿ ﻿… The great gloomy stations of the north-going trains﻿ ﻿… Durham﻿ ﻿… No! Alnwick.﻿ ﻿… No!﻿ ﻿… Wooler.﻿ ﻿… By God! Wooler! The junction for Bamborough﻿ ﻿…
It had been in one of the castles at Bamborough that he and Sylvia had been staying with the Sandbachs. Then﻿ ﻿… a name had come into his mind spontaneously!﻿ ﻿… Two names!﻿ ﻿… It was, perhaps, the turn of the tide! For the first time﻿ ﻿… To be marked with a red stone﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

打从西尔维娅告诉他、她已写了那封向律师团告发麦克马斯特和他女人的信起；打从西尔维娅提醒他、开战前一天爱丁堡开伦敦的快车上杜什明太太曾在他怀里起，他就一直在用下意识思考，许许多多北方的景象看得异常分明，虽叫不全那些地名。忘掉地名是不正常的：从贝里克到约克谷一带的地名他本该烂熟——可忘掉那些事情本身却相当正常。那些事本无关紧要：他宁可不去回想朋友恋情的种种阶段；再说，紧接着发生的事，其性质本就足以叫人极其正常地忘掉先前的种种。杜什明太太在锁上的走廊车厢里靠着他肩膀哭，在他看来毫无要紧：她是他挚友的情妇；她熬了一两个星期的苦日子，末了同她那位心烦意乱的情人大吵一场，闹得神经崩溃。她哭，自然是在宣泄那场争吵的余波——尤其折腾人的是，杜什明太太同他自己一样，素来自我克制得近乎过分。其实他本人不喜欢杜什明太太，而且相当有把握，她对他也不无厌恶；若不是对麦克马斯特共同的感情，他们根本走不到一起。可是坎皮恩将军无从知道这些。……火车刚开出去那会儿，他照走廊里的惯例朝车厢里望了一眼。……车站的名字他想不起来。……唐卡斯特……不！……达灵顿；也不是。达灵顿有一台“火箭号”的模型……也许不是“火箭号”。一台庞大笨重的洪荒巨兽似的机车，造它的人是……是……北行火车那些巨大阴沉的车站……达勒姆……不！阿尼克。……不！……伍勒。……天哪！伍勒！去班堡的枢纽站……
他就是同桑德巴赫夫妇一道，在班堡的一座城堡里住着的。这时……一个名字自己蹦进了脑子！……两个名字！……这兴许是潮水转向了！头一回……这以后要记上一颗红石子了：

===Original===

after this: some names, sometimes, on the tip of the tongue, might come over! He had, however, to get on.﻿ ﻿…
The Sandbachs, then, and he and Sylvia﻿ ﻿… others too﻿ ﻿… had been in Bamborough since mid-July: Eton and Harrow at Lord’s, waiting for the real house parties that would come with the 12th.﻿ ﻿… He repeated these names and dates to himself for the personal satisfaction of knowing that, amongst the repairs effected in his mind, these two remained: Eton and Harrow, the end of the London season: 12th of August, grouse shooting begins.﻿ ﻿… It was pitiful.﻿ ﻿…
When General Campion had come up to rejoin his sister he, Tietjens, had stopped only two days. The coolness between the two of them remained; it was the first time they had met, except in Court, after the accident.﻿ ﻿… For Mrs. Wannop, with grim determination, had sued the General for the loss of her horse. It had lived all right﻿—but it was only fit to draw a lawnmower for cricket pitches.﻿ ﻿… Mrs. Wannop, then, had gone bald-headed for the General, partly because she wanted the money, partly because she wanted a public reason for breaking with the Sandbachs. The General had been equally obstinate and had undoubtedly perjured himself in Court: not the best, not the most honourable, the most benevolent man in the world would not turn oppressor of the widow and orphan when his efficiency as a chauffeur was impugned or the fact brought to light that at a very dangerous turning he hadn’t sounded his horn. Tietjens had sworn that he hadn’t: the General that he had. There *could* not be any question of doubt, for the horn was a beastly thing that made a prolonged noise like that of a terrified peacock.﻿ ﻿… So Tietjens had not, till the end of that July, met the General again. It had been quite a proper thing for gentlemen to quarrel over and was quite convenient, though it had cost the General fifty pounds for the horse and, of course, a good bit over for costs. Lady Claudine had refused to interfere in the matter: she was privately of opinion that the General *hadn’t* sounded his horn, but the General was both a passionately devoted and explosive brother. She had remained closely intimate with Sylvia, mildly cordial with Tietjens and had continued to ask the Wannops to such of her garden parties as the General did not attend. She was also very friendly with Mrs. Duchemin.

===Chinese===

此后，有些名字，有时会到了嘴边就蹦出来！不过他得往前赶。……
于是，桑德巴赫夫妇，他同西尔维娅……还有旁人……七月中就到了班堡：伊顿对哈罗正在 Lord's 板球场打，等着十二号随松鸡开猎而来的真正的乡居聚会。……他对自己复述这些人名日期，为的是私下的安慰：在他脑子里修复好的东西当中，这两样还在：伊顿对哈罗，伦敦社交季的尾声；八月十二日，松鸡开猎。……可怜见。……
坎皮恩将军上来同妹妹会合之后，他蒂金斯只又待了两天。两人之间的冷淡还在；那是车祸之后、除法庭之外他们头一回见面。……因为沃诺普太太憋着一股狠劲，为她的马起诉了将军。马倒活了下来——只是从此只配拉板球场剪草的剪草机了。……沃诺普太太于是豁出去死缠将军，一半因为她要那笔钱，一半因为她要一个同桑德巴赫家绝交的公开理由。将军也一样的犟，而且毫无疑问在法庭上作了伪证：换作天下最好、最正派、最仁厚的人，一旦他开车的本事受了质疑、或“他在极危险的拐弯处没按喇叭”这一事实被揭出来，也都会变成欺压孤儿寡母的恶霸。蒂金斯发过誓说他没按；将军发誓说按了。这事不容有疑，因为那喇叭是个混账东西，响起来拖着长音，像只吓坏了的孔雀。……就这样，直到七月底，蒂金斯才再见到将军。这是绅士们大可一争的事，也相安无事，只是花了将军五十镑马钱，外加一笔不小的讼费。克劳迪娜夫人拒绝插手：她私下认为将军多半真没按喇叭，可将军既是位热烈护短、又一点就着的兄长。她同西尔维娅始终过从亲密，对蒂金斯淡淡客气，并且继续请沃诺普母女参加将军不到场的那些游园会。她同杜什明太太也极要好。

===Original===

Tietjens and the General had met with the restrained cordiality of English gentlemen who had some years before accused each other of perjury in a motor accident. On the second morning a violent quarrel had broken out between them on the subject of whether the General had or hadn’t sounded his horn. The General had ended up by shouting﻿ ﻿… really shouting:
“By God! If I ever get you under my command﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens remembered that he had quoted and given the number of a succinct paragraph in King’s Regs. dealing with the fate of general or higher field officers who gave their subordinates bad confidential reports because of private quarrels. The General had exploded into noises that ended in laughter.
“What a ragbag of a mind you have, Chrissie!” he said. “What’s King’s Regs. to you? And how do you know it’s paragraph 66 or whatever you say it is? I don’t.” He added more seriously: “*What* a fellow you are for getting into obscure rows! What in the world do you do it for?”
That afternoon Tietjens had gone to stop, a long way up in the moors, with his son, the nurse, his sister Effie and her children. They were the last days of happiness he was to know and he hadn’t known so many. He was then content. He played with his boy, who, thank God, was beginning to grow healthy at last. He walked about the moors with his sister Effie, a large, plain, parson’s wife, who had no conversation at all, though at times they talked of their mother. The moors were like enough to those above Groby to make them happy. They lived in a bare, grim farmhouse, drank great quantities of buttermilk and ate great quantities of Wensleydale. It was the hard, frugal life of his desire and his mind was at rest.
His mind was at rest because there was going to be a war. From the first moment of his reading the paragraph about the assassination of the Archduke Franz Ferdinand he had known that, calmly and with assurance. Had he imagined that this country would come in he would not have known a mind at rest. He loved this country for the run of its hills, the shape of its elm trees and the way the heather, running uphill to the skyline, meets the blue of the heavens. War for this country could only mean humiliation, spreading under the sunlight, an almost invisible pall, over the elms, the hills, the heather, like the vapour that spread from﻿ ﻿… oh, Middlesbrough!

===Chinese===

蒂金斯同将军再见面时，带着英国绅士间那种克制的好感——两人在几年前一场车祸中互相指责过伪证。第二天早上，两人为将军按没按喇叭大吵一场。将军最后竟喊了……真喊了：
“天哪！哪天你要落到我手底下……”
蒂金斯记得自己当场引用并背出了《国王条例》里简洁的一条的编号，讲的是将官或更高的野战军官因私怨给下属写恶劣密考报告的下场。将军炸了一通，末了大笑起来。
“你这脑子真是个破烂摊子，小克里斯！”他说。“《国王条例》关你什么事？你怎么知道是第六十六条还是你说的随便哪条？我都不知道。”他正经些补一句：“你这人可真会钻进不清不楚的烂架！到底图什么？”
那天下午，蒂金斯北上荒原深处，去同他的儿子、保姆、他姐姐艾菲和她的孩子们住在一起。那是他此生所知的最后几天幸福日子，而他享过的本也不多。他那时是安宁的。他陪儿子玩——感谢上帝，孩子总算开始长得结实了。他同姐姐艾菲在荒原上散步——她是个高大、相貌平平的牧师太太，全无谈兴，只是偶尔两人谈谈他们的母亲。那片荒原同格罗比山上的像极了，够叫人快活。他们住在一座光秃秃、冷峻的农舍里，喝大量的酪乳，吃大量的温斯利代奶酪。那正是他向往的艰苦、清简的日子，他的心是安宁的。
他的心安宁，因为要打仗了。从读到那条关于弗朗茨·斐迪南大公遇刺的短讯的头一刻起，他就知道了，平静地、笃定地。倘若他料到这个国家会参战，他心里就不得安宁了。他爱这个国家，爱它山峦的走势、榆树的形态、爱那石楠顺坡而上直抵天际线、同上天之蓝相接的样子。这个国家的战争只能意味着屈辱，在阳光底下铺开，一层几乎看不见的尸布，罩住榆树、山峦、石楠，像……哦，米德尔斯伯勒飘出的那股烟！

===Original===

We were fitted neither for defeat nor for victory: we could be true to neither friend nor foe. Not even to ourselves!
But of war for us he had no fear. He saw our Ministry sitting tight till the opportune moment and then grabbing a French channel port or a few German colonies as the price of neutrality. And he was thankful to be out of it; for his back-doorway out﻿—his second!﻿—was the French Foreign Legion. First Sylvia: then that! Two tremendous disciplines: for the soul and for the body.
The French he admired: for their tremendous efficiency, for their frugality of life, for the logic of their minds, for their admirable achievements in the arts, for their neglect of the industrial system, for their devotion, above all, to the eighteenth century. It would be restful to serve, if only as a slave, people who saw clearly, coldly, straight: not obliquely and with hypocrisy only such things as should deviously conduce to the standard of comfort of hogs and to lecheries winked at.﻿ ﻿… He would rather sit for hours on a bench in a barrack-room polishing a badge in preparation for the cruellest of route marches of immense lengths under the Algerian sun.
For, as to the Foreign Legion, he had had no illusion. You were treated not as a hero, but as a whipped dog; he was aware of all the *asticoteries*, the cruelties, the weight of the rifle, the cells. You would have six months of training in the desert and then be hurtled into the line to be massacred without remorse﻿ ﻿… as foreign dirt. But the prospect seemed to him one of deep peace: he had never asked for soft living and now was done with it.﻿ ﻿… The boy was healthy; Sylvia, with the economies they had made, very rich﻿ ﻿… and even at that date he was sure that, if the friction of himself, Tietjens, were removed, she would make a good mother.﻿ ﻿…
Obviously he might survive; but after that tremendous physical drilling what survived would not be himself, but a man with cleaned, sand-dried bones: a clear mind. His private ambition had always been for saintliness: he must be able to touch pitch and not be defiled. That he knew marked him off as belonging to the sentimental branch of humanity. He couldn’t help it: Stoic or Epicurean: Caliph in the harem or Dervish desiccating in the sand: one or the other you must be. And his desire was to be a saint of the Anglican variety﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

我们既经不起失败，也经不起胜利：我们对朋友对敌人都做不到忠诚。连对自己都做不到！
可我们打仗，他不怕。他看见我们的政府稳坐钓鱼台，等那恰当的时机一到，就抓一个法兰西海峡港口、或几块德国殖民地，作中立的价钱。他谢天谢地自己脱身在外；因为他的后门——第二道后门！——是法国外籍军团。先是西尔维娅；然后是这个！两重严酷的修行：一重修魂，一重修身。
他佩服法国人：佩服他们惊人的效率、清简的生活、头脑的条理、艺术上叫人叹服的成就、对工业体制的疏忽，尤其佩服他们对十八世纪的忠心。去伺候一群看得清、看得冷、看得直的人——哪怕只当奴隶——是安逸的：不至于像伺候那样一群人，斜着眼、带着伪善，只看得见那些歪歪扭扭能增进猪猡舒适水准、可以睁一眼闭一眼纵欲的东西。……他宁可一连几个钟头坐在营房的长凳上擦徽章，备着阿尔及利亚烈日下那顶残酷、顶顶漫长的长途拉练。
因为对外籍军团，他不存幻想。人家不会当你是英雄，只当你是条挨了抽的狗：那些施虐的下士、种种酷虐、步枪的分量、禁闭室，他全知道。你要在沙漠里受六个月的操练，然后被猛掷进战线，毫不留情地被屠戮……当作外国的脏东西。可这前景在他看来是一片深深的安宁：他从没求过安逸日子，如今更是断了念。……孩子结实了；西尔维娅靠着两人省下的钱，已经很富……而且直到那时他都相信，只要把他蒂金斯这层摩擦力撤掉，她会成为一位好母亲。……
他当然可能活下来；可经过那番严酷的肉身操练，活下来的将不再是他，而是一个洗过、被沙吹干的骨头架子的男人：一颗清明的头脑。他毕生的私愿从来是做圣徒：他要能摸得沥青而不沾黑。他知道这把他归入了人类里多情的一支。他没法子：斯多噶也罢，伊壁鸠鲁也罢：后宫里的哈里发，或沙地里烤干的托钵僧：二者必居其一。而他的愿望，是做一位英国国教式的圣徒……

===Original===

as his mother had been, without convent, ritual, vows, or miracles to be performed by your relics! That sainthood, truly, the Foreign Legion might give you.﻿ ﻿… The desire of every English gentleman from Colonel Hutchinson upwards.﻿ ﻿… A mysticism.﻿ ﻿…
Remembering the clear sunlight of those naivetes﻿—though in his blue gloom he had abated no jot of the ambition﻿—Tietjens sighed deeply as he came back for a moment to regard his dining-room. Really, it was to see how much time he had left in which to think out what to say to Port Scatho.﻿ ﻿… Port Scatho had moved his chair over to beside Sylvia and, almost touching her, was leaning over and recounting the griefs of his sister who was married to a lunatic. Tietjens gave himself again for a moment to the luxury of self-pity. He considered that he was dull-minded, heavy, ruined, and so calumniated that at times he believed in his own infamy, for it is impossible to stand up forever against the obloquy of your kind and remain unhurt in the mind. If you hunch your shoulders too long against a storm your shoulders will grow bowed.﻿ ﻿…
His mind stopped for a moment and his eyes gazed dully at Sylvia’s letter which lay open on the tablecloth. His thoughts came together, converging on the loosely-written words:
“For the last nine months a woman﻿ ﻿…”
He wondered swiftly what he had already said to Port Scatho: only that he had known of his wife’s letter; not when! And that he approved! Well, on principle! He sat up. To think that one could be brought down to thinking so slowly!
He ran swiftly over what had happened in the train from Scotland and before.﻿ ﻿…
Macmaster had turned up one morning beside their breakfast table in the farm house, much agitated, looking altogether too small in a cloth cap and a new grey tweed suit. He had wanted £50 to pay his bill with: at some place up the line above﻿ ﻿… above﻿ ﻿… Berwick suddenly flashed into Tietjens’ mind﻿ ﻿…
That was the geographic position. Sylvia was at Bamborough on the coast (junction Wooler); he, himself, to the northwest, on the moors. Macmaster to the northeast of him, just over the border: in some circumspect beauty spot where you did not meet people. Both Macmaster and Mrs. Duchemin would know that country and gurgle over its beastly literary associations.﻿ ﻿… The Shirra! Maida! Pet Marjorie﻿ ﻿… Faugh!

===Chinese===

像他母亲那样，不要修道院、不要仪轨、不要誓愿、也不要靠遗骨显的圣迹！那种圣洁，说真的，外籍军团倒真能给你。……从哈钦森上校以降，每一位英国绅士的心愿。……一种神秘主义。……
回想这些天真的念头上澄澈的阳光——虽然在他的蓝色阴郁里，那志向一分一毫也没有减退——蒂金斯深深叹了口气，回过神来，重新打量他的饭厅。说真的，是看看自己还剩多少时间，想清楚对斯卡索说什么。……斯卡索已把椅子挪到西尔维娅身边，几乎是贴着她俯身讲着他那位嫁了疯子的妹妹的种种苦楚。蒂金斯又纵容了自己一刻自怜。他认为自己脑子迟钝、身子沉重、家业败光，又遭尽诽谤，以至于有时连自己也信了自己的恶名——因为长年顶着同类的唾骂而心智无损，是不可能的。在暴风雨里耸肩耸得太久，肩就会驼。……
他的思路停了一停，眼睛怔怔地望着摊在桌布上的西尔维娅的信。他的念头聚拢来，聚在那行写得散漫的字上：
“过去九个月里，有一个女人……”
他飞快地盘算自己方才对斯卡索说了什么：只说了知道他妻子的信；没说什么时候知道的！还说了赞成！嗯，原则上！他坐直身子。想想吧，一个人竟能落到思考得这样慢！
他飞快地过了一遍从苏格兰来的火车上和之前发生的事。……
一天早晨，麦克马斯特突然出现在农舍里他们的早餐桌旁，激动万分，戴布帽、穿一身崭新的灰粗呢，显得格外矮小。他要五十镑付账：在铁路线上头某个地方……某个……贝里克蓦地闪进蒂金斯的脑子……
地理方位是这样：西尔维娅在海岸的班堡（枢纽站伍勒）；他自己在西边北面的荒原上。麦克马斯特在他东北面，刚过边界：在某个幽僻的风景区，碰不见人的地方。麦克马斯特和杜什明太太都熟那片地方，会为那些讨厌的文学掌故啧啧称奇。……希拉！梅达！小玛乔丽……呸！

===Original===

Macmaster would, no doubt, turn an honest penny by writing articles about it and Mrs. Duchemin would hold his hand.﻿ ﻿…
She had become Macmaster’s mistress, as far as Tietjens knew, after a dreadful scene in the rectory, Duchemin having mauled his wife like a savage dog, and Macmaster in the house.﻿ ﻿… It was natural: a Sadix reaction as it were. But Tietjens rather wished they hadn’t. Now it appeared they had been spending a week together﻿ ﻿… or more. Duchemin by that time was in an asylum.﻿ ﻿…
From what Tietjens had made out they had got out of bed early one morning to take a boat and see the sunrise on some lake and had passed an agreeable day together quoting, “Since when we stand side by side only hands may meet” and other poems of Gabriel Charles Dante Rossetti, no doubt to justify their sin. On coming home they had run their boat’s nose into the tea-table of the Port Scathos with Mr. Brownlie, the nephew, just getting out of a motor to join them. The Port Scatho group were spending the night at the Macmasters’ hotel which backed on to the lake. It was the ordinary damn sort of thing that must happen in these islands that are only a few yards across.
The Macmasters appear to have lost their heads frightfully, although Lady Port Scatho had been as motherly as possible to Mrs. Duchemin; so motherly, indeed, that if they had not been unable to observe anything, they might have recognised the Port Scathos as backers rather than spies upon themselves. It was, no doubt, however, Brownlie who had upset them: he wasn’t very civil to Macmaster, whom he knew as a friend of Tietjens. He had dashed up from London in his motor to consult his uncle, who was dashing down from the west of Scotland, about the policy of the bank in that moment of crisis.﻿ ﻿…
Macmaster, anyhow, did not spend the night in the hotel, but went to Jedburgh or Melrose or some such place, turning up again almost before it was light to have a frightful interview about five in the morning with Mrs. Duchemin, who, towards three, had come to a disastrous conclusion as to her condition. They had lost their nerves for the first time in their association, and they had lost them very badly indeed, the things that Mrs. Duchemin said to Macmaster seeming almost to have passed belief.﻿ ﻿…
Thus, when Macmaster turned up at Tietjens’ breakfast, he was almost out of his mind.

===Chinese===

麦克马斯特无疑要写文章捞一笔正当的外快，杜什明太太替他捧笔。……
她成了麦克马斯特的情妇——据蒂金斯所知——是在牧师住宅里那场可怕场面之后：杜什明像条疯狗一样撕咬他的妻子，而麦克马斯特就在那所房子里。……这也自然：算是一种萨迪克斯式的反应。可蒂金斯倒宁愿意他们没有。如今看来，他们已共度了一个星期……或者更久。杜什明那时已经进了疯人院。……
照蒂金斯拼凑出的情形：一天清晨，他们起个大早，要划船去看某个湖上的日出，快快活活过了一天，一路引着“从那时起我们并肩而立，只有手可以相触”，还有加布里埃尔·查尔斯·但丁·罗塞蒂旁的诗，无疑是为他们的罪作辩护。回家时，他们的船头正顶进波特·斯卡索夫妇的茶桌，外甥布朗利先生正从一辆汽车里下来要加入他们。波特·斯卡索一伙当夜就住在麦克马斯特那家背靠湖的旅馆。这就是这种方圆只有几码的小岛上必然要发生的倒霉事。
麦克马斯特夫妇看来是吓昏了头，虽然波特·斯卡索夫人对杜什明太太慈母到了家；慈母到这般地步——若不是他们已经什么都看不见了——本可认出波特·斯卡索夫妇是靠山而不是盯梢。不过，叫他们心乱的多半是布朗利：他对麦克马斯特不大客气，他认得他是蒂金斯的朋友。他是从伦敦开汽车冲来的，要同他舅舅——舅舅正从苏格兰西边赶来——商议那危急时刻的银行方针。……
反正，麦克马斯特没在旅馆过夜，去了杰德堡还是梅尔罗斯还是什么诸如此类的地方，天没亮又赶回来，在早晨五点光景同杜什明太太有一场可怕的会面——她凌晨三点断定了自己的身体状况是一场灾祸。他们相好以来头一回了失了方寸，而且失得厉害，杜什明太太对麦克马斯特说的话，听上去几乎叫人不能相信。……
就这样，麦克马斯特出现在蒂金斯的早餐桌上时，已经几乎魂不附体。

===Original===

He wanted Tietjens to go over in the motor he had brought, pay the bill at the hotel, and travel down to town with Mrs. Duchemin, who was certainly in no condition to travel alone. Tietjens was also to make up the quarrel with Mrs. Duchemin and to lend Macmaster £50 in cash, as it was then impossible to change cheques anywhere. Tietjens got the money from his old nurse, who, because she distrusted banks, carried great sums in £5 notes in a pocket under her under-petticoat.
Macmaster, pocketing the money, had said:
“That makes exactly two thousand guineas that I owe you. I’m making arrangements to repay you next week.﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens remembered that he had rather stiffened and had said: “For God’s sake don’t. I beg you not to. Have Duchemin properly put under trustees in lunacy, and leave his capital alone. I really beg you. You don’t know what you’ll be letting yourselves in for. You don’t owe me anything and you can always draw on me.”
Tietjens never knew what Mrs. Duchemin had done about her husband’s estate over which she had at that date had a power of attorney; but he had imagined that, from that time on, Macmaster had felt a certain coldness for himself and that Mrs. Duchemin had hated him. During several years Macmaster had been borrowing hundreds at a time from Tietjens. The affair with Mrs. Duchemin had cost her lover a good deal: he had weekended almost continuously in Rye at the expensive hostel. Moreover, the famous Friday parties for geniuses had been going on for several years now, and these had meant new furnishings, bindings, carpets, and loans to geniuses﻿—at any rate before Macmaster had had the ear of the Royal Bounty. So the sum had grown to £2,000, and now to guineas. And, from that date, the Macmasters had not offered any repayment.
Macmaster had said that he dare not travel with Mrs. Duchemin because all London would be going south by that train. All London had. It pushed in at every conceivable and inconceivable station all down the line﻿—it was the great rout of the 3﻿–﻿8﻿–﻿14. Tietjens had got on board at Berwick, where they were adding extra coaches, and by giving a £5 note to the guard, who hadn’t been able to promise isolation for any distance, had got a locked carriage. It hadn’t remained locked for long enough to let Mrs. Duchemin have her cry out﻿—but it had apparently served to make some mischief.

===Chinese===

他要蒂金斯坐他带来的汽车过去，替旅馆付账，然后陪杜什明太太坐车进城——她那样子实在没法独自动身。蒂金斯还要去同杜什明太太讲和，并借给麦克马斯特五十镑现钱，因为那时到处兑不开支票。钱是蒂金斯从他的老保姆那里拿的——她因为信不过银行，在衬裙底下的口袋里揣着大把五镑的票子。
麦克马斯特揣起钱，说过：
“这样我欠你的正好两千几尼。我正在安排下星期还你。…”
蒂金斯记得自己当时相当硬了一硬，说：“看在上帝份上别这样。求你别这样。给杜什明正式办妥疯癫托管，别动他的本金。我真的求你。你们不知道会给自己惹来什么。你不欠我什么，你随时可以支我的钱。”
杜什明太太后来怎么处置她丈夫的产业——她那时持有委托书——蒂金斯始终不知道；但他料想，从那时起，麦克马斯特对他存了一层冷淡，杜什明太太则恨上了他。好几年里，麦克马斯特一次几百几百地朝蒂金斯借钱。同杜什明太太的这段情叫他破费不小：他几乎每个周末都住在拉伊那家昂贵的客栈。再说，著名的星期五天才晚会也办了好几年了，这意味着新家具、新装帧、新地毯，还有借给天才们的钱——至少在麦克马斯特攀上王室恩俸之前。于是数目涨到了两千镑，如今又成了几尼。而自那日起，麦克马斯特夫妇再没提过还钱。
麦克马斯特说过，他不敢同杜什明太太同行，因为那趟车全伦敦都要南下。全伦敦真的南下。它沿着铁路在一切想得到想不到的站台上往里挤——那是一九一四年八月三日的大溃逃。蒂金斯在贝里克上的车，那里正在加挂车厢；他给了列车员五镑——列车员答应不了多远的独间——总算弄到一节锁闭的车厢。锁了没多久，不够让杜什明太太哭个痛快——可是显然种下了祸根。

===Original===

The Sandbach party had got on, no doubt at Wooler; the Port Scatho party somewhere else. Their petrol had run out somewhere and sales were stopped, even to bankers. Macmaster, who after all had travelled by the same train, hidden beneath two bluejackets, had picked up Mrs. Duchemin at King’s Cross and that had seemed the end of it.
Tietjens, back in his dining-room, felt relief and also anger. He said:
“Port Scatho. Time’s getting short. I’d like to deal with this letter if you don’t mind.”
Port Scatho came as if up out of a dream. He had found the process of attempting to convert Mrs. Tietjens to divorce law reform very pleasant﻿—as he always did. He said:
“Yes!﻿ ﻿… Oh, yes!”
Tietjens said slowly:
“If you can listen.﻿ ﻿… Macmaster has been married to Mrs. Duchemin exactly nine months.﻿ ﻿… Have you got that? Mrs. Tietjens did not know this till this afternoon. The period Mrs. Tietjens complains of in her letter is nine months. She did perfectly right to write the letter. As such I approve of it. If she had known that the Macmasters were married she would not have written it. I didn’t know she was going to write it. If I had known she was going to write it I should have requested her not to. If I had requested her not to she would, no doubt, have done so. I did know of the letter at the moment of your coming in. I had heard of it at lunch only ten minutes before. I should, no doubt, have heard of it before, but this is the first time I have lunched at home in four months. I have today had a day’s leave as being warned for foreign service. I have been doing duty at Ealing. Today is the first opportunity I have had for serious business conversation with Mrs. Tietjens.﻿ ﻿… Have you got all that?﻿ ﻿…”
Port Scatho was running towards Tietjens, his hand extended, and over his whole shining personage the air of an enraptured bridegroom. Tietjens moved his right hand a little to the right, thus eluding the pink, well-fleshed hand of Port Scatho. He went on frigidly:
“You had better, in addition, know as follows: The late Mr. Duchemin was a scatological﻿—afterwards a homicidal﻿—lunatic. He had recurrent fits, usually on a Saturday morning. That was because he fasted﻿—not abstained merely﻿—on Fridays. On Fridays he also drank. He had acquired the craving for drink when fasting, from finishing the sacramental wine after communion services.

===Chinese===

桑德巴赫一行无疑是在伍勒上的车；波特·斯卡索一行在别的什么地方。他们的汽油在路上耗尽了，停售，连银行家也不例外。麦克马斯特毕竟坐的同一趟车，藏在两名水兵底下，在国王十字站接走了杜什明太太，事情就算完了。
蒂金斯回到自己的饭厅，心里一松，又有火。他说：
“斯卡索。时间不多了。这封信，我想处理一下，你不介意吧。”
斯卡索像从梦里惊起。他方才劝蒂金斯太太皈依离婚法改革，劝得津津有味——他一向如此。他说：
“是！……哦，是！”
蒂金斯慢慢说：
“你听好。……麦克马斯特同杜什明太太结婚，到今天整九个月。……听明白了吗？蒂金斯太太直到今天下午才知道。她信中控告的期间正是九个月。她写信写得完全正当。因此我赞成。她若知道麦克马斯特夫妇已经结婚，就不会写。我不知道她要写。我若知道她要写，会请求她别写。我若请求她别写，她无疑就不写了。你进门那一刻，我确实知道这封信。十分钟前，午饭时我才听说。我本该早听说，可四个月来这是我头一回在家吃午饭。今天我得了休整假，因为接到开拔国外的通知。我一直驻在伊灵执勤。今天是我头一回有机会同蒂金斯太太谈正经事。……都听清了吗？……”
斯卡索朝蒂金斯奔过来，伸着手，整个人焕发着新郎官的狂喜。蒂金斯把右手往右一挪，避开了斯卡索那只粉红饱满的手。他冷冷地接着说：
“你最好还知道如下几件事：已故的杜什明先生是个秽迹——后来是杀人——的疯子。他周期性发作，通常在星期六早晨。那是因为他每逢星期五守斋——不是小斋，是大斋——的缘故。星期五他还喝酒。他这嗜酒的瘾，就是守斋时喝剩的圣餐酒落下的。

===Original===

That is a not unknown occurrence. He behaved latterly with great physical violence to Mrs. Duchemin. Mrs. Duchemin, on the other hand, treated him with the utmost consideration and concern: she might have had him certified much earlier, but, considering the pain that confinement must cause him during his lucid intervals, she refrained. I have been an eyewitness of the most excruciating heroisms on her part. As for the behaviour of Macmaster and Mrs. Duchemin, I am ready to certify﻿—and I believe society accepts﻿—that it has been most﻿ ﻿… oh, circumspect and right!﻿ ﻿… There has been no secret of their attachment to each other. I believe that their determination to behave with decency during their period of waiting has not been questioned.﻿ ﻿…”
Lord Port Scatho said:
“No! no! Never﻿ ﻿… Most﻿ ﻿… as you say﻿ ﻿… circumspect and, yes﻿ ﻿… right!”
“Mrs. Duchemin,” Tietjens continued, “has presided at Macmaster’s literary Fridays for a long time; of course since long before they were married. But, as you know, Macmaster’s Fridays have been perfectly open: you might almost call them celebrated.﻿ ﻿…”
Lord Port Scatho said:
“Yes! yes! indeed﻿ ﻿… I sh’d be only too glad to have a ticket for Lady Port Scatho﻿ ﻿…”
“She’s only got to walk in,” Tietjens said. “I’ll warn them: they’ll be pleased.﻿ ﻿… If, perhaps, you would look in tonight! They have a special party.﻿ ﻿… But Mrs. Macmaster was always attended by a young lady who saw her off by the last train to Rye. Or I very frequently saw her off myself, Macmaster being occupied by the weekly article that he wrote for one of the papers on Friday nights.﻿ ﻿… They were married on the day after Mr. Duchemin’s funeral.﻿ ﻿…”
“You can’t blame ’em!” Lord Port Scatho proclaimed.
“I don’t propose to,” Tietjens said. “The really frightful tortures Mrs. Duchemin had suffered justified﻿—and indeed necessitated﻿—her finding protection and sympathy at the earliest possible moment. They have deferred this announcement of their union partly out of respect for the usual period of mourning, partly because Mrs. Duchemin feels very strongly that, with all the suffering that is now abroad, wedding feasts and signs of rejoicing on the part of nonparticipants are eminently to be deprecated. Still, the little party of tonight is by way of being an announcement that they are married.﻿ ﻿…” He paused to reflect for a moment.

===Chinese===

这不罕见。他后来对杜什明太太屡施肉身暴力。杜什明太太呢，待他体谅周全到了极点：她本可以早得多送他去鉴定，只是想到监禁在他神志清明时会带给他的痛苦，才忍了没有。我亲眼见证过她顶顶揪心的英勇。至于麦克马斯特先生和杜什明太太的品行，我愿意具结作证——我相信社会也认了——它一直极其……哦，检点而正当！……他们彼此的感情从来不是秘密。我相信，他们那段等待期间行为端正的决心，从没有人质疑过。…”
波特·斯卡索勋爵说：
“没有！没有！从来没有……极其……正如你说……检点，而且，对……正当！”
“杜什明太太，”蒂金斯接着说，“主持麦克马斯特的文学星期五晚会很久了；当然，早在他俩结婚之前。可你知道，麦克马斯特的星期五向来是完全公开的：几乎可以说是远迩闻名。…”
波特·斯卡索勋爵说：
“是！是！可不是嘛……能给波特·斯卡索夫人弄张请柬，我只有太高兴…”
“她只管走进去就是，”蒂金斯说。“我打个招呼：他们会高兴的。……今晚您不妨就过去看看！他们有个特别的聚会。……不过麦克马斯特太太从前总有一位年轻小姐陪着，送她上回拉伊的末班车。有时也常是我去送，麦克马斯特星期五夜里要给一家报纸写每周文章，脱不开身。……他们在杜什明先生葬礼的第二天结的婚。…”
“这怪不得他们！”波特·斯卡索勋爵宣称。
“我也不打算怪，”蒂金斯说。“杜什明太太遭受过的那些真正可怕的折磨，证明她——而且实际上也要求她——尽早寻得保护与同情，是正当的。他们迟迟不宣布结合，一半是顾及惯常的服丧期，一半因为杜什明太太深感到，值此遍地苦难之际，局外人的婚宴和喜庆的标志尤其应当避免。不过，今晚的小聚会，就权当宣布他们已婚。…”他顿了顿，想了一刻。

“I perfectly understand!” Lord Port Scatho exclaimed. “I perfectly approve. Believe me, I and Lady Port Scatho will do everything.﻿ ﻿… Everything!﻿ ﻿… Most admirable people.﻿ ﻿… Tietjens, my dear fellow, your behaviour﻿ ﻿… most handsome.﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“Wait a minute﻿ ﻿… There was an occasion in August, ’14. In a place on the border. I can’t remember the name.﻿ ﻿…”
Lord Port Scatho burst out:
“My dear fellow﻿ ﻿… I beg you won’t.﻿ ﻿… I beseech you not to﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens went on:
“Just before then Mr. Duchemin had made an attack on his wife of an unparalleled violence. It was that that caused his final incarceration. She was not only temporarily disfigured, but she suffered serious internal injuries and, of course, great mental disturbance. It was absolutely necessary that she should have change of scene.﻿ ﻿… But I think you will bear me out that, in that case, too, their behaviour was﻿ ﻿… again, circumspect and right.﻿ ﻿…”
Port Scatho said:
“I know; I know﻿ ﻿… Lady Port Scatho and I agreed﻿—even without knowing what you have just told me﻿—that the poor things almost exaggerated it.﻿ ﻿… He slept, of course, at Jedburgh?﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“Yes! They almost exaggerated it.﻿ ﻿… I had to be called in to take Mrs. Duchemin home.﻿ ﻿… It caused, apparently, misunderstandings.﻿ ﻿…”
Port Scatho﻿—full of enthusiasm at the thought that at least two unhappy victims of the hateful divorce laws had, with decency and circumspectness, found the haven of their desires﻿—burst out:
“By God, Tietjens, if I ever hear a man say a word against you.﻿ ﻿… Your splendid championship of your friend.﻿ ﻿… Your﻿ ﻿… your unswerving devotion﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“Wait a minute, Port Scatho, will you?” He was unbuttoning the flap of his breast pocket.
“A man who can act so splendidly in one instance,” Port Scatho said.﻿ ﻿… “And your going to France.﻿ ﻿… If anyone﻿ ﻿… if *any*one﻿ ﻿… dares﻿ ﻿…”
At the sight of a vellum-cornered, green-edged book in Tietjens’ hand Sylvia suddenly stood up; as Tietjens took from an inner flap a cheque that had lost its freshness she made three great strides over the carpet to him.
“Oh, Chrissie!﻿ ﻿…” she cried out. “He hasn’t﻿ ﻿… That beast hasn’t﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens answered:
“He has﻿ ﻿…” He handed the soiled cheque to the banker. Port Scatho looked at it with slow bewilderment.
“ ‘Account overdrawn,’ ” he read. “Brownie’s﻿ ﻿… my nephew’s handwriting.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“我完全理解！”波特·斯卡索勋爵喊。“我完全赞成。相信我，我和波特·斯卡索夫人会尽力……尽力！…顶顶可敬的人。……蒂金斯，老兄，你的行止……漂亮极了。…”
蒂金斯说：
“且慢……有一件事，一九一四年八月。在边界一个地方。地名我想不起来。…”
波特·斯卡索勋爵脱口喊道：
“老兄……我求你别说了。……我恳求你别…”
蒂金斯接着说：
“就在那之前，杜什明先生对他妻子发动了一场无与伦比的暴行。正是那一遭叫他最终被收进疯人院。她不仅一时破了相，还受了严重的内伤，精神上自然也大受震动。她绝对需要换换环境。……可我想，这一点你也能为我作证：在那一场合，他们的行为也是……同样，检点而正当。…”
斯卡索说：
“我知道；我知道……波特·斯卡索夫人和我当时就认为——连你方才告诉我的都不知道——那对可怜人几乎做过头了。……他当然是睡在杰德堡的？…”
蒂金斯说：
“是！他们几乎做过头了。……只得叫我来接杜什明太太回家。……这显然引起了误会。…”
斯卡索——一想到至少有两个可恨的离婚法的可怜受害者，以检点和自持寻得了心愿的港湾，满腔热忱——脱口喊道：
“天哪，蒂金斯，往后谁敢当你面说你一个字。……你为朋友做的这场漂亮的辩护。……你的……你这份不移的忠义…”
蒂金斯说：
“且慢一下，斯卡索，行吗？”他正解开胸前口袋的盖。
“能在一件事情上做得如此漂亮的人，”斯卡索说。……“何况你还要去法兰西。……谁要是……谁要是……竟敢…”
一看见蒂金斯手里那本羊皮包角、绿边的书，西尔维娅霍地站起来；蒂金斯从内袋里抽出一张失去了新鲜气色的支票时，她三大步跨过地毯奔到他跟前。
“哦，克里斯！…”她喊。“他没……那畜生没…”
蒂金斯答：
“他干了…”他把那张脏了的支票递给银行家。斯卡索缓缓地、困惑地看着。
“‘账户透支，’”他念道。“布朗尼的……我外甥的手笔。…”

===Original===

To the club﻿ ﻿… It’s﻿ ﻿…”
“You aren’t going to take it lying down?” Sylvia said. “Oh, thank goodness, you aren’t going to take it lying down.”
“No! I’m not going to take it lying down,” Tietjens said. “Why should I?” A look of hard suspicion came over the banker’s face.
“You appear,” he said, “to have been overdrawing your account. People should not overdraw their accounts. For what sum are you overdrawn?”
Tietjens handed his passbook to Port Scatho.
“I don’t understand on what principle you work,” Sylvia said to Tietjens. “There are things you take lying down; this you don’t.”
Tietjens said:
“It doesn’t matter, really. Except for the child.”
Sylvia said:
“I guaranteed an overdraft for you up to a thousand pounds last Thursday. You can’t be overdrawn over a thousand pounds.”
“I’m not overdrawn at all,” Tietjens said. “I was for about fifteen pounds yesterday. I didn’t know it.”
Port Scatho was turning over the pages of the passbook, his face completely blank.
“I simply don’t understand,” he said. “You appear to be in credit.﻿ ﻿… You appear always to have been in credit except for a small sum now and then. For a day or two.”
“I was overdrawn,” Tietjens said, “for fifteen pounds yesterday. I should say for three or four hours: the course of a post, from my army agent to your head office. During these two or three hours your bank selected two out of six of my cheques to dishonour﻿—both being under two pounds. The other one was sent back to my mess at Ealing, who won’t, of course, give it back to me. That also is marked ‘account overdrawn,’ and in the same handwriting.”
“But good God,” the banker said. “That means your ruin.”
“It certainly means my ruin,” Tietjens said. “It was meant to.”
“But,” the banker said﻿—a look of relief came into his face which had begun to assume the aspect of a broken man’s﻿—“you must have other accounts with the bank﻿ ﻿… a speculative one, perhaps, on which you are heavily down.﻿ ﻿… I don’t myself attend to client’s accounts, except the very huge ones, which affect the bank’s policy.”
“You ought to,” Tietjens said. “It’s the very little ones you ought to attend to, as a gentleman making his fortune out of them. I have no other account with you. I have never speculated in anything in my life. I have lost a great deal in Russian securities﻿—a great deal for me. But so, no doubt, have you.”
“Then﻿ ﻿… betting!” Port Scatho said.

===Chinese===

给俱乐部的……这是……”
“你不打算忍气吞声吧？”西尔维娅说。“哦，谢天谢地，你不打算忍气吞声。”
“不！我不打算忍气吞声，”蒂金斯说。“凭什么要？”银行家脸上掠过一层冷硬的疑色。
“看来，”他说，“你一直在透支。人不应透支。你透支了多少？”
蒂金斯把存折递给斯卡索。
“我不明白你行事的道理，”西尔维娅对蒂金斯说。“有些事你忍了；这件你不忍。”
蒂金斯说：
“其实不要紧。除了为孩子。”
西尔维娅说：
“上星期四我替你担保了一千镑的透支额度。你不可能透支超过一千镑。”
“我根本没透支，”蒂金斯说。“昨天透支了大约十五镑。我并不知道。”
斯卡索一页页翻着存折，脸上全然空白。
“我干脆不明白，”他说。“你账上有余。……你好像一直有余，除了偶尔一小笔、透个一两天。”
“我昨天透支了十五镑，”蒂金斯说。“应该说透支了三四个钟头：一封信的路程，从我的陆军代理行到你们总行。就在这三四个钟头里，贵行从我六张支票里挑出两张退了——都不到两镑。另一张退回了我在伊灵的军官食堂，人家当然不肯再还我。那张也写着‘账户透支’，同一笔迹。”
“可是老天爷，”银行家说，“这等于毁了了你。”
“这当然等于毁了了我，”蒂金斯说。“存心如此。”
“可是，”银行家说——他脸上那副垮掉的人的神色，此时透出一层宽慰——“你在本行一定还有别的户头……兴许一个投机的户头，亏空很大。……我自己不管客户的户头，只管顶大的那些，关系银行方针的。”
“你该管的，”蒂金斯说。“该管的正是那些顶小的户头，你正是靠它们发的绅士财。我在你们行没有别的户头。我一辈子没投机过。我在俄国证券上损失很大——对我而言很大。您多半也一样。”
“那么……赌马！”斯卡索说。

===Original===

“I never put a penny on a horse in my life,” Tietjens said. “I know too much about them.”
Port Scatho looked at the faces first of Sylvia, then of Tietjens. Sylvia, at least, was his very old friend. She said:
“Christopher never bets and never speculates. His personal expenses are smaller than those of any man in town. You could say he had *no* personal expenses.”
Again the swift look of suspicion came into Port Scatho’s open face.
“Oh,” Sylvia said, “you couldn’t suspect Christopher and me of being in a plot to blackmail you.”
“No; I couldn’t suspect that,” the banker said. “But the other explanation is just as extraordinary.﻿ ﻿… To suspect the bank﻿ ﻿… the *bank*﻿ ﻿… How do *you* account?﻿ ﻿…” He was addressing Tietjens; his round head seemed to become square, below; emotion worked on his jaws.
“I’ll tell you simply this,” Tietjens said. “You can then repair the matter as you think fit. Ten days ago I got my marching orders. As soon as I had handed over to the officer who relieved me I drew cheques for everything I owed﻿—to my military tailor, the mess﻿—for one pound twelve shillings. I had also to buy a compass and a revolver, the Red Cross orderlies having annexed mine when I was in hospital.﻿ ﻿…”
Port Scatho said: “Good God!”
“Don’t you know they annex things?” Tietjens asked. He went on: “The total, in fact, amounted to an overdraft of fifteen pounds, but I did not think of it as such because my army agents ought to have paid my month’s army pay over to you on the first. As you perceive, they have only paid it over this morning, the 13th. But, as you will see from my passbook, they have always paid about the 13th, not the 1st. Two days ago I lunched at the club and drew that cheque for one pound fourteen shillings and sixpence: one ten for personal expenses and the four and six for lunch.﻿ ﻿…”
“You were, however, actually overdrawn,” the banker said sharply.
Tietjens said:
“Yesterday, for two hours.”
“But then,” Port Scatho said, “what do you want done? We’ll do what we can.”
Tietjens said:
“I don’t know. Do what you like. You’d better make what explanation you can to the military authority. If they court-martialled me it would hurt you more than me. I assure you of that. There *is* an explanation.”
Port Scatho began suddenly to tremble.
“What﻿ ﻿… what﻿ ﻿… what explanation?” he said. “You﻿ ﻿… damn it﻿ ﻿… you draw this out.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“我一辈子没在马身上押过一个便士，”蒂金斯说。“我对马懂得太多。”
斯卡索先看看西尔维娅的脸，再看看蒂金斯的。西尔维娅至少是他的老朋友。她说：
“克里斯托弗从不赌马，也从不投机。他个人的开销比城里随便哪个男人都小。可以说他根本没有个人开销。”
那阵疾掠而过的疑色又浮上斯卡索那张坦开的脸。
“哦，”西尔维娅说，“你总不至于疑心克里斯托弗和我串通了讹诈你。”
“不；这我不能疑心，”银行家说。“可另一个解释同样离奇。……疑心银行……银行……那你怎么解释？…”他对着蒂金斯说话；他的圆脑袋下部像要变成方的；激动咬着他的腮帮。
“我只简单告诉你事实，”蒂金斯说。“怎么办你看着办。十天前我接到开拔令。一交完接任军官的移交，我就把欠的全开了支票——军事裁缝、食堂——一张一镑十二先令。我还得买一个罗盘、一支左轮枪，我在医院里那阵，红十字会勤务员把我的拿走了。…”
斯卡索说：“天哪！”
“你不知道他们顺东西吗？”蒂金斯问。他接着说：“总数合下来透支十五镑，可我没往那上头想，因为我的陆军代理行本该在当月一号把军饷拨给你们。你也看到了，他们今天早上、十三号才拨过来。可你从我的存折上也看得出，他们从来都是十三号上下拨，不是一号。两天前我在俱乐部吃午饭，开了那张一镑十四先令六便士的支票：一镑十是个人开销，四先令六是午饭。…”
“可你事实上透支了，”银行家尖锐地说。
蒂金斯说：
“昨天，透了两小时。”
“那么，”斯卡索说，“你要我们怎么办？我们尽力。”
蒂金斯说：
“我不知道。随你们的便。你们最好对军方当局尽量解释。他们要真军事法庭办我，伤你们比伤我重。我向你担保。总归有个解释。”
斯卡索突然哆嗦起来。
“什么……什么……什么解释？”他说。“你……见鬼……你把这个抖出来。…”

===Original===

Do you dare to say my bank.﻿ ﻿…” He stopped, drew his hand down his face and said: “But yet﻿ ﻿… you’re a sensible, sound man.﻿ ﻿… I’ve heard things against you. But I don’t believe them.﻿ ﻿… Your father always spoke very highly of you.﻿ ﻿… I remember he said if you wanted money you could always draw on him through us for three or four hundred.﻿ ﻿… That’s what makes it so incomprehensible.﻿ ﻿… It’s﻿ ﻿… it’s﻿ ﻿…” His agitation grew on him. “It seems to strike at the very heart.﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“Look here, Port Scatho.﻿ ﻿… I’ve always had a respect for you. Settle it how you like. Fix the mess up for both our sakes with any formula that’s not humiliating for your bank. I’ve already resigned from the club.﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia said: “Oh, *no*, Christopher﻿ ﻿… not from the *club*!”
Port Scatho started back from beside the table.
“But if you’re in the right!” he said. “You *couldn’t*﻿ ﻿… Not resign from the club.﻿ ﻿… I’m on the committee﻿ ﻿… I’ll explain to them, in the fullest, in the most generous﻿ ﻿…”
“You couldn’t explain,” Tietjens said. “You can’t get ahead of rumour.﻿ ﻿… It’s half over London at this moment. You know what the toothless old fellows of your committee are.﻿ ﻿… Anderson! Ffolliott﻿ ﻿… And my brother’s friend, Ruggles.﻿ ﻿…”
Port Scatho said:
“Your brother’s friend Ruggles.﻿ ﻿… But look here.﻿ ﻿… He’s something about the Court, isn’t he? But look here.﻿ ﻿…” His mind stopped. He said: “People shouldn’t overdraw.﻿ ﻿… But if your father said you could draw on him I’m really much concerned.﻿ ﻿… You’re a first-rate fellow.﻿ ﻿… I can tell that from your passbook alone.﻿ ﻿… Nothing but cheques drawn to first-class tradesmen for reasonable amounts. The sort of passbook I liked to see when I was a junior clerk in the bank.﻿ ﻿…” At that early reminiscence feelings of pathos overcame him and his mind once more stopped.
Sylvia came back into the room; they had not perceived her going. She in turn held in her hand a letter.
Tietjens said:
“Look here, Port Scatho, don’t get into this state. Give me your word to do what you can when you’ve assured yourself the facts are as I say. I wouldn’t bother you at all, it’s not my line, except for Mrs. Tietjens. A man alone can live that sort of thing down, or die. But there’s no reason why Mrs. Tietjens should live, tied to a bad hat, while he’s living it down or dying.”

===Chinese===

你竟敢说是我的银行……”他顿住，一只手抹了一把脸，说：“可是……你是个明白事理的端正人。……我听人说过你的坏话。可我不信。……你父亲一向对你评价很高。……我记得他说过，你要用钱，随时可以经我们行支他三四百。……正因此这才叫人没法懂。……这……这……”他越发激动。“这简直捾在心口上。…”
蒂金斯说：
“听我说，斯卡索。……我一向敬重你。这事随你怎么了结。用一套不叫贵行丢脸的说法，把这一摊替你我两个人收拾干净。俱乐部我已经退了。…”
西尔维娅说：“哦，不，克里斯托弗……不能退俱乐部！”
斯卡索从桌边倒退一步。
“可你占着理呀！”他说。“你不能……不能退俱乐部。……我是委员……我会向他们解释，最充分、最大度地…”
“你解释不了，”蒂金斯说。“你跑不赢谣言。……这会儿已经传遍半个伦敦。你们委员会那帮没牙的老家伙是什么货色，你知道。……安德森！福利奥特……还有我哥哥的朋友拉格尔斯。…”
斯卡索说：
“你哥哥的朋友拉格斯。……可是听我说。……他在宫里当个什么差，不是吗？可是听我说……”他的思路停住了。他说：“人不该透支。……可你父亲既然说过你可以支他的钱，我实在很过意不去。……你是头等的人。……光看你这本存折就知道。……全是一等商家、数目合理的支票。我当年在行里当小办事员时，就爱看这样的存折。…”想到早年的光景，一阵感伤压倒了他，他的思路又停了。
西尔维娅回到屋里来；谁也没察觉她出去过。她手里也拿着一封信。
蒂金斯说：
“听我说，斯卡索，别这样失魂落魄。你先自己核实事实同我说的一样，然后给我一句话，尽力去办。我本不想麻烦你，这不是我的路数——若不是为了蒂金斯太太。一个男人独自一人，这口气熬得下去，要不就死了。可没有道理叫蒂金斯太太陪着一顶破帽子活，在他熬着或死着的当口。”
