他们沿河堤并肩缓步而行，因为同走一程的快意，背挺得直直的，肩端得方方的，为把这一程拉长，走得慢慢的。有一两回他们停下来，看河面上那层脏了的银子；因为两人都爱凄厉的风景。他们自觉力气很大，仿佛这片国土是他们的！
马克有一回轻声笑了：
“太他妈逗了。想想看，咱们俩居然都是……怎么说的来着？……一夫一妻主义者？嗐，守着一个女人总是件好事……这你没法说不是。省事。心里有底。”
在通向陆军部四方院的那道阴惨的门洞底下，克里斯托弗站住了。
“不。我也进去，”马克说。“我要同霍加斯说句话。我有些日子没同霍加斯说话了。说摄政公园里的运输车队停车场。那些破烂事还有一大堆，都归我管。”
“人家说你管得顶好，”克里斯托弗说。“人家说你少不了。”他察觉哥哥想尽量多陪他一刻。他自己也想。
“我当然少不了！”马克说。他补一句：“这路差事你在法国干得了吗？管运输，管马。”
“干得了，”克里斯托弗说，“不过我想我还会回去干联络。”
“我看你回不去了，”马克说。“我可以替你在运输那班人那里递句话。”
“巴不得你递，”克里斯托弗说。“我这条身子回不了前线了。再说我也不是什么该死的英雄！而且我是个獘脚的步兵军官。蒂金斯家从来没出过一个像样的军人。”
他们拐过门洞的角。像一件分毫不差、早有期待的景物恰好嵌进画框，瓦伦丁·沃诺普正立在那儿，望着挂在墙边的阵亡名单——头顶是一方便宜地染成绿色的杉木棚，既是向当今较弱的一路艺术流派致敬，也是体谅纳税人省钱的心意。

===Original===

With the same air of finding Christopher Tietjens fit in exactly to an expected landscape she turned on him. Her face was blue-white and distorted. She ran upon him and exclaimed:
“Look at this horror! And you in that foul uniform can support it!”
The sheets of paper beneath the green roof were laterally striped with little serrated lines: each line meant the death of a man, for the day.
Tietjens had fallen a step back off the curb of the pavement that ran round the quadrangle. He said:
“I support it because I have to. Just as you decry it because you have to. They’re two different patterns that we see.” He added: “This is my brother Mark.”
She turned her head stiffly upon Mark: her face was perfectly waxen. It was as if the head of a shopkeeper’s lay-figure had been turned. She said to Mark:
“I didn’t know Mr. Tietjens had a brother. Or hardly. I’ve never heard him speak of you.”
Mark grinned feebly, exhibiting to the lady the brilliant lining of his hat.
“I don’t suppose anyone has ever heard me speak of *him*,” he said, “but he’s my brother all right!”
She stepped on to the asphalt carriageway and caught between her fingers and thumb a fold of Christopher’s khaki sleeve.
“I must speak to you,” she said; “I’m going then.”
She drew Christopher into the center of the enclosed, hard and ungracious space, holding him still by the stuff of his tunic. She pushed him round until he was facing her. She swallowed hard, it was as if the motion of her throat took an immense time. Christopher looked round the skyline of the buildings of sordid and besmirched stone. He had often wondered what would happen if an air-bomb of some size dropped into the mean, grey stoniness of that cold heart of an embattled world.
The girl was devouring his face with her eyes: to see him flinch. Her voice was hard between her little teeth. She said:
“Were you the father of the child Ethel was going to have? Your wife says you were.”
Christopher considered the dimensions of the quadrangle. He said vaguely:
“Ethel? Who’s she?” In pursuance of the habits of the painter-poet Mr. and Mrs. Macmaster called each other always “Guggums!” Christopher had in all probability never heard Mrs. Duchemin’s Christian names. Certainly he had never heard them since his disaster had swept all names out of his head.

===Chinese===

她转过身来对着他，还是那副神情：仿佛克里斯托弗·蒂金斯分毫不差地嵌进一幅本就该有他的风景。她的脸白里透青，走了形。她冲上来喊：
“看看这惨状！你穿着那身恶心军装，居然还撑得住！”
绿棚底下的纸页横着一道道细锯齿线：每一道线意味着一个人当天死了。
蒂金斯从四方院人行道的路缘上退了一步。他说：
“我撑着，因为我只能撑。正像你骂它，因为你只能骂。那是咱们各自看见的两张图样。”他补一句：“这是我哥哥马克。”
她僵硬地把头转向马克：她的脸全然蜡一样。像柜台上那具木模特的头被人拧了过来。她对马克说：
“我不知道蒂金斯先生有个哥哥。或者说几乎不知道。我从没听他提起过你。”
马克淡淡一笑，向小姐亮了亮他帽子里那幅亮丽的衬里。
“我想也没谁听我说起过*他*，”他说，“可他千真万确是我弟弟！”
她跨上沥青路面，指头和拇指拈住克里斯托弗卡其袖子的一道褶。
“我必须同你说话，”她说；“说完我就走。”
她把克里斯托弗拉进这围起来的、坚硬而不客气的空间的正中央，还攥着他上衣的料子。她把他拨得转过来正对着她。她狠狠咽了一口，喉咙的动作仿佛用了无比长的时间。克里斯托弗环视那一圈灰污石头楼房的檐口。他常想：一颗够大的炸弹落进这座交战世界的冷酷心脏、这片卑琐的灰石里，会怎么样。
姑娘的眼睛正吞吃他的脸：要看他畏缩。她的小牙缝里挤出硬邦邦的声音。她说：
“埃塞尔怀的那个孩子，是不是你的？你太太说是。”
克里斯托弗衡量着四方院的长宽。他含糊地说：
“埃塞尔？她是谁？”沿袭那位画家诗人夫妇的习惯，麦克马斯特夫妇彼此永远只叫“咕咕姆”！克里斯托弗多半从没听说过杜什明太太的教名。自从那场大难把他脑子里的名字扫荡一空，他就肯定再没听过。

===Original===

He came to the conclusion that the quadrangle was not a space sufficiently confined to afford much bursting resistance to a bomb.
The girl said:
“Edith Ethel Duchemin! Mrs. Macmaster that is!” She was obviously waiting intensely. Christopher said with vagueness:
“No! Certainly not!﻿ ﻿… What was said?”
Mark Tietjens was leaning forward over the kerb in front of the green-stained shelter, like a child over a brookside. He was obviously waiting, quite patient, swinging his umbrella by the hook. He appeared to have no other means of self-expression. The girl was saying that when she had rung up Christopher that morning a voice had said, without any preparation at all: the girl repeated, without any preparation at all:
“You’d better keep off the grass if you’re the Wannop girl. Mrs. Duchemin is my husband’s mistress already. You keep off!”
Christopher said:
“She said that, did she?” He was wondering how Mark kept his balance, really. The girl said nothing more. She was waiting. With an insistence that seemed to draw him: a sort of sucking in of his personality. It was unbearable. He made his last effort of that afternoon.
He said:
“Damn it all. How could you ask such a tomfool question? *You!* I took you to be an intelligent person. The only intelligent person I know. Don’t you *know* me?”
She made an effort to retain her stiffening.
“Isn’t Mrs. Tietjens a truthful person?” she asked. “I thought she looked truthful when I saw her at Vincent and Ethel’s.”
He said:
“What she says she believes. But she only believes what she wants to, for the moment. If you call that truthful, she’s truthful. I’ve nothing against her.” He said to himself: “I’m not going to appeal to her by damning my wife.”
She seemed to go all of a piece, as the hard outline goes suddenly out of a piece of lump sugar upon which you drop water.
“Oh,” she said, “it *isn’t* true. I *knew* it wasn’t true.” She began to cry.
Christopher said:
“Come along. I’ve been answering tomfool questions all day. I’ve got another tomfool to see here, then I’m through.”
She said:
“I can’t come with you, crying like this.”
He answered:
“Oh, yes, you can. This is the place where women cry.” He added: “Besides there’s Mark. He’s a comforting ass.”
He delivered her over to Mark.
“Here, look after Miss Wannop,” he said. “You want to talk to her anyhow, don’t you?”

===Chinese===

他得出的结论是：这四方院不够逼仄，对一颗炸弹提供不了多少爆裂阻力。
姑娘说：
“伊迪丝·埃塞尔·杜什明！麦克马斯特太太！”她显然在拼命等。克里斯托弗含糊地说：
“不！当然不是！…她说了什么？”
马克·蒂金斯正从绿棚前俯身探过路缘，像个孩子俯在溪边。他显然在等，相当耐心，拿伞钩悠着伞。他似乎再没有别的自我表达方式。姑娘说：那天早上她给克里斯托弗打电话，一个声音毫无铺垫地说了——姑娘重复说，毫无任何铺垫——
“你要是那个沃诺普家的丫头，最好别蹚浑水。杜什明太太已经是我丈夫的情妇了。你识相点！”
克里斯托弗说：
“她这么说了，是吗？”他其实在纳闷马克究竟怎么保持住的平衡。姑娘不再说什么。她在等。带着一种仿佛把他往里吸的坚持：一种对他整个人格的吮吸。叫人承受不住。他拿出了那个下午最后的力气。
他说：
“见鬼。你怎么能问出这种蠢问题？*你*！我一向当你是个明白人。我认识的唯一明白人。你难道不*了解*我？”
她努力撑住那份僵硬。
“蒂金斯太太不是个诚实的人吗？”她问。“我在文森特和埃塞尔家见过她，那时我看她像诚实的。”
他说：
“她说的都是她信的。可她信的，只是她那一刻想信的。你要管这叫诚实，那她就诚实。我说不着她。”他自语：“我不打算靠骂我妻子来向她表白。”
她整个人似乎一下子塌了，像一块方糖叫你滴上水，硬棱顿时消融。
“哦，”她说，“*原来*不是真的。我*就知道*不是真的。”她哭起来。
克里斯托弗说：
“走吧。我答了一整天蠢问题。这儿还有一个蠢货要见，见完我就完事了。”
她说：
“哭成这样，我陪不了你。”
他答：
“哦，陪得了。这地方就是给女人哭的。”他补一句：“再说还有马克。他是头会安慰人的驴。”
他把她交给了马克。
“喏，照看一下沃诺普小姐，”他说。“反正你也想同她说话，对吧？”

===Original===

and he hurried ahead of them like a fussy shopwalker into the lugubrious hall. He felt that, if he didn’t come soon to an unemotional ass in red, green, blue or pink tabs, who would have fishlike eyes and would ask the sort of questions that fishes ask in tanks, he, too, must break down and cry. With relief! However, that was a place where men cried, too!
He got through at once by sheer weight of personality, down miles of corridors, into the presence of a quite intelligent, thin, dark person with scarlet tabs. That meant a superior staff affair: not dustbins.
The dark man said to him at once:
“Look here! What’s the matter with the Command Depots? You’ve been lecturing a lot of them. In economy. What are all these damn mutinies about? Is it the rotten old colonels in command?”
Tietjens said amiably:
“Look here! I’m not a beastly spy, you know? I’ve had hospitality from the rotten old colonels.”
The dark man said:
“I daresay you have. But that’s what you were sent round for. General Campion said you were the brainiest chap in his command. He’s gone out now, worse luck.﻿ ﻿… What’s the matter with the Command Depots? Is it the men? Or is it the officers? You needn’t mention names.”
Tietjens said:
“Kind of Campion. It isn’t the officers and it isn’t the men. It’s the foul system. You get men who think they’ve deserved well of their country﻿—and they damn well have!﻿—and you crop their heads.﻿ ﻿…”
“That’s the M.O.’s,” the dark man said. “They don’t want lice.”
“If they prefer mutinies﻿ ﻿…” Tietjens said. “A man wants to walk with his girl and have a properly oiled quiff. They don’t like being regarded as convicts. That’s how they are regarded.”
The dark man said:
“All right. Go on. Why don’t you sit down?”
“I’m a little in a hurry,” Tietjens said. “I’m going out tomorrow and I’ve got a brother and people waiting below.”
The dark man said:
“Oh, I’m sorry.﻿ ﻿… But damn. You’re the sort of man we want at home. Do you want to go? We can, no doubt, get you stopped if you don’t.”
Tietjens hesitated for a moment.
“Yes!” he said eventually. “Yes, I want to go.”
For the moment he had felt temptation to stay. But it came into his discouraged mind that Mark had said that Sylvia was in love with him. It had been underneath his thoughts all the while: it had struck him at the time like a kick from the hind leg of a mule in his subliminal consciousness.

===Chinese===

说罢，他抢在两人前头，像个殷勤的巡视员，匆匆进了那阴惨的大厅。他感到，若不能快点见上一位挂红、绿、蓝或粉领章、不动感情的蠢货——长一双鱼眼、问的都是鱼缸里的鱼才会问的那种问题——他自己也要垮下来哭了。痛快地哭！话说回来，那也是男人哭的地方！
他凭人格的分量一路畅通，穿过几英里长的走廊，来到一位相当精明的、瘦瘦的黑发人物面前，领章猩红。那意味着高级参谋事务：不是垃圾箱。
黑发人立刻对他说：
“听我说！各补给营到底怎么了？你去给好几个营讲过课。讲节约。这一连串该死的喐变怎么回事？是统兵的那些糟老头子上校不行？”
蒂金斯和气地说：
“听我说！我又不是什么该死的密探。糟老头子上校们可是招待过我。”
黑发人说：
“谅你被招待过。可派你转一圈，正是为这个。坎皮恩将军说你是他防区里脑子最灵的家伙。可惜他如今也出去了。……各补给营到底怎么了？是兵的问题，还是军官的问题？你不必点名。”
蒂金斯说：
“承坎皮恩夸奖。不是军官，也不是兵。是那套臭制度。你收着一帮自认对国家有功的兵——他们也确实他妈的有功！——你倒把人家头剃了。…”
“那是军医的事，”黑发人说。“他们不要虱子。”
“要是人家宁可唈变……”蒂金斯说。“一个兵想同姑娘遛遛弯，想把额发抹得油光水滑。人家不爱被当成囚犯看待。可他们受的正是那种看待。”
黑发人说：
“好。接着说。你怎么不坐？”
“我有点赶，”蒂金斯说。“我明天出国，底下还等着个哥哥和几位。”
黑发人说：
“哦，抱歉。……可是见鬼。你正是国内需要的那种人。你想去吗？你若不想，我们多半能把你拦下来。”
蒂金斯踌躇了一刻。
“想！”他终于说。“是，我想去。”
方才那一忽儿，他动过留下的心。可他灰败的心里蓦地想起马克说过：西尔维娅痴恋着他。这话一直垫在他思绪底下：当时就像骡子后蹄一脚踢在他的下意识里。

===Original===

It was the impossible complication. It might not be true; but whether or no the best thing for him was to go and get wiped out as soon as possible. He meant, nevertheless, fiercely, to have his night with the girl who was crying downstairs.﻿ ﻿…
He heard in his ear, perfectly distinctly, the lines:

> “The voice that never yet﻿ ﻿…

Made answer to my word﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

这是解不开的死结。兴许不是真的；可无论是真是假，他顶好赶紧走、尽快被打死。话虽如此，他还是狠狠打定主意，要同楼下哭着的那个姑娘过上一夜。……
他清清楚楚听见耳边那两行诗：

> “那嗓音，从未……

应答过我的话语……”

===Original===

He said to himself:
“That was what Sylvia wanted! I’ve got that much!”
The dark man had said something. Tietjens repeated:
“I’d take it very unkindly if you stopped my going﻿ ﻿… I want to go.”
The dark man said:
“Some do. Some do not. I’ll make a note of your name in case you come back﻿ ﻿… You won’t mind going on with your cinder-sifting if you do?﻿ ﻿… Get on with your story as quick as you can. And get what fun you can before you go. They say it’s rotten out there. Damn awful! There’s a hell of a strafe on. That’s why they want all you.”
For a moment Tietjens saw the grey dawn at railhead with the distant sound of a ceaselessly boiling pot, from miles away! The army feeling re-descended upon him. He began to talk about Command Depots, at great length and with enthusiasm. He snorted with rage at the way men were treated in these gloomy places. With ingenious stupidity!
Every now and then the dark man interrupted him with:
“Don’t forget that a Command Depot is a place where sick and wounded go to get made fit. We’ve got to get ’em back as soon as we can.”
“And do you?” Tietjens would ask.
“No, we don’t,” the other would answer. “That’s what this enquiry is about.”
“You’ve got,” Tietjens would continue, “on the north side of a beastly clay hill nine miles from Southampton three thousand men from the Highlands, North Wales, Cumberland.﻿ ﻿… God knows where, as long as it’s three hundred miles from home to make them rather mad with nostalgia.﻿ ﻿… You allow ’em out for an hour a day during the pub’s closing time: you shave their heads to prevent ’em appealing to local young women who don’t exist, and you don’t let ’em carry the swagger-canes! God knows why! To prevent their poking their eyes out, if they fall down, I suppose. Nine miles from anywhere, with chalk down roads to walk on and not a bush for shelter or shade﻿ ﻿… And, damn it, if you get two men, chums, from the Seaforths or the Argylls you don’t let them sleep in the same hut, but shove ’em in with a lot of fat Buffs or Welshmen, who stink of leeks and can’t speak English.﻿ ﻿…”
“That’s the infernal medicals’ orders to stop ’em talking all night.”
“To make ’em conspire all night not to turn out for parade,” Tietjens said. “And there’s a beastly mutiny begun.﻿ ﻿… And, damn it, they’re fine men. They’re first-class fellows.

===Chinese===

他自语：
“这就是西尔维娅要的！这一点我拿到了！”
黑发人方才说了句什么。蒂金斯重复道：
“你们要是拦下我，我可要见怪了……我要去。”
黑发人说：
“有人想，有人不想。我把你的名字记一笔，万一你回来……真回来了，再接着筛你的煤灰，你不介意吧？……把你的话快些讲完。走之前能捞什么乐子捞什么。据说外头糟透了。糟透了！大清洗正凶。所以才要你们全体去。”
有一刻，蒂金斯看见了铁路起点灰色的黎明，和几英里外传来的、像一只永远烧不开又永远在滚的水壶的声音！军旅的感觉重新罩下来。他开始讲各补给营，讲得又长又起劲。他讲到兵们在那些阴惨地方受的对待，气得喷鼻息。那样别出心裁的愚蠢！
黑发人不时打断他：
“别忘了，补给营是伤病员养好身体的地方。我们得尽快把他们送回去。”
“送回去了吗？”蒂金斯问。
“没有，”对方答。“这正是本调查要查的。”
“你们，”蒂金斯接着说，“在南安普顿九英里外一座该死的黏土山的北坡上，堆着三千个高地、北威尔士、坎伯兰来的兵。……天知道哪里凑的，只要离家三百英里，好叫他们想家想得发疯。……你们在酒馆关门钟点放他们出来一钟头：剃他们的头，免得他们去招惹本地根本不存在的姑娘；还不许他们拄那种神气的手杖！天知道为什么！我猜是怕他们摔倒时戳瞎眼睛。前后九英里不见人烟，白垩的山路，没一棵灌木遮荫挡风……还有，见鬼，好不容易来两个同乡的哥俩——西福斯家的或阿盖尔家的——你偏不叫他们睡一间棚屋，塞给一堆肥肥的巴夫团或威尔士人，一身韭萋味，一句英文不会。…”
“那是该死的军医的命令，怕他们整夜聊天。”
“倒叫他们整夜密谋不出操，”蒂金斯说。“一场该死的唈变已经开头了。……再说，见鬼，那是好兵。一流的汉子。

===Original===

Why don’t you﻿—as this is a Christian land﻿—let ’em go home to convalesce with their girls and pubs and friends and a little bit of swank, for heroes? Why in God’s name don’t you? Isn’t their suffering enough?”
“I wish you wouldn’t say ‘you,’ ” the dark man said. “It isn’t me. The only A.C.I. I’ve drafted was to give every Command Depot a cinema and a theatre. But the beastly medicals got it stopped﻿ ﻿… for fear of infection. And, of course, the parsons and Nonconformist magistrates﻿ ﻿…”
“Well, you’ll have to change it all,” Tietjens said, “or you’ll just have to say: thank God we’ve got a navy. You won’t have an army. The other day three fellows﻿—Warwicks﻿—asked me at question time, after a lecture, why they were shut up there in Wiltshire whilst Belgian refugees were getting bastards on their wives in Birmingham. And when I asked how many men made that complaint over fifty stood up. All from Birmingham.﻿ ﻿…”
The dark man said:
“I’ll make a note of that.﻿ ﻿… Go on.”
Tietjens went on; for as long as he stayed there he felt himself a man, doing work that befitted a man, with the bitter contempt for fools that a man should have and express. It was a letting up: a real last leave.

===Chinese===

这个国度既是基督教国度——你们为什么不放他们回家养伤，回到姑娘、酒馆、朋友身边，带一点好汉的神气？看在上帝份上，为什么不肯？他们受的苦还不够吗？”
“你别老说“你们”，”黑发人说。“不是我。我起草过的唯一一份全军通令，是给每个补给营配一家电影院和一座剧场。可叫那帮该死的军医搅黄了……怕传染。当然，还有牧师们和不奉国教的治安官们。…”
“那么，你们非得把它全改了，”蒂金斯说，“不然就只好说：感谢上帝咱们还有海军。陆军是没有的。前几天有三个家伙——沃里克团的——课后提问时问我，凭什么把他们关在威尔特郡，而比利时难民却在伯明翰搞大他们老婆的肚子。我问多少人有过这桩委屈，五十多个站了起来。全是伯明翰来的。…”
黑发人说：
“这条我记一笔。……接着讲。”
蒂金斯接着讲；因为只要还待在那儿，他就觉得自己是个人，干着配得上人的工作，带着一个男人该有、该说出口的对蠢货的刻骨轻蔑。那是一口松快：一次真正的末假。

### IV / 四
===Original===

Mark Tietjens, his umbrella swinging sheepishly, his bowler hat pushed firmly down on to his ears to give him a sense of stability, walked beside the weeping girl in the quadrangle.
“I say,” he said, “don’t give it to old Christopher too beastly hard about his militarist opinions.﻿ ﻿… Remember, he’s going out tomorrow and he’s one of the best.”
She looked at him quickly, tears remaining upon her cheeks, and then away.
“One of the best,” Mark said. “A fellow who never told a lie or did a dishonourable thing in his life. Let him down easy, there’s a good girl. You ought to, you know.”
The girl, her face turned away, said:
“I’d lay down my life for him!”
Mark said:
“I know you would. I know a good woman when I see one. And think! He probably considers that he *is*﻿ ﻿… offering his life, you know, for you. And me, too, of course!﻿ ﻿… It’s a different way of looking at things.” He gripped her awkwardly but irresistibly by the upper arm. It was very thin under her blue cloth coat. He said to himself:
“By Jove! Christopher likes them skinny. It’s the athletic sort that attracts him. This girl is as clean run as﻿ ﻿…” He couldn’t think of anything as clean run as Miss Wannop, but he felt a warm satisfaction at having achieved an intimacy with her and his brother. He said:
“You aren’t going away? Not without a kinder word to him. You think! He might be killed.﻿ ﻿… Besides. Probably he’s never killed a German. He was a liaison officer. Since then he’s been in charge of a dump where they sift army dustbins. To see how they can give the men less to eat. That means that the civilians get more. You don’t object to his giving civilians more meat?﻿ ﻿… It isn’t even helping to kill Germans.﻿ ﻿…”
He felt her arm press his hand against her warm side.
“What’s he going to do now?” she asked. Her voice wavered.
“That’s what I’m here about,” Mark said. “I’m going in to see old Hogarth. You don’t know Hogarth? Old General Hogarth? I think I can get him to give Christopher a job with the transport. A safe job. Safeish! No beastly glory business about it. No killing beastly Germans either.﻿ ﻿… I beg your pardon, if you like Germans.”
She drew her arm from his hand in order to look him in the face.
“Oh!” she said, “*you* don’t want him to have any beastly military glory!” The colour came back into her face: she looked at him open eyed.
He said:
“No! Why the devil should he?”

===Chinese===

马克·蒂金斯雨伞讪讪地晃着，圆顶礼帽死死压到耳朵上，好给自己一点安稳感，在四方院里挨着啼哭的姑娘走。
“我说，”他开口，“关于老克里斯托弗那套军国主义议论，你别太难为他。……记住，他明天就出发了，他是最好的那种人。”
她飞快看了他一眼，泪珠还挂在腮上，随即又别开。
“最好的那种，”马克说。“一辈子没说过一句谎、没干过一件不体面事的家伙。你手下留情，好姑娘。你该留情的，你知道。”
姑娘把脸偏着，说：
“我肯为他舍命！”
马克说：
“我知道你肯。好女人我一眼就认得出。再想想！多半在他自己看来，他也是……在拿命，你懂吗，来献给你。当然也献给我！……看事情的角度不同罢了。”他笨拙却不容抗拒地攥住她的上臂。蓝呢外套底下那条胳膊细得很。他自忖：
“天哪！克里斯就好这口瘦的。吸引他的是运动员那一型。这姑娘的身条利落得像……”他想不出什么能比得上万诺普小姐的身条，可同她、同他兄弟结下这份亲近，他心里暖洋洋的得意。他说：
“你不走吧？不给他句好话就走？想想！他兴许就没命了。……再说，他多半还没杀过德国人呢。他当过联络官。后来一直管一座堆场，篩军里的垃圾桶。琢磨怎么让士兵少吃点。那意味着平民多分点。他不叫平民多吃口肉，你没意见吧？……这甚至算不上帮着杀德国人。……”
他觉出她的胳膊把他的手压在温热的身侧。
“他现在要干什么去？”她问。声音发颤。
“我为这个来的，”马克说。“我这就去见老霍格思。你不认识霍格思吧？老霍格思将军？我想我能说动他，给克里斯派个运输的差事。安全的差事。差不多安全！没有混账荣耀的事。也不杀混账德国人。……您别见怪，要是您喜欢德国人的话。”
她把胳膊从他手里抽出来，好正眼看他。
“哦！”她说，“您也不想要他捞什么混账军功！”血色回到她脸上；她睁大眼睛望着他。
他说：
“不！他干吗要那个？”

===Original===

He said to himself: “She’s got enormous eyes: a good neck: good shoulders: good breasts: clean hips: small hands. She isn’t knock-kneed: neat ankles. She stands well on her feet. Feet not too large! Five foot four, say! A real good filly!” He went on aloud: “Why in the world should he want to be a beastly soldier? He’s the heir to Groby. That ought to be enough for one man.”
Having stood still sufficiently long for what she knew to be his critical inspection, she put her hand in turn, precipitately, under his arm and moved him towards the entrance steps.
“Let’s be quick then,” she said. “Let’s get him into your transport at once. Before he goes tomorrow. Then we’ll know he’s safe.”
He was puzzled by her dress. It was very businesslike, dark blue and very short. A white blouse with a black silk, man’s tie. A wideawake, with, on the front of the band, a cipher.
“You’re in uniform yourself,” he said. “Does your conscience let you do war work?”
She said:
“No. We’re hard up. I’m taking the gym classes in a great big school to turn an honest penny.﻿ ﻿… *Do* be quick!”
Her pressure on his elbow flattered him. He resisted it a little, hanging back, to make her more insistent. He liked being pleaded with by a pretty woman: Christopher’s girl at that.
He said:
“Oh, it’s not a matter of minutes. They keep ’em weeks at the base before they send ’em up.﻿ ﻿… We’ll fix him up all right, I’ve no doubt. We’ll wait in the hall till he comes down.”
He told the benevolent commissionaire, one of two in a pulpit in the crowded grim hall, that he was going up to see General Hogarth in a minute or two. But not to send a bellboy. He might be some time yet.
He sat himself beside Miss Wannop, clumsily on a wooden bench, humanity surging over their toes as if they had been on a beach. She moved a little to make room for him and that, too, made him feel good. He said:
“You said just now: ‘we’ are hard up. Does ‘we’ mean you and Christopher?”
She said:
“I and Mr. Tietjens. Oh, no! I and mother! The paper she used to write for stopped. When your father died, I believe. He found money for it, I think. And mother isn’t suited to freelancing. She’s worked too hard in her life.”
He looked at her, his round eyes protruding.
“I don’t know what that is, freelancing,” he said. “But you’ve got to be comfortable. How much do you and your mother need to keep you comfortable?

===Chinese===

他自忖：“她眼睛真大：脖子好：肩膀好：胸好：腰身干净：手小。不罗圈腿：脚踝利落。站得稳。脚不算大！五英尺四，大概！一匹真正的好母马！”他朗声接着说：“他干吗非要去当什么混账兵？他是格罗比的继承人。一个人占这一样也该够本了。”
她任他上上下下打量够了——知道他是在相看——便反手一把插进他臂弯，把他朝大门台阶推去。
“那就快点，”她说。“趁他明天还没走，立刻把他弄进您的运输队。这样我们就放心了。”
他看不透她的衣着。干练得很，深蓝，极短。白衬衫配一条黑绸的男人领带。一顶软呢帽，帽带正面绣着花押。
“你自己倒穿了制服，”他说，“你的良心过得去做战时工作？”
她说：
“过不去。我们穷。我在一所顶大的学校里教体育课，挣几个干净铜子。……您倒是快点呀！”
她压着他臂肘的那点劲头让他受用。他微微坠着身子不肯快走，好叫她催得更紧。漂亮女人求他——还是克里斯的姑娘——他喜欢。
他说：
“哦，不差这一时半刻。他们先在基地一搁就是好几个礼拜。……咱们把他安排妥，我有数。咱们就在门厅里等他下来。”
他告诉那好心的门警——拥挤阴沉的门厅里两座讲坛式门位之一——说他这就上楼见霍格思将军。先别叫听差引路。兴许还得等一会儿。
他在万诺普小姐身边，笨手笨脚在木凳上坐下；人流从他们脚边涌过，仿佛他们在海滩上。她挪了挪给他腾地方，这也叫他受用。他说：
“你方才说‘我们’穷。‘我们’是指你和克里斯托弗？”
她说：
“我和蒂金斯先生。哦，不！我和母亲！她原先写稿的那家报停了。多半就在您父亲去世那阵。那报纸的钱我想是您父亲出的。母亲又干不来投稿卖文那一路。她这辈子苦干得够了。”
他望着她，圆眼睛凸出来。
“投稿卖文是个什么行当，我不知道，”他说。“可你们必须过得舒服。你和母亲要多少钱才够舒服？

===Original===

And put in a bit more so that Christopher could have a mutton-chop now and then!”
She hadn’t really been listening. He said with some insistence: “Look here! I’m here on business. Not like an elderly admirer forcing himself on you. Though, by God, I do admire you too.﻿ ﻿… But my father wanted your mother to be comfortable.﻿ ﻿…”
Her face, turned to him, became rigid.
“You don’t mean﻿ ﻿…” she began. He said:
“You won’t get it any quicker by interrupting. I have to tell my stories in my own way. My father wanted your mother to be comfortable. He said so that she could write books, not papers. I don’t know what the difference is: that’s what he said. He wants you to be comfortable too.﻿ ﻿… You’ve not got any encumbrances? Not﻿ ﻿… oh, say a business: a hat shop that doesn’t pay? Some girls have.﻿ ﻿…”
She said: “No. I just teach﻿ ﻿… oh, *do* be quick.﻿ ﻿…”
For the first time in his life he dislocated the course of his thoughts to satisfy a longing in someone else.
“You may take it to go on with,” he said, “as if my father had left your mother a nice little plum.” He cast about to find his scattered thoughts.
“He has! He *has*! After all!” the girl said. “Oh, thank God!”
“There’ll be a bit for you, if you like,” Mark said, “or perhaps Christopher won’t let you. He’s ratty with me. And something for your brother to buy a doctor’s business with.” He asked: “You haven’t fainted, have you?” She said:
“No. I don’t faint. I cry.”
“That’ll be all right,” he answered. He went on: “That’s your side of it. Now for mine. I want Christopher to have a place where he’ll be sure of a mutton-chop and an armchair by the fire. And someone to be good for him. *You’re* good for him. I can see that. I know women!”
The girl was crying, softly and continuously. It was the first moment of the lifting of strain that she had known since the day before the Germans crossed the Belgian frontier, near a place called Gemmenich.
It had begun with the return of Mrs. Duchemin from Scotland. She had sent at once for Miss Wannop to the rectory, late at night. By the light of candles in tall silver stocks, against oak panelling she had seemed like a mad block of marble, with staring, dark eyes and mad hair. She had exclaimed in a voice as hard as a machine’s:
“How do you get rid of a baby? You’ve been a servant. You ought to know!”

===Chinese===

再添一点，好让克里斯托弗时不时吃上一块羊排！”
她其实没大听。他执拗地说：“听着！我是来办公事的。不是老色鬼硬缠小姑娘。虽然，老天作证，我真是也仰慕你。……可我父亲的意思，是要你母亲过得舒服。……”
她转向他的脸僵住了。
“您该不是说……”她开口。他说：
“你打断，我也快不了。我的话得照我的次序讲。我父亲要你母亲过得舒服。他说这样她就能写书，不写报。差别在哪儿我不知道：他是这么说的。他也要你过得舒服。……你没拖累吧？没有……哦，比如说一桩生意：一家不挣钱的帽子铺？有些姑娘有。……”
她说：“没有。我就是教书……哦，您快点。……”
生平头一遭，他把自己思路的次序拆散了，去迁就别人的渴望。
“你可以先这么记下，”他说，“就当我父亲给你母亲留下了一小笔甜头。”他四下里找他打散的思绪。
“他留下了！他留下了！到底是！”姑娘说。“哦，谢天谢地！”
“你愿意的话，也给你留了一份，”马克说，“要不，克里斯托弗兴许不让你拿。他正跟我闹别扭呢。还有给你弟弟的一份，够他买一间医生的诊所。”他问：“你没晕过去吧？”她说：
“没有。我不晕。我哭。”
“那就不要紧，”他答。他接着说：“那是你那一头。再说我的。我要克里斯托弗有个地方，稳稳吃得上羊排、火边有扶手椅。还得有个人对他有好处。你对他有好处。这我看得出来。女人我懂！”
姑娘哭着，轻轻地、不断线地哭。自德国人在一个叫盖梅尼希的地方附近越过比利时边境的前一天起，她还头一回尝到绷紧的弦松了一松。
那是从杜什明太太从苏格兰回来起的头。她连夜打发人请万诺普小姐去牧师宅。高高的银烛台上烛光照着，衬着橡木壁板，她像一块发了疯的大理石，乌黑的眼睛发直，头发散乱。她用机器一样硬的声音喊：
“怎么打掉孩子？你当过佣人。你该知道！”

===Original===

That had been the great shock, the turning-point, of Valentine Wannop’s life. Her last years before that had been a great tranquillity, tinged of course with melancholy because she loved Christopher Tietjens. But she had early learned to do without, and the world as she saw it was a place of renunciations, of high endeavour and sacrifice. Tietjens had to be a man who came to see her mother and talked wonderfully. She had been happy when he had been in the house﻿—she in the housemaid’s pantry, getting the tea-things. She had, besides, been very hard worked for her mother; the weather had been, on the whole, good, the corner of the country in which they lived had continued to seem fresh and agreeable. She had had excellent health, got an occasional ride on the *qui-tamer* with which Tietjens had replaced Joel’s rig; and her brother had done admirably at Eton, taking such a number of exhibitions and things that, once at Magdalen, he had been nearly off his mother’s hands. An admirable, gay boy, not unlikely to run for, as well as being a credit to, his university, if he didn’t get sent down for his political extravagances. He was a Communist!
And at the rectory there had been the Duchemins, or rather Mrs. Duchemin and, during most weekends, Macmaster somewhere about.
The passion of Macmaster for Edith Ethel and of Edith Ethel for Macmaster had seemed to her one of the beautiful things of life. They seemed to swim in a sea of renunciations, of beautiful quotations, and of steadfast waiting. Macmaster did not interest her personally much, but she took him on trust because of Edith Ethel’s romantic passion and because he was Christopher Tietjens’ friend. She had never heard him say anything original; when he used quotations they would be apt rather than striking. But she took it for granted that he was the right man﻿—much as you take it for granted that the engine of an express train in which you are is reliable. The right people have chosen it for you.﻿ ﻿…
With Mrs. Duchemin, mad before her, she had the first intimation that her idolised friend, in whom she had believed as she had believed in the firmness of the great, sunny earth, had been the mistress of her lover﻿—almost since the first day she had seen him.﻿ ﻿… And that Mrs. Duchemin had, stored somewhere, a character of an extreme harshness and great vulgarity of language.

===Chinese===

那是瓦伦丁·万诺普生平的大震动、大转折。之前那几年是一大段平静，当然染着忧郁，因为她爱着克里斯托弗·蒂金斯。可她早早学会了“不要”这门功课；她眼里的世界，是一个舍弃、奋进与牺牲的世界。蒂金斯只能是来看看她母亲、谈吐惊人的一个男人。他在屋里她就快活——她在女佣配餐室里收拾茶具。此外她为母亲苦干；天公大致作美；他们住的那个乡角始终清新宜人。她身体极好；偶尔还能骑一骑那匹基塔默（qui-tamer）——蒂金斯用它换了乔尔那套车马；弟弟在伊顿出息得漂亮，奖学金之类拿了一大把，进了莫德林以后几乎不用母亲操心。一个招人疼的、快活的男孩，若不因政治上出圈被开除，多半还要替他的大学去跑一跑——也给大学挣脸。他是个共产主义者！
牧师宅那边有杜什明夫妇——或者说杜什明太太，外加大多数周末在附近盘桓的麦克马斯特。
麦克马斯特对伊迪丝·艾塞尔的热恋、伊迪丝·艾塞尔对麦克马斯特的热恋，在她看是人生美事之一。他们仿佛游在一片舍弃之海里，满是美丽的引文和坚忍的等待。麦克马斯特本人引不起她多大兴味，可她凭伊迪丝·艾塞尔那浪漫的热恋、凭他是克里斯托弗·蒂金斯的朋友，就把他信下来了。她从没听他说过什么独到的见地；他用引文，熨帖有余，警策不足。可她想当然认为他是靠得住的人——就像你坐上一列快车，想当然认为那台机车靠得住。靠得住的人家已经替你挑好了。……”
在杜什明太太、在她之前发疯的杜什明太太嘴里，她头一回得知：她那位奉若神明的朋友——她信她，如同信洒满阳光的大地坚实——原来几乎从见他第一面起，就是她爱人的情妇。……而且这位杜什明太太骨子里存着一份极端刻毒、语言极其下流的性子。

===Original===

She raged up and down in the candlelight, before the dark oak panelling, screaming coarse phrases of the deepest hatred for her lover. Didn’t the oaf know his business better than to﻿ ﻿… ? The dirty little Port of Leith fish-handler.﻿ ﻿…
What, then, were tall candles in silver sticks for? And polished panelling in galleries?
Valentine Wannop couldn’t have been a little ashcat in worn cotton dresses, sleeping under the stairs, in an Ealing household with a drunken cook, an invalid mistress and three overfed men, without acquiring a considerable knowledge of the sexual necessities and excesses of humanity. But, as all the poorer helots of great cities hearten their lives by dreaming of material beauties, elegance, and suave wealth, she had always considered that, far from the world of Ealing and its county councillors who over-ate and neighed like stallions, there were bright colonies of beings, chaste, beautiful in thought, altruist and circumspect.
And, till that moment, she had imagined herself on the skirts of such a colony. She presupposed a society of beautiful intellects centring in London round her friends. Ealing she just put out of her mind. She considered: she had, indeed once heard Tietjens say that humanity was made up of exact and constructive intellects on the one hand and on the other of stuff to fill graveyards.﻿ ﻿… Now, what had become of the exact and constructive intellects?
Worst of all, what became of her beautiful inclination towards Tietjens, for she couldn’t regard it as anything more? Couldn’t her heart sing any more whilst she was in the housemaid’s pantry and he in her mother’s study? And what became, still more, of what she knew to be Tietjens’ beautiful inclination towards her? She asked herself the eternal question﻿—and she knew it to be the eternal question﻿—whether no man and woman can ever leave it at the beautiful inclination. And, looking at Mrs. Duchemin, rushing backwards and forwards in the light of candles, blue-white of face and her hair flying, Valentine Wannop said: “No! no! The tiger lying in the reeds will always raise its head!” But tiger﻿ ﻿… it was more like a peacock.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

她在烛光里、在黑橡木壁板前来回暴走，尖声咒骂她的情人，用的全是最深仇大恨的下流话。那蠢货连自己的本行都干不利索，还想……？利斯港来的下等贩鱼仔。……
那么，银座高烛是为什么？画廊里上光的壁板是为什么？
瓦伦丁·万诺普既然当过穿旧棉布裙的小灰丫头、睡过楼梯底下、伺候过伊灵一家子——醉厨娘、病太太、三个吃撑的男人——对人类性方面的必需与过度，就有了相当的知识。可是，正如大都市里一切贫苦的奴隶靠梦想物质之美、风雅与温柔富足来给自己打气，她一向认定：在伊灵那个吃撑了就公马似的嘶叫的市议员们的世界之外很远，另有一片片明亮的移民地，住着贞洁、思想美善、利他而有分寸的生灵。
直到那一刻，她一直想象自己就贴着这样一片移民地的边缘。她预设了一个以伦敦为中心、以她朋友为中枢的美好智识社会。伊灵，她索性逐出脑海。她记得——确实听蒂金斯说过——人类一半是精确而建设的头脑，另一半是填坟场的材料。……如今，那些精确而建设的头脑都到哪儿去了？
最糟的是，她对蒂金斯的那份美好倾心——她不许它超出倾心——又落在了哪里？她在女佣配餐室、他在她母亲书房的时候，她的心还能唱歌吗？而她明知蒂金斯对她也有那份美好倾心，那又落在哪里？她问自己那个永恒的问题——她知道那是永恒的问题——是不是任何一对男女，都永远不能止步于美好的倾心？望着烛光里来回奔突、脸青白、头发飞散的杜什明太太，瓦伦丁·万诺普说：“不！不！卧在芦苇里的老虎总归要抬头！”可老虎……这更像一只孔雀。……”
从茶桌那头抬起头、在她母亲身边用他那悠长的、沉思的目光望着她的蒂金斯：他的眼睛难道不该、代替那种蓝色和凸出，是在黑睛里纵向裂开的那种——在黄底上开合、张缩，闪着绿幽幽鬼火的光？

Tietjens, raising his head from the other side of the tea-table and looking at her with his long, meditative glance from beside her mother: ought he then, instead of blue and protruding, to have eyes divided longitudinally in the blacks of them﻿—that should divide, closing or dilating, on a yellow ground, with green glowings of furtive light?
She was aware that Edith Ethel had done her an irreparable wrong, for you cannot suffer a great sexual shock and ever be the same. Or not for years. Nevertheless she stayed with Mrs. Duchemin until far into the small hours, when she fell, a mere parcel of bones in a peacock blue wrapper, into a deep chair and refused to move or speak; nor did she afterwards slacken in her faithful waiting on her friend.﻿ ﻿…
On the next day came the war. That was a nightmare of pure suffering, with never a letup, day or night. It began on the morning of the fourth with the arrival of her brother from some sort of Oxford Communist Summer School on the Broads. He was wearing a German corps student’s cap and was very drunk. He had been seeing German friends off from Harwich. It was the first time she had ever seen a drunken man, so that was a good present to her.
Next day, and sober, he was almost worse. A handsome, dark boy like his father, he had his mother’s hooked nose and was always a little unbalanced: not mad, but always over-violent in any views he happened for the moment to hold. At the Summer School he had been under very vitriolic teachers of all sorts of notions. That hadn’t hitherto mattered. Her mother had written for a Tory paper: her brother, when he had been at home, had edited some sort of Oxford organ of disruption. But her mother had only chuckled.
The war changed that. Both seemed to be filled with a desire for blood and to torture: neither paid the least attention to the other.

第二天，战争来了。那是一场纯粹受罪的噩梦，日夜没有一口气松。四号早晨开的头：她弟弟从布罗兹湖区什么牛津共产主义暑期学校回来，戴一顶德国学生团帽子，烂醉。他从哈里奇送德国朋友上船回来。她生平头一回见醉汉——这也算一份“见面礼”。
第二天，酒醒了，他更糟。像他父亲一样俊俏黝黑的少年，长着母亲的鹰钩鼻，性子总是偏一分：不算疯，可对他一时信奉的任何主张，回回用力过猛。暑期学校里教过他各色主张的、说话浸着毒的老师。这在从前不碍事。母亲给托利党的报纸写稿；弟弟在家时编过牛津某种专事捣乱的刊物。母亲不过一笑。
战争把这一切改了。母子俩仿佛都灌满了嗜血与酷刑的欲望；彼此谁也不理谁。

It was as if﻿—so for the rest of those years the remembrance of that time lived with her﻿—in one corner of the room her mother, ageing, and on her knees, from which she only with difficulty rose, shouted hoarse prayers to God, to let her, with her own hands, strangle, torture, and flay off all his skin, a being called the Kaiser, and as if, in the other corner of the room, her brother, erect, dark, scowling and vitriolic, one hand clenched above his head, called down the curse of heaven on the British soldier, so that in thousands, he might die in agony, the blood spouting from his scalded lungs. It appeared that the Communist leader whom Edward Wannop affected had had ill-success in his attempts to cause disaffection among some units or other of the British army, and had failed rather gallingly, being laughed at or ignored rather than being ducked in a horsepond, shot or otherwise martyrised. That made it obvious that the British man in the ranks was responsible for the war. If those ignoble hirelings had refused to fight all the other embattled and terrorised millions would have thrown down their arms!
Across that dreadful phantasmagoria went the figure of Tietjens. He was in doubt. She heard him several times voice his doubts to her mother, who grew every day more vacant. One day Mrs. Wannop had said:
“What does your wife think about it?”
Tietjens had answered:
“Oh, Mrs. Tietjens is a pro-German.﻿ ﻿… Or no, that isn’t exact. She has German prisoner-friends and looks after them. But she spends nearly all her time in retreat in a convent reading novels of before the war. She can’t bear the thought of physical suffering. I can’t blame her.”
Mrs. Wannop was no longer listening: her daughter was.
For Valentine Wannop the war had turned Tietjens into far more of a man and far less of an inclination﻿—the war and Mrs. Duchemin between them. He had seemed to grow less infallible. A man with doubts is more of a man, with eyes, hands, the need for food and for buttons to be sewn on. She had actually tightened up a loose glove button for him.
One Friday afternoon at Macmaster’s she had had a long talk with him: the first she had had since the drive and the accident.
Ever since Macmaster had instituted his Friday afternoons﻿—and that had been some time before the war﻿—Valentine Wannop had accompanied Mrs.

那光景就仿佛——此后那些年里，那段日子在她记忆里始终是这样——屋子一角，衰老的、跪着难起身的母亲，朝上帝嘶声祷告，求上帝让她亲手掐死、上刑、活剥一个叫德皇的生灵的皮；屋子另一角，弟弟笔直、黝黑、横眉、浸着毒，一只拳头举过头顶，呼求上天的诅咒降到英国兵头上，让他们成千上万痛苦地死，燙伤的肺里喷血。原来爱德华·万诺普追随的那位共产领袖，在某几支部队里策动哗变，成绩不佳，碰了一鼻子灰——人家笑他、当他不存在，倒也没把他按进马塘、枪决或另加迫害。这一来，英国的大兵就成了战争的罪魁。倘若那些无耻的雇佣兵拒不开枪，其余几百万披甲的、吓怕的，早扔枪了！
在这幅可怕的幻灯上，穿来穿去的是蒂金斯的身影。他犹豫。她好几回听见他对她母亲说出他的疑虑；她母亲一天比一天恍惚。有一天万诺普太太问：
“你太太怎么看？”
蒂金斯答：
“哦，蒂金斯太太亲德。……不，也不确切。她有德国战俘朋友，照料他们。可她几乎所有时间都在修院避静，读战前的小说。她受不了肉体的苦痛这念头。我不能怪她。”
万诺普太太已不再听：她女儿在听。
对瓦伦丁·万诺普来说，战争——加上杜什明太太——把蒂金斯变得更像一个男人，更少像一份倾心。他显得不再那样全知无误。一个有疑虑的男人更像男人：有眼睛，有手，需要吃饭，需要钉上掉的扣子。她真的替他系紧过一粒松了的手套扣。
一个礼拜五的下午，在麦克马斯特家，她同他有过一次长谈：自那次乘车出游和那场事故以来的第一次。
自打麦克马斯特办起礼拜五下午茶会——那是战前一阵了——瓦伦丁·万诺普就一直陪杜什明太

Duchemin to town by the morning train and back at night to the rectory. Valentine poured out the tea, Mrs. Duchemin drifting about the large book-lined room amongst the geniuses and superior journalists.
On this occasion﻿—a November day of very chilly, wet﻿—there had been next to nobody present, the preceding Friday having been unusually full. Macmaster and Mrs. Duchemin had taken a Mr. Spong, an architect, into the dining-room to inspect an unusually fine set of Piranesi’s *Views of Rome* that Tietjens had picked up somewhere and had given to Macmaster. A Mr. Jegg and a Mrs. Haviland were sitting close together in the far window-seat. They were talking in low tones. From time to time Mr. Jegg used the word “inhibition.” Tietjens rose from the fire-seat on which he had been sitting and came to her. He ordered her to bring her cup of tea over by the fire and talk to him. She obeyed. They sat side by side on the leather fire-seat that stood on polished brass rails, the fire warming their backs. He said:
“Well, Miss Wannop. What have you been doing?” and they drifted into talking of the war. You couldn’t not. She was astonished not to find him so loathesome as she had expected, for, just at that time, with the facts that were always being driven into her mind by the pacifist friends of her brother and with continual brooding over the morals of Mrs. Duchemin, she had an automatic feeling that all manly men were lust-filled devils, desiring nothing better than to stride over battlefields, stabbing the wounded with long daggers in frenzies of sadism. She knew that this view of Tietjens was wrong, but she cherished it.
She found him﻿—as subconsciously she knew he was﻿—astonishingly mild. She had too often watched him whilst he listened to her mother’s tirades against the Kaiser, not to know that. He did not raise his voice, he showed no emotion. He said at last:
“You and I are like two people﻿ ﻿…” He paused and began again more quickly: “Do you know these soap advertisement signs that read differently from several angles? As you come up to them you read ‘Monkey’s Soap’; if you look back when you’ve passed it’s ‘Needs no Rinsing.’﻿ ﻿… You and I are standing at different angles and though we both look at the same thing we read different messages. Perhaps if we stood side by side we should see yet a third.﻿ ﻿… But I hope we respect each other. We’re both honest.

太乘早车进城，夜里再回牧师宅。瓦伦丁斟茶，杜什明太太在那间四壁图书的大屋里，在才子们和高级记者中间飘来飘去。
这一回——十一月一个阴冷潮湿的日子——人来得寥寥，因为前一个礼拜五人满为患。麦克马斯特和杜什明太太把一位斯庞先生——建筑师——让进饭厅，去看一套格外精的皮拉内西《罗马景观》，蒂金斯不知从哪儿淘来送给麦克马斯特的。杰格先生和哈维兰太太在远处窗座上坐得很近，低声交谈。杰格先生时不时用一用“抑制”这个词。蒂金斯从火凳上站起来，走到她跟前。他吩咐她端着茶到火边来同他说话。她照办了。两人并排坐在那张架在抛光铜轨上的皮面火凳上，火烘着背。他说：
“怎么样，万诺普小姐。近来做了些什么？”于是话头滑进了战争。想不谈也办不到。她惊讶地发现，他并不如她预想的那样可惜；因为恰恰在那阵子，弟弟那些和平主义朋友往她脑子里灌的事实，加上对杜什明太太道德的反复咀碍，使她生出一种机械的感受：一切有男子气的男人全是欲火中烧的魔鬼，最想干的就是跨过战场，用长匕首在施虐的狂热里捅死伤员。她知道这么看蒂金斯是错的，可她偏珍爱这个看法。
她发现他——潜意识里她也早知道——温和得出奇。她太多次看着他听她母亲声讨德皇，不会不知道这一点。他不抬嗓门，也不动声色。末了他说：
“你和我就像两个人……”他顿了顿，更快地重新起头：“你见过那种肥皂广告牌吧，从几个角度看字样不同？走近了看是‘猴牌肥皂’；走过了回头看，是‘无须漂洗’。……你我站的角度不同，看着同一件东西，读出的字样不同。也许并肩站，还会读出第三种。……可我希望我们彼此敬重。咱们两个都是老实人。”

I, at least, tremendously respect you and I hope you respect me.”
She kept silent. Behind their backs the fire rustled. Mr. Jegg, across the room, said: “The failure to coordinate﻿ ﻿…” and then dropped his voice.
Tietjens looked at her attentively.
“You don’t respect me?” he asked. She kept obstinately silent.
“I’d have liked you to have said it,” he repeated.
“Oh,” she cried out, “how can I respect you when there is all this suffering? So much pain! Such torture﻿ ﻿… I can’t sleep﻿ ﻿… Never﻿ ﻿… I haven’t slept a whole night since﻿ ﻿… Think of the immense spaces, stretching out under the night﻿ ﻿… I believe pain and fear must be worse at night.﻿ ﻿…” She knew she was crying out like that because her dread had come true. When he had said: “I’d have liked you to have said it,” using the past, he had said his valedictory. Her man, too, was going.
And she knew too: she had always known under her mind and now she confessed it: her agony had been, half of it, because one day he would say farewell to her: like that, with the inflection of a verb. As, just occasionally, using the word “we”﻿—and perhaps without intention﻿—he had let her know that he loved her.
Mr. Jegg drifted across from the window: Mrs. Haviland was already at the door.
“We’ll leave you to have your war talk out,” Mr. Jegg said. He added: “For myself, I believe it’s one’s sole duty to preserve the beauty of things that’s preservable. I can’t help saying that.”
She was alone with Tietjens and the quiet day. She said to herself:
“Now he must take me in his arms. He must. He *must*!” The deepest of her instincts came to the surface, from beneath layers of thought hardly known to her. She could feel his arms around her: she had in her nostrils the peculiar scent of his hair﻿—like the scent of the skin of an apple, but very faint. “You must! You *must*!” she said to herself. There came back to her overpoweringly the memory of their drive together and the moment, the overwhelming moment, when, climbing out of the white fog into the blinding air, she had felt the impulse of his whole body towards her and the impulse of her whole body towards him. A sudden lapse: like the momentary dream when you fall.﻿ ﻿… She saw the white disk of the sun over the silver mist and behind them was the long, warm night.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“至少，我极其敬重你；我希望你也敬重我。”
她不作声。背后炉火噼啪。屋子那头杰格先生说：“协调之不逮……”随后放低了声音。
蒂金斯凝神看她。
“你不敬重我？”他问。她执拗地沉默着。
“我本想听你亲口说，”他又说了一遍。
“哦，”她喊出声，“有这么多苦难，叫我怎么敬重你？这么多的痛！这样的酷刑……我睡不着……从来……打那以后我没睡过一夜整觉……想想那漫无边际的地方，在黑夜里铺开……我相信疼痛和恐惧到了夜里更厉害。……”她知道自己这样喊，是因为她的惧怕应验了。他说“我本想听你亲口说”——用的是过去时——他说的是诀别的话。她的男人，也要走了。
她也知道：这她心里一直有数，此刻她招认了：她的苦，一半就苦在，终有一天他要这样向她道别：就这样，用一个动词的时态。正如他偶尔用一用“我们”这个词——也许出于无意——让她知道他爱她。
杰格先生从窗边飘过来：哈维兰太太已经到了门边。
“你们的战争话，你们谈完它吧，”杰格先生说。又补一句：“至于我，我相信人唯一的本分，是保住可保的东西之美。这话我忍不住要说。”
她单独同蒂金斯、同这个安静的日子留在了一起。她自语：
“现在他必须搂我。他必须。他必须！”她最深的本能浮上水面，从她自己几乎不知道的思想层底下。她能觉出他的双臂环着她；鼻端是他头发的那么一点特异的气味——像苹果皮的香，却淡得很。“你必须！你必须！”她自语。压倒一切地涌回来的，是那次同车出游的记忆，是那要命的一刻：从白雾里爬进晃眼的亮处，她感到他整个身体向她倾来，她整个身体向他倾去。一次骤然的失守：像你坠落时那一瞬的梦。……她看见银雾之上太阳的白盘，而他们身后是漫长的、温热的夜。……

===Original===

Tietjens sat, huddled rather together, dejectedly, the firelight playing on the silver places of his hair. It had grown nearly dark outside: they had a sense of the large room that, almost week by week, had grown, for its gleams of gilding and hand-polished dark woods, more like the great dining-room at the Duchemins. He got down from the fire-seat with a weary movement, as if the fire-seat had been very high. He said, with a little bitterness, but as if with more fatigue:
“Well, I’ve got the business of telling Macmaster that I’m leaving the office. That, too, won’t be an agreeable affair! Not that what poor Vinnie thinks matters.” He added: “It’s queer, dear﻿ ﻿…” In the tumult of her emotions she was almost certain that he had said “dear.”﻿ ﻿… “Not three hours ago my wife used to me almost the exact words you have just used. Almost the exact words. She talked of her inability to sleep at night for thinking of immense spaces full of pain that was worse at night.﻿ ﻿… And she, too, said that she could not respect me.﻿ ﻿…”
She sprang up.
“Oh,” she said, “she didn’t mean it. *I* didn’t mean it. Almost every man who is a man must do as you are doing. But don’t you see it’s a desperate attempt to get you to stay: an attempt on moral lines? How can we leave any stone unturned that could keep us from losing our men?” She added, and it was another stone that she didn’t leave unturned: “Besides, how can you reconcile it with your sense of duty, even from your point of view? You’re more useful﻿—you know you’re more useful to your country here than﻿ ﻿…”
He stood over her, stooping a little, somehow suggesting great gentleness and concern.
“I can’t reconcile it with my conscience,” he said. “In this affair there is nothing that any man can reconcile with his conscience. I don’t mean that we oughtn’t to be in this affair and on the side we’re on. We ought. But I’ll put to you things I have put to no other soul.”
The simplicity of his revelation seemed to her to put to shame any of the glibnesses she had heard. It appeared to her as if a child were speaking. He described the disillusionment it had cost him personally as soon as this country had come into the war.

===Chinese===

蒂金斯坐着，身子微微缩作一团，神情沮丧，火光照着他头发上那些银亮的地方。外面差不多黑了；他们意识到这间大屋——几乎一礼拜一个样——凭着镀金的微光和手磨出光的老深色木料，越来越像杜什明家的大饭厅。他带着倦意从火凳上下来，仿佛那凳子极高。他说，带着一点苦味，却更像疲惫：
“好了，我还有一桩事：告诉麦克马斯特我要离开司里。这也不会是愉快的事！倒不是说我舍不得那点差事。”又道：“真怪，亲爱的……”在她的情感风暴里，她几乎肯定他说的是“亲爱的”。……“不到三个钟头之前，我太太对我说的，几乎正是你方才这些话。几乎一字不差。她也说，夜里想着那漫无边际的、夜里更疼的痛，睡不着。……她也是说，她没法敬重我。……”
她跳起来。
“哦，”她说，“她不是那个意思。我也不是那个意思。凡算得上男人的男人，多半都得像你这么做。可你难道不明白，这是拼命想留下你：从道义上下的手？只要能不失去我们的男人，我们还有什么石头不肯翻？”她又补上一块不肯不翻的石头：“再说，就算照你的看法，你对义务感怎么交代？你更有用——你知道你留在国内对你的国家更有用，比……”
他俯身立在她面前，不知怎么透出一股大温柔、大关切。
“我没法对良心交代，”他说。“在这桩事里，没有哪个男人能拿它对良心交代。我不是说我们不该卷进来、不该站这一边。应该。可我要对你说几句，我没对任何旁人说过的。”
他这一番剖白的简单，她觉得足以羞死她听过的任何巧舌。仿佛一个孩子在说话。他讲这个国家一参战，他个人幻灭的全部经过。

He even described the sunlit heather landscape of the north, where naively he had made his tranquil resolution to join the French Foreign Legion as a common soldier and his conviction that that would give him, as he called it, clean bones again.
That, he said, had been straightforward. Now there was nothing straightforward: for him or for any man. One could have fought with a clean heart for a civilisation: if you like for the eighteenth century against the twentieth, since that was what fighting for France against the enemy countries meant. But our coming in had changed the aspect at once. It was one part of the twentieth century using the eighteenth as a catspaw to bash the other half of the twentieth. It was true there was nothing else for it. And as long as we did it in a decent spirit it was just bearable. One could keep at one’s job﻿—which was faking statistics against the other fellow﻿—until you were sick and tired of faking and your brain reeled. And then some!
It was probably impolitic to fake﻿—to overstate!﻿—a case against enemy nations. The chickens would come home to roost in one way or another, probably. Perhaps they wouldn’t. That was a matter for one’s superiors. Obviously! And the first gang had been simple, honest fellows. Stupid, but relatively disinterested. But now!﻿ ﻿… What was one to do?﻿ ﻿… He went on, almost mumbling.﻿ ﻿…
She had suddenly a clear view of him as a man extraordinarily clear-sighted in the affairs of others, in great affairs, but in his own so simple as to be almost a baby. And gentle! And extraordinarily unselfish. He didn’t betray one thought of self-interest﻿ ﻿… not one!
He was saying:
“But now!﻿ ﻿… with this crowd of boodlers!﻿ ﻿… Supposing one’s asked to manipulate the figures of millions of pairs of boots in order to force someone else to send some miserable general and his troops to, say, Salonika﻿—when they and you and common sense and everyone and everything else, know it’s disastrous?﻿ ﻿… And from that to monkeying with our own forces.﻿ ﻿… Starving particular units for political﻿ ﻿…” He was talking to himself, not to her. And indeed he said:
“I can’t, you see, talk really before you. For all I know your sympathies, perhaps your activities, are with the enemy nations.”

他甚至讲了北面那片阳光下的石楠荒原，他怎样在那里天真地安静打定主意，去参加法国外籍军团当一名列兵；又讲他怎样确信，照他的说法，那能让他“骨头重新干净”。
那一段，他说，是直截了当的。如今再没有直截了当：他没有，谁也没有。人本可以捧着一颗干净的心为一种文明而战：随你怎么说，为十八世纪打二十世纪，因为为法国打敌国就是这个意思。可我们一参战，面目立时改了。这是二十世纪的一半，拿十八世纪当爪牙，去砸二十世纪的另一半。的确，除此之外别无他路。只要我们怀着像样的心气这么干，还勉强受得下去。人守着自己的差事——也就是伪造统计数字坑对方——守到你腻透了伪造、脑子发晕。而且还要更进一步！
替敌方夸大——夸张！——罪状，多半失策。恶果早晚要回来的，多半。也许不回来。那是上司们的事。明摆着！起先那一伙还是简单的、老实的人。蠢，但相对无私。可如今！……叫人怎么办？……他接着说，几乎是喃喃自语。……
她突然清楚地看到：这个人，在别人的事上、在大事上，目光异样地明；在自己的事上却简单得近乎婴孩。而且温柔！而且无私得出奇。他没露一丝一毫为自己盘算的心思……一丝也没有！
他正说：
“可如今！……跟这一伙贪官！……比方说，人家要你操纵几百万双靴子的数字，好逼谁谁把哪个倒霉的将军和他的部队派到，比如说，萨洛尼卡去——而他、你、常识、所有人、所有别的一切，都知道那是灾难？……从这再到在我们自己部队身上做手脚。……为了政治，饿着某一支特别的部队……”他这是自言自语，不是对她。果然他说：
“你知道，我没法当着你说透。你的同情，兴许还有你的活动，保不齐在敌国那一边，我怎么知道。”
她激动地说：
“不是！不是！你怎敢说这种话？”
他答：
“不要紧……不！

===Original===

I’m sure you’re not﻿ ﻿… But, anyhow, these things are official. One can’t, if one’s scrupulous, even talk about them﻿ ﻿… And then﻿ ﻿… You see it means such infinite deaths of men, such an infinite prolongation﻿ ﻿… all this interference for side-ends!﻿ ﻿… I seem to see these fellows with clouds of blood over their heads.﻿ ﻿… And then﻿ ﻿… I’m to carry out their orders because they’re my superiors.﻿ ﻿… But helping them means unnumbered deaths.﻿ ﻿…”
He looked at her with a faint, almost humorous smile:
“You see!” he said, “we’re perhaps not so very far apart! You mustn’t think you’re the only one that sees all the deaths and all the sufferings. All, you see: I, too, am a conscientious objector. My conscience won’t let me continue any longer with these fellows.﻿ ﻿…”
She said:
“But isn’t there any other﻿ ﻿…”
He interrupted:
“No! There’s no other course. One is either a body or a brain in these affairs. I suppose I’m more brain than body. I suppose so. Perhaps I’m not. But my conscience won’t let me use my brain in this service. So I’ve a great, hulking body! I’ll admit I’m probably not much good. But I’ve nothing to live for: what I stand for isn’t any more in this world. What I want, as you know, I can’t have. So﻿ ﻿…”
She exclaimed bitterly:
“Oh, say it! Say it! Say that your large hulking body will stop two bullets in front of two small anæmic fellows.﻿ ﻿… And how can you say you’ll have nothing to live for? You’ll come back. You’ll do your good work again. You know you did good work﻿ ﻿…”
He said:
“Yes! I believe I did. I used to despise it, but I’ve come to believe I did.﻿ ﻿… But no! They’ll never let me back. They’ve got me out, with all sorts of bad marks against me. They’ll pursue me, systematically.﻿ ﻿… You see in such a world as this, an idealist﻿—or perhaps it’s only a sentimentalist﻿—must be stoned to death. He makes the others so uncomfortable. He haunts them at their golf.﻿ ﻿… No; they’ll get me, one way or the other. And some fellow﻿—Macmaster here﻿—will do my jobs. He won’t do them so well, but he’ll do them more dishonestly. Or no. I oughtn’t to say dishonestly. He’ll do them with enthusiasm and righteousness. He’ll fulfil the order of his superiors with an immense docility and unction.

===Chinese===

我信你不是。……可这些事到底是公事。人若讲分寸，连谈都不能谈……再说……你明白，这意味着数不尽的男人送命，意味着无休无止地拖下去……全为这些旁出的目的！……我仿佛看见这班人头顶上团团的血云。……然后……我还得执行他们的命令，因为他们是我的上司。……可帮他们就是数不尽的死亡。……”
他带着一丝近乎幽默的浅笑望她：
“你瞧！”他说，“咱们兴许离得并不那么远！你别以为只有你一个人看见这一切死亡、这一切苦难。全看见呢：我，也一样，是个凭良心拒服兵役的人。我的良心不许我再跟这班人干下去。……”
她说：
“那就没有别的……”
他打断：
“没有！没有别的路。这种事里，人要么是身子，要么是脑子。我想我脑子多于身子。我想是。也许不是。可我的良心不许我把脑子用在这桩差事上。好在我有一副高大笨重的身子！我认，我多半不顶事。可我没有可活的：我所代表的东西，这世上已经没有了。我想要的，你知道，我要不到。所以……”
她苦楚地喊：
“哦，说吧！说吧！就说你这高大笨重的身子，要在两个瘦小贫血的家伙前头顶两颗子弹。……再说，你怎么能说没有可活的？你会回来。你还会做你的好事。你知道你做过好事……”
他说：
“是！我信我做过。从前我看不起它，如今我信我做过。……可是不！他们再不会让我回去。他们已经把我挤出来了，档案上给我记了一串劣迹。他们会有步骤地追着我。……你明白，在这样一个世界上，理想主义者——也许不过是个滥情的人——必得被石头砸死。他叫旁人太不自在。他闹得他们打不成高尔夫。……不；他们迟早收拾了我。而哪一位——就在这儿的麦克马斯特——会接我的活。他干得没我好，可他干得更加……不，我不该说不老实。他会带着热心和正气干。他会以无比的驯顺和热忱，把上司的命令办成。

He’ll fake figures against our allies with the black enthusiasm of a Calvin and, when *that* war comes, he’ll do the requisite faking with the righteous wrath of Jehovah smiting the priests of Baal. And he’ll be right. It’s all we’re fitted for. We ought never to have come into this war. We ought to have snaffled other peoples’ colonies as the price of neutrality.﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh!” Valentine Wannop said, “how can you so hate your country?”
He said with great earnestness:
“Don’t say it! Don’t believe it! Don’t even for a moment think it! I love every inch of its fields and every plant in the hedgerows: comfrey, mullein, paigles, long red purples, that liberal shepherds give a grosser name﻿ ﻿… and all the rest of the rubbish﻿—you remember the field between the Duchemins and your mother’s﻿—and we have always been boodlers and robbers and reivers and pirates and cattle thieves, and so we’ve built up the great tradition that we love.﻿ ﻿… But, for the moment, it’s painful. Our present crowd is not more corrupt than Walpole’s. But one’s too near them. One sees of Walpole that he consolidated the nation by building up the National Debt: one doesn’t see his methods.﻿ ﻿… My son, or his son, will only see the glory of the boodle we make out of this show. Or rather out of the next. He won’t know about the methods. They’ll teach him at school that across the counties went the sound of bugles that his father knew.﻿ ﻿… Though that was another discreditable affair.﻿ ﻿…”
“But you!” Valentine Wannop exclaimed. “*You!* what will *you* do! After the war!”
“I!” he said rather bewilderedly. “I!﻿ ﻿… Oh, I shall go into the old furniture business. I’ve been offered a job.﻿ ﻿…”
She didn’t believe he was serious. He hadn’t, she knew, ever thought about his future. But suddenly she had a vision of his white head and pale face in the back glooms of a shop full of dusty things. He would come out, get heavily on to a dusty bicycle and ride off to a cottage sale. She cried out:
“Why don’t you do it at once? Why don’t you take the job at once?” For in the back of the dark shop he would at least be safe.
He said:
“Oh, no! Not at this time! Besides the old furniture trade’s probably not itself for the minute.﻿ ﻿…” He was obviously thinking of something else.
“I’ve probably been a low cad,” he said, “wringing your heart with my doubts. But I wanted to see where our similarities come in.

他会带着加尔文那种阴沉的热劲，伪造坑害盟邦的数字；等那一仗打起来，他又会带着耶和华击杀巴力祭司的那种义怒，把该伪造的伪造齐。而且他是对的。我们就配干这个。我们压根不该卷进这场战争。我们本该把中立当价钱，把别家的殖民地捞过来。……”
“哦！”瓦伦丁·万诺普说，“你怎么能这样恨你的国家？”
他极其恳切地说：
“别这么说！别这么信！连想都别这么想一下！我爱它的田野一寸一寸，爱它篱墙里的每一株草：紫草、毛蕊花、野樱草、长长的红紫花——放羊人给它的名字更粗俗——还有其余一切杂草——你记得杜什明家和你母亲家之间的那块地——而我们从来就是贪官、强盗、劫掠者、海贼、偷牛贼，我们就是这样建立起我们热爱的伟大传统。……只是眼下，不好受。我们如今这一伙不比沃波尔那伙更贪。只是我们贴得太近。隔着年代看沃波尔，看见他靠筑起国债把国家凝聚成了一体；他的手段，你看不见了。……我儿子，或他的儿子，将只看得见我们从这场戏、或者从下一场戏里捞的赃的光荣。手段他是不会知道的。学校里会教他：群山诸郡之间，响彻过他父亲熟悉的号角声。……虽然那也是一桩不体面的旧案。……”
“可你！”瓦伦丁·万诺普喊。“你！你将来怎么办！战后！”
“我！”他颇为茫然地说。“我！……哦，我去干老家具那行。有人给过我一份事。……”
她不信他是当真的。她知道，他从不曾想过自己的将来。可她蓦地看见一幅图景：一间满是尘封旧物的铺子的后堂阴影里，他的白头发和苍白的脸。他会出门，费劲跨上一辆落满灰的自行车，骑去一场村舍拍卖。她喊：
“那你为什么不马上干？为什么不马上接下那份事？”因为在那间黑黢黢的铺子后堂，他至少是安全的。
他说：
“哦，不！眼下不行！再说老家具这行眼下多半自身也难保。……”他显然在想别的。
“我多半是个下流的混蛋，”他说，“拿我的疑虑绞你的心。可我想看看，咱们的相像处究竟在哪里。

===Original===

We’ve always been﻿—or we’ve seemed always to me﻿—so alike in our thoughts. I daresay I wanted you to respect me.﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh, I respect you! I respect you!” she said. “You’re as innocent as a child.”
He went on:
“And I wanted to get some thinking done. It hasn’t been often of late that one has had a quiet room and a fire and﻿ ﻿… you! To think in front of. You *do* make one collect one’s thoughts. I’ve been very muddled till today﻿ ﻿… till five minutes ago! Do you remember our drive? You analyzed my character. I’d never have let another soul﻿ ﻿… But you see﻿ ﻿… Don’t you see?”
She said:
“No! What am I to see? I remember﻿ ﻿…”
He said:
“That I’m certainly not an English country gentleman now; picking up the gossip of the horse markets and saying: let the country go to hell, for me!”
She said:
“Did I say that?﻿ ﻿… Yes, I said that!”
The deep waves of emotion came over her: she trembled. She stretched out her arms.﻿ ﻿… She thought she stretched out her arms. He was hardly visible in the firelight. But she could see nothing: she was blind for tears. She could hardly be stretching out her arms, for she had both hands to her handkerchief on her eyes. He said something: it was no word of love or she would have held it; it began with: “Well, I must be﻿ ﻿…” He was silent for a long time: she imagined herself to feel great waves coming from him to her. But he wasn’t in the room.﻿ ﻿…
The rest, till that moment at the War Office, had been pure agony, and unrelenting. Her mother’s paper cut down her money; no orders for serials came in: her mother, obviously, was failing. The eternal diatribes of her brother were like lashes upon her skin. He seemed to be praying Tietjens to death. Of Tietjens she saw and heard nothing. At the Macmasters she heard, once, that he had just gone out. It added to her desire to scream when she saw a newspaper. Poverty invaded them. The police raided the house in search of her brother and his friends. Then her brother went to prison: somewhere in the Midlands. The friendliness of their former neighbours turned to surly suspicion. They could get no milk. Food became almost unprocurable without going to long distances. For three days Mrs. Wannop was clean out of her mind. Then she grew better and began to write a new book. It promised to be rather good. But there was no publisher. Edward came out of prison, full of good-humour and boisterousness.

咱们一向——至少在我一向看来——想法那样相像。多半我想让你敬重我。……”
“哦，我敬重你！我敬重你！”她说。“你天真得像个孩子。”
他接着说：
“我还想做点思考。近来难得有一间安静的屋子、一炉火，还有……有你！当着人想事情。你真能叫人把思路理拢。到今天为止我一直糊里糊涂……到五分钟之前！记得咱们那次乘车出游吗？你分析过我的性格。换任何一个别人我都不容……可你瞧……你还不明白吗？”
她说：
“不！我该明白什么？我记得……”
他说：
“明白我如今分明不是英国乡绅了；不是那种听着马市的闲话、说‘这国家让它见鬼去，与我何干’的乡绅了！”
她说：
“我说过这话吗？……对，我说过这话！”
深沉的情感之浪漫过她：她发抖。她张开双臂。……她以为她张开了双臂。火光里他几乎看不见了。可她什么也看不见：眼泪叫她瞎了。她其实张不开双臂，因为她两只手都用手绢按在眼睛上。他说了句什么：不是情话，是情话她必会攋住不放；那句话以“好了，我得……”开头。他沉默了很久：她仿佛感到巨大的波澜从他那头传到她这头。可他已不在屋里。……”
此后直到陆军部那一刻，是纯粹的、不松口的酷刑。母亲的报馆削减了她的稿费；约稿全无；母亲眼看着在垮。弟弟没完没了的长篇攻击像鞭子抽在皮肉上。他仿佛在把蒂金斯祷告死。蒂金斯则音信全无。在麦克马斯特家，她有一回听说“他刚走”。她一见报纸就添一分想尖叫的欲望。贫穷进了家门。警察为搜弟弟和他的朋友们抄了家。随后弟弟进了监狱：在中部什么地方。旧日邻人的和气变成了悻悻的猜忌。牛奶订不上了。不跑远路几乎弄不到吃的。万诺普太太整整三天神志全失。随后好些了，动手写一部新书。看苗头写得相当好。可是没有出版家。爱德华出狱了，兴高采烈、咋咋呼呼。

===Original===

They seemed to have had a great deal to drink in prison. But, hearing that his mother had gone mad over that disgrace, after a terrible scene with Valentine, in which he accused her of being the mistress of Tietjens and therefore militarist, he consented to let his mother use her influence﻿—of which she had still some﻿—to get him appointed as an A.B. on a minesweeper. Great winds became an agony to Valentine Wannop in addition to the unbearable sounds of firing that came continuously over the sea. Her mother grew much better: she took pride in having a son in a service. She was then the more able to appreciate the fact that her paper stopped payment altogether. A small mob on the fifth of November burned Mrs. Wannop in effigy in front of their cottage and broke their lower windows. Mrs. Wannop ran out and in the illumination of the fire knocked down two farm labourer hobbledehoys. It was terrible to see Mrs. Wannop’s grey hair in the firelight. After that the butcher refused them meat altogether, ration card or no ration card. It was imperative that they should move to London.
The marsh horizon became obscured with giant stilts: the air above it filled with aeroplanes: the roads covered with military cars. There was then no getting away from the sounds of the war.
Just as they had decided to move Tietjens came back. It was for a moment heaven to have him in this country. But when, a month later, Valentine Wannop saw him for a minute, he seemed very heavy, aged and dull. It was then almost as bad as before, for it seemed to Valentine as if he hardly had his reason.
On hearing that Tietjens was to be quartered﻿—or, at any rate, occupied﻿—in the neighbourhood of Ealing, Mrs. Wannop at once took a small house in Bedford Park, whilst, to make ends meet﻿—for her mother made terribly little﻿—Valentine Wannop took a post as athletic mistress in a great school in a not very near suburb. Thus, though Tietjens came in for a cup of tea almost every afternoon with Mrs. Wannop in the dilapidated little suburban house, Valentine Wannop hardly ever saw him. The only free afternoon she had was the Friday, and on that day she still regularly chaperoned Mrs. Duchemin: meeting her at Charing Cross towards noon and taking her back to the same station in time to catch the last train to Rye. On Saturdays and Sundays she was occupied all day in typing her mother’s manuscript.

===Chinese===

监狱里他们像是喝了个痛快。可听说母亲为这桩丑事气疯了，在同瓦伦丁大闹一场之后——他骂她给蒂金斯当情妇、因此是军国主义者——他答应让母亲用她的余威——她还有几分余威——给他谋了个扫雷艇上二等水兵的差事。于是，除了不断隔海传来的、不堪忍受的炮声，大风也成了瓦伦丁·万诺普的酷刑。母亲倒大有起色：为儿子在军中服役而得意。她因而更经得起这个打击：她的报馆彻底停了付账。十一月五号，一小群暴民在他们村舍前烧万诺普太太的假人，砸了他们楼下的窗。万诺普太太冲出去，在火光里撂倒两个庄稼汉半大小子。火光里万诺普太太的灰发，看着叫人心碎。打那以后，肉铺干脆拒绝供肉，有没有配给卡都一样。搬伦敦已是刻不容缓。
沼泽的地平线竖起了巨人的支架：上空塞满了飞机：公路盖满了军车。战争的声响，再没处躲了。
刚定下搬家，蒂金斯回来了。有他在这个国家里，那一刻是天堂。可一个月后瓦伦丁·万诺普见了他一分钟，他显得那样沉重、苍老、迟钝。随后几乎同从前一样糟：在瓦伦丁看来，他简直像失了魂。
听说蒂金斯要驻扎——至少是办公——在伊灵一带，万诺普太太立刻在贝德福德帕克租了座小屋；为了贴补——母亲挣的钱少得吓人——瓦伦丁·万诺普在一所不算近的郊区大学校里谋了个体育教师的职位。于是，虽然蒂金斯几乎每天下午都到那座破败的小郊宅来，同万诺普太太喝杯茶，瓦伦丁·万诺普却几乎见不着他。她唯一空着的下午是礼拜五；那天她照旧定时给杜什明太太当陪伴：中午前后在查令十字接她，末班去拉伊的车之前把她送回同一个车站。礼拜六和礼拜天，她整天打她母亲的手稿。

Of Tietjens, then, she saw almost nothing. She knew that his poor mind was empty of facts and of names; but her mother said he was a great help to her. Once provided with facts his mind worked out sound Tory conclusions﻿—or quite startling and attractive theories﻿—with extreme rapidity. This Mrs. Wannop found of the greatest use to her whenever﻿—though it wasn’t now very often﻿—she had an article to write for an excitable newspaper. She still, however, contributed to her failing organ of opinion, though it paid her nothing.﻿ ﻿…
Mrs. Duchemin, then, Valentine Wannop still chaperoned, though there was no bond any more between them. Valentine knew, for instance, perfectly well that Mrs. Duchemin, after she had been seen off by train from Charing Cross, got out at Clapham Junction, took a taxicab back to Gray’s Inn after dark and spent the night with Macmaster, and Mrs. Duchemin knew quite well that Valentine knew. It was a sort of parade of circumspection and rightness, and they kept it up even after, at a sinister registry office, the wedding had taken place, Valentine being the one witness and an obscure-looking substitute for the usual pew opener another. There seemed to be, by then, no very obvious reason why Valentine should support Mrs. Macmaster any more on these rather dreary occasions, but Mrs. Macmaster said she might just as well, until they saw fit to make the marriage public. There were, Mrs. Macmaster said, censorious tongues, and even if these were confuted afterwards it is difficult, if not impossible, to outrun scandal. Besides, Mrs. Macmaster was of opinion that the Macmaster afternoons with these geniuses must be a liberal education for Valentine. But, as Valentine sat most of the time at the tea-table near the door, it was the backs and side faces of the distinguished rather than their intellects with which she was most acquainted. Occasionally, however, Mrs. Duchemin would show Valentine, as an enormous privilege, one of the letters to herself from men of genius: usually North British, written, as a rule, from the Continent or more distant and peaceful climates, for most of them believed it their duty in these hideous times to keep alive in the world the only glimmering spark of beauty. Couched in terms so eulogistic as to resemble those used in passionate love-letters by men more profane, these epistles recounted, or consulted Mrs.

===Chinese===

至于蒂金斯，她几乎见不着。她知道他那可怜的脑子空了事实、空了名字；可母亲说他帮了大忙。事实一旦喂给他，他的脑子就以惊人的速度推出纯正的托利党结论——或者惊人而迷人的理论。万诺普太太每逢要给哪家风风火火的报纸写文章——如今不常有——就觉得这妙用无穷。她仍旧给她那每况愈下的舆论阵地写稿，虽然分文不取。……”
杜什明太太，瓦伦丁·万诺普就仍旧陪伴着，虽然两人之间已没了情分。譬如瓦伦丁一清二楚：杜什明太太在查令十字被送上车之后，在克拉彭枢纽站下车，天黑后坐出租汽车折回格雷律师学院，同麦克马斯特过夜；杜什明太太也一清二楚瓦伦丁知道。这是谨慎与体面的一场阅兵；甚至在一家阴森的登记处婚礼办过之后还照演不疑——瓦伦丁做了一位证人，另一位是顶替惯常引座员的一个不起眼的家伙。到那时，瓦伦丁其实已没什么明摆着的理由，该在这样的凄清场合继续给麦克马斯特太太撑场面；可麦克马斯特太太说，不妨照旧，等他们觉得可以让婚事公开。麦克马斯特太太说，长舌的嘴多；就算日后驳倒，丑话也追不回来。再说，麦克马斯特太太认为，麦克马斯特家这些有才子们参加的下午会，对瓦伦丁该是一种开明的教育。可瓦伦丁大部分时间坐在靠门的茶桌边，同她相熟的主要是名流们的背和侧脸，不是他们的智力。不过偶尔，杜什明太太会把才子们写给她的一封信，当作天大的恩典给瓦伦丁看：写信的通常是北不列颠人，照例从大陆或更遥远、更安宁的气候里写来——他们多数人相信，值此可怖之世，他们有本分把世上仅存的那点美的火星保住。信上的措辞热烈得近乎亵慢者的情书，写的是、或请教的是他们同外国公主的风流、病情的进展，或他们的灵魂向更高的道德境域的攀升——他们的这位通信人，灵魂多么美，就飘在那境域里。

===Original===

Duchemin as to, their love affairs with foreign princesses, the progress of their ailments or the progresses of their souls towards those higher regions of morality in which floated their so beautiful-souled correspondent.
The letters entertained Valentine and, indeed, she was entertained by that whole mirage. It was only the Macmasters’ treatment of her mother that finally decided Valentine that this friendship had died; for the friendships of women are very tenacious things, surviving astonishing disillusionments, and Valentine Wannop was a woman of more than usual loyalty. Indeed, if she couldn’t respect Mrs. Duchemin on the old grounds, she could very really respect her for her tenacity of purpose, her determination to advance Macmaster and for the sort of ruthlessness that she put into these pursuits.
Valentine’s affection had, indeed, survived even Edith Ethel’s continued denigrations of Tietjens﻿—for Edith Ethel regarded Tietjens as a clog round her husband’s neck, if only because he was a very unpopular man, grown personally rather unpresentable and always extremely rude to the geniuses on Fridays. Edith Ethel, however, never made these complaints that grew more and more frequent as more and more the distinguished flocked to the Fridays, before Macmaster. And they ceased very suddenly and in a way that struck Valentine as odd.
Mrs. Duchemin’s grievance against Tietjens was that, Macmaster being a weak man, Tietjens had acted as his banker until, what with interest and the rest of it, Macmaster owed Tietjens a great sum: several thousand pounds. And there had been no real reason: Macmaster had spent most of the money either on costly furnishings for his rooms or on his costly journeys to Rye. On the one hand Mrs. Duchemin could have found Macmaster all the bric-a-brac he could possibly have wanted from amongst the things at the rectory, where no one would have missed them and, on the other, she, Mrs. Duchemin, would have paid all Macmaster’s travelling expenses. She had had unlimited money from her husband, who never asked for accounts. But, whilst Tietjens still had influence with Macmaster, he had used it uncompromisingly against this course, giving him the delusion﻿—it enraged Mrs. Duchemin to think!﻿—that it would have been dishonourable. So that Macmaster had continued to draw upon him.

===Chinese===

这些信逗乐了瓦伦丁；说真的，那整个蜃楼都逗乐了她。最终叫瓦伦丁认定这份友谊已死的，是麦克马斯特夫妇待她母亲的样子；因为女人家的友谊是极韧的东西，惊人的幻灭都熬得过，何况瓦伦丁·万诺普又是个分外出忠心的女人。的确，即便不能再照老理由敬重杜什明太太，她还可以真心敬重她的目的之坚、她提携麦克马斯特的决心、以及她投入这些营生时的那股狠劲。
瓦伦丁的感情，甚至熬过了伊迪丝·艾塞尔对蒂金斯絮絮不休的贬损——因为伊迪丝·艾塞尔把蒂金斯看作套在她丈夫脖子上的磨盘，哪怕只因为他人缘极坏、人变得相当不体面、逢礼拜五对才子们又总是粗鲁无比。不过这些抱怨，随着名流们越来越多地涌向礼拜五，越来越频繁，伊迪丝·艾塞尔却从不在麦克马斯特面前发。而且抱怨戛然而止，止得古怪，叫瓦伦丁纳罕。
杜什明太太对蒂金斯的怨在于：麦克马斯特是个意志薄弱的人，蒂金斯一直做他的银行，连本带利，麦克马斯特欠下蒂金斯一大笔：好几千镑。而这笔债本没有来由：钱多半花在给寓所置办昂贵的陈设、花在一次次昂贵的拉伊之行上了。一头，杜什明太太本可以从牧师宅的物件里，把麦克马斯特要的古董杂项尽数供给——反正没人会发现短少；另一头，她杜什明太太本可以全数负担麦克马斯特的差旅。她从丈夫那里拿钱毫无限制，丈夫从不查账。可蒂金斯对麦克马斯特还有影响力的那阵，用得不留情面，死堵这条路，给了他一种幻觉——想起来就叫杜什明太太冒火！——仿佛那样做就不体面。于是麦克马斯特照旧一张一张支他的钱。

===Original===

And, most enraging of all, at a period when she had had a power of attorney over all Mr. Duchemin’s fortune and could, perfectly easily, have sold out something that no one would have missed for the couple of thousand or so that Macmaster owed, Tietjens had very forcibly refused to allow Macmaster to agree to anything of the sort. He had again put into Macmaster’s weak head that it would be dishonourable. But Mrs. Duchemin﻿—and she closed her lips determinedly after she had said it﻿—knew perfectly well Tietjens’ motive. So long as Macmaster owed him money he imagined that they couldn’t close their doors upon him. And their establishment was beginning to be a place where you meet people of great influence who might well get for a person as lazy as Tietjens a sinecure that would suit him. Tietjens, in fact, knew which side his bread was buttered.
For what, Mrs. Duchemin asked, could there have been dishonourable about the arrangement she had proposed? Practically the whole of Mr. Duchemin’s money was to come to her: he was by then insane; it was therefore, morally, her own. But immediately after that, Mr. Duchemin having been certified, the estate had fallen into the hands of the Lunacy Commissioners and there had been no further hope of taking the capital. Now, her husband being dead, it was in the hands of trustees, Mr. Duchemin having left the whole of his property to Magdalen College and merely the income to his widow. The income was very large; but where, with their expenses, with the death duties and taxation, which were by then merciless, was Mrs. Duchemin to find the money? She was to be allowed, under her husband’s will, enough capital to buy a pleasant little place in Surrey, with rather a nice lot of land﻿—enough to let Macmaster know some of the leisures of a country gentleman’s lot. They were going in for shorthorns, and there was enough land to give them a small golf-course and, in the autumn, a little﻿—oh, mostly rough!﻿—shooting for Macmaster to bring his friends down to. It would just run to that. Oh, no ostentation. Merely a nice little place. As an amusing detail the villagers there already called Macmaster “squire” and the women curtsied to him. But Valentine Wannop would understand that, with all these expenses, they couldn’t find the money to pay off Tietjens. Besides, Mrs. Macmaster said she wasn’t going to pay off Tietjens.

===Chinese===

最冒火的还在后头：有一段，她握有杜什明先生全部财产的委托书，本来轻而易举就能变卖一件谁也不会惦记的东西，把麦克马斯特欠的那两千来镑还清；蒂金斯却断然不许麦克马斯特答应任何这类安排。他又往麦克马斯特那软弱的脑袋里塞了“不体面”。可杜什明太太——说完这句，她把嘴坚决地闭拢——对蒂金斯的动机一清二楚。只要麦克马斯特还欠他钱，他就以为人家不能对他闭门。而他们那个家，渐渐成了能遇上大权在握者的地方；对蒂金斯这样懒的人，那些人满可以谋个闲差。蒂金斯，说白了，知道自己的面包哪面抹了黄油。
试问，杜什明太太问，她提的那个安排，哪一点不体面？杜什明先生的钱，实际上几乎全要归她：他那时已经疯了；所以，道义上说，钱就是她自己的。可紧接着，杜什明先生被鉴定之后，产业落进了疯人事务委员会手里，动本金再无指望。如今丈夫死了，钱在受托人手里：杜什明先生把全部财产留给了莫德林学院，只给遗孀留了收益。收益极丰；可是，开销那么大，死亡税和捐税又都苛到不容情，杜什明太太上哪儿找这笔钱？按遗嘱，她可以领一笔够在萨里买一处可人的小产业的资本，带一块相当不错的地——够让麦克马斯特尝尝乡绅日子的几分清福。他们打算养短角牛；地够辟一座小高尔夫球场，秋天还能有点——哦，多半是野的！——猎场，让麦克马斯特邀朋友下来打打。刚刚够这么折腾。哦，并不铺张。不过一处可爱的小产业。有个好玩的细节：那边的村里人已经管麦克马斯特叫“老爷”，妇人们向他屈膝行礼。可瓦伦丁·万诺普会明白：开销这么多，他们实在挤不出钱来还蒂金斯。再说，麦克马斯特太太说了，她根本不打算还蒂金斯。

===Original===

He had had his chance once: now he could go without, for her. Macmaster would have to pay it himself and he would never be able to, his contribution to their housekeeping being what it was. And there were going to be complications. Macmaster wondered about their little place in Surrey, saying that he would consult Tietjens about this and that alteration. But over the doorsill of that place the foot of Tietjens was never going to go! Never! It would mean a good deal of unpleasantness; or rather it would mean one sharp: *C‑r‑r‑unch!* And then: Napoo finny! Mrs. Duchemin sometimes, and with great effect, condescended to use one of the more picturesque phrases of the day.
To all these diatribes Valentine Wannop answered hardly anything. It was no particular concern of hers; even if, for a moment, she felt proprietarily towards Christopher as she did now and then, she felt no particular desire that his intimacy with the Macmasters should be prolonged, because she knew he could have no particular desire for its prolongation. She imagined him turning them down with an unspoken and good-humoured gibe. And, indeed, she agreed on the whole with Edith Ethel. It was demoralizing for a weak little man like Vincent to have a friend with an ever-open purse beside him. Tietjens ought not to have been princely: it was a defect, a quality that she did not personally admire in him. As to whether it would or wouldn’t have been dishonourable for Mrs. Duchemin to take her husband’s money and give it to Macmaster, she kept an open mind. To all intents and purposes the money *was* Mrs. Duchemin’s, and if Mrs. Duchemin had then paid Christopher off it would have been sensible. She could see that later it had become very inconvenient. There were, however, male standards to be considered, and Macmaster, at least, passed for a man. Tietjens, who was wise enough in the affairs of others, had, in that, probably been wise; for there might have been great disagreeablenesses with trustees and heirs-at-law had Mrs. Duchemin’s subtraction of a couple of thousand pounds from the Duchemin estate afterwards come to light. The Wannops had never been large property owners as a family, but Valentine had heard enough of collateral wranglings over small family dishonesties to know how very disagreeable these could be.

===Chinese===

他有过一回机会了：如今嘛，为了她，他可以白白吃亏。麦克马斯特得自己还，可他永远还不起，他贴补家用的数目摆在那儿。而且，麻烦还在后头。麦克马斯特为萨里那处小产业动心思，说要这样那样改一改，得请教蒂金斯。可那地方的门槛，蒂金斯的脚永远休想踏进去！永远！那将意味着诸多不快；不如说，意味着干脆一下：咔——嚓！然后：没影儿喽！杜什明太太时常——而且效果极佳——屈尊用一两句当日最生动的行话。
对这一篇篇控诉，瓦伦丁·万诺普几乎不答腔。这本不特别关她的事；即便她一时对克里斯托弗起了独占的心——她偶尔会的——她也并不特别希望他同麦克马斯特夫妇的交情延续，因为她知道他本人也并不希望延续。她想象他不声不响、好脾气地一句挖苦，把他们打发了。说真的，这方面她大体同意伊迪丝·艾塞尔：文森特这样软弱的小男人，身边有个永远敞开钱袋的朋友，是会学坏的。蒂金斯原不该摆王爷谱：这是个毛病，一个她个人并不欣赏的品性。至于杜什明太太拿丈夫的钱给麦克马斯特用，体面不体面，她存而不论。那钱实际上就是杜什明太太的；她当时若把克里斯托弗的债还清，倒也明智。她看得出，后来这事变得极碍手脚。不过男人的标准也得考虑；麦克马斯特至少还算个男人。蒂金斯既然在别人的事上那样明，在这上头多半也明：万一杜什明太太从产业里挪两千镑的事日后败露，受托人和法定继承人间不知要生多少龃龉。万诺普家从来算不上大产业人家，可瓦伦丁也听过不少旁系亲属为小小的家内手脚争吵的事，深知那可以闹到多难堪。

===Original===

So she had made little or no comment; sometimes she had even faintly agreed as to the demoralisation of Macmaster and that had sufficed. For Mrs. Duchemin had been certain of her rightness and cared nothing at all for the opinion of Valentine Wannop, or else took it for granted.
And when Tietjens had been gone to France for a little time Mrs. Duchemin seemed to forget the matter, contenting herself with saying that he might very likely not come back. He was the sort of clumsy man who generally got killed. In that case, since no I.O.U.s or paper had passed, Mrs. Tietjens would have no claim. So that would be all right.
But two days after the return of Christopher﻿—and that was how Valentine knew he had come back!﻿—Mrs. Duchemin with a lowering brow exclaimed:
“That oaf, Tietjens, is in England, perfectly safe and sound. And now the whole miserable business of Vincent’s indebtedness.﻿ ﻿… Oh!”
She had stopped so suddenly and so markedly that even the stoppage of Valentine’s own heart couldn’t conceal the oddness from her. Indeed it was as if there were an interval before she completely realised what the news was and as if, during that interval, she said to herself:
“It’s very queer. It’s exactly as if Edith Ethel has stopped abusing him on my account.﻿ ﻿… As if she *knew*!” But how could Edith Ethel know that she loved the man who had returned? It was impossible! She hardly knew herself. Then the great wave of relief rolled over her: he was in England. One day she would see him, there: in the great room. For these colloquies with Edith Ethel always took place in the great room where she had last seen Tietjens. It looked suddenly beautiful and she was resigned to sitting there, waiting for the distinguished.
It was indeed a beautiful room: it had become so during the years. It was long and high﻿—matching the Tietjens’. A great cut-glass chandelier from the rectory hung dimly coruscating in the centre, reflected and re-reflected in convex gilt mirrors, topped by eagles. A great number of books had gone to make place on the white panelled walls for the mirrors, and for the fair orange and brown pictures by Turner, also from the rectory.

===Chinese===

于是她几乎不作评论；有时甚至淡淡附和一句，说麦克马斯特确实被带坏了，也就够了。因为杜什明太太自信自己有理，对瓦伦丁·万诺普的看法全不放在心上——或者干脆想当然。
蒂金斯去法国一小段时间后，杜什明太太似乎把这事忘了，只满足于说一句：他很可能回不来了。他属于那种笨手笨脚、十有八九送命的男人。真到那时，既然从没立过借据、没过一字纸据，蒂金斯太太什么名分也讨不着。那就万事大吉了。
可是克里斯托弗回来两天之后——瓦伦丁正是这样得知他回来了！——杜什明太太眉头一沉，嚷了起来：
“那个蠢货蒂金斯，人在英国，好端端毫发无伤。这下文森特那笔倒债的破事全来了。……哎呀！”
她收口收得那样突然、那样扎眼，就连瓦伦丁自己那颗骤停的心，也遮不住这份古怪。真的，仿佛隔了那么一瞬她才完全明白这消息是什么，仿佛就在那一瞬里她对自己说：
“真怪。恰恰像是伊迪丝·艾塞尔看在我的份上不再骂他了。……像是她*知道*！”可伊迪丝·艾塞尔怎么会知道她爱那个回来的人？不可能！她自己都几乎不知道。随即一阵如释重负的巨浪涌过她全身：他在英国了。总有一天她会见到他，就在那儿：在那间大厅里。因为她同伊迪丝·艾塞尔的这些谈话，向来都在那间大厅里进行——她最后一次见到蒂金斯也是在那里。那房间忽然显得美起来，她也甘愿坐在那儿，等着那些名流。
那确实是间美的屋子：是这些年间一年年美起来的。又长又高——与蒂金斯家的那间相仿。一盏从教区牧师宅搬来的大切割玻璃吊灯，在屋子中央幽幽地闪，映在凸面镀金镜里，一重又一重，镜顶上装点着鹰雕。许许多多的书让了位，给白镶板墙上腾出地方挂镜子，还有透纳那些橙色与棕色相间的清淡画幅——也是从教区牧师宅来的。

===Original===

From the rectory had come the immense scarlet and lapis lazuli carpet, the great brass fire-basket and appendages, the great curtains that, in the three long windows, on their peacock blue Chinese silk showed parti-coloured cranes ascending in long flights﻿—and all the polished Chippendale armchairs. Amongst all these, gracious, trailing, stopping with a tender gesture to rearrange very slightly the crimson roses in the famous silver bowls, still in dark blue silks, with an amber necklace and her elaborate black hair, waved exactly like that of Julia Domma of the Musée Lapidaire at Arles, moved Mrs. Macmaster﻿—also from the rectory. Macmaster had achieved his desire: even to the shortbread cakes and the peculiarly scented tea that came every Friday morning from Princes Street. And, if Mrs. Macmaster hadn’t the pawky, relishing humour of the great Scots ladies of past days, she had in exchange her deep aspect of comprehension and tenderness. An astonishingly beautiful and impressive woman: dark hair; dark, straight eyebrows; a straight nose; dark blue eyes in the shadows of her hair and bowed, pomegranate lips in a chin curved like the bow of a Greek boat.﻿ ﻿…
The etiquette of the place on Fridays was regulated as if by a royal protocol. The most distinguished and, if possible, titled person was led to a great walnut wood fluted chair that stood askew by the fireplace, its back and seat of blue velvet, heaven knows how old. Over him would hover Mrs. Duchemin: or, if he were *very* distinguished, both Mr. and Mrs. Macmaster. The not so distinguished were led up by turns to be presented to the celebrity and would then arrange themselves in a half-circle in the beautiful armchairs; the less distinguished still, in outer groups in chairs that had no arms: the almost undistinguished stood, also in groups or languished, awestruck on the scarlet leather window seats. When all were there Macmaster would establish himself on the incredibly unique hearthrug and would address wise sayings to the celebrity; occasionally, however, saying a kind thing to the youngest man present﻿—to give him a chance of distinguishing himself. Macmaster’s hair, at that date, was still black, but not quite so stiff or so well brushed; his beard had in it greyish streaks and his teeth, not being quite so white, looked less strong.

===Chinese===

从教区牧师宅来的还有那张深红与青金蓝的大地毯、那座黄铜大火篮及全套家什、那几大幅帷幔——三扇长窗上，孔雀蓝的中国绸面里，五彩仙鹤排成长阵冉冉升腾——还有全部打磨光亮的奇彭代尔扶手椅。在这一切之中，仪态雍容、裙裾曳地，不时停步，以一个温柔的手势把名贵银钵里的绯红玫瑰轻轻拨弄一两下，依旧一身深蓝绸衣，挂着琥珀项链，一头精工细作的黑色鬈发，纹丝不差地像阿尔勒石刻博物馆里朱莉娅·多玛的发型——麦克马斯特太太就这样走动——她也是从教区牧师宅来的。麦克马斯特如愿以偿了：连每星期五早晨从王子街送来的黄油酥饼和那气味别致的茶叶都在内。麦克马斯特太太虽没有往昔苏格兰贵妇那种含蓄有味的幽默，却另有一副深情体谅、温婉动人的气度。一个美得惊人、令人望之肃然的女人：黑发；黑而平直的眉毛；端正的鼻子；双眸深蓝，藏在发影里；双唇微抿如石榴，衬着希腊船弓般弯出的下巴。……
每逢星期五，此地的礼仪照章办理，俨然宫廷仪典。最尊贵、最好有头衔的客人，被引到壁炉旁一把歪斜放着的大胡桃木槽纹扶手椅上，椅背椅座蒙着蓝丝绒，天知道有多少年头了。杜什明太太会在他头顶盘桓献殷勤：倘若来客*极其*尊贵，便麦克马斯特先生太太一齐出马。次一等的客人挨个被领上前去谒见这位名流，随后在那些漂亮的扶手椅里围坐成半圈；再次一等的，在外圈成群坐着没有扶手的椅子；几乎不入流的便站着，也成堆，或者恹恹地、诚惶诚恐地坐在深红皮面的窗座上。人齐之后，麦克马斯特便在那块举世无双的炉前地毯上站定，向名流发表智慧的隽语；不过偶尔也说句体恤话给在座最年轻的男人——给他一个崭露头角的机会。那时候麦克马斯特的头发还是黑的，只是不那么硬挺、不那么梳理得一丝不苟了；胡子里添了灰白的一绺绺，牙也不那么白，看着便不那么结实。
