弟弟滚出家门，摔门摔得这偷工减料的狗窝上每一片瓦都跳起来又坐回去。
她上楼去翻她的裙子。她说不清翻的是什么裙子；它们像排齐的破布似的摊在床上，电话铃疯响。她听见母亲的声音忽然缓和下来：“哦！哦！……是你呀！”她关上自己的房门，一抽屉一抽屉地拉开又合上。她一停手，母亲的声音便半可闻了；提起来问话时，便字字可闻。她听见她说：“不会叫她惹麻烦的……当然！”随后又低下去，只剩一片高音。
她听见母亲喊：
“瓦伦丁！瓦伦丁！下来。……你不想同克里斯托弗（Christopher）说话吗？……瓦伦丁！瓦伦丁！…”接着又是一阵：“瓦伦丁……瓦伦丁……瓦伦丁……”把她当小狗儿！谢天谢地，万诺普太太站上了那吱吱作响的楼梯的最底一级。她已撂下电话。她朝上喊：
“下来。我要告诉你！这好孩子救了我！他总是救我！如今他走了，我可怎么办哪？”
“他救了别人：自己却救不了！”瓦伦丁辛酸地引了一句。她一把抓起阔边毡帽。她不要为他打扮。他得照她本来的样子要她。……自己救不了自己！可他在女人身上倒阔气！……粗俗了！可也许只在皮面上！她自己呢！……她正跑下楼去！
母亲退进了小客厅：九英尺见方；结果是，十英尺高，对它的面积来说太高了。可屋里有一张沙发，带靠垫。……头枕在那垫子上，也许。……要是他送她回家来！夜深了！……
母亲正说着：他是个好极了的人。……“战争婴儿”文章的一个好根子。大兵若是个体面人，他就克制，因为不愿叫姑娘惹麻烦。……他若不是体面人，他就豁出去碰运气，因为那也许是他最后的机会。……
“这是捎给我的口信！”瓦伦丁自忖。“可是哪一句。…”她心不在焉地把所有靠垫都挪到沙发一头。母亲叫起来：
“他捎了他的爱！他母亲有这样一个儿子，真福气！”说着一头钻进她那小窟窿似的工作间。
瓦伦丁顺着园径的碎砖跑下去，把阔边毡帽按紧。

===Original===

She had looked at her wrist watch; it was two and twelve: 14:45. If she was to walk to the War Office by 4:15﻿—16:15﻿—a sensible innovation!﻿—she must step out. Five miles to Whitehall. God knows what, then! Five miles back! Two and a half, diagonally, to High Street Station by half-past 19! Twelve and a half miles in five hours or less. And three hours dancing on the top of it. And to dress!﻿ ﻿… She needed to be fit﻿ ﻿… And, with violent bitterness, she said:
“Well! I’m fit.﻿ ﻿…” She had an image of the aligned hundred of girls in blue jumpers and men’s ties keeping whom fit had kept her super-fit. She wondered how many of them would be men’s mistresses before the year was out. It was August then. But perhaps none! Because she had kept them fit.﻿ ﻿…
“Ah!” she said, “if I had been a loose woman, with flaccid breasts and a soft body. All perfumed!”﻿ ﻿… But neither Sylvia Tietjens nor Ethel Duchemin were soft. They might be scented on occasion! But they could not contemplate with equanimity doing a twelve-mile walk to save a few pence and dancing all night on top of it! She could! And perhaps the price she paid was just that; she was in such hard condition she hadn’t moved him to﻿ ﻿… She perhaps exhaled such an aura of sobriety, chastity and abstinence as to suggest to him that﻿ ﻿… that a decent fellow didn’t get his girl into trouble before going to be killed.﻿ ﻿… Yet if he were such a town bull!﻿ ﻿… She wondered how she knew such phrases.﻿ ﻿…
The sordid and aligned houses seemed to rush past her in the mean August sunshine. That was because if you thought hard time went quicker; or because after you noticed the paper shop at this corner you would be up to the boxes of onions outside the shop of the next corner before you noticed anything else.
She was in Kensington Gardens, on the north side; she had left the poor shops behind.﻿ ﻿… In sham country, with sham lawns, sham avenues, sham streams. Sham people pursuing their ways across the sham grass. Or no! Not sham! In a vacuum! No! “Pasteurized” was the word! Like dead milk. Robbed of their vitamins.﻿ ﻿…
If she saved a few coppers by walking it would make a larger pile to put into the leering﻿—or compassionate﻿—taxicabman’s hand after he had helped her support her brother into the dog kennel door. Edward would be dead drunk. She had fifteen shillings for the taxi.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

她看过腕表；两点十二：14:45。她若要在4:15——16:15——前走到陆军部——一项合理的新规！——就得迈开步子。到白厅五英里。天知道之后还有什么！回来又是五英里！再斜着两英里半，19点半前赶到高街车站。五个钟头走十二英里半，或者更少。外加三个钟头跳舞。还得打扮！……她得身子硬朗……于是带着剧烈的辛酸，她说：
“好！我硬朗。…”她眼前现出那排齐的百来个蓝罩衫黑领带的姑娘；为保持她们硬朗，她自己保持得了超等的硬朗。她不知道年底以前她们中间会有多少人做男人的情妇。那时是八月。可也许一个也没有！因为是她保持她们硬朗的。……
“啊！”她说，“倘若我是个放荡女人，胸脯松塌，一身软肉，喷得香喷喷的！…”可西尔维娅·蒂金斯和艾塞尔·杜什明都不软。她们偶尔也许喷香！可要她们安安稳稳地为省几个铜板走上一段十二英里的路、外加跳一整夜舞，她们办不到！她办得到！而她付出的代价，也许正是这个：她身子这样硬，没能打动他去……她也许周身散着那样一股清醒、贞洁、有节制的人气，以致向他暗示……暗示一个体面人不会在上阵去死之前，先叫姑娘惹上麻烦。……可他若真是那么一头市镇上的种牛呢！…她纳闷自己打哪儿学来这些字眼。……
八月吝啬的日光里，那些排齐的寒碜房子仿佛朝她身后飞掠而去。那是因为你想得深，时间就过得快；或者因为，你注意到这个街角的报摊之后，还没来得及留意别的，人已到了下一个街角那家铺子门外的一箱箱洋葱跟前。
她到了肯辛顿园的北侧；那些穷铺子已被她甩在身后。……在假乡村里，有假草坪，假林荫道，假溪流。假人们在假草地上各赶各的路。或者不！不是假！是在真空里！不！“消过毒的”才是那字眼！像死牛奶。维生素被夺走了的。……
她若走路省下几个铜板，那一小把就添成更大的一堆，好放进那斜眼睨着的——或者慈悲的——出租汽车夫的手心，等他帮她把弟弟架进狗窝的门。爱德华会烂醉。她有十五个先令付车钱。……

===Original===

If she gave a few coppers more it seemed generous.﻿ ﻿… What a day to look forward to still! Some days were lifetimes!
She would rather die than let Tietjens pay for the cab!
Why? Once a taximan had refused payment for driving her and Edward all the way to Chiswick, and she hadn’t felt insulted. She had paid him; but she hadn’t felt insulted! A sentimental fellow; touched at the heart by the pretty sister﻿—or perhaps he didn’t really believe it was a sister﻿—and her incapable bluejacket brother! Tietjens was a sentimental fellow too.﻿ ﻿… What was the difference?﻿ ﻿… And then! The mother a dead, heavy sleeper; the brother dead drunk. One in the morning! He couldn’t refuse her! Blackness: cushions! She had arranged the cushions, she remembered. Arranged them subconsciously! Blackness! Heavy sleep; dead drunkenness!﻿ ﻿… Horrible!﻿ ﻿… A disgusting affair! An affair of Ealing.﻿ ﻿… It shall make her one with all the stuff to fill graveyards.﻿ ﻿… Well, what else was she, Valentine Wannop: daughter of her father? And of her mother? Yes! But she herself﻿ ﻿… Just a little nobody!
They were no doubt wirelessing from the Admiralty.﻿ ﻿… But her brother was at home, or getting a little more intoxicated and talking treason. At any rate the flickering intermittences over the bitter seas couldn’t for the moment concern him.﻿ ﻿… That bus touched her skirt as she ran for the island.﻿ ﻿… It might have been better.﻿ ﻿… But one hadn’t the courage!
She was looking at patterned deaths under a little green roof, such as they put over bird shelters. Her heart stopped! Before, she had been breathless! She was going mad. She was dying.﻿ ﻿… All these deaths! And not merely the deaths.﻿ ﻿… The waiting for the approach of death; the contemplation of the parting from life! This minute you were; that, and you weren’t! What was it like? Oh heaven, she knew.﻿ ﻿… She stood there contemplating parting from﻿ ﻿… One minute you were; the next﻿ ﻿… Her breath fluttered in her chest.﻿ ﻿… Perhaps he wouldn’t come﻿ ﻿…
He was immediately framed by the sordid stones. She ran upon him and said something; with a mad hatred. All these deaths and he and his like responsible!﻿ ﻿… He had apparently a brother, a responsible one too! Browner complexioned!﻿ ﻿… But he! He! He! He! completely calm; with direct eyes.﻿ ﻿… It wasn’t possible. “*Holde Lippen: klare Augen: heller Sinn.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

再多给几个铜板，便显得大方。……还有这样一天要熬呢！有些日子，本身就是一辈子！
她宁可死，也不肯让蒂金斯付车钱！
为什么？有一回，一个汽车夫不肯收送她和爱德华去奇西克那一程的车钱，她并没觉得受了侮辱。钱她照付了；可她没觉得受辱！一个多情的人；心里疼那标致的姐姐——也许他压根不信是姐姐——和她那不中用的水兵弟弟！蒂金斯也是个性情中人。……差别在哪儿呢？…再者！母亲睡得死沉；弟弟烂醉如泥。凌晨一点！他没法拒绝她！黑甜乡：靠垫！靠垫她已经排好了，她记得。是下意识里排的！黑甜！死睡；烂醉！……可怕！……一桩下流事！一桩伊灵式的事。……这就要把她归进那班只配填墓穴的货色里去。……哼，她瓦伦丁·万诺普本来又是什么：父亲的女儿？母亲的女儿？是！可她自己……不过一个小小的无名之辈！
海军部此刻无疑正给他发无线电。……可她弟弟在家，或者又多灌了几杯，正说着叛国的言语。反正那苦海之上明明灭灭的闪光，这一刻与他无关。……她朝安全岛奔去时，那辆公共汽车擦着了她的裙子。……本来也许更好。……可人没那份勇气！
她正望着一方小小绿顶底下的种种花样的死，那绿顶，就像他们搭在鸟类庇护所上的那种。她的心停了！先前她只是喘！她要疯了。她要死了。……这么多死！还不止是死。……等死临头；想着同生离别！这一分钟你在；一转身，你就不在了！那是什么滋味？哦天哪，她知道。……她站在那里，想着同……离别——这一分钟你在；下一分钟……她的呼吸在胸膛里扑动。……也许他不会来了……
他立刻在那寒碜的石楼中间显了出来。她扑上去，说了句什么；带着疯狂的恨。这么多死，他和他这一类人要负责！……他分明还有个兄弟，也是个要负责的！脸色深些！……可他！他！他！他！全然镇定；目光笔直。……这不可能是真的。“*Holde Lippen: klare Augen: heller Sinn.*…”（温软的唇：明亮的眼：清明的心智。……）

===Original===

*” Oh, a little bit wilted, the clear intellect! And the lips? No doubt too. But he couldn’t look at you so, unless﻿ ﻿…
She caught him fiercely by the arm; for the moment he belonged﻿—more than to any browner, mere civilian, brother!﻿—to her! She was going to ask him! If he answered: “Yes! I am such a man!” she was going to say: “Then you must take me too! If them, why not me? I must have a child. I too!” She desired a child. She would overwhelm these hateful lodestones with a flood of argument; she imagined﻿—she felt﻿—the words going between her lips.﻿ ﻿… She imagined her fainting mind; her consenting limbs.﻿ ﻿…
His looks were wandering round the cornice of these stone buildings. Immediately she was Valentine Wannop again; it needed no word from him. Words passed, but words could no more prove an established innocence than words can enhance a love that exists. He might as well have recited the names of railway stations. His eyes, his unconcerned face, his tranquil shoulders; they were what acquitted him. The greatest love speech he had ever and could ever make her was when, harshly and angrily, he said something like:
“Certainly not. I imagined you knew me better”﻿—brushing her aside as if she had been a midge. And, thank God, he had hardly listened to her!
She was Valentine Wannop again; in the sunlight the chaffinches said “Pink! pink!” The seed-heads of the tall grasses were brushing against her skirt. She was clean-limbed, clearheaded.﻿ ﻿… It was just a problem whether Sylvia Tietjens was good to him.﻿ ﻿… Good *for* him was, perhaps, the more exact way of putting it. Her mind cleared, like water that goes off the boil.﻿ ﻿… “Waters stilled at even.” Nonsense. It was sunlight, and he had an adorable brother! He could save *his* brother. Transport! There was another meaning to the word. A warm feeling settled down upon her; this was *her* brother; the next to the best ever! It was as if you had matched a piece of stuff so nearly with another piece of stuff as to make no odds. Yet just not the real stuff! She must be grateful to this relative for all he did for her; yet, ah, never so grateful as to the other﻿—who had done nothing!
Providence is kind in great batches! She heard, mounting the steps, the blessed word Transport! “They,” so Mark said: he and she﻿—the family feeling again﻿—were going to get Christopher into the Transport.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

”哦，那清明的心智有点蔫了！还有那唇？无疑也是。可他不能这样望你，除非……
她狠狠抓住他的胳膊；这一刻，他属于她——胜过属于任何那位肤色较深的、纯乎平民的兄弟！她就要问他了！他若答：“是！我是这样一个人！”她就要说：“那你也必须带上我！既然带上她们，为什么不带上我？我必须有个孩子。我也要！”她想要个孩子。她要用论证的洪流淹没这些可恨的磁石；她想象——她感到——那些话正穿过她的双唇。……她想象自己晕眩的心思；默许的肢体。……
他的目光正沿着这些石头建筑的檐口打转。顷刻间，她又变回了瓦伦丁·万诺普；这用不着他说一个字。话是过了几句，可话既证明不了一场既成的清白，正如话增益不了一场既存的爱。他满可以背一遍火车站的名字。他的眼睛，他漠然的脸，他平静的肩膀：这些才宣告他无罪。他给过她的、此后也永远给得出的最伟大的情话，是他粗暴而愤怒地说出类似这样的话的时候：
“当然不。我以为你更了解我。”——把她拂到一边，仿佛她是一只蠓虫。而谢天谢地，他几乎没有听她说！
她又变回了瓦伦丁·万诺普；日光里苍头燕雀叫着“乒克！乒克！”高草的种穗擦着她的裙子。她四肢匀净，头脑清明。……剩下的只是一个问题：西尔维娅·蒂金斯待他好不好。……对他有没有好处，也许这样说更贴切。她的心思清了，像退了沸的水。……“傍晚止息的水。”胡说。这是日光，而且他有个可爱的兄弟！他救得了他的兄弟。Transport（运输队）！这词还有另一层意思。一阵暖意落到她身上；这是她的兄弟；仅次于最好的那一个！就像你把一块料子同另一块料子配得几乎分毫不差。可到底不是那块真料子！这位亲戚为她做的一切，她该感激；可是啊，永远不及对另一位那样感激——那位什么也没为她做过！
天恩是成大批地降下的！她登上台阶时，听见了那个有福的字眼：运输队！“他们”，马克是这么说的：他同她——又是那份家族感情——要把克里斯托弗弄进运输队去。……

===Original===

By the kindness of God the First Line Transport was the only branch of the services of which Valentine knew anything. Their charwoman, who could not read and write, had a son, a sergeant in a line regiment. “Hooray!” he had written to his mother, “I’ve been off my feed; recommended for the D.C.M. too. So they’re putting me senior N.C.O. of First Line Transport for a rest; the safest soft job of the whole bally front line caboodle!” Valentine had had to read this letter in the scullery amongst black-beetles. Aloud! She had hated reading it as she had hated reading anything that gave details of the front line. But charity begins surely with the char! She had had to. Now she could thank God. The sergeant, in direct, perfectly sincere language, to comfort his mother, had described his daily work, detailing horses and G.S. limber wagons for jobs and superintending the horse-standings. “Why,” one sentence ran, “our O.C. Transport is one of those fishing lunatics. Wherever we go he has a space of grass cleared out and pegged and b﻿—﻿—﻿y hell to the man who walks across it!” There the O.C. practised casting with trout and salmon rods by the hour together. “That’ll show you what a soft job it is!” the sergeant had finished triumphantly.﻿ ﻿…
So that there she, Valentine Wannop, sat on a hard bench against a wall; downright, healthy middle-class﻿—or perhaps upper middle-class﻿—for the Wannops were, if impoverished, yet of ancient family! Over her sensible, moccasined shoes the tide of humanity flowed before her hard bench. There were two commissionaires, the one always benevolent, the other perpetually querulous, in a pulpit on one side of her; on the other, a brown-visaged sort of brother-in-law with bulging eyes, who in his shy efforts to conciliate her was continually trying to thrust into his mouth the crook of his umbrella. As if it had been a knob. She could not, at the moment, imagine why he should want to conciliate her; but she knew she would know in a minute.
For just then she was occupied with a curious pattern; almost mathematically symmetrical. *Now* she was an English middle-class girl﻿—whose mother had a sufficient income﻿—in blue cloth, a wideawake hat, a black silk tie; without a thought in her head that she shouldn’t have. And with a man who loved her: of crystal purity. Not ten, not five minutes ago, she had been﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

托天之福，第一线运输队是各项兵役里瓦伦丁唯一懂得一点的。她们那个不识字的女清洁工有个儿子，在一个团里当中士。“好哇！”他写信给母亲说，“我一直吃不下饭；还得了个D.C.M.的推荐。所以他们要调我去当第一线运输队的资深军士，歇一歇；整条该死的前线行当里最安稳的软差事！”瓦伦丁只好在厨房后间的黑甲虫中间，把这信念出声。她恨念它，像恨念一切讲到前线细节的东西。可慈善总得先从自家的打杂女工起！她不能不念。如今她可以谢天谢地了。那中士为了宽慰母亲，用直截了当、十足真诚的话描写过他的日常活计：为各项差遣派马匹和通用弹药车，督看马栏。有一句是这么写的：“告诉你，我们运输队的长官是个钓鱼疯子。走到哪儿，他都要清出一块草地，打上木桩，谁敢踩过去就叫他见鬼！”那位长官就在那儿一小时一小时地练甩鳟竿鲑竿。“这就可见这差事多软和！”中士得意洋洋地收了尾。……
于是，瓦伦丁·万诺普就坐在贴墙的一条硬长凳上；一个直统统、健健康康的中产阶级姑娘——或者也许中上层——因为万诺普家虽穷，却是古老人家！人潮在她那条硬长凳前、从她那双实惠的鹿皮鞋上流过。她这一边，一个小讲坛似的亭子里坐着两个门卫，一个永远和气，一个永远嘟囔；另一边，一位棕脸膛、鼓眼睛的姐夫模样的人，为了腼腆地向她讨好，一个劲儿想把伞柄的弯头塞进嘴里。仿佛那是个把手。她一时想不出他为什么要讨好她；但她知道，再过一分钟她就会知道。
因为就在那时，她正忙着对付一个古怪的图案；几乎是数学般对称的。此刻，她是个英国中产姑娘——母亲有一份够用的进项——一身蓝呢衣，阔边毡帽，黑丝领带；心里没有一个不该有的念头。还有一个爱她的男人：水晶般纯净。就在十分钟、五分钟之前，她还……

===Original===

She could not even remember what she had been! And he had been, he had assuredly appeared a town﻿ ﻿… No, she could not think the words.﻿ ﻿… A raging stallion then! If now he should approach her, by the mere movement of a hand along the table, she would retreat.
It was a Godsend; yet it was absurd. Like the weather machine of the old man and the old woman on opposite ends of the stick.﻿ ﻿… When the old man came out the old woman went in and it would rain; when the old woman came out﻿ ﻿… It was exactly like that! She hadn’t time to work out the analogy. But it was like that.﻿ ﻿… In rainy weather the whole world altered. Darkened!﻿ ﻿… The catgut that turned them slackened﻿ ﻿… slackened.﻿ ﻿… But, always, they remained at opposite ends of the stick!
Mark was saying, the umbrella crook hindering his utterance:
“We buy then an annuity of five hundred for your mother.﻿ ﻿…”
It was astonishing, though it spread tranquillity through her, how little this astonished her. It was the merely retarded expected. Mr. Tietjens senior, an honourable man, had promised as much years ago. Her mother, an august genius, was to wear herself out putting, Mr. Tietjens alive, his political views in his paper. He was to make it up to her. He was making it up. In no princely fashion, but adequately, as a gentleman.
Mark Tietjens, bending over, held a piece of paper. A bellboy came up to him and said: “Mr. Riccardo!” Mark Tietjens said: “No! He’s gone!” He continued:
“Your brother.﻿ ﻿… Shelved for the moment. But enough to buy a practice, a good practice! When he’s a full-fledged sawbones.” He stopped, he directed upon her his atrabilarian eyes, biting his umbrella handle; he was extremely nervous.
“Now you!” he said. “Two or three hundred. A year of course! The capital absolutely your own.﻿ ﻿…” He paused: “But I warn you! Christopher won’t like it. He’s got his knife into me. I wouldn’t grudge you﻿ ﻿… oh, any sum!”﻿ ﻿… He waved his hand to indicate an amount boundless in its figures. “I know you keep Christopher straight,” he said. “The only person that could!” He added: “Poor devil!”
She said:
“He’s got his knife into you? Why?”
He answered vaguely:
“Oh, there’s been all this talk.﻿ ﻿… Untrue, of course.”
She said:
“People have been saying things against you? To him? Perhaps because there’s been delay in settling the estate.”
He said:
“Oh, no! The other way round, in fact!”

===Chinese===

她甚至记不起自己方才是什么！而他呢，他分明曾显得是一头……不，那些字眼她想不下去。……那么，一匹狂怒的牡马吧！此刻他若走近她，哪怕只消顺着桌面把一只手移过来，她也要退开的。
这是天赐；可又是荒唐。像那老汉老妪分坐一根棍子两头的晴雨小人。……老汉出来，老妪进去，天就要下雨；老妪出来时……正是这样！她来不及细想这个比方。可就是那样。……落雨的天气里，整个世界都变了样。发暗了！……转动他们的羊肠线松了……松了。……可是，他们永远分坐棍子的两端！
马克说，伞柄的弯头妨碍着他的发音：
“那么我们给你母亲买一份五百镑的年金。…”
这事叫人吃惊——虽然它在她周身布开一片安宁——可几乎不叫她吃惊。这不过是迟迟兑现的意料中事。老蒂金斯先生，一位体面人，多年前就许过这个愿。她母亲，一位威严的天才，要在蒂金斯先生生前替他的报纸鼓吹他的政见，把自己磨尽。他要补偿她。他正在补偿。不是以王爷的气派，而是以绅士的方式，尽够了。
马克·蒂金斯俯过身来，捏着一张纸。一个旅馆侍者走上来叫：“里卡尔多先生！”马克·蒂金斯说：“不！他走了！”他接着说：
“你弟弟。……暂且压下了。可够他买一份诊籍，一份好诊籍！等他当上正式的开业郎中。”他停住，把他那双忧郁质的眼晴定在她身上，咬着伞柄；他紧张得厉害。
“现在是你！”他说。“两三百。当然是每年！本金绝对归你自有。…”他顿了顿：“可我警告你！克里斯托弗不会乐意的。他记恨我。我是不吝给你的……哦，多大的数目都行！”……他一挥手，表示一个数字上无边的数目。“我知道你能叫克里斯托弗行正路，”他说。“唯一办得到的人！”又添一句：“可怜鬼！”
她说：
“他记恨你？为什么？”
他含糊地答：
“哦，这一阵什么闲话都有。……当然是假话。”
她说：
“有人说了你的坏话？当着他的面？也许因为遗产一直没结清。”
他说：
“哦，不！倒过来才对！”

===Original===

“Then they have been saying,” she exclaimed, “things against﻿ ﻿… against me. And him!”
He exclaimed in anguish:
“Oh, but I ask you to believe﻿ ﻿… I beg you to believe that I believe﻿ ﻿… *you*! Miss Wannop!” He added grotesquely: “As pure as dew that lies within Aurora’s sun-tipped﻿ ﻿…” His eyes stuck out like those of a suffocating fish. He said: “I beg you not on that account to hand the giddy mitten to﻿ ﻿…” He writhed in his tight double collar. “His wife,” he said, “… she’s no good to﻿ ﻿… *for* him.﻿ ﻿… She’s soppily in love with him. But no *good*﻿ ﻿…” He very nearly sobbed. “You’ve the only﻿ ﻿…” he said, “I *know*﻿ ﻿…”
It came into her head that she was losing too much time in this Salle des Pas Perdus! She would have to take the train home! Fivepence! But what did it matter. Her mother had five hundred a year.﻿ ﻿… Two hundred and forty times five.﻿ ﻿…
Mark said brightly:
“If now we bought your mother an annuity of five hundred.﻿ ﻿… You say that’s ample to give Christopher his chop.﻿ ﻿… And settled on her three﻿ ﻿… four﻿ ﻿… I like to be exact﻿ ﻿… hundred a year.﻿ ﻿… The capital of it: with remainder to you﻿ ﻿…” His interrogative face beamed.
She saw now the whole situation with perfect plainness. She understood Mrs. Duchemin’s:
“You couldn’t expect us, with our official position﻿ ﻿… to connive﻿ ﻿…” Edith Ethel had been perfectly right. She *couldn’t* be expected.﻿ ﻿… She had worked too hard to appear circumspect and right! You can’t ask people to lay down their whole lives for their friends!﻿ ﻿… It was only of Tietjens you could ask that! She said﻿—to Mark:
“It’s as if the whole world had conspired﻿ ﻿… like a carpenter’s voice﻿—to force us﻿ ﻿…” she was going to say “together.﻿ ﻿…” But he burst in, astonishingly:
“He must have his buttered toast﻿ ﻿… and his mutton chop﻿ ﻿… and Rhum St. James!” He said: “Damn it all.﻿ ﻿… You were made for him.﻿ ﻿… You can’t blame people for coupling you.﻿ ﻿… They’re forced to it.﻿ ﻿… If you hadn’t existed they’d have had to invent you﻿ ﻿… Like Dante for﻿ ﻿… who was it?﻿ ﻿… Beatrice? There *are* couples like that.”
She said:
“Like a carpenter’s vise.﻿ ﻿… Pushed together. Irresistibly. Haven’t we resisted?”
His face became panic-stricken; his bulging eyes pushed away towards the pulpit of the two commissionaires. He whispered:
“You won’t﻿ ﻿… because of my ox’s hoof﻿ ﻿… desert.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“那么他们一直在说，”她叫起来，“说……说我的坏话。还有他的！”
他痛苦地叫道：
“哦，可我求你相信……我求你相信，我本人相信……你！万诺普小姐！”他古里古怪地添上一句：“纯得像奥罗拉那阳光点染的……上的露珠！”他的眼睛鼓出来，像一条憋死的鱼。他说：“我求你别为这个就把那只晕乎乎的手套回给……”他在紧硬的双层领里扭动。“他的妻子，”他说，“……她对他没有……没有好处。……她痴痴地恋着他。可没有好处……”他简直要哭了。“你是唯一能…”他说，“我知道……”
她忽然想到，她在这失步廊里耽搁得太久了！她还得搭火车回家！五便士！可那有什么要紧。母亲往后有五百镑一年了。……二百四十乘五。……
马克起劲地说：
“要是现在就给你母亲把五百的年金买下。……你说那尽够给克里斯托弗吃他的排骨了。……再给她定下三百……四百……我喜欢数目确切……镑一年。……本金：日后归你……”他那张探询的脸放着光。
她如今把全局看得清清楚楚。她懂了杜什明太太那句：
“你不能指望我们，凭我们的官家地位……去默许……”伊迪丝·艾塞尔完全对。她确实不能被那样指望。……她太辛苦地装出谨慎正派的样子了！你不能要求人家为朋友赔上一辈子！……这话，只有对蒂金斯才问得出口！她对马克说：
“这仿佛全世界共了谋……像木匠的嗓门……逼我们…”她本要说“到一处。”可他惊人地闯进来：
“他得吃他的黄油烤面包……他的羊排……还有圣詹姆斯朗姆酒！”他说：“见鬼。……你天生是为他的。……人家把你们配成对，你不能怪人。……他们是没法子。……倘若没有你，他们也只得造出一个你来……像但丁的……那是谁来着？……贝雅特丽齐？世上就有这样的对子。”
她说：
“像木匠的老虎钳。……推到一处。不可抗拒。我们难道没抵抗过么？”
他的脸变得惊惶；鼓眼睛朝那两个门卫的小讲坛方向推了出去。他悄声说：
“你不会……因为我这只牛蹄子……就丢下…”

===Original===

She said:﻿—she heard Macmaster whispering it hoarsely.
“I ask you to believe that I will never﻿ ﻿… abandon﻿ ﻿…”
It was what Macmaster had said. He must have got it from Mrs. Micawber!
Christopher Tietjens﻿—in his shabby khaki, for his wife had spoilt his best uniform﻿—said suddenly from behind her back, since he had approached her from beyond the pulpit of the two commissionaires and she had been turned towards Mark on his bench:
“Come along! Let’s get out of this!” He was, she asked herself, getting out of this! Towards what?
Like mutes from a funeral﻿—or as if she had been, between the brothers, a prisoner under escort﻿—they walked down steps; half righted towards the exit arch; one and a half righted to face Whitehall. The brothers grunted inaudible but satisfied sounds over her head. They crossed, by the islands, Whitehall, where the bus had brushed her skirt. Under an archway﻿—
In a stony, gravelled majestic space the brothers faced each other. Mark said:
“I suppose you won’t shake hands!”
Christopher said:
“No! Why should I?” She herself had cried out to Christopher:
“Oh, *do*!” (The wireless squares overhead no longer concerned her. Her brother was, no doubt, getting drunk in a bar in Piccadilly.﻿ ﻿… A surface coarseness!)
Mark said:
“Hadn’t you better? You might get killed! A fellow just getting killed would not like to think he had refused to shake his brother by the hand!”
Christopher had said: “Oh﻿ ﻿… well!”
During her happiness over this hyperborean sentimentality he had gripped her thin upper arm. He had led her past swans﻿—or possibly huts; she never remembered which﻿—to a seat that had over it, or near it, a weeping willow. He had said, gasping, too, like a fish:
“Will you be my mistress tonight? I am going out tomorrow at 8:30 from Waterloo.”
She had answered:
“Yes! Be at such and such a studio just before twelve.﻿ ﻿… I have to see my brother home.﻿ ﻿… He will be drunk.﻿ ﻿…” She meant to say: “Oh, my darling, I have wanted you so much.﻿ ﻿…”
She said instead:
“I have arranged the cushions.﻿ ﻿…”
She said to herself:
“Now whatever made me say that? It’s as if I had said: ‘You’ll find the ham in the larder under a plate.﻿ ﻿…’ No tenderness about it.﻿ ﻿…”
She went away, up a cockle-shelled path, between ankle-high railings, crying bitterly.

===Chinese===

她说：——她听见麦克马斯特哑着嗓子悄声说过这句话。
“我请你相信，我永不……抛弃……”
这正是麦克马斯特当年说的。他准是从米考伯太太那儿学来的！
克里斯托弗·蒂金斯——穿着他那身寒酸的卡其，因为他最好的军装叫他妻子毁了——忽然从她背后开口；原来他已从两个门卫的小讲坛那头绕到她身后，而她一直朝着长凳上的马克：
“走！我们离开这儿！”他这是要脱身了，她自问！往哪儿去？
像送葬的哑役——又像她夹在兄弟俩中间，是个押解之下的囚徒——他们走下台阶；半转身朝出口的拱门；一个半转身朝白厅。兄弟俩在她头顶上咕噜出听不清然而满意的声响。他们踩着安全岛穿过白厅，就是公共汽车擦过她裙子的地方。一道拱门底下——
一片石铺的、砾石的、庄严的场地上，兄弟俩对面站定。马克说：
“我想你是不会握手的！”
克里斯托弗说：
“不握！为什么握？”她自己曾朝克里斯托弗喊：
“哦，握吧！”（头顶上无线电的方格阵她已不再挂心。她弟弟这会儿，不用问，正在皮卡迪利哪家酒吧里灌酒。……皮面上的粗俗！）
马克说：
“握一握不好么？你可能战死呢！一个眼看要死的人，想起来自己曾拒绝同兄弟握手，心里会不好受的！”
克里斯托弗说了句：“哦……也罢！”
在她为这北极式的多愁善感快活的当儿，他已攥住她细细的上臂。他领她走过一排天鹅——或者可能是小屋子；她从来记不清是哪个——来到一张长椅前，椅子上方，或者近旁，有一棵垂柳。他说，也像鱼一样喘着气：
“今夜你肯做我的情妇么？我明天8:30从滑铁卢（Waterloo）出发。”
她答：
“肯。十二点差一点到那家画室。……我得送我弟弟回家。……他会醉的。…”她本想说：“哦，亲爱的，我这样想你。…”
她却说了：
“靠垫我已经排好了。…”
她自忖：
“我怎么会说出这句来？这就像我说：‘火腿在食器柜里，盘子底下。……’一点柔情也没有。…”
她走开了，沿一条贝壳铺的小径，两边是齐踝的小栏杆，哭得伤心。

===Original===

An old tramp, with red weeping eyes and a thin white beard, regarded her curiously from where he lay on the grass. He imagined himself the monarch of that landscape.
“That’s women!” he said with the apparently imbecile enigmaticality of the old and the hardened. “Some do!” He spat into the grass; said: “Ah!” then added: “Some do not!”

===Chinese===

一个老流浪汉，红红的含泪眼，稀稀的白胡子，躺在草地上，好奇地打量她。他自以为是那片风景的君王。
“女人就是这德行！”他以老迈者和硬了心肠者那种显然痴騃的莫测高深说。“有的肯！”他朝草地啐了一口；说：“啊！”又添一句：“有的不肯！”

### VI / 六
===Original===

He let himself in at the heavy door; when he closed it behind him, in the darkness, the heaviness of the door sent long surreptitious whisperings up the great stone stairs. These sounds irritated him. If you shut a heavy door on an enclosed space it will push air in front of it and there will be whisperings; the atmosphere of mystery was absurd. He was just a man, returning after a night out.﻿ ﻿… Two-thirds, say, of a night out! It must be half-past three. But what the night had lacked in length it had made up in fantastic aspects.﻿ ﻿…
He laid his cane down on the invisible oak chest and, through the tangible and velvety darkness that had always in it the chill of the stone of walls and stairs, he felt for the handle of the breakfast-room door.
Three long parallelograms existed: pale glimmerings above, cut two-thirds of the way down by the serrations of chimney pot and roof-shadows! Nine full paces across the heavy piled carpet; then he ought to reach his round-backed chair, by the left-hand window. He reached his round-backed chair by the left-hand window. He sank into it; it fitted exactly his back. He imagined that no man had ever been so tired and that no man had ever been so alone! A small, alive sound existed at the other end of the room; in front of him existed one and a half pale parallelograms. They were the reflection of the windows of the mirror; the sound was no doubt Calton, the cat. Something alive, at any rate! Possibly Sylvia at the other end of the room, waiting for him, to see what he looked like. Most likely! It didn’t matter!
His mind stopped! Sheer weariness!
When it went on again it was saying:
“Naked shingles and surges drear﻿ ﻿…” and, “On these debatable borders of the world!” He said sharply: “Nonsense!” The one was either “Calais Beach” or “Dover Sands” of the whiskered man: Arnold.﻿ ﻿… He would be seeing them both within the twenty-four hours.﻿ ﻿… But no! He was going from Waterloo. Southampton, Havre, therefore!﻿ ﻿… The other was by that detestable fellow: “the subject of our little monograph!”﻿ ﻿… What a long time ago!﻿ ﻿… He saw a pile of shining despatch cases: the inscription “*This rack is reserved for﻿ ﻿…*”; a coloured﻿—pink and blue!﻿—photograph of Boulogne sands and the held up squares, the proofs of “our little﻿ ﻿…” What a long time ago!

===Chinese===

他推开那扇沉重的门自己进去；在黑暗中把门掩上时，门的分量顺着大楼梯送上去长长的、偷偷的耳语。这些声响叫他烦躁。你若把一扇重门关在一个封闭的空间里，门会把空气往前推，就会有耳语；这神秘气氛实在荒唐。他不过是个男人，在外头过了一夜回来。……就算三分之二个夜吧！准是三点半了。可这一夜缺在长度上，都补在光怪陆离上头了。……
他把手杖搁在看不见的橡木柜上，穿过那摸得着的、天鹅绒般的黑暗——黑暗里永远带着墙壁和楼梯的石头寒意——去摸早餐室门的把手。
存在着三条长长的平行四边形：上方微微发亮，往下三分之二处被烟囱管帽和屋顶阴影的锯齿切断！跨过厚重的长绒地毯要走整整九步；然后该到他那把圆背椅，左手窗边。他到了左手窗边的圆背椅。他陷进椅子里；椅背正好合他的背。他想，从没有男人这样累过，从没有男人这样孤单过！屋子另一头有一点细小的、活着的声音；他面前存在着一条半发白的平行四边形。那是镜子里映出的窗户；那声音多半是猫，卡尔顿。总之是个活物！也可能是西尔维娅在屋子另一头等着他，要看他一副什么样子。多半是！无所谓！
他的脑子停了！纯粹的困乏！
再转起来的时候，它是在念：
“赤裸的卵石滩，阴郁的浪涌……”还有，“在这世界争议未决的边疆上！”他尖声说：“胡扯！”前一句不是那蓄胡子的写的“加来海滩”就是“多佛滩”：阿诺德。……不出二十四小时他就要见到那两个地方了。……可是不对！他从滑铁卢走。那么是南安普顿、勒阿弗尔！……另一句是那个可厌的家伙写的：“我们小专著的主人公！”……多久以前的事了！……他看见一摞闪亮的公文箱：题着“*此架留作……*”；一张彩色的——粉的和蓝的！——布洛涅沙滩的照片，还有举起来的方形纸，“我们小小的……”的校样。多久以前的事了！

===Original===

He heard his own voice saying in the new railway carriage, proudly, clearly and with male hardness:
“*I stand for monogamy and chastity. And for no talking about it. Of course if a man who’s a man wants to have a woman he has her. And again no talking about it.﻿ ﻿…*” His voice﻿—his own voice﻿—came to him as if from the other end of a long-distance telephone. A damn long-distance one! Ten years﻿ ﻿…
If then a man who’s a man wants to have a woman.﻿ ﻿… Damn it, he doesn’t! In ten years he had learnt that a Tommie who’s a decent fellow.﻿ ﻿… His mind said at one and the same moment, the two lines running one over the other like the two subjects of a fugue:
“Some beguiling virgins with the broken seals of perjury,” and:
“Since when we stand side by side, only hands may meet!”
He said:
“But damn it; damn it again! The beastly fellow was wrong! Our hands didn’t meet.﻿ ﻿… I don’t believe I’ve shaken hands.﻿ ﻿… I don’t believe I’ve touched the girl﻿ ﻿… in my life.﻿ ﻿… Never once!﻿ ﻿… Not the handshaking sort.﻿ ﻿… A nod!﻿ ﻿… A meeting and parting!﻿ ﻿… English, you know﻿ ﻿… But yes, she put her arm over my shoulders.﻿ ﻿… On the bank!﻿ ﻿… *On such short acquaintance!* I said to myself then﻿ ﻿… Well, we’ve made up for it since then. Oh no! Not made up! Atoned﻿ ﻿… As Sylvia so aptly put it; at that moment mother was dying.﻿ ﻿…”
He, his conscious self, said:
“But it was probably the drunken brother.﻿ ﻿… You don’t beguile virgins with the broken seals of perjury in Kensington High Street at two at night supporting, one on each side, a drunken bluejacket with intermittent legs.﻿ ﻿…”
“Intermittent!” was the word. “Intermittently functioning!”
At one point the boy had broken from them and run with astonishing velocity along the dull wood paving of an immense empty street. When they had caught him up he had been haranguing under black hanging trees, with an Oxford voice, an immobile policeman:
“You’re the fellows!” he’d been exclaiming, “who make old England what she is! You keep the peace in our homes! You save us from the vile excesses.﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens himself he had always addressed with the voice and accent of a common seaman; with his coarsened surface voice!
He had the two personalities. Two or three times he had said:
“Why don’t you kiss the girl? She’s a *nice* girl, isn’t she? You’re a poor b﻿—﻿—﻿y Tommie, ain’t cher?

===Chinese===

他听见自己的声音在新车厢里说，骄傲、清晰、带着男性的硬气：
“*我主张一夫一妻，主张贞洁。还主张不去谈它。当然，一个男人中的男人想要一个女人，他就要。也还是不去谈它。……*”他的声音——他自己的声音——传来，仿佛来自长途电话的另一头。够长的长途！十年……
那么一个男人中的男人想要一个女人……见鬼，他不想！十年里他懂得了，一个正派的汤米。……他的脑子同一时刻说着两句，像赋格的两个主题一句叠着一句：
“几个用背誓封蜡诱人的少女，”和
“自从我们并肩而立，只有手可以相触！”
他说：
“可是见鬼；再见一次鬼！那混蛋错了！我们的手没有相触。……我不信我握过手。……我不信我这辈子碰过这姑娘……一次也没有！……不是握手的那类人。……点个头！……见了，散了！……英国人，你知道……可是是的，她把胳膊搭上过我的肩。……在堤岸上！……认识这么短！我当时对自己说……咳，那以后我们补上了。哦不！不是补上！是赎了……正像西尔维娅说得那么贴切；那一刻母亲正咽气。……”
他，他有意识的自己，说：
“可那多半是喝醉的弟弟。……你不会半夜两点在肯辛顿大街上，一人架着一边，扶着一个两腿时好时坏的醉水兵，去用什么背誓封蜡诱骗少女……”
“时好时坏！”正是这个词。“间歇性运作！”
有一阵那小伙子挣脱了他们，沿一条空荡荡的大街昏暗的木铺路面飞跑，快得惊人。等他们追上，他正用牛津腔对一名纹丝不动的警察，在黑压压的垂树下演讲：
“你们这些人！”他一直喊着，“就是把老英格兰造成今天这般模样的！你们守着我们家里的安宁！你们救我们于卑劣的放纵。……”
对蒂金斯本人，他说话永远用普通水手的嗓音和腔调；用他那粗糙的表层嗓子！
他有双重人格。有两三次他说过：
“你怎么不亲亲这姑娘？她是个*好*姑娘，不是吗？你是个可怜的混——账汤米，是不是？”

===Original===

Well, the poor b﻿—﻿—﻿y Tommies ought to have all the nice girls they want! That’s straight, isn’t it?﻿ ﻿…”
And, even at that time they hadn’t known what was going to happen.﻿ ﻿… There are certain cruelties.﻿ ﻿… They had got a four-wheel cab at last. The drunken boy had sat beside the driver; he had insisted.﻿ ﻿… Her little, pale, shrunken face had gazed straight before her.﻿ ﻿… It hadn’t been possible to speak; the cab, rattling all over the road had pulled up with frightful jerks when the boy had grabbed at the reins.﻿ ﻿… The old driver hadn’t seemed to mind; but they had had to subscribe all the money in their pockets to pay him after they had carried the boy into the black house.﻿ ﻿…
Tietjens’ mind said to him:
“Now when they came to her father’s house so nimbly she slipped in, and said: ‘There is a fool without and there is a maid within.﻿ ﻿…’ ”
He answered dully:
“Perhaps that’s what it really amounts to.﻿ ﻿…” He had stood at the hall door, she looking out at him with a pitiful face. Then from the sofa within the brother had begun to snore; enormous, grotesque sounds, like the laughter of unknown races from darkness. He had turned and walked down the path, she following him. He had exclaimed:
“It’s perhaps too﻿ ﻿… untidy﻿ ﻿…”
She had said:
“Yes! Yes﻿ ﻿… Ugly﻿ ﻿… Too﻿ ﻿… oh﻿ ﻿… *private*!”
He said, he remembered:
“But﻿ ﻿… forever﻿ ﻿…”
She said, in a great hurry:
“But when you come back.﻿ ﻿… Permanently. And﻿ ﻿… And﻿ ﻿… oh, as if it were in public.”﻿ ﻿… “I don’t know,” She had added. “*Ought* we?﻿ ﻿… I’d be ready.﻿ ﻿…” She added: “I will be ready for anything you ask.”
He had said at some time: “But obviously.﻿ ﻿… Not under *this* roof.﻿ ﻿…” And he had added: “We’re the sort that﻿ ﻿… *do not!*”
She had answered, quickly too:
“Yes﻿—that’s it. We’re that sort!” And then she had asked: “And Ethel’s party? Was it a great success?” It hadn’t, she knew, been an inconsequence. He had answered:
“Ah﻿ ﻿… *That’s* permanent.﻿ ﻿… *That’s* public.﻿ ﻿… There was Rugeley. The Duke﻿ ﻿… Sylvia brought him. She’ll be a great friend!﻿ ﻿… And the President of the﻿ ﻿… Local Government Board, I think﻿ ﻿… And a Belgian﻿ ﻿… equivalent to Lord Chief Justice﻿ ﻿… and, of course, Claudine Sandbach.﻿ ﻿… Two hundred and seventy; all of the best, the modestly-elated Guggumses said as I left! And Mr. Ruggles﻿ ﻿… Yes!﻿ ﻿… They’re established.﻿ ﻿… No place for me!”
“Nor for *me*!”

===Chinese===

“是啊，可怜的混——账汤米们，想要多少好姑娘就该有多少！这才叫直截了当，不是吗？……”
而即使在那时，他们也还不知道将要发生什么。……世上有那么些残忍。……他们最后叫到了一辆四轮马车。醉小子坐到了车夫旁边；他非要这样。……她那小小的、苍白的、瘦削的脸直望着前方。……没法说话；马车满路哐当，小子一伸手抓缰绳，就猛地一顿，顿得吓人。……老车夫倒像并不在意；可等他们把小子抬进那黑黢黢的房子后，他们只好把口袋里的钱全凑出来付给他。……
蒂金斯的心对他说：
“如今他们到了她父亲的家，她轻快地一闪身进去了，说：‘外头一个傻子，里头一个姑娘。……’”
他木然答：
“也许归根到底就是这么回事。……”他站在门厅门口，她带着可怜的脸色望着他。随后屋里沙发上弟弟打起了呼噜；巨大的、怪诞的声响，像黑暗里未知种族的笑声。他转身走下小径，她跟着。他嚷过：
“这也许太……不体面……”
她说过：
“是啊！是啊……难看……太……哦……*私密*了！”
他说，他记得：
“可是……永远……”
她急急地说：
“可等你回来。……长住下来。还有……还有……哦，就像当着众人一样。”……“我不知道，”她补了一句。“*应该*吗？……我愿意。……”她补上一句：“你要求什么我都愿意。”
他在某个时候说过：“那自然。……不能在*这个*屋顶下。……”他还说过：“我们这种人是……*不干*的那种！”
她也立刻答了：
“对——就是这样。我们是那种人！”随后她问：“艾塞尔的庆会呢？大获成功吧？”她知道，这并不是不相干的岔话。他答：
“啊……*那个*是长久的。……*那个*是公开的。……有拉吉利。公爵……西尔维娅领来的。她会是个好朋友！……还有那个……地方政府委员会的主席，我想……还有一位比利时人……相当于首席法官……当然，还有克劳丁·桑德巴赫。……二百七十位；全是顶好的，谦逊而得意的古古姆斯两口子在我走时这么说！还有拉格尔斯先生……是啊！……他们立住了脚。……没有我的位置了！”
“*也没有我的*！”

she had answered. She added: “But I’m glad!”

“I suppose the rooms looked lovely.”
He had answered:
“Lovely.﻿ ﻿… They’d got all the pictures by that beastly fellow up from the rectory study in the dining-room on dark oak panelling.﻿ ﻿… A fair blaze of bosoms and nipples and lips and pomegranates.﻿ ﻿… The tallest silver candlesticks of course﻿ ﻿… you remember silver candlesticks and dark oak.﻿ ﻿…”
She said:
“Oh, my dear﻿ ﻿… Don’t﻿ ﻿… *Don’t!*”
He had just touched the rim of his helmet with his folded gloves.
“So we just wash out!” he had said.
She said:
“Would you take this bit of parchment.﻿ ﻿… I got a little Jew girl to write on it in Hebrew: It’s ‘God bless you and keep you: God watch over you at your goings out and at﻿ ﻿…’ ”
He tucked it into his breast pocket.
“The talismanic passage,” he said. “Of course I’ll wear it.﻿ ﻿…”
She said:
“If we *could* wash out this afternoon.﻿ ﻿… It would make it easier to bear.﻿ ﻿… Your poor mother, you know, she was dying when we last﻿ ﻿…”
He said:
“You remember *that*﻿ ﻿… Even then you﻿ ﻿… And if I hadn’t gone to Lobscheid.﻿ ﻿…”
She said:
“From the first moment I set eyes on you.﻿ ﻿…”
He said:
“And I﻿ ﻿… from the first moment﻿ ﻿… I’ll tell you﻿ ﻿… If I looked out of a door﻿ ﻿… It was all like sand.﻿ ﻿… But to the half left a little bubbling up of water. That could be trusted. To keep on forever.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

她答了。她补一句：“可我高兴！”
一阵阵沉默横在他们中间；他们还没改掉那个习惯——总以为得架着那个醉弟弟。那一段似乎绵延了一千个月。……长得足以养成一个习惯。弟弟仿佛在吼：“哈——哈——库里亚什。……”过两分钟：“哈——哈——库里亚什。……”想必是匈牙利语！
他说：
“看见文森特站在公爵身边，真叫壮观。给公爵看一部初版书！当然，对一场——说到底——婚庆来说*不怎么*合宜！可拉吉利哪知道呢。……而文森特毫无谄媚之态！他甚至纠正了拉吉利表亲对‘*卷尾题记*’一词的理解！他头一回纠正一位上司！……立住脚了，你瞧！……而且*差不多*算拉吉利表亲了。……亲爱的西尔维娅·蒂金斯的表亲，顶近的近亲了！麦克马斯特爵士夫人*最老*朋友的妻子。……西尔维娅要去他们在萨里那处——相当朴素！——的小宅子。……至于我们，”他归结道，“他们也在服役，那些只站着等待的人。……”
她说：
“我猜那些房间看着很美。”
他答过：
“美。……他们把那个可厌的家伙画的画全从教区书房挂到了餐厅的深色橡木护壁板上。……一片酥胸、乳尖、朱唇和石榴的煌煌之光。……当然是最高的银烛台……你记得银烛台和深色橡木。……”
她说：
“哦，亲爱的……别……*别说了*！”
他刚用折起的手套碰了碰盔沿。
“咱们就这么一笔勾销了！”他说过。
她说：
“你要不要收下这一小片羊皮纸。……我让一个犹太小姑娘用希伯来文写了：是‘愿上帝赐福你、护佑你：上帝看顾你的出，你的……’”
他把它掖进胸袋。
“护身的那段经文，”他说，“我当然戴着。……”
她说：
“要是咱们*能*把今天下午勾销掉。……那就好受些了。……你可怜的母亲，你知道，咱们上回……那会儿她正咽气……”
他说：
“你记得*那个*……那时候你就……我若不是去了洛布谢德。……”
她说：
“从第一眼看见你。……”
他说：
“而我……从第一刻……我告诉你……我若从一扇门里望出去……四下全是沙。……可是偏左一点，有一小股水往上冒。那水靠得住。永远冒下去。……你也许不懂。”
她说：
“不！我懂！”
“他们那时候在看风景。……沙丘；草皮剃得短短的。……几条不起眼的船；一艘桅杆截短的阿尔汉格尔双桅船。……”
“从第一刻，”他重复。

===Original===

You, perhaps, won’t understand.”
She said:
“Yes! I know!”
“They were seeing landscapes.﻿ ﻿… Sand dunes; close-cropped.﻿ ﻿… Some negligible shipping; a stump-masted brig from Archangel.﻿ ﻿…”
“From the first moment,” he repeated.
She said:
“If we *could* wash out﻿ ﻿…”
He said, and for the first moment felt grand, tender, protective:
“Yes, you *can*,” he said. “You cut out from this afternoon, just before 4:58 it was when I said that to you and you consented﻿ ﻿… I heard the Horse Guards clock.﻿ ﻿… To now.﻿ ﻿… Cut it out; and join time up.﻿ ﻿… It *can* be done. You know they do it surgically; for some illness; cut out a great length of the bowel and join the tube up.﻿ ﻿… For colitis, I think.﻿ ﻿…”
She said:
“But I *wouldn’t* cut it out.﻿ ﻿… It was the first spoken sign.”
He said:
“No it wasn’t.﻿ ﻿… From the very beginning﻿ ﻿… with every word.﻿ ﻿…”
She exclaimed:
“You felt that.﻿ ﻿… Too!﻿ ﻿… We’ve been pushed, as in a carpenter’s vise.﻿ ﻿… We couldn’t have got away.﻿ ﻿…”
He said: “By God! That’s it.﻿ ﻿…”
He suddenly saw a weeping willow in St. James’s Park; 4:59! He had just said: “Will you be my mistress tonight?” She had gone away, half left her hands to her face.﻿ ﻿… A small fountain; half left. That could be trusted to keep on forever.﻿ ﻿…
Along the lake side, sauntering, swinging his crooked stick, his incredibly shiny top-hat perched sideways, his claw-hammer coat tails, very long, flapping out behind, in dusty sunlight, his magpie pince-nez gleaming, had come, naturally, Mr. Ruggles. He had looked at the girl; then down at Tietjens, sprawled on his bench. He had just touched the brim of his shiny hat. He said:
“Dining at the club tonight?﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said: “No; I’ve resigned.”
With the aspect of a long-billed bird chewing a bit of putridity, Ruggles said:
“Oh, but we’ve had an emergency meeting of the committee﻿ ﻿… the committee was sitting﻿ ﻿… and sent you a letter asking you to reconsider.﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“I know.﻿ ﻿… I shall withdraw my resignation tonight.﻿ ﻿… And resign again tomorrow morning.”
Ruggles’ muscles had relaxed for a quick second, then they stiffened.
“Oh, I say!” he had said. “Not that.﻿ ﻿… You couldn’t do that.﻿ ﻿… Not to the *club*!﻿ ﻿… It’s never been done.﻿ ﻿… It’s an insult.﻿ ﻿…”
“It’s meant to be,” Tietjens said. “Gentlemen shouldn’t be expected to belong to a club that has certain members on its committee.”

===Chinese===

她说：
“要是咱们*能*把下午勾销……”
他说，而且头一回感到宏大、温柔、护惜：
“能，你*能*，”他说。“你把从今天下午剪掉——我说那话、你答应的时候，正是四点五十八分之前……我听见禁卫骑兵团的钟。……剪到现在。……剪掉；把时间接上。……这*办*得到。你知道外科上就这么办；治某种病；剪掉一大段肠子，把管道接上。……治结肠炎，我想。……”
她说：
“可我*不肯*剪掉。……那是第一个说出口的记号。”
他说：
“不，不是。……从头就是……每一句话都是。……”
她喊：
“你也觉到了。……我也是！……咱们是被推着走的，像夹在木匠的老虎钳里。……逃不掉的。……”
他说：“老天！正是。……”
他蓦地看见圣詹姆斯公园里一株垂柳；四点五十九分！他刚说完：“今夜你肯做我的情人吗？”她走开了，两只手半掩着脸。……一挂小小的喷泉；偏左。那靠得住，永远喷下去。……
沿着湖边，信步走来，摆着他的弯柄手杖，那顶亮得不可思议的大礼帽歪扣着，燕尾服的尾巴长得晃出去拍打着，在蒙尘的日光里，他那喜鹊似的夹鼻眼镜闪闪的——来的自然是拉格尔斯先生。他看了看那姑娘；又低头看摊在长椅上的蒂金斯。他碰了碰亮帽子的帽檐。他说：
“今晚到俱乐部吃饭吗？……”
蒂金斯说：“不；我退了会了。”
带着长嘴鸟嚼一块腐肉的神情，拉格尔斯说：
“哦，可我们刚开了委员会紧急会议……委员会正在开……给你发了一封信，请你重新考虑。……”
蒂金斯说：
“我知道。……今晚我就撤回辞呈。……明早再辞一次。”
拉格尔斯的肌肉松弛了一瞬，随即又绷紧了。
“哦，喂，我说！”他说。“别那样。……你不能那样。……对*俱乐部*不能！……从来没有这种事。……那是侮辱。……”
“就是存心侮辱，”蒂金斯说。“绅士不该被指望属于一个委员会里坐着某些人的俱乐部。”

===Original===

Ruggles’ deepish voice suddenly grew very high.
“Eh, I say, you know!” he squeaked.
Tietjens had said:
“I’m not vindictive.﻿ ﻿… But I am deadly tired: of all old women and their chatter.”
Ruggles had said:
“I don’t﻿ ﻿…” His face had become suddenly dark brown, scarlet and then brownish purple. He stood droopingly looking at Tietjens’ boots.
“Oh! Ah! Well!” he said at last. “See you at Macmaster’s tonight.﻿ ﻿… A great thing his knighthood. First-class man.﻿ ﻿…”
That had been the first Tietjens had heard of Macmaster’s knighthood; he had missed looking at the honours’ list of that morning. Afterwards, dining alone with Sir Vincent and Lady Macmaster, he had seen, pinned up, a back view of the Sovereign doing something to Vincent; a photo for next morning’s papers. From Macmaster’s embarrassed hushings of Edith Ethel’s explanation that the honour was for special services of a specific kind Tietjens guessed both the nature of Macmaster’s service and the fact that the little man hadn’t told Edith Ethel who, originally, had done the work. And﻿—just like his girl﻿—Tietjens had let it go at that. He didn’t see why poor Vincent shouldn’t have that little bit of prestige at home﻿—under all the monuments! But he hadn’t﻿—though through all the evening Macmaster, with the solicitude and affection of a cringing Italian greyhound, had hastened from celebrity to celebrity to hang over Tietjens, and although Tietjens knew that his friend was grieved and appalled, like any woman, at his, Tietjens’, going out again to France﻿—Tietjens hadn’t been able to look Macmaster again in the face.﻿ ﻿… He had felt ashamed. He had felt, for the first time in his life, ashamed!
Even when he, Tietjens, had slipped away from the party﻿—to go to his good fortune!﻿—Macmaster had come panting down the stairs, running after him, through guests coming up. He had said:
“Wait﻿ ﻿… You’re not going.﻿ ﻿… I want to﻿ ﻿…” With a miserable and appalled glance he had looked up the stairs; Lady Macmaster might have come out too. With his black, short beard quivering and his wretched eyes turned down, he had said:
“I wanted to explain.﻿ ﻿… This miserable knighthood.﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens patted him on the shoulder, Macmaster being on the stairs above him.
“It’s all right, old man,” he had said﻿—and with real affection: “We’ve powlered up and down enough for a little thing like that not to﻿ ﻿… I’m very glad.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

拉格尔斯低沉的嗓音突然拔得极高。
“欸，我说，你可得知道！”他尖叫。
蒂金斯说过：
“我不记仇。……可我腻透了：腻透了一切老娘们儿和她们的嚼舌。”
拉格尔斯说过：
“我不……”他的脸忽然变成深棕色，转猩红，又转褐紫。他垂头丧气地站着，看蒂金斯的靴子。
“哦！啊！唉！”他终于说。“今晚麦克马斯特家见。……他封了爵是件大事。一等的人物。……”
那是蒂金斯头一回听说麦克马斯特封爵；他没看那天早晨的授勋名单。后来，单独同文森特爵士和麦克马斯特爵士夫人进餐时，他看见钉着一张照片：君上的背影正对文森特行什么礼；次日晨报用的照片。从麦克马斯特慌忙压低伊迪丝·艾塞尔的解释——说这荣誉是为某种特殊劳绩而授——的样子，蒂金斯既猜到了麦克马斯特那桩“劳绩”的性质，也猜到这小个子没有告诉伊迪丝·艾塞尔原先做那活儿的是谁。于是——正像他的姑娘一样——蒂金斯听之任之。他不明白可怜的文森特为什么不该在家里得那一点威望——在一大堆纪念碑底下！可是他没有——虽然整整一晚上麦克马斯特带着一条讨好的意大利灵缇的殷勤和情热，从一位名人奔到另一位名人，俯身围着蒂金斯转，虽然蒂金斯知道他的朋友为着他蒂金斯再度赴法而悲伤、惊惶，同任何一个女人一样——蒂金斯始终没能再看麦克马斯特一眼脸。……他感到羞愧。他生平第一次感到了羞愧！
甚至当他，蒂金斯，从庆会上溜走——去赴他的好运！——麦克马斯特还喘着追下楼梯，穿过上楼的客人。他说：
“等等……你不是要走。……我想……”他带着凄惶惊惧的一瞥朝楼上看；麦克马斯特爵士夫人也许会跟着出来。他的黑短须抖着，可怜的眼睛垂着，他说：
“我想解释。……这桩要命的封爵。……”
蒂金斯拍了拍他的肩，麦克马斯特在楼梯上高他一级。
“没事的，老兄，”他说——带着真情：“咱们一起闯荡的日子够久了，这么点小事犯不上……我很高兴。……”

===Original===

Macmaster had whispered:
“And Valentine.﻿ ﻿… She’s not here tonight.﻿ ﻿…”
He had exclaimed:
“By God!﻿ ﻿… If I thought﻿ ﻿…” Tietjens had said: “It’s all right. It’s all right. She’s at another party﻿ ﻿… I’m going on﻿ ﻿…”
Macmaster had looked at him doubtingly and with misery, leaning over and clutching the clammy banisters.
“Tell her﻿ ﻿…” he said﻿ ﻿… “Good God! You may be killed.﻿ ﻿… I beg you﻿ ﻿… I beg you to believe﻿ ﻿… I will﻿ ﻿… Like the apple of my eye.﻿ ﻿…” In the swift glance that Tietjens took of his face he could see that Macmaster’s eyes were full of tears.
They both stood looking down at the stone stairs for a long time.
Then Macmaster had said: “Well﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens had said: “Well﻿ ﻿…” But he hadn’t been able to look at Macmaster’s eyes, though he had felt his friend’s eyes pitiably exploring his own face.﻿ ﻿… “A backstairs way out of it,” he had thought; a queer thing that you couldn’t look in the face a man you were never going to see again!
“But by God,” he said to himself fiercely, when his mind came back again to the girl in front of him, “this isn’t going to be another backstairs exit.﻿ ﻿… I must tell her.﻿ ﻿… I’m damned if I don’t make an effort.﻿ ﻿…”
She had her handkerchief to her face.
“I’m always crying,” she said.﻿ ﻿… “A little bubbling spring that can be trusted to keep on.﻿ ﻿…”
He looked to the right and to the left. Ruggles or General Someone with false teeth that didn’t fit *must* be coming along. The street with its sooty boskage was clean empty and silent. She was looking at him. He didn’t know how long he had been silent, he didn’t know where he had been; intolerable waves urged him towards her.
After a long time he said:
“Well﻿ ﻿…”
She moved back. She said:
“I won’t watch you out of sight.﻿ ﻿… It is unlucky to watch anyone out of sight.﻿ ﻿… But I will never﻿ ﻿… I will never cut what you said then out of my memory﻿ ﻿…” She was gone; the door shut. He had wondered what she would never cut out of her memory. That he had asked her that afternoon to be his mistress?﻿ ﻿…
He had caught, outside the gates of his old office, a transport lorry that had given him a lift to Holborn.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

麦克马斯特低声说：
“还有瓦伦丁。……她今晚没来。……”
他喊过：
“老天！……我要是以为……”蒂金斯说过：“没事的。没事的。她在另一个庆会上……我就过去……”
麦克马斯特狐疑而凄惶地望着他，探着身，攥着黏潮的栏杆。
“告诉她……”他说……“上帝！你也许会阵亡。……我求你……我求你相信……我会……当我眼珠子一样。……”蒂金斯飞快扫过他的脸，看见麦克马斯特眼里满是泪。
两人垂目望着石阶，站了很久。
然后麦克马斯特说过：“那么……”
蒂金斯说过：“那么……”可他没能看麦克马斯特的眼睛，虽然他感到朋友的眼睛在可怜地探着他自己的脸。……“一条后楼梯的脱身”，他想过；你竟不敢正眼看一个你从此再也见不到的人，怪事！
“可是老天在上，”等他的脑子回到面前这姑娘身上，他狠狠对自己说，“这一回可不能又是后楼梯脱身。……我必须告诉她。……我豁出去也要开这个口。……”
她拿手帕掩着脸。
“我老是哭，”她说。……“一小股冒水的泉，靠得住，一直冒。……”
他左右看了看。拉格尔斯，或者哪位假牙不合口的某将军，*准*会打这儿过。煤烟熏黑的街头树影里，街上空空荡荡，寂然无声。她正望着他。他不知道自己沉默了多久，不知道自己方才去了哪里；难耐的波涛一浪一浪把他推向她。
过了很久他说：
“那么……”
她退后一步。她说：
“我不看你走远。……看着人走远是不吉利的。……可我永远不会……我永远不会把你那天说的话从记忆里剪掉……”她走了；门关上了。他想过她永远不会从记忆里剪掉的会是什么。是他那天下午求她做他的情人？……
他在旧部的大门外面搭上了一辆运输卡车，捎他到了霍尔本。……
